# Version control for hundreds of AI coding agents working in parallel on one repository — Research Report

*Outline created 2026-11-24. Time range of the web search: since January 2026 for new items; current state for older tools (jj, Sapling, P4, worktrees). 29 items, 43 fields each.*

## Summary

*Hand-written from the 29 result files. Numbers are self-reported by the maker unless I say otherwise. What I checked myself against primary sources is listed under "What was checked". Nobody ran these tools under load; the only measurement of our own is a worktree timing on one Windows machine.*

**Question.** Which version control fits several hundred AI agents that work and push in parallel on one repository, with few conflicts and fast synchronisation, operated by the agents themselves or managed for them? Scion, Anthropic's answer, is not available. Scenario: 5-30 agents today, several hundred as the target, Git compatibility open.

### Short answer

1. **No publicly available tool has been shown to land changes from hundreds of agents on one repository.** Systems that work at that scale (Scion, Pando, Mononoke at Meta) are internal, or open source without support. Public figures at 100 or more writers are synthetic and self-reported: GitHub 5,000 agents in an internal test, Veil 10,000 simulated clients at 207 landings/s, bnsd55/openscion 200 writers, gitai 100 agents.
2. **Scion is not a conflict solver.** It makes landing and serving fast. Textual conflicts are stopped before main by a check Anthropic has not described (the author on Hacker News: they are never allowed); semantic conflicts surface in tests after the merge and Claude fixes them. No conflict rate is published. Fewer conflicts and faster synchronisation are separate problems and need separate measures.
3. **Today (5-30 agents): stay on Git.** One worktree or clone per agent, one owner per file or module, a merge queue as the only way onto main, tests on the merged tree.
4. **For hundreds:** move landing from the agents to a server, test after landing with bisect and revert, replace worktrees on one disk with sandboxes, and choose a host that takes the push rate. No single product covers all of that now.
5. **Closest available things to the question:** AVP (tasks instead of branches, each landing proven on the exact head, MCP server, Git mirror; built for 2-64 agents) and gitai (a Scion-style server with Git objects underneath; pre-1.0, one maintainer). Pilot AVP beside Git; try gitai only in a disposable repository.

### Which layer solves which problem

| Problem | What helps | What does not |
|---------|------------|---------------|
| Agents overwrite each other's files | worktree or clone per agent (today), sandbox or copy-on-write workspace (hundreds) | one shared checkout with locks |
| Two agents do the same task | planner, claims on tasks, one owner per module | one shared task file behind a lock |
| Textual conflicts | partitioning, small short-lived changes, split hot files, structural merge driver | a faster Git host, a merge queue, jj |
| Semantic conflicts | tests on the merged tree, interfaces published before the work starts | file locks, a clean textual merge |
| Push, rebase, push again loops | a server or queue that rebases and moves main | better prompts |
| Broken main | queue with batching and bisection, or post-merge tests with automatic revert and a known-good pointer | tests on each branch alone |
| Push and clone throughput | Git storage built for agents, partial clone, batched pushes | conflict tools |

### Variant 1: Git-compatible (recommended)

Do now:

- **Isolation.** One worktree per agent: create them under a lock, commit and push before any cleanup, give each its own ports and database. LayerFS: wait. BranchFS: skip.
- **Ownership before writing.** A planner gives each agent whole files or modules and its own failing test. Split shared append-only files (changelogs, registries, migrations) into one file per entry. Publish changed signatures before dependent work starts.
- **One way onto main.** A queue without bypass, the full test suite in the queue, cheap checks on push. GitHub's own queue is limited to public repositories owned by an organisation and to private repositories on Enterprise Cloud; a repository owned by a personal account is not eligible. Otherwise Mergify (free up to 5 users and for open source) or Trunk (free up to 5 committers).
- **What an agent does:** commit, push its own branch, enqueue, read the ejection reason as data, repair within a capped number of attempts. Who resolves a conflict is open: CAID and Cursor in January 2026 leave it to the author of the branch, Cursor in July 2026 moved it to a neutral agent. Start with the author, the simpler option, and switch if resolutions lose the other side's work (my judgement).
- **Batch pushes.** GitHub documents recommendations, not hard limits: 6 pushes and 1 merged pull request a minute per repository. Thirty agents pushing once every 5 minutes are exactly at 6 (my arithmetic).
- **Measure for two weeks** before choosing anything else: run `git merge-tree` for every open branch against main and against the others, and log conflicts per 100 landings by size, age and path.

Pilot next, in this order (my judgement: cheapest and most reversible first):

1. Mergiraf or Weave as a merge driver, with tests on the merged tree as the gate. Weave's own replay of human merges removed 32.7% of the conflicts (344 of 1,052) before counting its 86 regressions, and not the advertised 95%. Nobody has published the figure for agent merges. Wrong silent merges are the risk.
2. Jujutsu as the client of a few agents: conflicts as data, easy undo, landing left to the queue. Expect less rebase friction, not fewer conflicts.
3. AVP on a branch mirror, next to the existing CI. Strictly it belongs to variant 2: agents use `avp_open` and `avp_land`, and Git gets a one-way mirror commit per landing. It is listed here because it can run beside Git and be dropped. Elastic License 2.0; macOS and Linux, Windows through WSL.
4. Only for special cases: GitButler for 2-5 agents in one checkout; Agent Mail for a shared checkout, after a licence review (its rider excludes OpenAI, Anthropic and those acting for them); Entire's CLI for checkpoints; Gas City on a side repository.

### Variant 2: agents do not use the Git client

No candidate is ready to replace Git on a real repository; AVP beside Git (variant 1, pilot 3) is the closest. Leaving Git costs pull requests, CI and the tooling, and most of what it buys at 5-30 agents, no push races on main, a queue in variant 1 already gives.

- **gitai** (feder-cr/git-ai, own commands, Git objects underneath, cheap exit): pilot in a disposable repository. Roughly 800-1,900 landings a minute with up to 100 synthetic agents on one machine, self-reported. Clean rebases are tested only after they land; the main branch has been quiet since 2026-11-05.
- **Watch:** Veil (right shape, Git interop ruled out by its owner, weeks old), bnsd55/openscion (alpha), Oak (built and then removed a merge queue and a CI system), Atomic (recording serialised, tested to 8 sessions).
- **Perforce P4:** only if the code is already there. **CarterT27/scion:** design reference, no active maintainer.

### Path to several hundred agents

- **Storage.** 300 agents pushing once every 5 minutes make 60 pushes a minute, ten times GitHub's recommendation. GitHub's rebuilt storage is in private preview with broader rollout planned for 2027; GitLab's is in private beta; Entire is usable under preview terms; Cloudflare Artifacts (1 GB per repository) is a building block; ERSC has no public access. None of them publishes a landing rate on one main, and none lowers conflicts.
- **Landing.** 3,000 landings a day with 12-minute CI need about 25 parallel builds before retries, and exceed 1 merge a minute (arithmetic). First lanes by affected targets and batches with bisection; above that, post-merge tests with automatic revert and a known-good pointer that agents start from. No source shows a commercial queue at that rate.
- **Workspaces.** Sandboxes with a partial clone or a copy-on-write snapshot of a prepared checkout, not hundreds of worktrees on one .git directory (reasoning, not measured).
- **Coordination.** Planner tree, neutral conflict resolver, hot-file detection that blocks and splits. Cursor reports a drop from more than 70,000 to fewer than 1,000 conflicts with this design on one task, but the new run also made far fewer commits, and the version control system it used is unpublished.

### What the evidence says about conflicts

- AgenticFlict: 27.67% of agent pull requests conflict with their base, but only unmerged ones were counted. Do not use it as a forecast. The pull-request rate studies share one dataset (AIDev, five agents, December 2024 to July 2025), so they do not confirm each other independently.
- arXiv 2607.04697: conflicts in 19.8% of same-agent pairs (119 of 601) and 41.7% of cross-agent pairs (48 of 115); the matched comparison gives p=0.078 and the authors claim no cross-vendor penalty.
- MergeGym: 79 of 167 conflicts (47.3%) were between pull requests without a common authored file. A stale base predicts conflict better than file overlap (AUROC 0.873 against 0.694-0.702).
- Meta, ordinary production traffic: 4.9% of 163,655 server-side rebase attempts were rejected for path conflicts (8,076); Meta estimates that a text merge on the server could resolve 57.7% of those.
- CooperBench: success falls from 68.6% to 46.5% to 30.0% with 2, 3 and 4 agents. "Passes Alone, Fails Together": 1 failure in 834 real merged runs against 97% on constructed pairs. No benchmark covers more than 4 agents, and no coordination paper has a controlled result above 8.
- StudioOS, one team with many agents: text conflicts in 1 of 35 open pull requests, while 53% of queue runs failed (38 of 72). Joint failures and flaky tests cost more than textual conflicts there. About 92% of its last 100 pushes bypassed the queue.
- Mergify, about 153,000 merges: share of merges in a failed batch from 0.77% at 2-5 developers to 12.5% above 40.
- Locks: Cursor's first attempt, locks on a shared task file (not on code), cut 20 agents to the output of 2-3. In Anthropic's C compiler, claim files took two thirds of the commits and the same work was still done twice. No source compares locking with partitioning under control.

### Claims to discount

- Weave's "95%", Grit's "0% loss" (its benchmark adds conflicting lines to the Git arm), openscion.tech's marketing, and "over 30,000 merges a day" for Scion (a peak; the 7-day average is about 18,000, read from the chart).
- Entire's 418 pushes a second: pushes to separate refs, nothing lands.
- Veil's 207 landings a second: load-generator clients, and every public CI run was failing when read.
- Gas Town's bisecting merge queue: not found in the running path; its author calls the project retired.

### What was checked

- **By me, against primary sources:** GitHub's recommendations, queue eligibility, stacked pull requests GA (2026-10-06) and the queue incident of 2026-04-23 (2,092 pull requests in 658 repositories); Meta's commit; both Cursor posts; AgenticFlict, 2607.04697, MergeGym and "Passes Alone"; Mergify's report; Weave's benchmark page; StudioOS; the AVP and Agent Mail licences; gastown issue 4469; repository metadata.
- **Everything else** is the research agents' reading of public sources on 2026-11-24 and 25. 35 of 1,247 fields are flagged as uncertain and marked.
- **Not assessed:** `land` (useland.dev), a hosted merge-and-test service on Weave. Where the agents run was not stated, so a missing Windows build did not lower any verdict. Of the tools rated use or pilot for today, worktrees, Weave, Mergiraf, jj, GitButler, Agent Mail and Entire's CLI run natively on Windows; AVP and Gas City need WSL there.
- **Not verified by anyone outside the makers:** how any of these tools behaves with a hundred or more real agents on one repository. A trial on your own repository is the only way to know.

Grit: usable today, v0.4.0 (2026-06-14), no push since; Cargo metadata calls it experimental. | Git: Agent Mail: keeps ordinary Git, adds hooks (pre-commit, optional pre-push, post-commit receipts); ledger in a separate Git archive. Documented incompatibility: Git 2.51.0 index race, with an optional lock to serialise Git calls. | Landing: Agent Mail: none. Agents edit the same checkout and commit with Git themselves, or a human does; the guard only blocks commits touching other agents' reservations. No merge, queue, rebase or tests
25. [Oak](#oak) - 5-30 agents: skip | Hundreds: wait | Availability: Public beta, usable by anyone after free sign-up at oak.space (checked 2026-11-24). | Git: Own protocol and store; the server does not speak Git, so Git clients cannot clone from or push to Oak. | Landing: 1. The agent runs oak start with a summary. It gets a new copy-on-write worktree on a new branch cut from main's current head on the server, with the summary as…
26. [Atomic](#atomic) - 5-30 agents: skip | Hundreds: wait | Availability: Open source and usable today, pre-1.0: v0.22.1 of 2026-11-13, with prebuilt binaries. | Git: Own model, with a Git projection. atomic git import converts Git history (first-parent stream, tree-verified) | Landing: 1. Each writing agent gets a sandbox: atomic sandbox create NAME --from dev makes a copy-on-write working tree and a new draft view parented on dev
27. [Perforce P4](#perforce-p4) - 5-30 agents: skip | Hundreds: wait | Availability: GA for decades. Current server release 2026.2 (2026.2/3151160, 2026-11-18). | Git: None for the main depot: own protocol, client and history. | Landing: Each agent lands its own work. It syncs its workspace, runs p4 edit on the files it will touch, edits, and runs p4 submit on a changelist.
28. [Gas Town and Gas City](#gas-town-and-gas-city) - 5-30 agents: pilot (Gas City); skip (Gas Town) | Hundreds: wait (Gas City); skip (Gas Town) | Availability: Both are public and installable today. Gas Town v1.2.1 (2026-06-06) is the last release; its author calls it retired, its repository does not. Gas City v1.5.1 (2026-10-24) is the current stable release. | Git: Full: ordinary Git repositories, worktrees, branches and pushes. Gas Town keeps a shared bare repository per project with a worktree per worker. | Landing: Common idea: workers never push to main; each hands a pushed branch to a Refinery, an LLM agent session that lands branches for one repository. Gas Town (formula mol-refinery-patrol v16): the worker runs gt done, which pushes the branch and files a merge request bead
29. [CarterT27 scion](#cartert27-scion) - 5-30 agents: skip | Hundreds: skip | Availability: Public source since 2026-10-14. An outsider can build and self-host it, but there are no releases, tags, prebuilt binaries, Docker images or hosted service… | Git: One-way import only. scion-import copies one Git ref at a time into one Scion ref, incrementally and fast-forward only, and keeps Git commit ids so git:\<sha> lookups… | Landing: The agent runs 'scion commit', which uploads missing blobs and creates a changeset that the server sees at once (no separate push), then 'scion land [--ref main]'.

## Left out of the research

- **Mega2, Libra, ScorpioFS (gitmono-dev):** open edition is trunk and storage only, no change list or multi-branch workflow
- **Code Storage (Pierre):** OpenAI announced its acquisition on 2026-11-04; covered under Pando
- **Pijul:** no agent or Git-interop statement; covered as theory under Atomic
- **GoogleCloudPlatform/scion:** unrelated namesake: agent-orchestration testbed
- **Intent and provenance experiments (re_gent, aura, claw-vcs, drift, anubis-vcs, thallium-vcs):** record who and why, do not address landing
- **Small local merge queues and lock servers (claude-code-merge-queue, DeployBot, banyan, mergetrain, mergeloop, SVRF, coord, asynkor, clash):** long tail; mention only where a kept item needs it
- **Freestyle Git, tursodatabase/agentfs, GitSwarm (2610.04862):** storage or inference search, not synchronisation of one repository

## Items

Fields marked ⚠ contain claims the research agent flagged as uncertain ([uncertain] tags are left in the text). They are kept on purpose.

### Anthropic Scion

*Layer: A lab-internal system*

#### Basics

- **Name:** Anthropic Scion
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > Official: https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/redesigning-source-control (also served at anthropic.com/research/redesigning-source-control). No public repository; client command is scn. Not to be confused with: GoogleCloudPlatform/scion (unrelated agent-orchestration testbed, Apache-2.0); scionproto/scion (SCION Internet Architecture, unrelated); independent Scion-inspired projects that are not Anthropic's, e.g. bnsd55/openscion, tinyhumansai/openscion, rizaust/open-scion, jmsbooth/openscion, fenwii/openscion, adrianchifor/scn, feder-cr/git-ai. Design details in those READMEs (for example path-level conflict checks) are not evidence about Scion.
- **Maker:**
  > Anthropic, internal source control team; post written by Nick G. with S.G., R.K., Dan C., Garrett H., Ben L., Colin M., Joe P. Seven people built Scion and Antique (code review) and migrated the monorepo in five months (self-reported).
- **What it is:**
  > A centralised, server-centric version control system that replaced git for Anthropic's monorepo. The unit of work is a changeset, an entry in a write-ahead log: it lists the files changed relative to the first parent, with content-addressed pointers to compressed file contents in blob storage (metadata in Spanner, contents in Cloud Storage per the post's diagrams). The server creates every commit, rebase and merge; nothing is pushed. Blame, path history, commit-graph skip lists and other indices are 'derived data', defined recursively from a changeset's parents through one generic mechanism and computed eagerly or lazily. Clients are assumed always online and ask only for the trees and blobs they want. Three tiers answer identical RPCs: scion-server (authoritative), scion-cache (regional tree and blob cache), scion-daemon (one per node). The post credits Google's Piper and Meta's Sapling, and since the 2026-10-15 update specifically Mononoke's Bonsai data model.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > Addresses: serving throughput (about 25 million CI checkouts a day, self-reported, production), landing contention (commit creation takes no lock and touches no ref), workspace cost (sparse and virtualised checkouts, no local repository needed for many jobs). Does not address: semantic conflicts (accepted, caught after merge, fixed by Claude), task collision (no mechanism described; the author says agents still conflict needlessly and ideas are being explored). Textual conflicts: the post describes nothing; the author says on Hacker News they are never allowed to land, without saying how.
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > Central server with server-side commits, rebases and merges, plus compare-and-swap on one Spanner row per ref. Optimistic landing with post-merge testing, not write-time detection or pre-write claims.

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > Internal only; described 2026-10-13, updated 2026-10-15. Anthropic says it is hard to offer in a broadly useful form and that Scion is not a suitable replacement for git for general use. No code, binary, API or waitlist as of 2026-11-24.
- **Licence and hosting:** n/a: proprietary and internal, no licence published, not offered as SaaS or self-host.
- **Price:**
  > n/a: not offered. Build cost published only as 7 people for 5 months plus an undisclosed amount of Claude tokens; Anthropic notes its internal token spend far exceeds what other businesses would want.
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > Production at one company. Timeline: started as a read-only service that ingested the git repo, then months of hybrid git and Scion operation with a git-to-Scion ingestion loop, then full migration 'in five months' (post). The post's chart shows Antique change requests replacing pull requests between roughly late May and July 2026 (read by eye). Only known user: Anthropic. No version, stars or releases (closed source). No follow-up post on Antique or landing found by 2026-11-24.
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > No data-loss incident, backup design or SLA is published. Durable state rests on Spanner and cloud blob storage; caches and daemons hold no long-lived state. Vendor continuity does not matter to an outsider because nothing can be adopted.
- **OS support:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] Not published. The post mentions developer machines, CI and cluster nodes and a per-node daemon, but names no operating system.

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > None after migration: own client (scn), repository format and protocols that deviate from git's fetch protocol. A git-to-Scion ingestion pipeline and git compatibility existed during the migration. History is still a content-addressed DAG that accepts merge commits (author, HN, 2026-10-16). Anthropic suggests git servers could adopt the same ideas (server-side RPCs, different negotiation, composable sparse and shallow features).
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > Replaces them. Anthropic built its own code review (Antique), code search, source-control IAM and landing tools; the post says adopting Scion would require its own code review, code search and CI, all highly bespoke internally. CI jobs query the server by API (diff against merge base, ancestry, blame) and often need no checkout. The post's chart shows Antique change requests replacing pull requests during 2026.
- **Migration and exit:**
  > n/a for outsiders: cannot be adopted. As a reference: Anthropic migrated a monorepo with almost 100 GB of main history in five months, starting with metadata queries only, then a hybrid period with continuous git-to-Scion sync, then its own client and protocol. Exit path not published. The post warns that diverging from git forces migration of every part of the development lifecycle.

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > The server lands changes. A client or API caller uploads content and asks scion-server to create a changeset; the post's architecture diagram labels the client-to-server control plane 'upload, commit, land', and Antique (code review) is one of the clients. Creating a changeset involves no ref, lock or cross-server coordination, so it is instantly visible everywhere. Moving a ref (main or a release branch) is a separate compare-and-swap on a single Spanner row; the development lifecycle discourages other refs. There is no global repository lock: the post says the team could not effectively replicate the industry-standard global lock on the merge queue and invested in post-merge and pre-deploy testing instead. An Antique merge queue does exist (it has its own monitoring agent) while the post also says a proper merge queue does not make much sense at agent scale; what that queue checks before landing is not published. Landing on main happens through merge commits, a habit kept from git; the team is probably moving to squash and rebase (author, HN, 2026-10-16). The batching, retry and rebase algorithm behind landing is not published.
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > The post publishes no conflict-detection design. The post's author on Hacker News (2026-10-14): textual conflicts are never allowed; semantic conflicts are an unavoidable fact of very large repositories unless merging is fully serialised with extensive validation at the batch tip, which is not practical at a certain commit rate. So textual conflicts are stopped before they reach main by some unpublished check at unpublished granularity (file, path, hunk or line is not stated), and semantic conflicts surface after merge in post-merge or pre-deploy tests. Do not attribute a path-level check to Scion; that comes from reimplementations.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > Semantic conflicts on main: Claude fixes them, 'typically' faster than humans can identify them (self-reported, no numbers). For textual conflicts, what the agent receives (rejection, structured response, markers, automatic replay) is not published.
- **Stale base behaviour:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] Not published. Rebases and merges run on the server, so an agent does not need a local copy of main to replay; median rebase in developer environments is 2.7 s with scn against 13 s with git (self-reported, production). Whether a stale base is replayed automatically, how often, and what a retry costs are not stated.
- **Pre write coordination:**
  > None described. The author says Anthropic generally does not let agents use source control for coordination (HN, 2026-10-13), that orchestrators managing teams of agents are common, and that needless conflicts between agents remain an open opportunity with ideas being explored. No locks, claims or messaging in Scion itself. Scion has an undescribed extra feature: commits bundled into shareable workspaces that track heads dynamically.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > No evidence that conflicts fall: neither the post nor the author's comments publish a conflict rate, a before-and-after comparison, a rate of broken main or a fix time. The only statement is qualitative and self-reported: concurrent changes can conflict, and Claude can typically fix these faster than humans identify them. All published numbers concern throughput, latency and capacity, not conflicts.

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > Server: metadata (changesets, branches, trees) in Spanner, compressed content-addressed file contents in cloud blob storage, no packfiles, no repository maintenance, no fully materialised copy of the repository anywhere. Client: bounded local object cache; sparse checkout by design; virtualised checkout materialises files on demand; many reads and writes need no checkout and go through the API. The client walks from a root tree and requests only missing trees and blobs; it does not tell the server what it has, so any cache can answer and downloads resume. One scion-daemon per node shares objects between jobs and starts from a periodic snapshot. Cache hit rate over 99% per tier; CPU and memory footprint 200x smaller than the previous fleet of git mirrors, several hundred at peak (both self-reported, production). Repository size: almost 100 GB of main history, over 25 GB compressed working copy.
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > No number of concurrent agents or writers is published. Landing throughput (self-reported, production): the text says over 30,000 changes merged per day as of September 2026, against about 2,500 in May; the chart ends at its peak, 30,401 on 9 September 2026, when the 7-day average was roughly 18,000 (read by eye), so whether that level was sustained is not shown. 30,401 a day is about 0.35 merges per second on average (our arithmetic). The chart counts all merged changes and does not separate automation from human-initiated ones. Read side: about 25 million CI checkouts a day (self-reported, production).
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > Production, one company, self-reported by the builder; January to September 2026 charts, medians without sample sizes or percentile spread. No independent measurement.
- **Coordination limit:**
  > No degradation point published. The author says raw write speed is 'not even remotely close to the bottleneck' and scales horizontally (HN, 2026-10-13). The limit the post does admit is validation: testing every change on the exact tip is not practical once hundreds or thousands of changes merge during one test run.

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > Post-merge and pre-deploy automated testing instead of a globally locked merge queue. Whether any CI gates a change before landing is not stated; CI performs about 25 million checkouts a day, which the author puts down to parallel test shards and to testing, building and deploying (self-reported). Batching, speculation and affected-target selection are not described. CI checkout median 3.5 s against 31 s with git (chart); CI got a 25% CPU capacity gain (self-reported, production).
- **Broken main recovery:**
  > Automated bisect and revert pipelines, made practical by cheap API access to history; Claude fixes forward. No known-good pointer, detection time or revert rate published.
- **Review model:**
  > Antique, an in-house review surface with semantic approval rules: comment-only changes are separated from sensitive logic changes so that humans focus on the consequential ones. Adversarial review by Claude is used extensively. For the Scion and Antique codebases themselves, team members still review every change request but look at intent rather than every line; the company-wide policy is not stated.

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > scn command-line client plus server APIs that expose every query the client uses (diffs, merge bases, blame, history, ancestry, blob fetch) and allow writes without a checkout. Claude is by far the heaviest user (author, HN). MCP support, machine-readable output and error formats are not published.
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > Automated bisect and revert pipelines exist at repository level. Every commit is uploaded at once and immutable, so work is not stranded on one machine (author, HN: the commit hash works everywhere). No operation log, undo command or client-side recovery is described. No offline commits.
- **Agent cost:**
  > Chart medians, self-reported, production: total source-control time per change request in agent environments 34 min with git, 3.7 min with scn (x9.2, about 11,000 agent hours a day); new worktree 7.1 min to 5.1 s; pull latest 3.0 min to 9.0 s; commit 18 s to 761 ms; status 4.3 s to under 0.2 s. Developer environments: rebase 13 s to 2.7 s, pull latest 87 s to 3.5 s, commit 1.4 s to 1.1 s. Token cost and the cost of Claude fixing semantic conflicts are not published.
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > Access control on repositories and permissions on individual directories inside the monorepo, with an in-house source-control IAM system. Antique adds semantic approval constraints. Changesets carry author, committer and an optional signature (the post's figure shows Claude as author and Scion as committer, as an illustration). Intent and provenance context sits above source control, mostly in Antique (author, HN). Per-agent identity, session, model or prompt attribution is not described.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > The server. The agent asks for a commit, rebase or land; it does not merge or push locally. What stays with agents is fixing semantic conflicts and failed tests; separate Claude 'superfan' agents watch the health of each component.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Vendor engineering post plus author comments on Hacker News. Detailed on architecture, thin on conflicts. Main weaknesses: self-reported, no conflict metrics, the only chart behind the 30,000 a day headline ends on the peak day, and the prior art was under-credited at first.
- **Independent reports:**
  > No independent user exists. Criticism: (1) sunshowers (led the 2018 team effort behind Mononoke's data model), 2026-10-14 and 2026-10-15: the changeset model and derived data are Mononoke's; credit to Sapling alone was not enough. The author replied on HN on 2026-10-15 and a footnote crediting Mononoke's Bonsai model was added; the critic then called the attribution sufficient. (2) A Databricks source-control engineer (typesanitizer.com, on HN 2026-11-01; personal view): most principles are known practice, Mononoke also skips a merge queue with full CI and Uber's SubmitQueue runs partial CI; several git baselines look too slow, 3.5 s for pull latest looks high for the new system, the 600 PB baseline is not meaningful, automation and human-initiated changes are not separated, and nothing is said about error or undo rates for agents. (3) Hacker News thread, 900 points and about 1,010 comments (2026-10-13): several commenters argue that Perforce already handled comparable commit rates, or that large companies built such systems before and only the speed of deployment is new. (4) Other: secondthoughts.ai (on HN 2026-10-14) calls it a well-documented example of fast, serious engineering (positive); Simon Willison (2026-10-13, neutral) compares it to a reinvention of centralised version control.

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > By Anthropic's own words: truly enormous monorepos with tens of thousands of engineers or swarms of agents. For others it is a design reference for server-side landing and API-first source control.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > Semantic conflicts reach main by design, so main can be broken until Claude or a revert fixes it; this depends on abundant tokens and strong post-merge tests. Cannot be used offline; since commits are made on the server, a control-plane outage would stop them, and one Spanner row per ref is the serialisation point for main (both our inference). Leaving git costs review, search, CI and tooling. Reported engineering pitfalls: agents violated core constraints (for example scanning the commit log linearly) and over-engineered defensive checks until people said no. Reimplementations claim Scion's name without its internals.
- **Minimum useful scale:**
  > Anthropic: only truly enormous monorepos, with tens of thousands of engineers or prolific agent swarms. No merges-per-day threshold given; for reference, it merged about 2,500 changes a day in May 2026, while Scion was being built, on a repository with almost 100 GB of main history.
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Skip. It cannot be obtained, and it solves serving and landing throughput at a scale that 5-30 agents on one repository do not reach. Their conflict problem is the part Scion leaves to Claude.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Skip as a product (no plan to release it); use as a design reference. At several hundred agents the transferable parts are server-side rebase and compare-and-swap landing, and post-merge tests with automatic bisect and revert, obtained through an available tool. It offers no published way to lower the conflict rate.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > Land through a server or queue that does the rebase and moves main with compare-and-swap instead of letting agents push. Keep commit creation separate from moving main, and avoid long-lived branches. Let agents query diff, blame and merge base through an API instead of cloning. Use sparse or on-demand checkouts and a shared per-node object cache. Refuse textual conflicts before landing; catch semantic ones with post-merge tests plus automatic bisect and revert. Give a monitoring agent one component each with access to telemetry. Keep people on design constraints and intent, with risk-tiered review. Migrate in stages: read-only metadata service first, then hybrid, then full.

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > 1) Anthropic Engineering, 'Redesigning source control at Anthropic', 2026-10-13, updated 2026-10-15: https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/redesigning-source-control (text, footnotes, and the charts 'Changes merged per day' and 'Everyday source control commands on the monorepo'). 2) Author comments by nickg-anthropic on Hacker News: textual and semantic conflicts https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50082255 (2026-10-14); agents not using source control for coordination https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50076277 (2026-10-13); needless conflicts between agents https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50078031 (2026-10-13); write speed not the bottleneck https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50079857 (2026-10-13); always-online commits and workspaces https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50078963 (2026-10-13); provenance sits in Antique https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50079521 (2026-10-13); attribution and Mononoke https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50107559 (2026-10-15); incremental build and hybrid period https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50108199 (2026-10-15); landing by merge commits https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50120029 (2026-10-16). Independent: https://sunshowers.io/posts/anthropic-mononoke/ (2026-10-14), https://sunshowers.io/posts/anthropic-mononoke-2/ (2026-10-15), https://typesanitizer.com/blog/scion.html (on HN 2026-11-01), https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50073633 (2026-10-13), https://simonwillison.net/2026/Oct/13/redesigning-source-control-at-anthropic/, https://secondthoughts.ai/p/anthropics-scion-software-development (2026-10-14).
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > Not published: conflict-check mechanism and granularity, stale-base handling, conflict and broken-main rates, OS support, agent counts, token cost. Disagreements: (a) text 'over 30,000 per day as of September' against a chart that ends on its peak day, 30,401 on 9 September, with a 7-day average of about 18,000 (by eye); (b) text 'two orders of magnitude faster' CI and research checkouts against chart medians of x8.8 (CI) and x8.0 (compute job), the largest chart ratio being x207 for status; (c) text 'increased by 10x' against about 12x from 2,500 to 30,000; (d) the post says a proper merge queue makes little sense but names an Antique merge queue. The 'textual conflicts never allowed' statement comes from the author's HN comment, not from the post. Chart values were read from images and checked against their printed ratios; the bar chart of merges was read by eye except the labelled 30,401.
