### OpenAI Pando

*Layer: A lab-internal system*

#### Basics

- **Name:** OpenAI Pando
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > Official: https://openai.com/index/scaling-git-at-openai/. No public Pando repository. Related public code: openai/git (OpenAI's git client fork, not Pando), patch tracker https://oai-git.openai.chatgpt.site. Not OpenAI's: chrishayuk/chuk-pando-server (independent experimental reproduction, Apache-2.0) and ishaanko/pando (independent, MIT). Unrelated namesakes: pandonetwork/pando (archived 2019 git remote protocol over IPFS and Ethereum) and Pando by Pando Team (self-hosted app deployment). Acquisition target Pierre is pierrecomputer/pierre and code.storage, a different product.
- **Maker:**
  > OpenAI; post by Aaron Friel, Taylor Blau and Ted Nyman. Core designed by one engineer, implemented by teams of agents, deployed by a handful of engineers, 10 weeks from kick-off to production with Codex (self-reported).
- **What it is:**
  > A git-compatible server backend, written in Rust, that stores and serves OpenAI's monorepo. Each push's packfile is stored as an immutable blob; each branch move records a 'ref version' (new tip, the pack, earlier ref versions it depends on) in a horizontally scalable metadata store. Consistency is causal rather than repository-wide: each ref is serialisable on its own, and a reader of several branches may see them at different points in time. Fetches do not build a custom pack: the server picks stored packs by the client's haves and wants (O(log delta) metadata reads) and returns packfile URIs served by a regional cache called Express. Background workers compact small L0 packs into larger covers (up to L4 for the monorepo), which also act as a skip list. Pando is one part of a larger set of internal source-control tools.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > Addresses: throughput of concurrent pushes to different branches and of fetches and clones; contention between unrelated refs; reliability of merge infrastructure. Does not address: textual conflicts, semantic conflicts, task collision, or contention on one hot ref such as trunk, which is still guarded by one compare-and-swap-like operation. Workspace cost only indirectly, through faster fetch and clone.
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > Isolate then merge, as in ordinary git: agents commit locally and push branches. Server side: compare-and-swap-like update per ref with causal consistency across refs. 'Conflict' in the post means two pushes racing for the same ref.

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > Internal only as of 2026-11-24. The primary post (2026-11-02) says OpenAI is 'currently exploring ways to share the technology' with its users; it gives no product, date or preview. OpenAI's 2026-11-04 announcement calls Pando its in-house source control backend. Secondary reports quote Greg Brockman on X (2026-11-03) as considering offering it and inviting interest; the original post could not be opened. emergent.sh's claims of a limited enterprise preview and a Q1 2027 rollout contradict the primary post and are uncorroborated.
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > Pando: proprietary, internal, not offered. The client fork openai/git is public under git's GPLv2 (GitHub API reports NOASSERTION).
- **Price:**
  > n/a: Pando is not offered and has no price (2026-11-24). Adjacent and obtainable: Pierre Code Storage list price on 2026-11-24 is USD 0.005 per GB-hour hot, USD 0.0002 per GB-hour cold, USD 0.06 per GB inbound, USD 0.15 per GB outbound.
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > In production since April 2026 and now powering all development in OpenAI's monorepo (self-reported). Only known user: OpenAI. Public activity is on the client side: openai/git created 2026-07-10, 306 stars, last push and release 2026-11-18 (v2.55.0-openai.1772, checked 2026-11-24); its tracker, dated 2026-10-31, lists 50 patches for upstream, 23 topics in the build and 92 commits on top of upstream. OpenAI reports commits landed on master in Q3 2026 up 1,378% on Q1 2024, with Q4 2026 estimated at +2,442% (chart; no absolute counts).
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > No data-loss incident or SLA published. Packs at every level are kept, not garbage collected. OpenAI announced on 2026-11-04 that it will acquire Pierre (Code Storage); subject to regulatory approval; no report of closing found by 2026-11-24. That announcement mentions Pando once as context and states no technical or product link between Pando and Code Storage. Code Storage customers may continue after closing while its features move into Codex.
- **OS support:**
  > Server: n/a for outsiders, internal. Client: any git client works; OpenAI's own git builds are published for macOS, Ubuntu and Windows, each on arm64 and x64 (openai/git release of 2026-11-18).

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > Git protocol and client kept; the backend is replaced. It relies on the packfile-uri protocol feature. By default it advertises only a trunk branch on push, so branches pushed repeatedly or stacked resend everything outside trunk. No point-in-time view of all refs within a fetch. Best performance needs OpenAI's git build (parallel pack download and indexing, 'explicit haves' that let --force-with-lease check a feature branch while building its pack against trunk). Several changes are upstream in git, others only in openai/git.
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > Replaces the previous hosting internally: OpenAI migrated hundreds of integrations that depended on legacy APIs, webhooks and repository downloads, and built its own tools for change requests, code review and automation APIs. CI fetches from Pando through regional caches. The earlier system is not named. GitHub or external CI integration is not described.
- **Migration and exit:**
  > n/a for outsiders. As a reference: Pando first ran as a read- and write-through proxy in front of the old system, keeping both in sync without downtime; clients moved in phases behind an internal wrapper around the git CLI; Codex skills migrated checkouts and services. Developer workflow did not change because the git protocol stayed, which also makes leaving easy in principle (any clone is a normal git repository; our inference).

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > Agents and engineers edit files, commit locally and run git push, as before. Pando streams and validates the pack into blob storage, then one multi-key metadata transaction records the ref version and atomically moves the ref pointer with a compare-and-swap-like operation (O(1) metadata reads and writes; read-your-own-write). Unrelated refs never wait on each other; two pushes to the same ref compete. How a change reaches trunk, that is who rebases or merges, whether there is a merge queue and what it checks, is not described: the post says only that OpenAI built tools for change requests and code review, that merge reliability improved and that the interactive simulation has optional 'Simulate merge' and 'Batch trunk merges' switches.
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > Pando detects only ref races: at push, per ref, when the ref pointer has moved. Textual and semantic conflict detection is left to git and to internal merge tools that are not described.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > Ref race: the pointer update is guarded by an atomic compare-and-swap-like operation; on Pando, clients use --force-with-lease to check that a feature branch has not moved, which is standard git behaviour. The post does not describe what the losing client is told. Merge conflicts: nothing described, so the agent resolves them as in any git workflow.
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > Ordinary git at ref level (our inference from the unchanged client): if the ref moved, the client must fetch and retry; per the post, pushes to unrelated refs do not affect each other. A feature branch is packed against the advertised trunk, so a reader that already has that trunk history fetches it with one pack. Fetch planning cost grows with the logarithm of the distance in ref versions. Behaviour when trunk moved during a merge or landing is not published.
- **Pre write coordination:** None described. Agents work on separate branches; no claims, locks or messaging.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > No merge-conflict rate is published, before or after. What is published is infrastructure reliability, self-reported and production: merge reliability 'at times falling below 90%' before; a chart of estimated daily merge infrastructure success over 37 weekdays shows 28 days before cutover (mean 96.4% by our arithmetic, lowest 87.1%) and 9 days after (99.6% to 100%), subtitled 'Post-merge reliability 99.99%'. This measures whether the merge system worked, not whether changes conflicted. Claims elsewhere that Pando offers conflict-resolution hints (emergent.sh) have no basis in the primary post.

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > Client: ordinary git clone and fetch; sparse or partial checkout is not discussed for Pando. Server: immutable packs in blob storage plus a metadata store; no server keeps an unpacked repository. LSM-style compaction L0 to L4, all levels kept. Regional Pando clusters with Express pack caches (consistent hashing, over 99% hit rate) and a metadata cache. Pushed packs in the monorepo: median under 20 KiB, 99th percentile under 3 MiB. Clone and fetch saturate 10 Gbps links on standard cloud VMs. All figures self-reported, production or internal testing.
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > Claimed: many thousands of clients pushing concurrently without impact, in real-world testing on the monorepo and in simulation (self-reported, no counts or duration). Simulated: 1,000 writers on distinct branches, 10,000 pushes (OpenAI's own label: synthetic teaching models, not production benchmarks). No count of concurrent agents, pushes per second or merges per day on one repository is published. Read side, production, self-reported: nearly 1 PB of git data a day, more than 57 TB of packs in one hour; median push latency down nearly 5x (nearly 80%).
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > Production at one company (self-reported) for push latency, data served and merge infrastructure success; simulation for the architecture comparison. Data-served and merge-success charts cover 37 weekdays, 9 of them after cutover; undated.
- **Coordination limit:**
  > No degradation point published; OpenAI claims scaling by adding Pando servers, metadata units or blob stores. By design the limit is one ref: pushes to the same branch serialise on its pointer.

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] Not published. The post says each push can set several jobs fetching the same commit, and its simulation excludes builds and tests. Pre-merge or post-merge testing, batching and affected targets in OpenAI's monorepo are not described.
- **Broken main recovery:** ⚠ [uncertain] Not published.
- **Review model:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] Not published beyond the statement that OpenAI created tools for change requests and code review.

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > The normal git command line; agents did not have to change workflow. Codex skills prepare existing checkouts and guide agents on the new system. Pando can also be read as an API for arbitrary objects, and automation APIs exist internally; none is public. MCP and machine-readable errors are not described.
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > Ordinary git mechanisms on the client (reflog, reset, revert; our inference from the unchanged client). Pando keeps ref versions and packs at every level, but whether old ref versions can be restored by a user is not stated.
- **Agent cost:**
  > No workflow change for agents according to the post, since git stays; tokens are not discussed. Cost side effect: repeated or stacked branch pushes resend everything outside trunk. No per-agent measurement published.
- **Access scope and provenance:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] Not published: no statement on per-agent identity, per-folder permissions or attribution. Security work mentioned: pack validation while streaming, formal models in Lean and TLA+, conformance tests against git, and agents that red-team git and Pando around the clock.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > The agent or engineer runs git commit, push and fetch; the server publishes the ref; unnamed internal tools handle merging. Conflict handling stays with the agent and those tools.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Vendor engineering post with unusual architectural detail, interactive simulation and charts. Main weakness: all self-reported, no baseline system named, merge and conflict handling not described, short post-cutover window.
- **Independent reports:**
  > No independent user or benchmark. Hacker News 2026-11-02 (272 points, about 180 comments): mostly off topic; one technical worry (2026-11-03) is that validating packs while streaming, without unpacking, puts trust in the client; another commenter counts it as the fifth major post of 2026 on scaling git. Developers Digest and SmartScope (2026-11) read it as a case study, not something installable. emergent.sh published unsupported product claims (limited preview, Q1 2027, conflict hints). SiliconANGLE (2026-11-04) and CIO/InfoWorld (2026-11-06) report the Pierre deal next to Pando without stating a technical link.

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > A very large git monorepo with many writers on separate branches and very many readers (CI, dev environments), where git must stay. For others, a reference architecture.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > Not obtainable. Does nothing for merge conflicts or hot-trunk contention. Relaxed consistency: several refs may be seen at different times within one clone or fetch, so tools that assume a repository snapshot can misread state. Extra bandwidth from resending stacked branches and from clients downloading objects they already have. Full benefit depends on a custom git build. Storage should grow because packs are kept at every level (our inference). Code largely written by agents; OpenAI answers with conformance tests, deterministic simulation and formal models.
- **Minimum useful scale:**
  > Not stated. OpenAI's trigger was a monorepo where fetches, pushes and merges slowed and reliability at times fell below 90%, around the end of 2025, when commits landed per quarter stood at +264% on Q1 2024 (chart, Q4 2025). Far above 5-30 agents on one ordinary repository (our reading).
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Skip. Internal only, and it removes a server bottleneck that 5-30 agents on one repository do not hit; it does not reduce conflicts.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Wait. Several hundred agents pushing to separate branches is the load it was built for, and keeping git would make adoption cheap, but there is no product, date or price; OpenAI only says it is exploring ways to share it. Landing and conflict handling would still need another layer. Track the Pierre (Code Storage) closing and Codex announcements. Code Storage itself is usable today, but nothing published says it runs on Pando.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > Give every agent its own branch and use --force-with-lease for updates; keep branches short and based on recent trunk so packs stay small. Make the server coordinate per ref, not per repository. Advertise few refs on push. Reuse packs and serve them from caches through packfile-uri; OpenAI's client improvements are in openai/git and partly upstream. Migrate through a write-through proxy that keeps old and new in sync. Validate agent-written infrastructure with conformance tests, deterministic simulation and formal models. Measure merge infrastructure success separately from conflicts.

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > 1) OpenAI, 'Scaling Git at OpenAI', 2026-11-02: https://openai.com/index/scaling-git-at-openai/ (text plus embedded chart data: quarterly commit growth, daily merge infrastructure success, daily data served). 2) OpenAI, 'OpenAI to acquire Pierre Computer Company, Inc.', 2026-11-04: https://openai.com/index/openai-to-acquire-pierre/. 3) openai/git, https://github.com/openai/git (checked 2026-11-24) and patch tracker https://oai-git.openai.chatgpt.site (dated 2026-10-31). 4) Pierre Code Storage, https://code.storage (checked 2026-11-24). Secondary: https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50345924 (2026-11-02), https://smartscope.blog/en/blog/pando-openais-git-compatible-backend-cuts-median-push-2026/, https://www.developersdigest.tech/blog/openai-pando-git-backend-codex-scale-monorepo-2026, https://buttondown.com/humnsolutions/archive/openai-cuts-the-wait-to-send-code-changes/ (Brockman), https://emergent.sh/news/openai-launches-pando-git-backend-ai-scale (unsupported availability claims), https://siliconangle.com/2026-11-04/openai-to-acquire-developer-tooling-startup-pierre-computer/, https://www.cio.com/article/4245726/openai-buys-tiny-software-developer-to-improve-codex-2.html (2026-11-06).
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > Not published: trunk landing and merge queue, CI gating, review, broken-main recovery, access control, absolute counts of agents, pushes or merges, the replaced system, chart dates. 'Nearly a petabyte of git data per day' against the chart, whose highest of 37 weekdays is about 842 TB, covers production and staging and has outliers removed. 99.99% merge reliability is a chart subtitle over an estimated series with 9 post-cutover weekdays. Availability: Brockman's X post seen only through secondary sources (X returned an error); The New Stack article refused access. Pierre link: OpenAI states none; code.storage describes a three-phase-commit quorum design and lists 30+ concurrent writes, which differs from Pando's ref-level design (our observation), and OpenAI says only that Code Storage features will come into Codex.

**Unverified fields (4):**

- ci_gating
- broken_main_recovery
- review_model
- access_scope_and_provenance

### Meta Sapling and Mononoke

*Layer: A lab-internal system*

#### Basics

- **Name:** Meta Sapling and Mononoke
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > github.com/meta/sapling (GitHub redirects the old facebook/sapling; the repository dates from 2016 as the Eden project). Site: sapling-scm.com. Parts: sl (client, eden/scm), Mononoke (server, eden/mononoke), EdenFS (virtual filesystem, eden/fs). facebookarchive/mononoke is the old, archived standalone repository. Namesakes: sapling.ai (a commercial language product whose 'on-prem' page turns up in searches about self-hosting, unrelated); npm @os-eco/sapling-cli (appears unrelated, not examined); the film Princess Mononoke.
- **Maker:**
  > Meta (source control team). Mononoke's data model was led in 2018 by the engineer who blogs as sunshowers (Rain), who has since left Meta.
- **What it is:**
  > Three components that form Meta's internal source control. (1) sl: a client forked from Mercurial, works against Git servers or Mononoke. (2) Mononoke: a Rust server whose single source of truth is the Bonsai changeset, a commit stored as a flat list of changed paths plus content ids; manifests, blame, history and Git or Mercurial representations are derived data computed off the write path. It serves Sapling and Git clients from the same backend. (3) EdenFS: a virtual checkout that fetches files on demand (FUSE on Linux, NFSv3 on macOS, Projected File System on Windows). Landing is pushrebase: the server rebases the pushed stack onto the moving target bookmark and moves it by compare-and-swap. Anthropic's Scion post credits Mononoke and the Bonsai model in a footnote that was added in its 2026-10-15 update.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > Addresses: throughput (writes store only changed paths, reads come from derived data), landing contention (server-side rebase so a push race needs no client pull-rebase-push loop), workspace cost (EdenFS and lazy fetching, sparse profiles), and part of textual conflicts (since 2026 an optional server-side 3-way text merge of non-overlapping edits). Does not address: semantic conflicts (deliberately left to tests; some path prefixes are excluded from auto-merge so type checks still fire) and task collision (no claims or assignment).
- **Sync philosophy:** Isolate then merge, with a central compare-and-swap and server-side replay (pushrebase).

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > Split by tier, as of 2026-11-24. sl client: public, released, usable with any Git host. Mononoke and EdenFS: source is public and OSS-buildable, but the README labels both as not supported publicly and for unsupported experimentation only; neither is part of the public GitHub release assets. Meta's production deployment (fbsource): internal only.
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > GPL-2.0 for the main project; website and Interactive Smartlog MIT; some libraries differ. sl runs anywhere. No SaaS and no vendor-supported self-host of Mononoke. What an outsider can realistically run: sl on top of an existing Git host. Mononoke can be built and started locally (backends listed: MySQL, SQLite, files, S3) without configuration docs; a 2024 issue asking for build-and-run docs (#812) is still open, where a Meta maintainer said the code was mostly there for information.
- **Price:**
  > Free (GPL-2.0), checked 2026-11-24. Running Mononoke would cost your own infrastructure and engineering time; no public cost figure.
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > Checked 2026-11-24: 7,270 stars, 417 forks, 246 open issues and 139 open PRs, pushed the same day. 10,403 commits in the previous 12 months, 4,007 of them touching eden/mononoke, 2,795 eden/scm, 2,385 eden/fs; about 300 in the last week. Latest release 0.2.20261103 (published 2026-11-04); GitHub releases had a gap from 2025-05-21 to 2026-03-18, then seven releases between March and November 2026. Commits are exported from Meta's internal monorepo. Known users: Meta in production for the whole stack; outside users are sl-on-Git users, no public outside production use of Mononoke found.
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > Very active and load-bearing for Meta, so abandonment risk is low; the risk is that external needs come second. Public Mononoke CI shows it: the Mononoke Linux workflow last succeeded on 2026-05-05 and was set to manual-only on 2026-10-21 because it failed on every run (#1473, unpinned dependencies against a pinned Rust version); the Mononoke integration and macOS workflows show no successful run in the GitHub run history queried on 2026-11-24. Interfaces are removed without external notice, e.g. EdenFS dropped its plain-Git backing store on 2026-11-05. No data-loss notice and no pending acquisition found.
- **OS support:**
  > sl: native Windows, macOS (arm64) and Linux (x64, arm64) release assets; Git for Windows needed on Windows. Mononoke: Linux is the primary target, macOS secondary, Windows not a target. EdenFS: designed for Linux, macOS and Windows, but the README documents only a Linux OSS build; Linux and Windows CI had passing runs on 2026-11-24, and a macOS arm64 build and startup failure (#1478, 2026-10-26) is open.

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > Client: works inside a .git clone (git commands keep working, with caveats) or in its own .sl mode (git commands do not work). Server: Mononoke serves the Git smart HTTP protocol and LFS and can import and export Git, with Bonsai as the source of truth. Important limit: the sl push documentation says Git pushes never rebase, so pushrebase is available only against Mononoke.
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > With sl on GitHub: sl pr submit creates and updates pull requests, including stacks, and existing CI keeps working. The stacked PRs contain overlapping commits and look wrong on GitHub unless reviewers use ReviewStack. Git LFS and submodules are only partly supported in .git mode. With Mononoke: GitHub PRs and CI triggers are replaced; Meta's own review and landing tools (Phabricator, Landcastle, Sandcastle) are referenced throughout the code but are not part of the open-source package.
- **Migration and exit:**
  > sl: near-zero cost and fully reversible; one developer or agent can use it on an existing Git repository, and leaving means using git again (mutation history kept under .git/sl is lost). EdenFS for a Git repository: experimental, needs a source build and the .sl mode; the older plain-Git mount was removed on 2026-11-05. Mononoke: high cost, since you must build, configure, operate and secure a server with no configuration docs, and replace review and CI triggers. Exit exists through gitexport or Git clone; recorded copies and renames are not carried in Git commits. A public wrinkle: the in-repo local-testing guide calls bootstrap scripts under a facebook/ directory that is not exported.

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > Tier 1, Meta production (Mononoke): the client pushes a commit stack to a bookmark; the server finds the common ancestor, checks conflicts between the stack's paths and everything landed since, rewrites the stack on top of the current head, runs hooks and moves the bookmark in one transaction by compare-and-swap. If the bookmark moved meanwhile, the server retries itself (limit 100 attempts in the code) and re-checks only the new delta. On fbsource all lands go through the land service, which queues per bookmark and batches requests; conflict information is precomputed before queueing so the critical section stays short. A pessimistic per-bookmark lock was tried from April 2026 and removed on 2026-09-28: consistent routing plus batching made it unnecessary. Further 2026 additions: multi-repo landing, and replay from the fork point when a stack's parent landed under another identity (about 30 affected lands a day before the fix; knob still at 0 when the change landed on 2026-10-11). Tier 2, OSS Mononoke: the land service was moved into the exported tree on 2026-11-02 with a smoke test that lands a stack; clients reach it over a loopback host:port only, and every request gets one local identity with full authorisation. Tier 3, sl on a Git server: no server-side landing; the agent rebases and pushes, or uses the host's pull requests and merge queue.
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > At landing, on the server; nothing before or during writing. Two layers. Layer 1, always on: path level. The same file touched on both sides, or one path being a prefix of the other, counts as a conflict, even when the edited lines differ; case-folding conflicts are checked too. Layer 2, optional since March 2026: for path conflicts the server tries a 3-way text merge, so non-overlapping edits in one file land and only overlapping line regions reject. A commit message of 2026-05-06 says this had been at 100% on fbsource since 2026-04-20, followed by a 50/50 experiment that ended by 2026-09-01. Guards: caps on file count and size (initially 10 files and 10 MB per file, configurable; a later commit mentions 20 MB), no merge for copies, type changes or path-prefix conflicts, excluded path prefixes, and rejection of commits that become empty. In an OSS build layer 2 is off: the public JustKnobs stub returns false for booleans and 0 for numbers unless a knob file or a per-knob override is supplied (read from source, not run). No semantic or entity-level detection.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > Clean path-disjoint stacks: the server rebases. Non-overlapping edits in the same file: the server merges when layer 2 is on. Real overlaps: the push is rejected with the list of conflicting paths, nothing is written, and the client must pull, rebase, resolve and push again. Since 2026-10-27 a caller may ask per push that conflicting regions resolve to the pushed side (meant for bot-generated artifacts; off unless enabled per repository; delete-versus-modify still rejects). On the client, sl restack stops on a conflict and leaves the commits where they were. Conflicts are not stored as data as in jj.
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > The central strength. A stale base is normal: the server rebases without any client round trip as long as paths, or with layer 2 lines, do not overlap. Limits: the server gives up after 100 attempts; a stack whose base is too far behind is rejected as root too far behind. Meta reported on 2026-11-12 that this was the second largest land failure on fbsource, about 4,500 pushrebases a week with a limit of 64k commits, and added a manifest-based check behind a knob that is off by default. Through the Git pathway outside Mononoke the agent pays the full pull-rebase-push retry.
- **Pre write coordination:**
  > None for editing: no claims, file locks or messaging. Related mechanisms only: per-directory read ACLs (restricted paths), repository locks, hooks, and sl follow or adopt-style aliases for agents sharing one stack.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > Baseline, measured, self-reported production (commit fc27415ddc, 2026-03-02; 48 hours, 25-27 February 2026; which repositories are covered is not stated; ordinary production traffic, not agents as such): 163,655 pushrebase attempts, 8,076 rejected for path conflicts, 4.9%. Estimate, not measured: 57.7% of those conflicts resolvable by a server-side text merge. Projection (commit 6f5066b688, 2026-03-04): about 2,330 conflicts a day removed, success rate from 90.4% to about 93.9%, 3.2M CI machine-hours and 125K developer-hours a year. Our own arithmetic from the published counts gives about +2.8 percentage points rather than +3.5; the denominators may differ. Outcome: the A/B experiment ran in mid-2026 and its results are published only inside Meta; Meta's own engineers noted in July 2026 that comparing the dry-run control with the live arm was not a valid measure. Second data point (commit 71543c0fab, 2026-09-01, self-reported replay): on an Android prebuilts branch where 99.7% of commits touch one shared JSON manifest, all 216 replayed races conflicted under the path rule and all merged cleanly with Git-style line merge. Third (2026-10-22): about 85 rebase conflicts a day on one prebuilts repository, nearly all from bots bumping the same artifact, which line merge cannot fix. Not published: the realised drop in conflicts, so no measured before-and-after effect exists outside Meta.

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > Virtual checkout: EdenFS materialises files on access and is production at Meta, experimental outside. Without EdenFS: physical checkout with in-repo sparse profiles, lazy fetching only against a Sapling-protocol server. sl worktree (EdenFS or native Git worktrees) was made available in builds without EdenFS on 2026-10-28; whether the 2026-11-04 release binary includes it was not tested. Server: immutable content-addressed blobs (internally Manifold; OSS backends SQL, files, S3) plus a SQL metadata database for bookmarks and mappings, stateless horizontally scaled front ends, derived data computed asynchronously.
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > Production, self-reported in commit messages: 1.3M commits carrying a diff number landed to master in the 7 days to 2026-11-17, with the land rate given as 2.5 lands/s; a commit five days earlier gives 1.3 lands/s for fbsource master, so the two figures have different scopes. In February 2026 the attempt rate was about 82K a day (163,655 in 48 hours). Separately, 111.7M rows for newly created sl commits in 7 days (2026-11-24); these are drafts and snapshots, not lands. The number of concurrent agents is not published.
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > Production at Meta, self-reported through commit messages and repository docs, windows of 48 hours to 7 days. No independent benchmark; maintainers declined to make one (#1069, April 2025).
- **Coordination limit:**
  > Known software limits: 100 server retries, base too far behind (64k commits on fbsource), and contention on a single hot bookmark, which Meta handles by per-bookmark queueing and batching and is moving towards finer linearisation. Not published: the agent count or load at which throughput or quality degrades.

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > Server-side hooks (bookmark, changeset and file level) can reject a land. Tests are outside Mononoke: the code refers to an internal land pipeline (Landcastle, Sandcastle) that rebases, runs post-rebase checks such as Hack type checking and retries the push. Auto-merge is switched off for path prefixes whose downstream checks rely on a conflict signal. None of this pipeline is open source. Not described in the sources read: test batching, speculation and affected-target selection at Meta.
- **Broken main recovery:** ⚠
  > Not part of Sapling or Mononoke. Linear history keeps bisect simple (sl has bisect); the bookmark update log records every move. [uncertain] Meta's automatic revert tooling is not described in the sources read.
- **Review model:**
  > Tool-agnostic. At Meta: Phabricator diffs, reviewer named in commit messages. Outside: GitHub pull requests through sl pr, or ReviewStack.

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > CLI with machine-readable templates (-Tjson on commands such as sl worktree list). Built-in agent support, visible in source: the client treats a process as agent-driven when the CODING_AGENT_METADATA environment variable is set; an experimental check (off by default) makes agents read sl help agent and set an acknowledgement variable before any other command; config limits cap how many files, trees or commits one agent command may fetch and abort with a hint to narrow the query. The help agent text itself is supplied by configuration, so the OSS build ships the mechanism, not Meta's guidance (inferred from the tests). Server API: Thrift source control service and land service; pushes return structured conflicts. No path in the repository tree is named for MCP (path-name check only).
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > Strong on the client: undo and redo of the last commands (with an interactive mode), uncommit, unamend, hide and unhide, shelve, recorded mutation history so descendants restack. At Meta commit cloud backs up every commit as it is made; that needs Mononoke. Undo does not revert what has already landed.
- **Agent cost:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] No published token or setup figure. Known friction: agents know git better than sl, so instructions are needed; the practitioner report cited here added two custom aliases and has worktrees that stay on rewritten commits until they run sl follow. Mononoke would remove rebase-retry loops for agents.
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > Meta production: per-directory read ACLs called restricted paths, enforced by the server on every access, declared in config or in-repo ACL files, plus write authorisation on bookmarks. Agent provenance is recent: a commit of 2026-11-24 forwards a client-supplied agent id into the new-commit log (it was empty for all 111.7M rows in the preceding week) and calls the value self-reported, while authorisation checks use certificate identities. OSS builds: the land service gives every request one local identity with full authorisation and refuses named service identities, so there is no per-agent identity or permission.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > Meta: the server rebases, merges and retries; the agent or its land pipeline only resolves real overlaps. Outside with sl on Git: the agent does it all, helped by automatic restacking.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Vendor self-report, but unusually concrete: source code, design docs and commit messages with production counts. Main weaknesses: outcome of the merge experiment unpublished, no agent-specific figures, and the in-repo pushrebase document is older than the code.
- **Independent reports:**
  > Edward Yang, 2026-03-13 (his homepage, undated, lists Facebook as employer and the post describes Meta's internal tool, so treat it as vendor-adjacent): reports that Sapling stacks with several worktrees suit a few parallel agents on one stack, using two aliases. sunshowers, 2026-10-14 and 10-15 (former Mononoke engineer): says Scion copies Mononoke's data model and that the problem is attribution; the claim that Claude was pointed at Mononoke rests on private correspondence. Anthropic's author replied on Hacker News on 2026-10-15 that the design drew on several sources and that Claude was not simply told to build it, and a footnote was added; the dispute is about credit, not performance. Hacker News, 2026-10-14: former Meta users praise the whole stack and regret that it cannot be used outside. Failed or partial self-host attempts: #683 (EdenFS, 2023), #812 (Mononoke, 2024), #1478 (EdenFS macOS, 2026). No independent evaluation of pushrebase found.

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > sl: developers or small agent groups working on stacked changes over an existing Git host. Mononoke: organisations at Meta-like scale with a source control team, or anyone who wants a reference design for server-side landing.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > Self-hosting Mononoke: no supported path, no configuration docs, CI red and then manual-only, no MySQL failover; in OSS builds the land service gives every caller one fully authorised local identity (the main server takes TLS certificates; its OSS authorisation was not examined). Path-level rule without layer 2 makes any hot shared file a permanent conflict. Auto-merge of disjoint lines can land semantically broken combinations; Meta's own early version lost merge results on retry and needed a fail-closed check (fixed in March and April 2026). A percentage rollout of the merge knob can flip between retries, so Meta advises 0 to 100% per repository. Take-incoming accepts whatever is pushed last without ordering. In .git mode, mixing sl and git commands can leave a detached head or a stale index (#1481, 2026-10-29).
- **Minimum useful scale:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] Judgement of this research: sl pays off from a handful of agents on stacked work; running Mononoke is not worth it below tens of thousands of lands a day plus a dedicated team. No published threshold.
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Skip Mononoke and EdenFS: unsupported, no docs, and a Git host with a merge queue covers this load. Optional pilot of sl as the client for agents that work on one stack in several worktrees; it is free and reversible, but on a Git host it gives no pushrebase, so landing contention stays with the merge queue. Jujutsu fills the same client role with conflicts stored as data.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Wait, and watch the OSS land service work begun on 2026-11-02. The design is the best-evidenced answer to landing contention at this scale, but an outsider cannot rely on it today: no support, no caller authentication in the OSS land service, no passing public Mononoke CI since May 2026. Use it as the specification when choosing or building a landing layer. Revisit when Meta publishes configuration docs or the Mononoke CI is green again.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > 1) Rebase on the server and retry the compare-and-swap there instead of sending a stale-base error to the agent. 2) Detect at path level first, then try a capped 3-way text merge; reject on overlap. 3) Measure first: Meta logged conflict rejections and dry-ran the merge for weeks before enabling it. 4) Exclude paths where a clean text merge hides a semantic break. 5) Queue and batch per branch, compute conflict information before queueing, prefer optimistic retry over a lock. 6) On retry re-check only the delta and fail closed if an earlier merge result is lost. 7) Split hot shared files or give generated artifacts an explicit last-writer policy: in one prebuilts tree 99.7% of commits touched a single shared manifest, so every replayed race collided. 8) Keep a mapping from original to landed commits so stacked work can be placed. 9) Give agents narrow, capped queries with errors that name the next step.

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > README https://github.com/meta/sapling (Mononoke and EdenFS wording, EdenFS build section changed 2026-11-01). Mononoke README https://github.com/meta/sapling/blob/main/eden/mononoke/README.md. Pushrebase doc https://github.com/meta/sapling/blob/main/eden/mononoke/docs/4.1-pushrebase.md. What is Mononoke, Bonsai, Git support, hooks, servers: https://github.com/meta/sapling/tree/main/eden/mononoke/docs. Pushrebase source https://github.com/meta/sapling/blob/main/eden/mononoke/features/pushrebase/src/lib.rs (read 2026-11-24). Commits, all at https://github.com/meta/sapling/commit/\<sha>: fc27415ddc (2026-03-02, measured conflict baseline), 6f5066b688 (2026-03-04, server-side merge, projections), 719a07b8f4 (2026-05-06, 100% on fbsource), 7d9dbaad37 (2026-07-06, measurement caveat), 78ab925a9d (2026-09-01, experiment ends), 71543c0fab (2026-09-01, 216-race replay), 50c7767ed0 (2026-09-28, locking removed), 98f1cb1851 (2026-10-11, fork-point replay), 5899810864 (2026-10-22) and 577784bceb (2026-10-27, take-incoming), b4f3924f44, c10fb4e831 and cd39a05e59 (2026-11-02, OSS land service), d31e4b4eb0 (2026-11-05, Git backing store removed), d38aeb8b56 (2026-11-12, far-behind roots, 1.3 lands/s), e73d35f9e5 (2026-11-19, 1.3M commits in 7 days), 97b4a15547 (2026-11-24, agent id). JustKnobs OSS stub https://github.com/facebookexperimental/rust-shed/blob/main/shed/justknobs_stub/src/lib.rs. Git support modes https://sapling-scm.com/docs/git/git_support_modes. sl push https://sapling-scm.com/docs/commands/push. Issues #812, #683, #1069, #1473, #1478, #1481 in meta/sapling. Anthropic Scion post, footnote 2: https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/redesigning-source-control (2026-10-13, updated 10-15). sunshowers https://sunshowers.io/posts/anthropic-mononoke/ (2026-10-14) and https://sunshowers.io/posts/anthropic-mononoke-2/ (2026-10-15). Anthropic author's reply https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50107559 (2026-10-15). Edward Yang https://blog.ezyang.com/2026/03/parallel-agents-heart-sapling/ (2026-03-13).
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > Document versus code: the in-repo pushrebase document says conflicts are path-level only, while the current code and commit history show a 3-way merge layer; the code is followed here. README versus activity: the Mononoke README still lists Thrift APIs as omitted from OSS and says CI runs, while November 2026 commits add OSS Thrift land service support and the public Mononoke workflows have been manual-only since 2026-10-21. Two land rates (1.3/s and 2.5/s) have different, unstated scopes. Merge caps: the March commit states 10 files and 10 MB per file as configurable limits and an October commit mentions 20 MB, so current production values are not certain. Docs say EdenFS is unsupported in .git mode, while a commit says dotgit repositories mount through EdenFS; not tested. Nothing was built or run for this item. The result of Meta's merge experiment, agent counts and Meta's CI and revert pipeline are not public.

### Perplexity Projects Git forge

*Layer: A lab-internal system*

#### Basics

- **Name:** Perplexity Projects Git forge
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > No product name, repository or API. Described only in the Perplexity engineering post 'Building shared workspaces for people and agents' (2026-10-12), https://www.perplexity.ai/hub/blog/building-shared-workspaces-for-people-and-agents. It is the storage layer behind Perplexity Projects in Perplexity Computer. Not to be confused with Cursor Origin, Cloudflare Artifacts or Entire, which are forges offered to outsiders.
- **Maker:**
  > Perplexity (Se Won Jang, Peter Hart, Kartik Kapur, Kevin Lau, Sidhant Rajadnya, Ishbir Singh, Thomas Wang, Jarrod Bryan, Claire Beamer, Jeremy Yang)
- **What it is:**
  > A stripped-down Git forge in which each Perplexity Project is one Git repository shared by people and agents, for knowledge work rather than code. Five parts: a small CLI in each agent's sandbox that checks out lazily, syncs and submits; a control plane that assigns a repository to a storage cell and issues short-lived credentials, never touching repository data; a stateless Git service (a thin wrapper over Git's own ref discovery, upload-pack, receive-pack and LFS) that decides which submit becomes the accepted head; bare repositories on AWS S3Files, a filesystem view over S3; and workers that parse changed documents outside the repository. DynamoDB holds placement, accepted heads, locks and an outbox of accepted writes. It has no pull requests, branch management or notifications.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > Addresses: lost or overwritten work (a submit on an old head is refused), workspace cost (lazy checkout, LFS pointers), persistence and history across sessions, service scale-out. Partly addresses landing contention, through one serialised accepted head per project. Does not address: textual conflict resolution (left to the agent), semantic conflicts (no tests or review), task collision (no claims or assignment described), or throughput on one large repository.
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > Central compare-and-swap with replay by the agent, with submits also serialised by a short-lived lock (the lock is stated in Perplexity's X thread and in the blog's figure descriptions, not in the blog's main text).

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > Internal only. The forge is not offered to outsiders; they meet it only as users of Perplexity Projects, which is live (as of 2026-11-24). No release or open-sourcing plan stated.
- **Licence and hosting:** Proprietary, not published. Runs in Perplexity's cloud on AWS; no self-host.
- **Price:**
  > n/a: the forge is not sold. It comes with Perplexity Projects; the help centre says Project file limits are the same on every plan. Plan prices were not checked.
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > In production behind Projects; the post is dated 2026-10-12 and gives production latency figures without period or volume. It says the team began with an off-the-shelf forge server for prototyping and is building its own lighter one; the post does not say clearly whether the replacement is complete. No public code, stars or releases.
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > Repository data is stored durably on S3-backed storage; only unsubmitted work in a crashed sandbox is at risk (self-reported). History is not durable by design: compaction folds older commits into one snapshot and replays recent commits under new ids. Dependence on one vendor and on AWS S3Files. No acquisition or shutdown notice found.
- **OS support:**
  > n/a: server-side; agents run in Perplexity's own cloud sandboxes (SPACE). The CLI's operating systems are not stated and it is not distributed.

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > Git underneath: bare Git repositories, stock Git server commands and Git LFS. Git clients outside Perplexity's sandboxes are not documented; access goes through its CLI, control plane and agent proxy.
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > n/a: not a code host. No GitHub mirror, pull requests or CI are described; contract checks at submit replace review.
- **Migration and exit:**
  > n/a: it cannot be adopted. Users of Projects add and remove files through the product; the help page read here does not mention Git clone, history export or a restore option. The pattern itself (Git plus compare-and-swap on one head) can be copied with little lock-in.

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > The agent commits each unit of work in its sandbox and calls the CLI to submit. The CLI takes a short-lived lock so submits are serialised, sends objects through an agent proxy that checks access, and the Git service accepts the submit only if it was built on the current accepted head and the result satisfies the repository's contract (mechanical invariants, for example large and binary files must be in LFS). On acceptance the head and an outbox entry (repository, commit, user, changed paths) are updated in DynamoDB. A submit on an older head gets a structured stale-base response; the agent re-applies its change to the new head and resubmits. After a successful submit the CLI asks whether compaction is due. There is one head per project: no branches, merge commits, queue or review. Background knowledge writers, interactive agents and people go through the same gate.
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > At submit, at the level of the whole repository head: any other accepted submit makes the base stale, even if the files are disjoint. Nothing is detected before or during writing, and no file, line or semantic check is described beyond the contract. Not stated: whether replay itself detects overlapping edits, and at what level.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > The losing agent gets a structured stale-base response and is responsible for replaying its own change. Not stated: what happens when the replay overlaps with the winner's edit, that is, whether the CLI does a Git rebase, whether the agent sees conflict markers, or whether the model rewrites the change. Silent overwrite is said to be prevented; a wrong manual replay is not.
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > Explicit and cheap to handle: refusal with a machine-readable reason, then fetch, replay and resubmit. Self-reported production: fewer than 2% of submits are rejected as stale; overall submit latency is under 1.7 s at the median and under 8 s at p99. Those latencies cover submits in general; the cost of a replayed submit, including model tokens, is not published.
- **Pre write coordination:**
  > None before editing. A short-lived lock held by the CLI serialises the submit step only; it is enforced at submit time, not a claim on files. Not published: its scope (per project is our guess), whether it covers replay or only the push, expiry, and behaviour on crash. Background memory updates are kept in separate repositories so that they do not compete with project edits, which is partitioning rather than coordination.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > Self-reported production: under 2% of submits rejected as stale (2026-10-12). This is not a conflict rate: it counts base staleness over project files (documents, notes, generated files), with no sample size, period, number of concurrent writers or before-and-after comparison, and submits are serialised by a lock first, so the stale check may be mostly a backstop. How many stale submits were real overlaps is not given. No evidence exists on textual or semantic conflicts, and none for a code monorepo.

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > Isolated sandbox per agent with a local, lazily materialised checkout: large and binary files arrive as LFS pointers that show the real size, documents are read as parsed text first, downloads only when needed, LFS transfers go directly to and from S3 through short-lived links. Attaching to a warm checkout takes about 2.9 s against about 8 s cold (self-reported). A FUSE virtual filesystem was tested and rejected: about 400 times slower on filesystem-heavy work in their implementation, where every request needed a network round trip, and it usually needs privileged sandboxes. Server: bare repositories on S3Files, split into cells of shared storage plus stateless replicas with sticky routing for warm caches; compaction bounds history. Product limits on a Project: 10,000 files, 5 GB total, 100 MB per file (help centre, checked 2026-11-24), so repositories are small.
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] Not published: no number of concurrent agents per project, submits per second or total repositories. The figures show several agents, scheduled agents and people writing to one project. Product limit: 5 contributors per Project on personal accounts, up to 9,999 for owners in an organisation; each may run several agents.
- **Scale evidence type:** Production, vendor self-report, no N, duration or workload description.
- **Coordination limit:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] Not published. Model by this research, not a measurement: if the lock were held for the whole 1.7 s median submit, one project would top out near 35 submits a minute; the real hold time is unknown and should be shorter. Whole-head staleness gets worse with the number of writers even when they edit different files.

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > No tests. Deterministic contract checks at submit (repository stays a valid state and within size rules). Parsing of documents happens after acceptance and does not block.
- **Broken main recovery:**
  > Commit history allows restoring earlier versions, limited by compaction. No detection, bisect or automatic revert is described; the post frames traceability and rollback as goals. The help page read here does not describe a user-facing restore.
- **Review model:**
  > None by humans: the post says pull requests are unnecessary because nobody reviews each change. Mechanical contract check only.

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > A small non-interactive CLI inside the sandbox (checkout, sync, submit) and ordinary files that agents read with grep and directory listings; structured stale-base response. Not exposed as a public CLI, API or MCP server.
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > Roll back through Git history while it is retained; a crashed sandbox loses only unsubmitted work. No operation log or undo command is described.
- **Agent cost:**
  > Self-reported: setup about 2.9 s warm or 8 s cold; submit under 1.7 s at the median; under 2% of submits cost a replay. Token cost of a replay is not published.
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > Short-lived credentials per repository from the control plane; an agent proxy checks access for sandbox requests; projects have owner, editor and viewer roles. No per-folder permissions are described. Each accepted write records the repository, commit, user and changed paths, and history shows who changed what. Attribution to a specific agent, session, model or prompt is not described.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > Shared: the CLI and the Git service serialise and accept, the agent replays when stale. No orchestrator or human is involved.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Vendor self-report in one engineering post plus its own X thread. Main weakness: headline numbers have no sample, period or load, and the submit lock that frames them is missing from the post's main text. The 150-question benchmark in the post measures document retrieval and answering, not version control.
- **Independent reports:**
  > None found. Searches on 2026-11-24 of the web and Hacker News returned only what look like restatements of the post (Complete AI Training, ToolNavs, Welcome.AI; seen as search summaries, not opened) and MemoryLake's 2026-10-13 article, which is marketing for a competing memory product; that article also notes the help centre documents no version history view. A New Stack article on Perplexity could not be read (HTTP 403).

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > Many small shared document workspaces where people and agents write often, files rarely overlap and nobody reviews each edit. Also a clear small example of compare-and-swap with replay for anyone building an agent workspace.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > Whole-head compare-and-swap makes disjoint edits stale; a single lock and head per repository limits landing rate; an agent can replay wrongly with no test or reviewer to notice; compaction rewrites commit ids, which breaks references held by agents and shortens history; attribution stops at the user; lock failure modes are undisclosed; unsuited to code that must build.
- **Minimum useful scale:** ⚠
  > n/a: cannot be adopted. [uncertain] As a pattern it helps as soon as two or more agents push to one branch without review.
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Skip as a product (not available). The pattern fits for documents, notes or generated content at this size, but for code a Git host with a merge queue already gives head checks plus tests; copying the structured stale-base response into a submit script is a cheap improvement.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Skip. Not available, and whole-repository head staleness with a serialising lock and no tests is the wrong granularity for several hundred agents on one code repository: Mononoke's pushrebase, for comparison, rebases on the server and checks at path or line level. Copy the interface ideas, not the landing model.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > 1) Refuse stale submits with a structured response that says what moved, so the agent can replay without parsing Git output. 2) Make one CLI call do lock, check and submit. 3) Put accepted writes in an outbox with commit, user and paths so other systems react without polling. 4) Keep high-frequency background writes (memory, parsed text, generated artifacts) out of the shared repository; parsed outputs are keyed by content hash and never committed. 5) Enforce a mechanical repository contract at submit, including LFS for large files. 6) Use one ephemeral sandbox per agent with a lazy checkout, and measure before choosing a virtual filesystem. 7) Compact history only where nobody relies on stable commit ids.

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > Perplexity engineering post, 2026-10-12: https://www.perplexity.ai/hub/blog/building-shared-workspaces-for-people-and-agents (full text and figure descriptions read 2026-11-24). Perplexity X thread, 2026-10-12: https://x.com/perplexity_ai/status/2109706972723609733 (opening post) and https://x.com/perplexity_ai/status/2109707014771531952 (submit lock). Perplexity help centre on Projects, limits and roles, undated, read 2026-11-24: https://www.perplexity.ai/help-center/en/articles/10352961-what-are-projects. Secondary: MemoryLake, 2026-10-13, vendor marketing: https://www.memorylake.ai/en/blogs/perplexity-projects-git-repo.
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > Blog versus thread: the post's text describes only the head check, while the X thread and the figure descriptions show a lock that serialises submits; its scope and hold time are unknown. Not stated anywhere read: number of writers behind the under-2% figure, whether replay is a Git rebase or a model rewrite, what happens on overlapping edits, whether the team's own server has replaced the off-the-shelf one, compaction threshold, outside Git access. It is a project-file workload, so nothing here can be read as a conflict rate for code.
