### gitai by feder-cr

*Layer: B open-source Scion-style*

#### Basics

- **Name:** gitai by feder-cr
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > feder-cr/git-ai (https://github.com/feder-cr/git-ai), product name gitai, internal name and CLI 'graft'. Unrelated namesakes: git-ai-project/git-ai (usegitai.com, AI-code attribution, Rust, about 3,009 stars); tenfyzhong/gitai (AI commit-message helper); yuchenZhangTG/graft (another agent VCS that also uses the name graft).
- **Maker:**
  > Federico Elia (GitHub feder-cr), individual developer, also author of Jobs_Applier_AI_Agent (AIHawk). Four accounts appear in the GitHub contributors list; three outside contributors had pull requests merged (docs, fsync work, Windows CLI).
- **What it is:**
  > A self-hosted Git server for agents, written in Go as a fork of GitLab's Gitaly with GitLab removed ('standalone' mode). It adds gRPC services on top of normal bare Git repositories: UserLand (server-side rebase or merge plus compare-and-swap, with batching), ObjectService (single objects and sparse trees), ChangesetService (ordered changelog that clients list or watch), derived data (blame, path history), server-side search, reviews and per-agent access policy. Optional storage core keeps refs and a changeset write-ahead log in SQLite (one node) or PostgreSQL and packs in a bucket. Companion programs: graft (CLI), Go SDK, graft-cache (object cache tiers), graft-verify (post-merge tests, bisect, revert), graft-resolve (pluggable conflict resolver), graft-superfan (change monitors). The server itself never calls a language model. Its own LIMITATIONS.md says it is 'Not a Scion implementation' (the README calls itself an open-source version of that design): it adopts ideas from Anthropic's public article.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > Addressed: landing contention (main goal, batching plus CAS), throughput of landing, workspace cost (sparse materialisation without a full clone), part of textual conflicts (pluggable resolver and a structured conflict package). Only partly addressed: semantic conflicts (post-merge graft-verify with bisect and optional revert; verify.command covers only conflict resolutions, not clean merges). Not addressed: task collision, because there is no claim, lock or messaging before writing.
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > Central compare-and-swap with server-side replay (rebase or merge) at landing; isolate-then-land from the agent's point of view. No pre-write coordination.

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > Usable today by an outsider as source code: public repository, v0.4.2 released 2026-11-05, pre-1.0 with interfaces that may change. No prebuilt binaries in any release; you build the server with Go 1.26, a C toolchain, Meson and Ninja. No hosted service.
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > Own code is MIT: the LICENSE file is Gitaly's MIT text with an added copyright line and an 'ee/' carve-out, which is why GitHub reports NOASSERTION; the ee/ directory is not in the repository. Open question: builds compile and embed a bundled Git (GPL-2.0). Issue #5 (2026-10-21) claims the project cannot be MIT; PR #8 (2026-11-03, outsider) and PR #11 (2026-11-22, maintainer) both propose stating that bundled Git keeps GPL-2.0 and runs as a separate process; all three were still open on 2026-11-24. Self-host only.
- **Price:**
  > Free (checked 2026-11-24). Cost is your own server, PostgreSQL if more than one node, bucket storage, and model calls if you configure an LLM resolver (maintainer reports about USD 0.0005 per conflict file, self-reported).
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > Created 2026-10-15; releases v0.1.0 (2026-10-15) to v0.4.2 (2026-11-05), seven in three weeks. 457 commits on main. The last commit on main is 2026-11-05; the 2026-11-22 push is only the branch of PR #11, and a work-in-progress branch from 2026-11-05 is marked 'unfinished, untested, do not merge'. Pull requests from outsiders (#8, #9, #10, opened 2026-11-03 to 2026-11-17) are unmerged. 972 stars, 100 forks, 13 watchers, one issue ever filed. Star counts are unstable: Wayback Machine shows 1,032 (2026-10-16) and 1,066 (2026-10-17), the API shows 972 on 2026-11-24, and one article claimed about 1,890 on 2026-10-18 (not verified); cause unknown. No named production user found. CI on main was green on 2026-11-05.
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > Single-maintainer project whose main branch has been quiet since 2026-11-05. Data-safety caveats from its own docs: durability assumes ext4 or xfs, not NFS; S3, GCS and Azure buckets untested; nodes on separate machines untested; v0.4.1 and v0.4.2 fixed bugs where a push rejected to the client could still be recorded in the database. It must track Gitaly security fixes as a fork. Repositories stay ordinary Git, which limits lock-in.
- **OS support:**
  > Server: Linux and macOS, CI on Linux only. CLI and Go SDK also run on Windows (CI builds graft.exe and runs the workspace tests since v0.3.1). Benchmarks were run under WSL2.

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > Git underneath, but not Git-first for agents. Repositories are normal bare Git repositories, commits are Git commits and upstream Gitaly RPCs keep working. Agents use the graft CLI or Go SDK over gRPC; graft does not speak git push or pull. By default agent tokens may not use the Git protocol at all; an operator can allow it, and such pushes bypass the UserLand path with its retries and conflict resolution. SSH and smart-HTTP exist as Gitaly gRPC services and no end-user front end ships: the conformance harness writes its own SSH transport for fetch. In authoritative storage mode a non-atomic push can be refused as a whole where plain Git would land part of it.
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > Replaces rather than integrates. No GitHub or GitLab app, no pull requests, issues, web UI or CI ('Not a hosting product' in LIMITATIONS.md). No GitHub mirror feature for agents: mirror RPCs such as UpdateRemoteMirror exist from Gitaly but are operator-only. CI attaches by following the changeset stream (graft watch --json) or by running graft-verify --once, which prints one JSON outcome and exits 0 (passed or skipped), 2 (test failed) or 1 (error). Own review service with approval rules by file class, but landing does not check reviews yet.
- **Migration and exit:**
  > Adoption: build and run a server, create a repository (graft repo create) or import, issue one token file per agent, write a TOML policy. Partial adoption is possible: run with the storage core off (plain repositories on disk), then shadow, then authoritative. Exit is cheap for code because the store is ordinary Git. Lost on exit: changeset log, reviews and land-identity records; leaving authoritative mode has no export of the changelog and reviews into files, so sequences and review IDs restart at 1. Agents must be retrained from git to graft commands.

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > Server lands, agent requests. The agent writes a commit to a temporary branch refs/heads/graft/\<agent>/\<random> (UserCommitFiles) and calls UserLand with strategy rebase (default), merge or ff_only. The server reads the tip; fast-forwards if possible; answers already_landed if the commit, or an earlier rebased copy of it (land identity), is on the branch; otherwise rebases or merges onto the tip in a quarantine; checks the per-agent policy; then moves the ref by compare-and-swap. In standalone mode, lands on an existing branch enter an in-memory FIFO lane per branch and per server process: a batch (10 ms window, up to 50 requests by default, adaptive) is stacked on the running tip and published with one CAS; one conflicting member fails alone. A lost CAS rebuilds the batch at most 10 times. Unbatched lands retry up to max_attempts (default 20). Lanes of two nodes do not coordinate, and queued requests are lost on process stop (callers retry Unavailable). There is no pre-merge test gate for a clean rebase: it lands untested.
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > At landing, on the server, by a Git merge (git merge-tree) during rebase or merge, so text-hunk level within files plus path-level cases (delete, rename, mode, submodule). Nothing is detected before or at write time. Semantic conflicts are not detected at landing; graft-verify finds them after landing by running the operator's tests on the tip.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > Default: the land fails with merge_conflict and the agent must fix and land again. If the client sets resolve_conflicts and the operator configured a resolver for that actor and path, the server sends base, ours and theirs to an external resolver (command or HTTP). Deterministic rules (trivial, union, rerere, command) can land directly, or after verification when verify.enabled is on. A language model's answer is only a proposal: it lands by itself only if verify.command passes on the exact merged tree that would land, compared by tree object ID; otherwise the agent gets a structured package (files, hunks with line ranges, object IDs of the three sides, reason, proposals). graft land --resolve --conflicts-dir writes diff3-marked files and graft land --resolved DIR lands the edited result. Without --resolve nothing is written to disk (PR #10, open, would change that). Resolutions carry Graft-Resolved-By and Graft-Checked-By trailers.
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > No rejection merely because main moved: the server rebases onto the current tip, so an agent with a stale base pays one RPC when nothing overlaps. In the published benchmark a batched land needed 1.0 attempts per landed change (self-reported, synthetic). If the tip moves during a verified resolution, the tree is rebuilt and verified again until the deadline, then returned as a proposal. The local workspace is refreshed with graft update, which refuses if local changes touch updated files. A retried land is idempotent (already_landed, also after a revert).
- **Pre write coordination:**
  > None. No claims, locks, reservations or messaging before editing. Server-side file tickets exist only to order conflict resolutions on hot files during landing, in memory of one process. Agents can watch the changeset stream to learn what others landed.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > No evidence that conflicts fall. The published benchmark (self-reported, synthetic, 50 agents x 5 changes, 20% planned conflicts in one shared file, one 16-core machine, two runs on 2026-10-20 on the 0.3.0 tree) compares batching on with batching off on the same server: 200 of 250 changes landed against 100-106, 1.0 against 26.5-28.7 attempts per landed change. The 50 changes that did not land are exactly the planned conflicts. With a deterministic union-style resolver all 250 land, because the planned conflicts are appends at the same place. The maintainer states that the conflict rate 'is a stress setting, not a forecast'. For AI resolution the maintainer's own target (land at least 20% of conflict files with at most 5% wrong) was 'not met, by any gate': best gate 15.5% wrong on open-source conflicts (upper bound 20.1%) and 43-52% wrong on replayed conflicts of agent pull requests under LLM judges; a later AI-judged relabelling of 65 agent-PR files (Go, Python, TypeScript only) gave 9.2% (4.3-18.7). With the project's tests also passing on the resolved tree, wrong rates were 7.7% Go, 3.2% Python, 4.1% TypeScript (upper bounds 16.8%, 7.2%, 8.6%). All self-reported; labels are mostly AI references, not human.

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > Client: sparse, server-centric workspace (graft clone --sparse, graft sparse) that materialises only chosen paths and has no local history; no virtual filesystem. Optional cache tiers (memory LRU daemon, regional tier with disk). Server: ordinary bare Git repository on local disk; with the storage core, refs and changesets in SQLite or PostgreSQL and content-addressed packs in a bucket, with each node's local repository acting as a rebuildable cache. Pack garbage collection is off by default.
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > 100 agents x 5 changes on one branch, one machine (phase 4b acceptance runs): 392 of 500 landed with the 108 planned conflicts returned, about 1,459-1,678 lands/min on the final tree, p99 4.6-5.4 s. 50 agents: 1,487-1,944 lands/min, p99 1.9-2.7 s (0.3.0 tree). On the 0.4.1 release workload (50 agents x 20 changes, PostgreSQL authoritative mode, 2026-11-05): 794-981 lands/min, p99 3.3-5.0 s. v0.4.2 notes also mention 200 concurrent writers against PostgreSQL: 919 of 2,000 changes failed before a pool fix, 0 after. All self-reported, synthetic.
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > Synthetic benchmark, self-reported, goroutine 'agents' on one developer machine (Intel Core Ultra 7 255H, 16 logical CPUs, WSL2), runs of seconds to minutes; no real tests executed; no production evidence; not reproduced independently.
- **Coordination limit:**
  > No published landing benchmark above 100 agents (all figures here self-reported, synthetic, one machine). At 100 agents the benchmark saturates the machine and p99 rises to about 4.6-5.4 s against 0.75-0.82 s at 20 agents. Structural limits: one lane per branch per process, batches run one at a time, one reference lock per repository in authoritative mode (about 125-190 ms queue at 50-100 agents, self-reported synthetic, failure if the wait passes the bounds of about 10 s each), no lane coordination between nodes.

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > Post-merge by design. graft-verify watches a branch, batches changesets (30 s window), runs one operator command on the tip, re-runs once for flakiness, and advances refs/graft/green/\<branch> on success. Pre-merge: only conflict resolutions can be gated, by verify.command on the exact merged tree; clean rebases and fast-forwards are not gated. No affected-target selection, no speculation, no CI service. Tests run unsandboxed unless the operator wraps them in a container.
- **Broken main recovery:**
  > graft-verify bisects first-parent commits between the last green tip and the failing tip and can revert the culprit; automatic revert is off by default and capped per hour. Known-good pointer: refs/graft/green/\<branch>. One branch at a time.
- **Review model:**
  > Built-in review service with semantic file classes (code, comment-only, docs, tests, generated, config) and approval rules, authors excluded unless allowed, but approval does not gate landing yet ('landing does not check reviews yet'). In practice: policy checks plus post-merge tests.

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > graft CLI (clone, status, commit --land, land, update, sparse, log, watch, show, blame, history, search, review) non-interactive, with --json on log, watch, show, search, review, storage, repo and policy commands; exit codes 0, 1 (error), 2 (usage), 130 (interrupt). Go SDK is the only supported client library; gRPC stubs otherwise. No MCP server found in the repository or docs. The repository's AGENTS.md is for contributors to the code, not for agents using gitai.
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > graft land --abort drops a pending commit and keeps files; graft revert restores server versions; commits stay on the agent's temporary branch when a land is refused; server-side UserRevert and the Graft-Resolved-By trailers let AI resolutions be found and reverted; changelog records every reference update. No operation log with undo in the jj sense.
- **Agent cost:**
  > No token or setup-time measurement published. Per change: one UserCommitFiles and one UserLand RPC (plus branch deletion), so no local rebase loop for the agent; a lone land waits up to the 10 ms batch window. Conflicts cost a package read and a re-land; a model resolver costs about USD 0.0005 per file (self-reported).
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > Per-agent tokens; the token identity overrides any client-sent user; an RPC allowlist denies agents administrative calls and the Git protocol by default; ordered reference policy (`[[graft.policy]]`) reloads on SIGUSR1 since v0.4.1. Directory-level permissions are not implemented. The changeset log names the agent of each update (in a batch the entry's actor is graft-batch and the real actors are listed as members, truncated beyond 32 KiB). No record of session, model or prompt. No sandbox: verify commands run as the server user.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > The server does rebase, batching and CAS. The agent commits, calls land, and handles returned conflicts; an operator runs the server, policy, resolver and graft-verify.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Vendor self-report, unusually candid: LIMITATIONS.md and BENCH.md list failed criteria, noise and superseded numbers. Main weaknesses: one machine, synthetic conflicts, comparison only against its own unbatched mode, AI-judged resolver accuracy, no independent reproduction.
- **Independent reports:**
  > Wavect, Kevin Riedl, published 2026-10-21, updated 2026-11-11 (https://wavect.io/blog/git-ai-concurrent-agents-merge-verification/): source and documentation review of v0.4.2, nothing executed; notes that clean merges skip the verifier, that the benchmark does not show a speedup over GitHub or plain Git, and that review approval does not gate landing; advises a disposable-repository pilot only if landing contention is measured. explainx.ai, 2026-10-18 (v0.2.0): benchmark compares against a handicapped version of itself, found no independent reproduction and no Hacker News thread. DEV Community, 'Alex (EngLead AI)', 24 November (https://dev.to/alexr/gitai-a-git-server-built-for-ai-agents-4323): repeats that an acceptance test failed on latency; that is the superseded phase 4a result, phase 4b meets it per LAND-BATCHING.md. No hands-on user report found: Hacker News (Algolia search) returned nothing for this repository and web searches aimed at Hacker News and Reddit found none (2026-11-24).

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > A team willing to self-host and experiment, whose measured problem is many agents losing pushes to one branch, that wants Git objects underneath and a gRPC or CLI landing path instead of pull requests.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > Clean but semantically broken merges reach main; review does not gate landing; model-resolved conflicts are wrong often enough that tests must gate them, and verify.command runs model- and agent-written code as the server user (prompt-injection path stated in the docs); single maintainer and a quiet main branch since 2026-11-05; unresolved licence-notice question for bundled Git; untested object stores and multi-machine nodes; recent correctness fixes in the storage core; agents cut off from ordinary Git tooling; no GitHub, pull-request or CI integration; SQLite serves one node only.
- **Minimum useful scale:**
  > No published threshold. Researcher judgement: when agents on one branch repeatedly lose the push race. In its own benchmark the unbatched path on the same server needed about 11 attempts per landed change at 20 agents (earlier phase 3 and 4a runs) and 26.5-28.7 at 50 agents on the 0.3.0 tree (self-reported, synthetic), so the benefit starts at tens of simultaneous landers, not at 5.
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Skip for the real repository; pilot only in a disposable one. At 5-30 agents a merge queue or a small rebase-and-retry script usually copes, while gitai costs a self-hosted server, new agent commands, no pull requests or CI, and no pre-merge tests for clean rebases. Pilot if you have measured landing contention at the upper end of the range.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Wait, with a contained pilot. Compared with bnsd55/openscion, tinyhumansai/openscion and Lakshya-2440/OpenScion it has the most complete landing path with Git objects underneath (batching, idempotent retries, post-merge bisect), and its self-reported synthetic rate (roughly 800-1,900 lands/min on one machine) is far above what several hundred agents would need (researcher judgement). But evidence stops at 100 synthetic agents on one machine, lanes do not span nodes, object stores and separate machines are untested, and maintenance looks fragile.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > Batch concurrent lands per branch into one CAS instead of letting every agent retry; make land idempotent by source commit so retries are safe; return conflicts as a structured package with three sides and proposals instead of a bare error; let a model-resolved conflict land only if tests pass on the exact merged tree, compared by tree ID; keep a green pointer ref and build from it rather than from main; bisect and revert after landing with an hourly cap; give each agent its own token and take identity from the token; mark AI resolutions with commit trailers.

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > https://github.com/feder-cr/git-ai (README, checked 2026-11-24); release notes https://github.com/feder-cr/git-ai/releases (v0.3.1 2026-10-22, v0.4.0 2026-10-23, v0.4.1 and v0.4.2 2026-11-05); docs on main at a17ad0f7 (2026-11-05): https://github.com/feder-cr/git-ai/blob/main/doc/graft/LIMITATIONS.md, LAND-BATCHING.md, RESOLVE.md, VERIFY.md, BENCH.md, STORAGE.md, AGENT-SECURITY.md, REVIEW.md, ARCHITECTURE.md, QUICKSTART.md, PLAN10.md (resolver measurements, section 15) and PLAN11.md (land identity) in the same folder; https://github.com/feder-cr/git-ai/blob/main/LICENSE; issue https://github.com/feder-cr/git-ai/issues/5 (2026-10-21) and PRs /pull/8 (2026-11-03), /pull/10 (2026-11-17), /pull/11 (2026-11-22); internal/graft/conformance/backends_test.go (how stock git is driven); Wayback captures of the repository page 2026-10-16 and 2026-10-17. Secondary: Wavect review (2026-11-11), explainx.ai (2026-10-18, https://explainx.ai/blog/git-ai-gitai-open-source-scion-style-source-control-for-agents-2026), DEV Community (24 November).
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > Nothing was built or run here. Correction to the brief: verify.command gates conflict resolutions only, not every land. Star history cannot be explained (1,066 to 972; a 1,890 claim is unverified). The licence effect of embedding GPL-2.0 Git in a build is argued but not settled by the maintainer. How an ordinary git client reaches a standalone server in practice is read from tests and AGENT-SECURITY.md, not tried. ARCHITECTURE.md describes the file changelog, STORAGE.md the database one; they differ by storage mode. The year of the DEV Community post (24 November) is inferred. The 200-writer figure in the v0.4.2 notes does not say whether all writers shared one repository.

### OpenScion reimplementations

*Layer: B open-source Scion-style*

#### Basics

- **Name:** OpenScion reimplementations
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > bnsd55/openscion (https://github.com/bnsd55/openscion; the code and old links use the former name open-scion); tinyhumansai/openscion (https://github.com/tinyhumansai/openscion; owns the crates.io name openscion); Lakshya-2440/OpenScion (the project behind https://www.openscion.tech); hannsxpeter/open-scion (a GitHub fork of bnsd55/openscion, not a separate project). More same-name repositories, not assessed: jmsbooth/openscion, fenwii/openscion, rizaust/open-scion, abhishekray07/openscion. Other Scion reimplementations outside this item: CarterT27/scion (320 stars, Apache-2.0, last push 2026-10-16), OpenCSGs/scion. Unrelated: Anthropic's internal Scion, SCION Internet architecture, GoogleCloudPlatform/scion, the crate 'scion'.
- **Maker:**
  > bnsd55/openscion: Ben Shaharizad, individual (945 of 978 commits), with bnich (18) and hannsxpeter (3) as outside contributors; tinyhumansai/openscion: Tiny Humans ('AI lab'), commits by Steven Enamakel; Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: Lakshya Gupta, individual; hannsxpeter/open-scion: a contributor to bnsd55/openscion.
- **What it is:**
  > Four repositories that rebuild the design in Anthropic's 2026-10-13 article; none contains Anthropic code or is affiliated. bnsd55/openscion: Rust server (scion-server) plus client (scn) and read caches; changesets list only changed paths, objects are BLAKE3-addressed, metadata in PostgreSQL 16-18, blobs as files; gRPC. tinyhumansai/openscion: Rust in-memory prototype of the data model with HTTP routes for blobs, changesets and refs, a CLI (opnscn: import, commit, clone) and a web page; landing is not written. Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: Go single-binary VCS (scion/scn) with Git-like commands, SQLite or PostgreSQL metadata, chunked content store and a server-side merge queue; its README says 'Inspired by Anthropic Scion, Google Piper, and Meta Sapling'. hannsxpeter/open-scion: an old snapshot of bnsd55's code used to send upstream patches.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > bnsd55/openscion: landing contention and workspace cost (sparse checkout through caches); textual conflicts only as path-level rejection; not semantic conflicts, not task collision. tinyhumansai/openscion: workspace cost only (clone one directory); landing, conflicts and persistence are roadmap items. Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: landing contention and textual conflicts (line-level three-way merge); not semantic conflicts, not task collision. hannsxpeter/open-scion: same as bnsd55 in October 2026.
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > bnsd55/openscion: central compare-and-swap with server-side replay, behind one in-process lock per ref; tinyhumansai/openscion: plain compare-and-swap on a ref, no replay; Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: central queue with a per-repository mutex, three-way merge and compare-and-swap; hannsxpeter/open-scion: as bnsd55.

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > Only bnsd55/openscion is realistically usable by an outsider today, as alpha: v0.1.0 (2026-11-01) with signed binaries for Linux x86-64 and macOS arm64; its README says 'Not ready for production code yet'. tinyhumansai/openscion: source and crates.io 0.2.1 (2026-10-13), prototype that loses all data on restart. Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: v1.0.0 binaries (2026-10-19, macOS and Linux) and a curl-to-bash installer, untested by others. hannsxpeter/open-scion: no releases, do not use.
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > bnsd55/openscion: MIT OR Apache-2.0, self-host; tinyhumansai/openscion: GPL-3.0-only, self-host; Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: README and website say Apache-2.0 but the repository has no LICENSE file and GitHub detects none (the website's licence link leads to a missing file), self-host; hannsxpeter/open-scion: inherits MIT OR Apache-2.0.
- **Price:**
  > All free, no paid tier found (checked 2026-11-24). bnsd55/openscion needs a PostgreSQL server and states an 8 GiB host in its production profile.
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > bnsd55/openscion: created 2026-10-14, v0.1.0 2026-11-01, main 364 commits ahead of it, 978 commits, pushed 2026-11-24 (daily), issue and PR numbers above 1,400 mostly filed by the author, 30 stars, 5 forks, no known users; Linux x64 server binary downloaded 3 times. Nightly soak job failed on 2026-11-23 and 2026-11-24, CI run of 2026-11-24 passed; open release gates: 30-day production trial, outside security review, second operator. tinyhumansai/openscion: 160 stars, 24 forks, last commit on main 2026-10-13 (the 2026-10-27 push is a dependabot branch), no GitHub release, crate 15 downloads; stalled. Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: created 2026-10-19, 17 commits, last 2026-10-27, 2 stars, release binaries downloaded 8 times in total. hannsxpeter/open-scion: 0 ahead and 753 behind upstream, main last commit 2026-10-19.
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > bnsd55/openscion: single active server with no failover by design; 12-hour fault soak with 11 kills reported no acknowledged land lost (81,244 lands, 2 writers, self-reported synthetic run); the 72-hour soak was stopped from outside at 66 h 40 min; one very active author. tinyhumansai/openscion: everything is in memory, a server restart starts from an empty repository (its own server docs and issue #7), project stalled. Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: no durability evidence; one author, quiet since 2026-10-27. hannsxpeter/open-scion: abandoned snapshot.
- **OS support:**
  > bnsd55/openscion: Linux x86-64 and macOS Apple Silicon supported, Linux arm64 best effort, Windows not supported (WSL not mentioned); tinyhumansai/openscion: Rust source, no platform statement; Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: macOS and Linux binaries (amd64, arm64), an install.ps1 exists but no Windows release asset; hannsxpeter/open-scion: as bnsd55.

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > None of them keeps the Git protocol. bnsd55/openscion: one-way import from a local SHA-1 Git repository (repeatable, forward-only), no Git export, no Git remote, own object IDs; tinyhumansai/openscion: imports a directory tree, Git ingestion is on the 'later' list; Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: README describes a Git bridge for importing (pkg/local/git_bridge.go), agents are told not to use git add or git commit; hannsxpeter/open-scion: as bnsd55.
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > bnsd55/openscion: no pull requests or GitHub sync; docs map scn commands to gh; CI would read through scn checkout or the API; tinyhumansai/openscion: none, states code review and CI are out of scope; Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: none found (a Git bridge only); hannsxpeter/open-scion: none. All would replace GitHub for the repository concerned.
- **Migration and exit:**
  > bnsd55/openscion: import a Git clone, run PostgreSQL plus server, rewrite agent workflows to scn; exit only through its own archive format (scion-server export, versioned and checkable) or by checking out files, so Git history made after adoption is not exportable to Git; commit signatures are dropped on import. tinyhumansai/openscion: nothing to migrate, prototype. Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: clone through the bridge or install; no documented way back to Git beyond the bridge. Partial adoption is possible only as a side repository in all cases.

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > bnsd55/openscion: server lands. The agent uploads blobs, creates a single-parent changeset (scn commit) and calls Land (scn land). The server records a pending row under a request key, takes an in-process lock per ref (one land at a time), checks the paths changed since the base, and if none overlap stores the changeset with its parent replaced by the head and moves the ref by compare-and-swap in one PostgreSQL transaction, up to 3 attempts; retries with the same key return the first result; no batching. tinyhumansai/openscion: no landing. The client moves a ref with compare-and-swap and gets a ref conflict if the head moved; Rebase, Land and Revert are 'Next' in ROADMAP.md even though the README describes landing as a server-side rebase. Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: server queue. scion push submits to one mutex per repository in the server process, three-way merges the trees against main, up to 50 compare-and-swap retries, main only. hannsxpeter/open-scion: bnsd55's design as of 2026-10-19.
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > bnsd55/openscion: at landing, whole-path level: a path conflicts if the same path, anything below it, or a file at a parent path changed on the ref after the base; content is never compared or merged, so two edits to different functions of one file conflict. This path rule is bnsd55's own choice; Anthropic's article publishes no conflict-detection design. tinyhumansai/openscion: whole-ref level only (any move of the ref rejects the update). Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: at landing, line level (diff3) within files; the website's 'AST verification' was not found in the merge or queue code. hannsxpeter/open-scion: as bnsd55.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > bnsd55/openscion: land state CONFLICT with the list of paths, exit code 2, no markers and no merge; the agent re-reads main and creates a new changeset. tinyhumansai/openscion: ref-conflict error with the current target, client's problem. Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: non-overlapping line edits merged automatically; otherwise REBASE_CONFLICT with path, reason and base, ours and theirs hashes; README mentions structured conflict markers. hannsxpeter/open-scion: as bnsd55. None calls a model or runs tests.
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > bnsd55/openscion: main moving costs nothing unless the same path changed; no base is too old to check (path history); the agent does not pull. tinyhumansai/openscion: any move of main rejects the update, so under load every agent retries. Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: server re-merges onto the new tip. Code-reading finding, not tested: in pkg/queue/queue.go, when the parent tree cannot be loaded the current head is used as merge base, which would let the submitted tree overwrite changes landed since. hannsxpeter/open-scion: as bnsd55.
- **Pre write coordination:**
  > None in any of the four: no claims, locks, leases or messaging before editing. bnsd55/openscion's per-ref lock and write rules act at landing or as access control, not as coordination.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > No evidence for any. bnsd55/openscion: a path-level check is stricter than Git's text merge, so it should raise rejections when agents touch the same file (researcher inference); no conflict-rate data published. tinyhumansai/openscion: none. Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: self-reported synthetic burst of 100 simulated agents: 91 landed, 9 rejected; the test script builds it that way (80 agents edit separate files, 10 edit separate functions of one file, the rest touch one shared line), so it shows the merge works on a constructed case. hannsxpeter/open-scion: none. openscion.tech figures are not measurements: 'Automated Merges Today 52,481' and the peak are initial values and Math.random numbers in the page's queue-sim.js.

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > bnsd55/openscion: sparse checkout through server, regional cache and per-node daemon; read-only NFS mount is experimental; blobs as one file each on local disk, metadata in PostgreSQL. In its own benchmark (self-reported, synthetic, shared GitHub runner) 50 cold checkouts of 377 files took 1.50 s against 33.15 s for git clone and 13.96 s for a blobless clone, but git with a local mirror was faster warm (0.80 s against 1.35 s). tinyhumansai/openscion: full or one-directory clone over HTTP, server storage in memory only (README's SQLite and filesystem stores do not exist, issue #7). Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: chunked content store, sparse patterns, lazy virtual filesystem per README. hannsxpeter/open-scion: as bnsd55.
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > bnsd55/openscion: 200 writers on one ref, 2,000 lands per cell: 28.9 lands/s with no readers (write p99 end-to-end 9.3 s), 8.6 lands/s with 20 readers beside them (34.9 s); 50 writers: 32.0 lands/s, p99 3.5 s; self-reported, synthetic, GitHub-hosted 4-vCPU runner (docs/operations.md section 7, run 42390384979). tinyhumansai/openscion: none. Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: 100 simulated agents, 91 lands in 287 ms (README, 316.6 merges/s), self-reported, synthetic, 82-file repository on localhost. hannsxpeter/open-scion: none of its own.
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > bnsd55/openscion: synthetic benchmark, self-reported, 2 repetitions per cell, plus 12-hour and partial 66-hour self-run soaks; tinyhumansai/openscion: synthetic micro-benchmark against Git on 1,000 and 10,000 files, contested by issue #15 (in-process OpenScion against subprocess Git); Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: one sub-second synthetic burst; hannsxpeter/open-scion: none. No production and no independent evidence for any.
- **Coordination limit:**
  > bnsd55/openscion: throughput is flat at about 28-32 lands/s from 2 to 200 writers and falls to 8-14 with readers (self-reported, synthetic, one 4-vCPU runner), so latency grows linearly with writers; the queue is in changeset creation; one server process per database. tinyhumansai/openscion: effectively one writer at a time per ref. Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: one in-process mutex per repository; limit not measured. hannsxpeter/open-scion: as bnsd55 or worse.

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > None in any: no pre-merge or post-merge test hook. bnsd55/openscion leaves CI to whoever reads the log or checks out; the others likewise.
- **Broken main recovery:**
  > None in any: no known-good pointer, bisect service or automatic revert. bnsd55/openscion has no revert command (a fix is a new changeset); tinyhumansai lists Revert as planned; Lakshya-2440/OpenScion has no documented revert.
- **Review model:**
  > None built in. bnsd55/openscion: no review before landing, only write rules per path; tinyhumansai/openscion: declares code review out of scope; Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: none; hannsxpeter/open-scion: none.

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > bnsd55/openscion: scn CLI, three-command loop (cat, commit, land), --json on every command, exit codes 0, 1 (is-ancestor 'no'), 2 (error), gRPC API with a documented contract, scn bench load generator; no MCP or skill. tinyhumansai/openscion: opnscn import, commit, clone and HTTP routes. Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: Git-like CLI, HTTP API, generates AGENTS.md and CLAUDE.md that tell agents to use scion instead of git. hannsxpeter/open-scion: as bnsd55.
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > bnsd55/openscion: immutable changesets, idempotent land retries, backup and restore runbooks, fsck; no undo command. tinyhumansai/openscion: none, and restart loses data. Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: local stash and log; no undo documented. hannsxpeter/open-scion: as bnsd55.
- **Agent cost:**
  > No token, setup-time or message measurement published for any of them. bnsd55/openscion needs no clone and about three commands per change; a path conflict costs a full re-read and a new changeset.
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > bnsd55/openscion: an access file maps token digests to principals with repository read rules and per-path write rules (prefix read rules experimental), audit log that cannot be turned off, TLS; author is free text, no session or model field. tinyhumansai/openscion: no authentication found (ScionHub snapshot 2026-10-14). Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: one optional shared bearer token (off unless SCION_AUTH_TOKEN is set); commit fields for agent model, session and trace included in the changeset. hannsxpeter/open-scion: as bnsd55.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > bnsd55/openscion: the server rebases and moves the ref; the agent sends changes and redoes conflicts. tinyhumansai/openscion: the agent or client does everything except the final compare-and-swap. Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: the server merges and queues; the agent pushes and redoes conflicts. hannsxpeter/open-scion: as bnsd55.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > bnsd55/openscion: vendor self-report, detailed, candid and rerunnable (scripts, named CI runs), no outside check. tinyhumansai/openscion: README overstates the code (landing and persistence claimed, not implemented), contested benchmark. Lakshya-2440/OpenScion and openscion.tech: marketing, with simulated counters presented as live figures. hannsxpeter/open-scion: nothing of its own.
- **Independent reports:**
  > tinyhumansai/openscion: 14 issues by user tg12 on 2026-10-13 (README contradicts the code, claimed stores missing, benchmark unequal, CI red), answered only by the project's own triage bot (checked on #1 and #7) and still open on 2026-11-24 except #4; ScionHub (scionhub.net, checked 2026-10-14) calls v0.2.1 experimental, no persistence or authentication; Hacker News submission 2026-10-13 got 7 points and no discussion (https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50079329). bnsd55/openscion: outside contributors hannsxpeter and bnich filed umask, archive, import and mount bugs with fixes, 2026-10-19 to 2026-10-29 (issues and PRs #301-#303, #345, #501, #502, #693-#741); no user report or review found. Lakshya-2440/OpenScion and hannsxpeter/open-scion: none found.

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > bnsd55/openscion: studying or testing a small, well-documented Scion-like server where agents mostly touch different files. tinyhumansai/openscion: reading its specs and Scion research notes only. Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: a toy local demo. hannsxpeter/open-scion: nothing.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > bnsd55/openscion: path-level conflicts on hot files, no merge of any kind, no Git way back, one server without failover, alpha, recent soak failures, push p99 of 9-35 s at 200 writers (self-reported, synthetic). tinyhumansai/openscion: total data loss on restart, no landing, stalled, misleading README. Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: no licence file, installer piped to a shell, optional shared token, possible silent overwrite when the parent is unknown (code reading), made-up website counters, no maintenance signal. hannsxpeter/open-scion: stale copy. All: name confusion between repositories and with Anthropic's Scion.
- **Minimum useful scale:**
  > No published threshold. Researcher judgement: bnsd55/openscion could only pay off for hundreds of agents on mostly disjoint files where clone cost dominates; the others pay off at no scale today.
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > bnsd55/openscion: skip for real code, lab test at most (alpha, no Git protocol, no tests or review, path-level conflicts are worse than Git's text merge at this size); tinyhumansai/openscion: skip (no landing, in-memory); Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: skip (unlicensed, unproven, marketing claims); hannsxpeter/open-scion: skip (stale fork).
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > bnsd55/openscion: wait. Sensible design notes and an active author, and about 29 lands/s without readers (self-reported, synthetic) would cover the volume, but latency at 200 writers, single server, path-level conflicts and no Git export block adoption; look again when its own gates (30-day trial, security review, v1.0) are met. tinyhumansai/openscion: skip. Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: skip. hannsxpeter/open-scion: skip.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > From bnsd55/openscion: idempotent landing with a client request key and stored outcome; a path index by generation that answers 'did this path change since my base' with one lookup per path part; conflict as a normal result state with paths instead of an error; partition work so that agents rarely share a file, since a path-level check then becomes free; publish capacity as a table of writers, readers and p99, not one headline rate. From Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: store agent model, session and task trace in the change record. From tinyhumansai's issues: check README claims against the roadmap and code before adopting.

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > bnsd55/openscion, checked 2026-11-24: https://github.com/bnsd55/openscion (README), docs/architecture.md section 5 (landing), docs/operations.md section 7 (capacity table, run 42390384979), docs/benchmarks.md (bench.yml run 39306408455), docs/cli.md, docs/cli-vs-git.md, docs/ROADMAP.md, docs/stability.md, docs/support.md, release https://github.com/bnsd55/openscion/releases/tag/v0.1.0 (2026-11-01), issue #399 (soak reports 2026-10-20 to 2026-10-24). tinyhumansai/openscion: https://github.com/tinyhumansai/openscion (README), ROADMAP.md, crates/openscion-core/src/repo/mod.rs ('Not here yet: ... rebase, and landing'), issues #1, #7, #9, #15 (2026-10-13), https://crates.io/crates/openscion (0.2.1, 2026-10-13). Lakshya-2440/OpenScion: https://github.com/Lakshya-2440/OpenScion (README, pkg/queue/queue.go, pkg/merge/merge.go, cmd/stress100/main.go at 82adfb38, 2026-10-27), https://www.openscion.tech and its queue-sim.js (fetched 2026-11-24). hannsxpeter/open-scion: https://github.com/hannsxpeter/open-scion (fork metadata and compare with upstream, 2026-11-24). Secondary: https://scionhub.net (2026-10-14).
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > Nothing was built or run here. bnsd55's own figures differ between documents: architecture.md says 23-25 lands/s with no readers, the newer capacity table in operations.md says 28.0-32.0; the table with its run is used. The soak failures of 2026-11-23 and 2026-11-24 were not diagnosed beyond open issues #1419 and #1421. The overwrite risk in Lakshya-2440/OpenScion is a code reading, not a reproduced bug. openscion.tech names no maker; the link to Lakshya-2440 comes from its repository links and the repository's homepage field. OpenCSGs/scion appears in search results but returned 404 through the GitHub API on 2026-11-24. The tinyhumansai issues end with a block addressed to LLM readers; it was treated as data and ignored.

### Veil

*Layer: B open-source Scion-style*

#### Basics

- **Name:** Veil (CLI: vl; server: Veil Hub)
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > RKKatakam/veil, https://github.com/RKKatakam/veil (repository created 2026-11-03). No rename found. Unrelated namesakes: lemonadesaltbagel/veil and FullFran/veil (privacy proxies for coding agents), cmdrvl/veil (file-read guard), harishb2006/Veil, and many other projects called Veil. No homepage or package-registry entry is listed on the repository.
- **Maker:**
  > Individual developer: GitHub account RKKatakam, commits signed 'kittu'. One human contributor (1,982 commits, read 2026-11-24) plus Dependabot. No company. The README says it is independent of Jujutsu, Anthropic and t3.gg and not endorsed by them.
- **What it is:**
  > A from-scratch, server-centric version control system in Rust. The client (vl) uses the Jujutsu model: the working copy is always a change, no staging area, stable change ids, undoable operation log, automatic rebase of descendants, conflicts stored as data. The server (Veil Hub) is built on Anthropic's public description of Scion: central store, thin clients that fetch files on demand, lock-free uploads, batched server-side landing with one compare-and-swap on one database row per branch, tests after landing, and main@tested as the starting point for agents. Metadata sits in PostgreSQL or YugabyteDB, file contents in S3-compatible storage. It adds per-folder permissions enforced on every server answer, Linux bubblewrap sandboxes per workspace, automatic encryption of secret files, embargoes, pull requests with risk tiers, signed agent attribution, and recorded dead ends (vl attempt drop).
- **Problem addressed:**
  > Addresses: landing contention (batched compare-and-swap, no client pull-rebase-retry), throughput (stateless servers over a database and object storage), workspace cost (virtual and FUSE-mounted workspaces, thin clones), and broken main (post-landing bisect and revert). Textual conflicts: partly; the server merges non-overlapping and cleanly mergeable text, and real overlaps go back to the agent. Not addressed: semantic conflicts before landing (only post-landing tests), and task collision (no claims, locks or messaging between agents were found in the docs). Access control and provenance are extra problems it targets.
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > Central compare-and-swap with server-side replay and merge (Scion-style), plus a jj-style client where a rebase always finishes and leaves conflicts as data. Agents isolate in workspaces and land through a server queue; there is no write-time detection and no pre-write claim.

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > Usable today by an outsider only as source code: pre-1.0 preview, version 0.1.0, no GitHub release and no prebuilt binary, build with cargo (read 2026-11-24). No hosted service found. The old 1.0 checklist says 1.0 would not mean audited or ready for the general public; that file is marked out of date (2026-11-21).
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > Dual MIT OR Apache-2.0 according to the workspace Cargo.toml and the two licence files; GitHub's API reports only Apache-2.0. Self-host only: one veil-server binary plus PostgreSQL or YugabyteDB, plus S3-compatible storage (a local data directory works for trials).
- **Price:**
  > Free software, no paid offer found (2026-11-24). Running cost is your own database, object storage and machines. The only published cost figure: about \$39 of Google Cloud Spot machines for two nights of benchmarks on up to 12 machines (self-reported, 2026-11-20 and 21). Append-only storage means storage cost only grows.
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > Version 0.1.0, three weeks old (first commits 2026-11-03), 29 stars, 2 forks, 1 watcher, 4 open pull requests, all from Dependabot, and no issues from users (read 2026-11-24). Last push 2026-11-24. 1,982 commits in 21 days and about 15 MB of Rust by one author; the repository's CLAUDE.md and review skill show a heavily agent-driven process. docs/BUGS.md holds 921 tracked rows and ids up to BUG-2944; the README counts only 'about 75' from review passes. One row is marked open (BUG-2944, undo/restore operation-log property). README claims about 3,540 tests green (self-reported). Public GitHub CI: all 59 CI and Nightly runs in the latest 100 workflow runs concluded 'failure', and the only 7 successful runs in the Actions history are Dependabot's. On the 2026-11-24 push the Ubuntu build, tests, doc tests and clippy passed and only the docs-warnings step failed, while the macOS job failed at Build (fuser crate). No known users outside the author; 'Veil hosts itself' is still an unticked item in its (outdated) 1.0 checklist.
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > Bus factor of one, no company, three weeks old. Design choices favour durability: commits fsync objects and operations, server storage is append-only in v1 with no garbage collection at all, and a self-reported failover test landed 10,473 of 10,473 changes exactly once (synthetic benchmark). Risks: repository formats changed quickly (format 8 opens only format 8; formats 1 to 5 were dropped; attribution needs format 10), server support for PostgreSQL versus YugabyteDB was reversed within days, append-only storage never shrinks and has no purge for leaked content (design waiting, not built), and SECURITY.md still carries a placeholder contact address.
- **OS support:** ⚠
  > Linux x86_64 and aarch64. macOS is documented as supported, but sandboxes run unconfined there and the macOS CI job fails to build at the 2026-11-24 head [uncertain]. Windows is not supported natively (listed as backlog); WSL is not mentioned in the docs read, so a Linux build under WSL2 is plausible but untested [uncertain].

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > None, and declared permanent by the owner (2026-11-05): no import, export, sync, colocation, submodules or Git-format patches. An earlier Git interop existed and was removed (tag 'pre-git-removal'; bug ids of 'the removed Git interop' are retired).
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > Replaces GitHub: its own pull requests, review rules, web UI with passkeys, protected branches and rules in .veilrules. It does not run tests itself. External CI runners get a short-lived, path-narrowed read session (vl session ci), read a snapshot, and report results through POST /v1/projects/{p}/checks; the server then moves main@tested, requests bisect probes through an event stream and queues reverts. No GitHub Actions, GitHub pull-request or webhook bridge was found, so existing CI must be adapted to fetch from the Hub and report back.
- **Migration and exit:** ⚠
  > Adoption is all or nothing for a repository: no Git importer, so a project enters as a snapshot of its files ('vl init') and Git history is not carried over; GitHub pull requests, Actions triggers and Git tooling stop working on it. Server needs PostgreSQL or YugabyteDB plus object storage, built from source. Partial adoption is not possible through a mirror; only a side experiment on a copy is. Leaving: files can be checked out and committed to Git by hand, but there is no exporter, so change ids, operation log, attribution, dead-end records, pull requests and per-change history are lost unless you write your own export through the API [uncertain] (no exit guide was found).

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > The agent commits locally (or sends edits through the API with no checkout) and runs 'vl land'. The server runs the same checks as a push and inserts one queue row (up to 64 stacked changes, oldest first). One leased landing service per branch picks a batch, places each change on the newest tip as its own server-signed snapshot (fast path when paths do not overlap, server-side 3-way content merge when they do), and moves the branch once for the whole batch with a single compare-and-swap. The agent does not pull, rebase or retry; it waits for, or polls by ticket, 'landed', 'conflict' or 'rejected'. Protected branches move only this way, and 'vl push' to one is turned into a landing. 'vl land --onto BRANCH --delete' merges a subagent's branch into its parent. Reverts queued by the server use a priority lane. A change that edits policy or rules files lands alone. Tests do not gate the landing. Measured batch size on the cloud run was about 64 landings per batch (self-reported, synthetic benchmark, 2026-11-20).
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > At landing, before the change reaches main, so main never holds a conflict (unlike Scion as Veil's authors read it, where conflicts are fixed afterwards). Granularity is documented inconsistently. The vl land reference and the server guide describe an exact path-level check: the change lands if no path it edits changed since its base. HUB.md section 13.30 ('as built', updated 2026-11-12) says overlapping paths then get a line-based 3-way text merge inside the batch, with 'conflict' only if that is not clean. Binary files, non-files, newly added files with different content, and paths the submitter cannot read always conflict; .veilattributes can set binary or union merge. Semantic conflicts are seen only by tests after landing. On the client, rebases and merges detect conflicts per file content and record them as data.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > Two different mechanisms. At landing: a clean merge is automatic on the server; an unclean one is a rejection with a structured report (paths, base, main and yours object ids, up to 256 'landed since' commits on those paths, redacted when a hidden path is involved), sent as the command answer and as a land.conflict event, and the agent pulls, redoes the change on the new tip and lands again. In the client: conflicts are stored as data on the change, rebases always finish, and 'vl resolve' lists sides or keeps one, or the agent edits the file until the markers are gone. No server-side or built-in LLM resolver.
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > Normal and cheap for the agent: a moved main does not require a pull. The server merges onto the newest tip in the same batch. Only an unclean overlap returns, at the cost of one pull, rework and a new submit. Workspaces and API changes start from main@tested by default, so an agent's base is normally behind the tip by design. Edits sent with 'vl change new --onto main' are reapplied server-side unless an edited file changed. Under overload the server answers 'come back later' with a delay (busy, queue full, 429) and the client waits and retries; in the 1,000-per-second overload run the median wait was about 62 s (self-reported, synthetic benchmark). Below capacity, 300 landings/s on a protected main with the injected overlap mix gave p50 41 ms and p99 589 ms (self-reported, synthetic benchmark, one machine, older code).
- **Pre write coordination:**
  > None found: no path claims, leases, locks or agent messaging before editing. Coordination is by isolation (one workspace per agent), by narrowing an agent session to folders with --only (enforced by the server for reads and writes), and after the fact by recorded dead ends that the next agent can read. Folder narrowing can be used as a hard partition of work, but it is an access rule and not a claim protocol.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > No evidence that conflicts between real agents fall. The repository reports 0.00 to 0.02 collisions per landing 'at agent scale' (self-reported, synthetic benchmark) and 0.020 conflicts delivered per landing at 300 landings/s, but that rate is an input: the load tool writes a new file in each agent's own folder for 95 % of landings, changes a line of a shared file for 3 % and writes one of 16 hot files for 2 %. LOAD.md says of the base workload that 'changes never overlap'. What is shown is less push racing: 50 clients pushing to one main fell to 6.6 pushes/s with 27 collisions per operation (README) or about 8/s (BENCHMARKS.md), and pull-rebase-retry gave 20 to 30 landings/s, against about 200/s through the landing service (all self-reported, synthetic benchmark). The one outside analysis (mikenrafter/mstack, 2026-11-12, code read and not run) classes the merge as the same 3-way line class as Git and infers that hot files would conflict the same way.

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > Virtual workspaces with no directory by default; 'vl ws exec' gives each workspace a persistent sandbox directory (copy-on-write file clones where the filesystem supports them), 'vl ws exec --mount' and 'vl ws mount' give a FUSE view that fetches files on demand, '--only' gives a sparse one. Thin clones fetch from the Hub, with an optional per-machine cache daemon. Server: metadata and landing queue in PostgreSQL or YugabyteDB, one content-addressed object per file version in S3-compatible storage, append-only. Self-reported, synthetic benchmark: a 200,000-file workspace starts in under 300 ms; default thin clone of a 1 GB, 35,000-file project takes 9.4 s against 29 s for a full Git clone (a thin-versus-full comparison).
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > Load-generator clients, not LLM agents. One main: 10,000 simulated clients offering 1,000 landings/s; 207 landings/s held for 3 minutes and 198/s for 10 minutes, 182/s protected with 3 % overlapping and 2 % conflicting changes, p50 wait about 62 s (self-reported, synthetic benchmark, Google Cloud, build 8efc94ab, 2026-11-21). Realistic-pace run: 1,000 clients with think time compressed 20 times (presented as 'about 20,000 agents', a modelled equivalence): 5.2 main landings/s and 16.0 subagent landings/s with 0 errors on 6 YugabyteDB nodes; 3.8 main landings/s with 118 errors on one 56-vCPU PostgreSQL machine (self-reported, synthetic benchmark). Merges to main from 300 clients on branches, PostgreSQL: 31.3/s, 0 errors. The per-day figures (450,000, 2.7 million, 18 million) are extrapolations of 3 to 10 minute rates. The 995 landings/s figure is older code on one 32-core machine (self-reported, synthetic benchmark, 2026-11-14).
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > Synthetic benchmark only, run by the author on rented Google Cloud Spot machines (2026-11-20 and 21) and on one 32-core machine (2026-11-12 and 14). In-process clients, small synthetic projects (nothing above 1 GB), runs of 3 to 10 minutes, one zone. Scripts and a reproduction guide are in the repository. No production use and no independent run found.
- **Coordination limit:**
  > Self-reported, synthetic benchmark: one main stops at about 200 landings/s on the cloud setup (the object store wait of about 150 ms per batch); PostgreSQL merges to one main collapse from 31.3/s at 300 clients to 1.1/s with 8,823 errors at 3,000; YugabyteDB collapses when its CPUs are full and costs 6 to 10 times the database CPU per push; landings across 100 projects (not on one main) fell from 254/s with 1 server to 161/s with 3, unexplained. No limit measured with real coding agents.

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > Land first, test after. Failing or missing checks never block a landing. External runners report named checks per snapshot; main@tested advances only when every required check in .veilrules passes on a snapshot, and never moves backwards. Checks can be narrowed by path, which acts as a coarse affected-targets rule. Landings are batched by the server, and one test run on the newest snapshot can cover many landings; there is no speculation. A per-landing pre-merge test mode was not found.
- **Broken main recovery:**
  > Built and described in detail (HUB.md 13.36): when a required check fails ahead of main@tested, the server bisects the landings in between with up to 8 parallel probes, requires two failing runs on the culprit and a pass on its parent before acting (flake guard), then lands a server-signed revert in a priority lane. Reverts of reverts stop for a person, and a change reverted twice needs human approval. Revert conflicts are reported, not forced. Known-good pointer: main@tested. No production evidence.
- **Review model:**
  > Risk-tiered pull requests: low (docs and tests) needs one approval by someone other than the author, agent approvals count; normal and high need one person by default; checks-only paths are possible. Approvals bind to file contents. A pull request is optional; unprotected branches and 'vl land' without review rules need none.

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > CLI with versioned JSON output on read commands (schemas such as veil.status/2, errors as veil.error/1) and stable exit codes; JSON-RPC 2.0 over a local Unix socket (vl serve --local) with change notifications; HTTPS API for committing without a checkout, landing by ticket, checks and a server-sent event stream. Short-lived agent sessions (12 hours at most) narrowed to folders. Every command is usable non-interactively, including hunk-level split. No MCP server, skill or agent instruction file for users was found (repository tree and GitHub code search returned nothing, 2026-11-24). The docs tell agents what to run; agents have no training data for vl.
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > Every command is recorded in a lock-free operation log: vl undo and vl redo per workspace, vl undo --op ID for one operation, vl op restore for the whole repository. Work is snapshotted before every command, and a workspace run is recorded whatever its exit status. Known gap: undo right after stale-working-copy recovery can hide the recovered change (recoverable with vl op restore), and BUG-2944 is an open wrong-result case in operation-log merges.
- **Agent cost:**
  > No token or dollar measurement with real agents. Expected extra cost: learning an unfamiliar CLI through docs in context, and running a server, database and object store. Landing needs one command and no pull-rebase loop, so coordination messages are few. Sandbox overhead is about 5 ms per confined run (self-reported, synthetic benchmark).
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > The strongest part. Per-folder permissions enforced by the server on reads, writes, logs, blame, search, diffs, pull requests and CI; private by default; agents see at most what their person sees, and a session can be narrowed to paths. Linux bubblewrap sandbox per workspace hides \~/.ssh, other projects and processes, with memory, CPU, process and time limits; macOS runs unconfined; environment variables and network are passed through by default. Attribution (agent, task, prompt reference, run id) is inside the signed snapshot and cannot be set from inside the sandbox; API commits are stamped by the server. Caveats: not audited; identity private keys are stored unencrypted on disk (0600); snapshot signatures are advisory.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > The server lands, merges, bisects and reverts. The agent commits (automatic snapshots), calls vl land, and resolves returned conflicts. A CI runner you supply reports test results. Humans set rules and, by default, approve normal and high-risk pull requests.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Vendor self-report by a single author, unusually candid (misses, voided runs and caveats are listed) and with reproduction scripts, but all synthetic. Main weakness: no real agents, no production, no independent run, small repositories, short runs, and a conflict mix that was chosen by the benchmark.
- **Independent reports:**
  > One substantive outside analysis: mikenrafter/mstack research/16-veil-vs-jj.md (2026-11-12, code and docs at commit 1384ff8e read, Veil not run). It recommends against adoption for a Git and GitHub project: nine days old, one author, no release, needs Postgres and S3, permanent lack of Git interop, throughput far beyond need. Two passing mentions: corescript/evolution VISION.md (2026-11-14/15) lists it as a design to borrow from, and it appeared in an agent-repository listing on 2026-11-12. No Hacker News hits (Algolia search, 2026-11-24); Reddit and LinkedIn could not be searched, and no review was found in general web search.

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > Studying an open, readable implementation of Scion-style landing (batching, main@tested, bisect and revert, path-visible conflict reports), or a throwaway greenfield project on Linux where Git compatibility does not matter and per-folder agent permissions do.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > No Git escape hatch; single maintainer; format and database decisions still moving; every public CI run failing at the time of reading; macOS build broken in CI; unaudited security code with a placeholder security contact; unbounded storage growth and no purge of leaked content; overload collapse on PostgreSQL at 3,000 clients and on YugabyteDB at full CPU; unexplained drop with 3 servers; Hub is required for sharing, whole-history queries and unfetched files; post-landing testing means a red main can be built on until the revert lands unless agents stay on main@tested; agents must learn an unknown CLI without MCP.
- **Minimum useful scale:**
  > By its design goals, hundreds of landings an hour on one branch from many agents. It does not pay off over worktrees plus a merge queue at tens of merges a day, as the mstack analysis also concluded for a project with about 20 pushes on its busiest day (independent, production, one project). No measured break-even.
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Skip for real work. At 5 to 30 agents Git with worktrees and a merge queue covers the landing load, while Veil costs Git history, GitHub, CI integration and a self-run Postgres and S3 stack on three-week-old, single-author, unreleased code. Reasonable as a sandbox experiment on a copy of the repository, to learn the main@tested and landing-ticket workflow.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Wait. The architecture is the right shape for several hundred agents (batched compare-and-swap, server merge, conflict returned as structured data, post-landing bisect and revert), and the synthetic numbers leave headroom: several hundred agents landing every few minutes is a few landings per second at most (modelled). But there is no run with real agents, no repository above 1 GB, no production record and no Git bridge. Reconsider if it ships a release with green CI, a second maintainer, an independent load test and a Git import or mirror. In a non-Git variant of the recommendation it is a candidate to watch rather than to pilot.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > Copy without the tool: let the server or a queue rebase and land in batches so that agents never run a pull-rebase-push loop; start agents from a known-good pointer (a 'tested' ref updated by CI) instead of the raw tip; on failure, bisect across the batch with a double-fail plus parent-pass confirmation before an automatic revert, and stop automatic revert loops; return conflicts as structured data (path, base, main, yours, landed since); record abandoned attempts with reasons next to the change; put agent, task and run ids in commit trailers; narrow each agent's write access to its task folders; advise agents to wait (retry-after) instead of failing when the queue is full.

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > https://github.com/RKKatakam/veil README (head 8a14e622, 2026-11-24); https://github.com/RKKatakam/veil/blob/main/docs/BENCHMARKS.md (runs of 2026-11-20/21 and 2026-11-12/14); https://github.com/RKKatakam/veil/blob/main/docs/DEPLOY.md section 12.11 (cloud run log); https://github.com/RKKatakam/veil/blob/main/docs/HUB.md sections 13.19, 13.30, 13.35, 13.36 (landing, merges, checks, bisect); https://github.com/RKKatakam/veil/blob/main/docs/design/scale/LOAD.md (load method and mix); https://github.com/RKKatakam/veil/blob/main/docs/guide/the-server.md and docs/guide/workspaces-and-agents.md; https://github.com/RKKatakam/veil/blob/main/docs/reference/land.md; https://github.com/RKKatakam/veil/blob/main/docs/BACKLOG.md, docs/BUGS.md, docs/RELEASE-1.0.md, SECURITY.md, CLAUDE.md, Cargo.toml; GitHub API for repository, commits, contributors and Actions runs (read 2026-11-24). Independent: https://github.com/mikenrafter/mstack/blob/main/research/16-veil-vs-jj.md (2026-11-12); https://github.com/corescript/evolution/blob/main/VISION.md (2026-11-15). Background: https://www.anthropic.com/research/redesigning-source-control (cited by Veil for Scion).
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > Veil was not installed or run for this report. Docs disagree on conflict granularity at landing (path-level in vl land and the server guide, 3-way content merge in HUB 13.30). README says Rust 1.80+ while Cargo.toml sets 1.88. README counts about 75 bugs, BUGS.md has 921 rows. The README's '1,000 agents is about 20,000 at the real pace' and all per-day figures are modelled. macOS and WSL usability not verified. Whether code search covers the whole repository for MCP is not certain. Author's X account was not read. Reddit was not reachable from the search tool. Veil links Anthropic's Scion post at anthropic.com/research/redesigning-source-control, while the research outline gives anthropic.com/engineering/redesigning-source-control; neither was fetched for this item, and statements about Scion here are Veil's reading of it.
