### AVP by Murmell

*Layer: B open-source Scion-style*

#### Basics

- **Name:** AVP by Murmell (Agent Versioning Protocol)
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > Mossab28/avp, https://github.com/Mossab28/avp (repository created 2026-10-24); hosted service AVP Cloud at https://cloud.murmell.com (avp.murmell.com redirects there). Earlier internal name: trame (the server still opens trame.sqlite). The acronym AVP is heavily reused elsewhere (none of the results read was a related version-control project); izagood/avcs is a different, unrelated project.
- **Maker:**
  > Murmell Inc., a Delaware corporation according to the licence; a two-person team, co-founder Moss'Ab Mirande-Ney (GitHub Mossab28), maker of the Murmell agent canvas. All 14 public commits carry one author name (read 2026-11-24); GitHub's contributor list shows one account.
- **What it is:**
  > A source-available Rust server, CLI and MCP server that replaces branches and pull requests with tasks on one line. An agent opens a task, gets a folder ('view') at the head of the line, writes, and lands. Before landing, a 'proof' (typecheck plus tests related to the touched files, defined in avp.toml) runs on the exact head; only green lands as the next numbered position. If the line moved, AVP replays the task's writes on the new head by a 3-way merge, and proves again when what landed touches what the proof covered. The full suite runs after landings, once per batch; a position that breaks it twice is found and reverted. Every position is mirrored to Git as an ordinary commit (branch avp/line by default, or main). It also covers proof workers on your own runners, environments and promotion, approvals, secrets for tests, blame and 'why' queries, offline outbox and an HTTP API.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > Addresses: landing contention (one server lands in turn, no client rebase loop), CI cost and throughput (related tests per task, full suite per batch), textual conflicts (replay, plus automatic settling when both sides only added text), some task collision (advisory leases shown when a task opens), and dependency deadlocks between tasks ('land --with'). Workspace cost: partly (reused folders, batched copies, not virtualised). Not addressed: semantic conflicts beyond what the chosen tests catch, and very large repositories or very high landing rates.
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > Central server with serialized landing, replay on the exact head and proof before landing (pre-merge selective tests), combined with advisory write-time leases. Isolation in per-task folders, then a server-side merge; conflicts only for the same lines.

#### Availability

- **Public availability:** ⚠
  > Usable today: release v0.7.12 (2026-11-19) with binaries for macOS and Linux, and full source. AVP Cloud lists a free plan and can be signed up for according to the docs, while an October 2026 Product Hunt forum post called access invitation-only; its current status was not confirmed [uncertain]. No beta or GA label on the site, and docs/RUNS.md still says AVP 'is still being tested internally'. First public article 2026-10-25.
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > Elastic License 2.0 (source-available, not OSI open source, although the Product Hunt copy says 'open source'): free use, change and self-hosting including commercial, forbidden to offer to third parties as a hosted or managed service. Releases up to and including 0.5.0 were AGPL-3.0. Contributions need a CLA that lets Murmell relicense. Self-host (all features in every build) or SaaS (AVP Cloud).
- **Price:**
  > Self-hosted: free. AVP Cloud (site read 2026-11-24): Free 0 USD for up to 3 people and 3,000 proof minutes a month; Team 10 USD per person per month with 5,000 pooled proof minutes per person and 0.003 USD per extra minute; Enterprise by quote. GUIDE.md gives the free cap as 2,000 minutes unless configured, which disagrees with the site.
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > v0.7.12 (2026-11-19), 12 releases between 2026-10-29 and 2026-11-19, last push 2026-11-23. 14 stars, 1 fork, 2 watchers, zero issues and zero pull requests ever visible, release binaries downloaded 0 to 9 times each (read 2026-11-24). About 2 MB of Rust. Public commits are large squashed releases, so development history is not visible; the only GitHub workflow is the release build, so there is no public test CI (the project says its tests run in AVP). The only known user in production is Murmell itself (two people and many agents), on one monorepo since 2026-10-24.
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > Early startup project with a single public contributor; the October post said Murmell was applying to Y Combinator. Public history was reset: release pages from 0.3.8 to 0.5.0 (AGPL era) are still in the search index, but the GitHub API returns 404 for the two checked (0.4.4 and 0.5.0), and the repository history begins with one 36,713-line commit on 2026-10-29. State lives in one SQLite file plus an object folder on one server, so backup is the operator's job. The Git mirror is the main safety net: every landed position stays readable as a commit. Licence moved from AGPL to Elastic within days, and the CLA allows further change.
- **OS support:**
  > macOS and Linux, x86_64 and arm64, with release binaries. Windows only inside WSL (stated in the README); no native Windows build.

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > Own storage with a one-way Git mirror plus inbound bridges. Outbound: each position is a commit with AVP-Position and AVP-Task trailers, tags mirrored too, fast-forward only. Inbound: import of the last 50 commits (or more), following main, importing open pull requests, 'git push' to the server lands a push as a proven task, and 'git clone' from the server works. No branches; the Git protocol is supported for main only. Agents use avp or MCP, not git.
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > Can run beside GitHub (mirror on avp/line), deliver to main through one always-green pull request with status avp/proof, or make main the mirror so pull requests are no longer merged. It replaces GitHub pull-request review and CI gating for landings. 'avp import-ci' translates GitHub Actions and GitLab CI into avp.toml; remaining workflows still run on mirrored commits (Murmell first saw 9 runs and 144 jobs pile up on main). A bridge can dispatch GitHub deploy workflows. GitHub merge queue is replaced, not combined.
- **Migration and exit:**
  > Partial adoption is a documented path: install, 'avp up', mirror to avp/line, import CI as avp.toml, land a few tasks, then switch main to the mirror. Each step has a documented way back; the vendor says an afternoon, one case exists (Murmell), no independent migration was found. Real cost: writing fast related-test checks, running a server and runners, guarding avp.toml, retraining people off pull requests, and adding follow mode while others still push to main. Leaving: stop the mirror and lift branch protection; main holds every landed position as ordinary commits. Lost on exit: proofs, leases, task and agent conversation links, undo journal and environment history. Elastic licence prevents reselling it as a hosted service but not leaving.

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > Agent: avp_open (or avp open) gives a task and folder; it edits files there; avp_land syncs the writes and asks to land. Server: replays the task on the current head by 3-way merge, runs or reuses the proof on the result, waits for a person when a guarded path or 'proof_and_approval' applies, then appends the next position when it is the task's turn. Landings on one repository queue in order of arrival (ticket-based turn in the server), and proofs can run in parallel before a task's turn; a task that loses its head is proven at most twice in the project's own test of 6 concurrent landings on one shared file. Proofs start speculatively after 3 s without a write, so landing often reuses a green proof of the same tree. Stacks (--on, --stack) land bottom first; tasks that need each other land as one position ('land --with'). After landing: mirror commit, batch full suite, automatic revert. Rejections: red proof with output, conflict with markers, blocked by another task's file, or waiting for approval.
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > At open and at write: advisory leases per path show who else holds a file (strict policy refuses the write instead). At landing: the server replays and 3-way merges file by file and hunk by hunk; identical or purely additive overlapping edits are settled automatically, only changes to the same lines leave markers. Semantic conflicts: only through proof and full-suite tests. Binary files and delete-versus-edit keep the task's version and are named. No entity- or token-level merge.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > Automatic first: replay by 3-way merge, and settle_additions merges both sides when each only inserted text. Otherwise the landing answers 'not landed: conflict', and the files with ours, original and theirs markers are left in the agent's folder; the agent writes one version that keeps both sides and lands again. A red proof comes back with output; a red caused by a teammate's unlanded file names that task, and 'avp wait' blocks until it lands. Cycles of mutually dependent tasks are detected and landed together. No built-in LLM resolver; the agent is the resolver.
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > Expected and handled on the server: a task opened on an old position is replayed on the head at landing. A proof is reused if the line did not touch what it covered, otherwise it runs again (seconds when related tests are few; on Murmell's central modules one developer's proofs took a median of 7 minutes until steps were split; self-reported, production). The agent redoes work only for same-line conflicts. The line moved under a task 14 times in Murmell's first 31 landings, each replayed without conflict (self-reported, production, about 6 hours, one monorepo).
- **Pre write coordination:**
  > Leases on paths: taken by the declared scope at open or on first write, released when the task lands, is abandoned, or writes nothing for 10 minutes. Advisory by default, another task may write and take the lease over and the first is told; enforced only with a repository created with '--policy strict'. avp_tasks, avp_status and avp_claim/avp_release expose who holds what; 'avp wait' blocks on a task or path. No messaging between agents beyond task state, and no cross-agent plan negotiation.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > Mixed and small, all self-reported. For: on go-chi/chi, two agents changing the same function at once landed with no markers through AVP, whereas plain Git ended in a conflict (self-reported, synthetic test, one pair among 3 Claude Code agents); Murmell's first \~6 hours had 14 replays without conflict over 31 landings (production, N=1 repository). Against: the source of merge.rs records a 25 October test with 5 conflicts in 6 landings of the kind 'both agents added an import and a register call' and a 29 October test with 1,066 conflict answers on a single file before additive settling was added (synthetic benchmark). No conflict rate is published for the current version, and the sample does not support a claim of a conflict reduction versus Git with a merge queue. The cooperBench figure quoted in the blog (8 of 10 tasks with AVP versus 1 of 10 with a shared Git remote, 2 agents, self-reported, synthetic benchmark, 10 tasks) is about passing tasks, not conflict rate.

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > Server: one SQLite database (avp.sqlite) plus a content-addressed object folder; not a distributed store. Client: each task is laid out in its own folder under \~/avp/\<repo>-\<n>, reused from landed tasks with its install, files copied from the checkout or fetched in batches; on Murmell's 3,592-file repository the first folder took 11.2 s and the next session 1.0 s (self-reported, production). Each view keeps a local replica for offline use. No sparse, virtual or FUSE checkout, so cost grows with repository size and with the number of agents.
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > 64 Codex agents on one line for 40 minutes on a 96-CPU machine, on a 112-section generated TypeScript spec (self-reported, synthetic benchmark, one run per model, 2026-10-29/30): GPT-6.1 Sol delivered all 112 in 10 min 24 s through AVP versus 8 through Git plus CI; Terra 77 versus 0; GPT-5.6 Luna 105 through AVP versus 110 through Git plus CI at lower cost (4.28 USD versus 7.16 USD). 32 agents (Luna; three runs for AVP and for Git plus CI, two for the merge queue; self-reported, synthetic benchmark): AVP 56, 52 and 56 of 56 sections, Git plus CI 56, 53 and 56, GitHub merge queue with default settings 34 and 32 of 56. Production: 131 landings in 48 hours at Murmell (self-reported), about 1 per 22 minutes. No landing-rate figure was published and no run above 64 agents.
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > Self-reported, all by the maker: (1) production at one company, 48 h versus 48 h plus a first day of about 6 h; (2) synthetic benchmark of 16, 32 and 64 agents, one run at 64 and 16, three at 32; (3) 12-agent, 48-task benchmark with two runs per tool on the marketing site. Duration up to 40 minutes. No independent or peer-reviewed run found.
- **Coordination limit:**
  > Not measured beyond 64 agents. From the code, landings on one repository take turns on a single server process (first come, first served) over one SQLite file, and 4 checks run at once per machine by default unless workers are added; this is inferred from the code, not measured. In the 64-agent run the Git plus CI arm stalled first for two of three models (self-reported, synthetic benchmark); AVP's own limit is unknown. The project's own test of six concurrent landings on one shared file shows each landing waiting its turn.

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > Pre-landing proof of related tests and typecheck on the exact head (affected targets by test imports and declared files), then the full suite once per batch of landings, on the server or on your own workers; red shards rerun once for flakiness, and slow tests that never fail can be skipped from proofs automatically. No speculative merge train: proofs run in parallel, landings are serialized. The proof is not the whole suite, so some breaks reach the line and are caught by the next batch. Reported effect at Murmell, 48 h of pull requests versus 48 h on AVP: median time to main 25 min to 3.4 min, median proof 15 s, CI machine time 77 % less, 57 pull requests versus 131 landings (self-reported, production, no control).
- **Broken main recovery:**
  > A full suite that goes red is run again, and if red twice AVP bisects positions and reverts the culprit in its own proven task; avp bisect and avp revert do the same by hand; production is only a promotion of what staging serves, and environments roll back. No separate known-good pointer is needed for landing because each landing is proven, but the last green full suite is what promotion requires. One vendor case: a revert was refused by its proof because later work depended on it (2026, go-chi test).
- **Review model:**
  > None by default. Landing rule can be proof only, proof and approval, or approval on guarded paths (avp guard); on AVP Cloud an approval must be a person's. Agents have no review role of their own, but avp show, avp diff and avp why are built for human review after landing. Critique from a Product Hunt commenter: an agent that also writes its own tests can prove a wrong change green.

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > MCP server (avp_open, avp_land, avp_check, avp_wait, avp_status, avp_why, avp_undo, avp_claim and others, about 26 tools) and a CLI with --json on most commands, plus an HTTP API. 'avp up' wires 13 agents (Claude Code, Codex, Cursor, Gemini CLI, GitHub Copilot, OpenCode, Qwen Code, Kiro, Zed, Amp, Windsurf, Cline, Aider) and writes a rule into AGENTS.md or the file each agent reads. Landing and proof failures return the failing step output and what to do next. Subagents share one MCP process and each gets a task. Claude Code used it without being told in 3 of 3 vendor trials, Codex in 3 of 3 only with an AGENTS.md rule (self-reported, synthetic test).
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > Per task: avp undo, avp saves, save points and 'undo --to'; every write is in the task journal. Landed work: avp revert \<position> opens a proven revert task, avp pick reapplies one. Offline work is queued in an outbox. Server data recovery is a restore of SQLite and objects; the Git mirror keeps landed state but not proofs or open tasks.
- **Agent cost:**
  > Self-reported and inconsistent across sources. Murmell: tokens per change about the same, 10.0 M against 10.6 M (self-reported, production). 12-agent benchmark: 4.67 M versus 10.27 M tokens and 2.11 USD versus 3.65 USD per run against Git plus CI (self-reported, synthetic benchmark, marketing site, medians of two runs). 64-agent run: 4.28 USD versus 7.16 USD for Luna, 19.77 versus 31.58 USD for Sol (self-reported, synthetic benchmark, one run each; the Sol Git arm was stopped at 40 minutes with 8 of 112 sections done). Early defect, since fixed: an AVP agent cost twice a Git agent, 15 to 24 turns versus 5 to 6 (self-reported, synthetic test). Per single agent AVP cost and time were about equal to Git (146 s and 0.330 USD against 151 s and 0.332 USD, 3 tasks; self-reported, synthetic test). Extra cost: proof machine minutes and a server.
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > Per-person tokens and roles (read, write, admin) per repository, per-environment promoters and approvers, guarded paths that need a person. A token can be made for one repository only. No per-folder read permissions for agents found. Proof workers can run in Docker, Podman or gVisor sandboxes with no ambient secrets and limits; the agent's own folder is not sandboxed by AVP. Provenance is a strength: each position records goal, person, agent, session trailer, proof and full-suite result, commits are authored as 'person (agent)', and avp why \<file>:\<line> can lead to the agent's conversation. A token acts as its person.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > The server replays, proves, lands, mirrors and reverts. The agent opens a task, edits, lands, and settles same-line conflicts or red proofs. Humans approve where configured, resolve mirror blocks, and own avp.toml and deploys.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Vendor self-report and marketing. Main weaknesses: one company's 48-hour windows with no control, benchmark designed by the maker and developed alongside the tool ('land --with' for mutually dependent tasks shipped in 0.5.3, the same release as the benchmark whose spec has such cycles, and the merge.rs comments tie additive settling to the 25 and 29 October bench runs), one run per point at 64 agents, a custom Git plus CI arm that forbids pushes when any test fails, and different, partly inconsistent figures for the same migration (see uncertainty_notes). The method and scripts for the 64-agent benchmark are public.
- **Independent reports:**
  > No independent benchmark or user experience with AVP itself was found. Product Hunt comments (listing read 2026-11-24, shows 83 upvotes) ask three questions that have no maker answer in the page read: what happens to the queue when one task fails, whether same-line edits need a person, and whether AVP can tell when the same agent wrote the change and its tests. A Product Hunt forum thread (about 30 days earlier) is similarly exploratory. No Hacker News thread about AVP in Algolia search (only a Show HN for Murmell's canvas product, 2026-08-30). Reddit could not be searched. Listed without comment in one Chinese repository collection (killop/anything_about_game AI.md).

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > Small team and tens of agents on one Node, Python, Rust, Go, Java or Ruby repository with good related-test selection, slow CI and many pull-request rebases, that wants to keep Git commits on main and is willing to run a server.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > Proof is not the full suite: red breaks land and are caught later; agents can make weak proofs green by writing their own tests; an agent may settle a conflict by dropping a teammate's side (documented, and the tool now warns 'keep both'); avp.toml is powerful and must be guarded; the mirror stops if anything else writes to its branch; full CI on mirrored commits can pile up; workers with few CPUs slow proofs; the server is a single point of failure (SQLite) and serializes landings; licence and public history changed within weeks; small release adoption, one contributor, no issue tracker history; AVP is applied only to one known production user.
- **Minimum useful scale:**
  > The maker says gains are small with one agent and appear with several agents and slow CI. Evidence starts at 2 agents (same-function replay) and is most visible at 32 to 64 agents where Git plus CI stalled. No measured break-even against worktrees plus a merge queue with batching.
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Pilot on a branch mirror. This is the size the tool was built and tested for (2 to 64 agents), it keeps a Git commit for every landing, and adoption steps are reversible. Condition: run it beside Git (avp/line) first, keep the existing full CI on main, compare its verdicts with yours for days, guard avp.toml, and put a person on risky paths. Do not use it as the only gate yet because evidence is one company and the tests only select related suites.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Wait. Nothing above 64 agents is shown, landing is serialized on one server over SQLite, the proof queue depends on runner capacity, and there is no published landing rate or conflict rate at scale. For several hundred agents it would need the maker's or an independent test on a repository like yours; the design would then still be a single-writer line, so landings per hour are bounded by proof time unless proofs stay in seconds and workers are ample. Keep as a pattern source.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > Copy without the tool: prove only related tests and typecheck on the exact head, run the full suite once per batch afterwards, and revert the position that breaks it twice; declare path scope when a task opens and show agents who else holds those paths (advisory leases with 10-minute expiry); start the proof while the agent is still testing; let tasks that depend on each other be merged and tested as one; auto-merge pure additions to the same place (union) and return only same-line conflicts to the agent with an instruction to keep both sides; write person, agent, task and proof into commit trailers; make main a fast-forward mirror with no direct pushes; keep the Git repository as the exit route.

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > https://github.com/Mossab28/avp README (head bf32c5ae, 2026-11-23); docs/CONCEPTS.md, docs/GUIDE.md, docs/QUICKSTART.md, docs/MIGRATE.md, docs/CASE-MURMELL.md, docs/RUNS.md, docs/TROUBLESHOOTING.md, docs/API.md, bench/README.md (read 2026-11-24); src/engine.rs, src/merge.rs, src/mcp.rs, Cargo.toml, tests/land-queue.sh, LICENSE and CONTRIBUTING.md (read 2026-11-24); https://murmell.com/blog/agents-outgrew-pull-requests (2026-10-25, read by summary); https://cloud.murmell.com/ (pricing and benchmark, read 2026-11-24); https://www.producthunt.com/products/avp and https://www.producthunt.com/p/murmell/open-source-alternative-to-git-for-ai-coding-agents (read 2026-11-24); GitHub API for releases, commits, contributors and issues (read 2026-11-24).
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > AVP was not installed or run for this report. Vendor figures for the same migration differ: README and CASE-MURMELL give 15,310 to 3,475 CI minutes (77 % less) over two 48-hour windows; the Cloud page gives 12,039 to 36 GitHub minutes plus 2,689 on AVP machines (99.7 % less on GitHub); the blog compares 3.8 days before to about 6 hours after and cites 4 CI runs a day; the claims of broken-state counts (0 of 43, Git 9 of 45, Jujutsu 12 of 52) have no definition of 'broken' on the page, and the README says main was never red in any run. Search summaries of an older avp.murmell.com page said Git reached its last delivery first in one twelve-agent run and that three bugs were found in AVP; that page now redirects and was not read. Cloud free minutes (2,000 versus 3,000) and Cloud access (invitation-only versus listed plans) disagree. Product Hunt launch date was not confirmed (pages show 30 days old and 2026). Why early releases vanished was not explained. Reddit and LinkedIn were not reachable.
