### GitHub agent-scale Git storage

*Layer: C Git hosting*

#### Basics

- **Name:** GitHub agent-scale Git storage
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > GitHub's internal codename is Project Rubicon; its reference service is Ref-CAS (Reference Compare-and-Swap); the routing layer is Spokes. Described in a series of three github.blog posts: 2026-10-06 (updated 2026-10-26), 2026-10-21 and 2026-11-05. No repository, package or product page found. The 2026-11-05 post resolves under both /engineering/infrastructure/ and /engineering/architecture-optimization/. Not the same thing as OpenAI Pando or Cursor Continuity, which are separate backends with a similar split between packs and references.
- **Maker:**
  > GitHub (Microsoft). Post authors: Brian Celenza (2026-10-06); Eleanor White and Victoria Dye (2026-10-21); Karthik Raman and Brian Celenza (2026-11-05).
- **What it is:**
  > A replacement for the data plane under github.com Git hosting. Spokes still does routing, load balancing, placement and tenant fairness, and the Git protocol and API stay. Below it, disposable virtual replicas serve reads and writes from a local disk cache that can be rebuilt at any time. Packs, indexes, read accelerators and reference checkpoints sit in Azure Blob Storage. References and the list of packs (pack manifest) sit in Ref-CAS, a small replicated log that stores pointers only. Write path: a replica indexes the incoming pack, uploads it to Blob Storage, then commits one Ref-CAS transaction that lists the pack and moves the references, so a reference can only point at objects that are already durable. Read path: the replica sends the version (log sequence number) it holds; if nothing changed the check is one comparison. Compaction and garbage collection run on separate nodes that lease shards of the repository. For contrast, the current system keeps five full copies per repository by default and orders writes with a three-phase commit under a repository-wide lock, so the slowest healthy copy sets the latency of every write. The middle post describes the bridge on the current system: the quorum narrowed from the whole repository to one reference, plus object storage as a quorum member and on-demand read replicas.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > Addresses: throughput (pushes per second and reads per second on one repository), push latency, and maintenance work that used to run on serving hosts. Partly addresses: landing contention, because updates to different references no longer wait on each other; updates to the same reference are still one after another and no figure for one contended reference is published. Does not address: textual conflicts, semantic conflicts, task collision. The first post names merges contending on one reference (trunk-based development, release trains, merge queues) as a demand, but the published design contains no parallel landing on main. Workspace cost: planned only (work without a clone, lighter partial, shallow and sparse clones).
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > Central compare-and-swap per reference, without server-side replay. Branches and pull requests stay, so the working model remains isolate then merge.

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > Not usable by an outsider on request. Limited private preview as of the 2026-11-05 post; broader rollout planned for early 2027. Announced at GitHub Universe in the week before that post. No sign-up page, eligibility rule or changelog entry was found (searched up to 2026-11-24).
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > Proprietary; SaaS on github.com, running in Azure. The posts do not mention a self-hosted or GitHub Enterprise Server version.
- **Price:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] None of the three posts states a price, a plan requirement or a change to existing plans.
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > Design work began in May 2026; first benchmark figure 2026-10-06; private preview by 2026-11-05. No named preview customer. Background migration (self-reported, production): the first repository copy in Azure dates from August 2025; by 2026-10-21 nearly all repositories (99.99999%) had a copy in Azure, most had two of five replicas there, and Azure served about half of Git traffic. That is the older Spokes design hosted in Azure, not Rubicon. Nothing newer than the 2026-11-05 post found up to 2026-11-24.
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > Durable state is in Azure Blob Storage plus a majority quorum in Ref-CAS; compaction verifies that new packs hold the same objects before they are published. Not published: Ref-CAS failover behaviour, recovery objectives, and the details of safe object removal in garbage collection. No data-loss report found. Vendor continuity risk is low; the practical risk is dependence on one provider in the middle of a large storage migration.
- **OS support:** n/a: server-side service; any operating system with a standard Git client or HTTP client works.

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > Full by GitHub's statement: Git client and protocol are kept, and branch protection, required reviews and audit logs are preserved. Not independently tested.
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > It is GitHub, so pull requests, branch protection, merge queue and Actions stay. CI fetch load is the part most helped, because read capacity is meant to scale separately from writes. Planned, not delivered as of 2026-11-24: reading files without a clone, an atomic multi-file commit API that commits whole or is rejected on conflict, event streams, near-live shallow copies of the default branch.
- **Migration and exit:**
  > Adoption cost for a repository already on GitHub should be near zero: GitHub's stated aim is one architecture for every repository, without user migration. A team elsewhere pays the normal move to GitHub. Partial adoption: none, apart from the private preview. Exit: Git history leaves with an ordinary mirror clone; pull requests, reviews and Actions history do not travel with Git (general property of GitHub, not a statement from the posts).

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > Unchanged from GitHub today for the agent: it commits and rebases locally, pushes its own branch, and main moves through a pull request, optionally through the merge queue. Inside the server, one push becomes one Ref-CAS transaction: it locks only the references it touches, checks that each still has the value the writer expects, and becomes visible once a majority of log replicas have recorded it. When two pushes update the same reference, only one is appended. The server does not rebase, replay or merge for the loser at this layer. Because main is one reference, landings on it remain one after another; GitHub publishes no number for updates per second on one contended reference. Already available for agents without a clone: GraphQL createCommitOnBranch (since 2021-09-13) appends one multi-file commit to a branch if the head still matches expectedHeadOid. The planned atomic commit API covers a similar need; whether it would retry against a newer base is not described.
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > At push, at reference level only: the expected old value no longer matches. File, hunk and semantic conflicts are found where they are today: in the client's merge or rebase, in the pull request mergeability check, in the merge queue build, or in CI. The planned atomic commit API is described as rejecting on conflict; its granularity is not stated.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > The losing writer resolves it on the client. The posts say that only one of two updates to a reference is appended and describe no structured payload, automatic replay or conflict-as-data for the other. With createCommitOnBranch the known answer to an outdated expectedHeadOid is a GraphQL error typed STALE_DATA in an HTTP 200 response (shown in a public gist, not in the reference page read).
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > An agent on its own branch is unaffected when main moves; it meets the newer base when the branch is merged. An agent that updates a reference which has moved fails the compare-and-swap and has to fetch, rebase and push again. GitHub publishes no retry rate or retry cost. An independent write-up (2026-11-23) infers that the loser's pack has by then been uploaded, so a hot reference leaves unreferenced packs for garbage collection.
- **Pre write coordination:** None. No claims, locks or messages before editing. Planned event streams could support it later.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > No evidence and no claim that conflicts fall. None of the three posts reports a conflict rate, a rejected-push rate or a merge success rate, and no independent measurement was found. Cheaper pushes may even raise the number of open branches waiting to merge; that is an argument from an independent analysis (Byte-Sized Design, 2026-11-23) and from the design, not a measurement.

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > Client: full clone, or Git's partial clone, shallow clone and sparse checkout, which GitHub says have gaps and do not always combine well; improvements are planned. Server: packs in Azure Blob Storage, references and manifest in Ref-CAS, rebuildable cache on virtual replicas, tiered compaction that keeps the pack count roughly flat while pushes rise. Planned: single-object reads, packs grouped by object type, partial replicas, global replicas with bounded staleness, scale to zero.
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > All figures are self-reported by GitHub. Synthetic (internal test, 2026-11-05): 5,000 concurrent agents and 7,200 pushes per second on one repository while serving reads; that is 1.44 pushes per second per agent (arithmetic here), far above what a coding agent needs. Synthetic (internal benchmark, 2026-10-06): up to 35 times higher write throughput, baseline not stated. Synthetic (one component): Ref-CAS at tens of thousands of operations per second. Synthetic (bridge on the current system, 2026-10-21): 3-4 times higher throughput for writes to different references. Synthetic (rejected prototype, one blob as log): mid-hundreds of writes per second. Production, old architecture: the busiest repositories receive at most dozens of writes in one second, in short bursts. Landings per second on main: not published.
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > Vendor-run synthetic load test. Duration, number of references, pack sizes, hardware and latency values are not published. No production figures for the new architecture.
- **Coordination limit:**
  > No degradation point is published for the new architecture. For GitHub today the documentation recommends at most 6 pushes per minute and 15 read operations per second per repository, 1 merged pull request per minute, 1,000 open pull requests against one branch and 5,000 branches (docs read 2026-11-24; recommendations, while push size and object size limits are enforced).

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > Unchanged: required checks, merge queue, Actions. The storage work makes CI fetches cheaper but adds no batching, speculation or affected-target logic. See the merge queue items for that.
- **Broken main recovery:** n/a: storage layer; the posts describe no detection, bisect, automatic revert or known-good pointer.
- **Review model:**
  > Unchanged: pull request with branch protection and required reviews; keeping those controls is a stated requirement.

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > Today (all older than Rubicon): Git client, gh CLI, REST and GraphQL APIs, non-interactive and machine-readable. createCommitOnBranch commits several files in one call, signed and authored as the credential owner, without an author override. API limits are the practical ceiling for API-driven agents: 5,000 requests per hour per user (15,000 for some Enterprise Cloud apps), 100 concurrent requests, 80 content-creating requests per minute and 500 per hour (docs read 2026-11-24). Planned only: atomic commit API, clone-free reads, event streams.
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > Ordinary Git: revert commits, restore branches, client reflog. Nothing new for agents is described; warnings before costly mistakes appear only among planned Git improvements.
- **Agent cost:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] No figure for tokens, setup time, retries or push latency per agent is published; low commit latency is a stated requirement without a value.
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > GitHub's existing model is kept: accounts, GitHub Apps, fine-grained tokens, branch protection, audit logs. The storage work adds no per-folder write permission and no per-agent identity. Storing agent session context next to each commit is named as a possible materialised view and is not delivered.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > The server orders reference updates. Fetch, rebase, conflict resolution and retry are left to the agent or its orchestrator; the merge queue is the optional server-side landing step.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Vendor self-report: technically detailed and open about two rejected prototypes. Main weakness: the headline test has no published method or latency and does not show many writers landing on one reference. No independent benchmark.
- **Independent reports:**
  > Commentary only; no independent preview user found. Hacker News 2026-11-06 (245 points, about 70 comments): one reader sees future planning that does not fix current outages; one argues that thousands of pushes per second is an unreasonable pattern and asks about issues, pull requests and Actions; one says large private repositories with a high merge rate find the mergeability check slow; one finds it close to Cursor Continuity with a higher claimed rate; one reports HTTP 500 on push that week. Hacker News 2026-10-21 (70 points, 20 comments): mainly reliability complaints about the Azure move. Andrew Nesbitt, 2026-11-11: neutral summary. Byte-Sized Design, 2026-11-23: 1.4 pushes per agent per second is a stress test, main is one reference so merges are not parallel, and latency, failover and cost are missing. dev.to piekwerk, 2026-11-09: says Rubicon drops the single snapshot across references; GitHub's text does not say so (it lists ordering per reference plus quick linearisation across references as a requirement and gives the repository one version number), so that claim is contested. All of these are anecdotal or analytical, not measurements.

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > Teams staying on GitHub whose agents push often to separate branches and whose CI fetches heavily from one repository.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > Cannot be obtained on request; the 2027 date can slip. Improves pushes and reads, not landings: main, CI capacity and review remain the limit. API and pull request limits are separate from Git storage, and nothing says they rise with it. The headline number is easily misread as 5,000 agents landing on main. Users complained about Git and Actions reliability during the Azure migration (anecdotal; cause not established). Contended references may leave unreferenced packs (third-party inference).
- **Minimum useful scale:**
  > Judgement, nothing published: the documented recommendation of 6 pushes per minute per repository is the first trigger (30 agents pushing once every 5 minutes reach it), CI fetches above about 15 read operations per second the second. It never replaces a merge queue.
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Wait. It is a private preview without a published way in, and 5-30 agents can stay near the documented recommendations by batching pushes. Use ordinary GitHub with one branch per agent, fewer but larger pushes, shallow or partial clones where they work, and the merge queue.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Wait, then pilot when the rollout opens in 2027 if the repository is on GitHub; ask GitHub about the preview only if push or fetch limits already hurt. 300 agents pushing once every 5 minutes is 60 pushes per minute, ten times today's recommendation (arithmetic here), so this kind of change is needed at that size. It has to be paired with a landing layer (queue, batching) and conflict avoidance: the documented advice is 1 merged pull request per minute, and nothing published says the new storage raises it.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > Give each agent run its own branch, so that reference updates do not contend. Upload data first and move one pointer last, with compare-and-swap. Use expected-old-value writes (git push --force-with-lease, git push --atomic, createCommitOnBranch with expectedHeadOid), so that a stale writer fails cleanly. Keep the ordered part small, and run compaction and garbage collection away from the serving path. Give the repository one increasing version number so that an unchanged read is cheap. If building on object storage: GitHub reports that conditional writes to one blob stop in the mid-hundreds per second, and that splitting the log over several blobs moves the cost to reads.

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > GitHub Blog, Building Git infrastructure for agent-scale development, 2026-10-06 (updated 2026-10-26), https://github.blog/engineering/architecture-optimization/building-git-infrastructure-for-agent-scale-development/ ; GitHub Blog, The bridge to agent-scale Git infrastructure, 2026-10-21, https://github.blog/engineering/architecture-optimization/the-bridge-to-agent-scale-git-infrastructure/ ; GitHub Blog, Inside GitHub's new Git storage architecture, 2026-11-05, https://github.blog/engineering/infrastructure/inside-githubs-new-git-storage-architecture/ ; GitHub Docs, Repository limits, read 2026-11-24, https://docs.github.com/en/repositories/creating-and-managing-repositories/repository-limits ; GitHub Docs, REST API rate limits, read 2026-11-24, https://docs.github.com/en/rest/using-the-rest-api/rate-limits-for-the-rest-api ; GitHub Docs, GraphQL commits reference, read 2026-11-24, https://docs.github.com/en/graphql/reference/commits ; GitHub Changelog, createCommitOnBranch, 2021-09-13, https://github.blog/changelog/2021-09-13-a-simpler-api-for-authoring-commits/ . Secondary: gist with the STALE_DATA answer, https://gist.github.com/brasic/964dfc371d524a09d602745ae3b238ff ; This Week in Source Control, 2026-11-11, https://nesbitt.io/2026/11/11/this-week-in-source-control.html ; Byte-Sized Design, 2026-11-23, https://read.bytesizeddesign.com/p/github-git-storage-rubicon-ref-cas ; dev.to piekwerk, 2026-11-09, https://dev.to/piekwerk/5000-agents-7200-pushes-a-second-inside-githubs-rubicon-and-the-git-guarantee-it-drops-2l9b ; Hacker News, 2026-11-06, https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50397099 and 2026-10-21, https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50183344
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > Not published: price, preview eligibility, latency values, test duration, how the 7,200 pushes per second were spread over references, the landing rate on one reference, the baseline of the 35x figure, Ref-CAS failover, and whether today's documented repository limits will change. That the test writers were mostly on separate branches is a third-party reading; GitHub says only that agents mostly push to their own branches. The New Stack and Visual Studio Magazine articles returned HTTP 403 and were not used. The research brief speaks of two posts; the series has three.

**Unverified fields (2):**

- price
- agent_cost

### Multi-agent coordination research

*Layer: G coordination*

#### Basics

- **Name:** Multi-agent coordination research
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > STORM: arXiv 2605.20563 v1 (2026-05-19), code RISE-X-Lab/STORM (the older dreamyang-liu/STORM redirects there); unrelated to Stanford STORM, and the package is named async-swe-agents, the same name as in CAID's code. CAID: arXiv 2603.21489 v1 (2026-03-23), v2 (2026-07-08; v2 numbers used here), code JiayiGeng/CAID. CoAgent/MTPO: arXiv 2606.15376 v1 (2026-06-13), no code link found; CoAgent is the system, MTPO (Monotonic Trajectory Pre-Order) its protocol; the name CoAgent is used by other projects too. AgentRoom: arXiv 2608.23740 v1 (2026-08-24), v2 (2026-11-12), page https://seongland.com/article/agentroom, no public code repository found. ATM: arXiv 2607.00041 v1 (2026-06-29), code eaglhuang/AI-Atomic-Framework (npm @ai-atomic-framework/cli). CodeCRDT: arXiv 2510.18893 v1 (2025-10-18), code spugachev/CodeCRDT.
- **Maker:**
  > STORM: Mengyang Liu (Shanghai Jiao Tong University / Cortices AI), Taozhi Chen (Emory), Zhenhua Xu, Xue Jiang, Yihong Dong (Peking University). CAID: Jiayi Geng and Graham Neubig (Carnegie Mellon, LTI; support from Fujitsu). CoAgent: Hongtao Lyu, Dingyan Zhang, Mingyu Wu, Xingda Wei, Haibo Chen (Shanghai Jiao Tong University). AgentRoom: Seonglae Cho (Holistic AI), Donghyun Lee (UC Berkeley). ATM: Eagl Huang (single independent author). CodeCRDT: Sergey Pugachev (single author).
- **What it is:**
  > Six papers on when parallel agents should learn about each other's changes. CAID: a manager builds a dependency graph, each engineer agent works in its own git worktree and self-tests, the manager merges each finished commit to main, and the engineer who meets a conflict pulls main and resolves it. STORM: engineers share one workspace; every file carries a version, and an edit is rejected when the target file or any file the agent has read has changed since; agents also leave intent comments in the code; only the manager commits. CoAgent/MTPO: the order of agents is fixed at launch, reads are filtered by that order, writes happen speculatively in place, and an agent whose premise changed is notified and patches its plan; misplaced writes are undone with inverse operations. AgentRoom: a CRDT-merged shared filesystem plus MCP tools to claim files, read room state and broadcast; claims are atomic but advisory. ATM: agents declare what they will change; a broker admits, serialises or routes the change to a neutral steward before any write (seven layers down to a file lock). CodeCRDT: one shared Yjs document; an outliner writes TODOs and agents claim them by watching the document.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > STORM: textual conflicts and stale reads, part of semantic conflicts (through intent comments); not landing on main, not throughput, not workspace cost. CAID: workspace interference, textual conflicts at integration, task collision through a dependency-aware plan; semantic conflicts only through tests. CoAgent: stale premises and lost updates in shared live state; not source code merging, not Git. AgentRoom: task collision and early abandonment of work; semantic conflicts only through visibility. ATM: task collision and textual/structural conflicts before the write, inside one governance domain; not Git merge, not conflicts between clones or pull requests. CodeCRDT: textual conflicts only; its own data shows semantic conflicts remain. None addresses server throughput, merge queues or workspace cost at hundreds of agents.
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > STORM: write-time detection (optimistic, per file). CAID: isolate then merge. CoAgent: write-time notification and repair under a pre-fixed order. AgentRoom: CRDT plus advisory pre-write claim. ATM: enforced pre-write claim with compare-and-swap on a base hash. CodeCRDT: CRDT plus optimistic claim.

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > All are research. STORM: code public (last push 2026-10-15). CAID: code public (last push 2026-04-02). ATM: alpha CLI on npm (0.1.30, 2026-11-15; the paper is tagged v0.9.0-alpha.1). CodeCRDT: code public, stalled since 2025-10-23. CoAgent and AgentRoom: paper only, no code repository found by 2026-11-24.
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > STORM: no licence file found, self-run. CAID: no licence file found, self-run. Without a licence, reuse rights for both are unclear. ATM: Apache-2.0, self-run. CodeCRDT: MIT, self-run. CoAgent, AgentRoom: n/a, no code published.
- **Price:**
  > No licence fee for any of them; the cost is model API spend, all self-reported. CAID per Commit0-Lite task with Sonnet 4.5: USD 1.9 single agent, USD 8.1 with 4 engineers; per PaperBench task USD 3.3 and USD 6.5. STORM over the 16 Commit0-Lite repositories with Sonnet 4.6, derived from its cost-per-score figures: about USD 66 single agent, USD 212 worktrees, USD 292 STORM with 4 engineers; its scaling run gives about USD 199, 292 and 429 for at most 2, 4 and 8 engineers. The others publish token or time ratios only.
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > Read 2026-11-24. STORM: preprint, 11 stars, last push 2026-10-15. CAID: preprint, no venue confirmed, 80 stars, last push 2026-04-02; a guest post on the OpenHands blog (2026-04-27). CoAgent: preprint marked as submitted to ATC 2026, acceptance not verified, 5 citations in Semantic Scholar. AgentRoom: NeurIPS 2026 poster (confirmed on neurips.cc), earlier ICML 2026 FAGEN workshop; 1 citation. ATM: preprint, 3 stars, last push 2026-11-16. CodeCRDT: preprint, 4 stars, last push 2025-10-23. No known production users apart from ATM's author using it on his own projects.
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > Research code with one or a few maintainers. CodeCRDT is stalled; CAID has had no push since April 2026; ATM depends on one person. STORM keeps all engineers in one workspace, so an unsafe shell command affects everyone. CAID's code falls back to a merge that keeps the engineer's version when no rounds are left, which can drop other work.
- **OS support:**
  > STORM and CAID: Docker on linux/amd64 with Python (STORM needs 3.12 or newer). ATM: Node 20 or newer, Windows not mentioned. CodeCRDT: Node. CoAgent: evaluated on Ubuntu 22.04. AgentRoom: not stated; driven through Claude Code, Codex and Gemini CLIs.

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > CAID: plain Git worktrees, commits and merges. STORM: Git repository inside a container, where only the manager commits; engineers do not use Git. ATM: works inside a Git worktree and states that it does not replace Git merge. CoAgent: middleware over tools, not Git. AgentRoom and CodeCRDT: none, shared CRDT state.
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > None of the papers integrates with GitHub pull requests or CI. CAID is the closest, since its output is ordinary commits. ATM ships integrations for coding tools (Cursor, Claude Code, Codex, Copilot, Gemini, Antigravity), not for a code host, and says cross-clone and pull request conflicts are out of scope.
- **Migration and exit:**
  > Copying CAID's scheme costs little, since worktrees and merges already exist, and leaving loses nothing. STORM needs agents to edit through its file editor in one container; leaving means going back to worktrees, with the Git history kept. ATM needs task declarations and its registry in the repository; leaving is removing it. CoAgent, AgentRoom and CodeCRDT cannot be adopted as tools today in practical terms (no code, or a stalled demo).

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > CAID: engineers commit on their branch after self-testing; the manager runs git merge into main as each one finishes, so integration is sequential; on conflict the engineer pulls main, resolves and resubmits; shared files such as package init files are restricted. STORM: nothing lands by merge while engineers work; edits are accepted one by one into the shared workspace, and the manager reviews the diff, runs tests and commits. CoAgent: there is no landing; writes act directly on live state, and the result is serialisable in the launch order if agents judge correctly. AgentRoom: there is no landing; files converge through the CRDT and the final folder is the result. ATM: admitted agents write inside their declared scope; overlapping but mergeable work goes to a neutral steward that applies it in order; the result is committed through Git as usual. CodeCRDT: no commits; the shared document is the result. So only CAID and ATM describe a path to a Git main branch, and neither covers several clones or a merge queue.
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > STORM: at write, per file version, covering both the target and the files read. CAID: at merge, per text hunk (git merge), plus the engineer's own tests. CoAgent: at write and read of shared objects, per object, by order. AgentRoom: before write for claimed files (advisory, per file); the CRDT merges characters silently. ATM: before write, from declared identifiers, shared surfaces, read/write dependencies and ranges, down to a file lock. CodeCRDT: after merge, by claims on TODOs and by tests or reading; characters merge silently.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > STORM: the edit is rejected and the agent gets the current content, a unified diff and the list of outdated dependencies with version differences, plus a short reservation to retry. CAID: conflict markers through git merge; the engineer who wrote the branch resolves. CoAgent: one-way notification; the agent re-judges and patches, and the framework undoes writes that violated the order. AgentRoom: a claim on a held file is refused with an explicit error and the agent picks another file; the CRDT merges any text silently. ATM: a structured verdict rather than a bare refusal (verdict label, blocking layer, conflicting identifier), leading to apply, queue, steward review or block. CodeCRDT: automatic character merge; the claim loser takes another TODO.
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > STORM: an edit based on an outdated read is rejected at once; the retry is one write, with context still fresh. CAID: the base goes stale silently until the merge; the retry is a pull, a resolution and a new self-test. CoAgent: a stale reader is told and patches only what is affected; the paper counts 0.95 aborts per trial and 1.83 times the tokens for classic optimistic control on the same workloads (self-reported, synthetic). AgentRoom and CodeCRDT: agents see each other's edits continuously, so the base is never formally stale, but the agent's understanding can be. ATM: compare-and-swap on a base hash; a mismatch sends the task back or to the steward.
- **Pre write coordination:**
  > CAID: dependency graph and file assignment by the manager; advisory. STORM: disjoint file sets by the manager, intent comments, short reservations after a rejection. CoAgent: serialisation order fixed at launch. AgentRoom: file claims with atomic mutual exclusion but only prompt-level enforcement, plus broadcasts. ATM: enforced admission before writing, from declarations; a wrong declaration breaks the guarantee. CodeCRDT: optimistic claim of a TODO with a 50 ms verification.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > All figures are self-reported by the authors, on benchmarks or simulations unless marked otherwise; none counts conflicts in production Git history. STORM (synthetic benchmark, Commit0-Lite, 16 repositories, 4 engineers, Sonnet 4.6): macro/weighted test pass rate 66.4/20.7 single, 63.8/24.6 worktrees, 65.4/24.0 instruction-only shared workspace, 82.5/46.2 STORM. Macro with Qwen 3.6 Plus: 75.3 single, 57.4 worktrees, 70.5 STORM; with DeepSeek V4 Pro: 65.2, 44.0, 63.2, so on macro STORM is below the single agent with two of three models, while on the weighted score it leads with all three (46.2, 61.4, 32.3). PaperBench Code-Dev, Sonnet (2 engineers): 68.7 single, 72.7 worktrees, 74.1 STORM. The often quoted 87.6 and 78.2 are STORM-Combined, a best-of union with the single agent. Conflicts are moved, not removed: acceptance of reviewed writes is 91.8% for worktrees, 81.2% for STORM at 4 engineers and 67.1% at 8 (proxy measures). STORM's own ablation without intent comments gives 70.9/26.6, so against worktrees the write check alone keeps +7.1 macro and +2.0 weighted of a total of +18.7 and +21.6. CAID (synthetic benchmark, v2): Commit0-Lite with 4 engineers, macro: Sonnet 4.5 53.1 to 59.1 (p=0.006), MiniMax 2.5 42.3 to 57.0 (p=0.007), GLM 4.7 42.9 to 46.5 (p=0.095, not significant; one-sided paired t-tests); worktrees against instruction-only shared workspace 59.1 against 56.1 (Commit0-Lite) and 63.3 against 55.5 (PaperBench). The abstract's +25.6 and +14.7 are the MiniMax best case; Claude gains about 6 points. CoAgent (synthetic, 10 hand-built contended pairs, two agents each, 10 trials, deepseek-v4-flash, office and Kubernetes tasks rather than code): within 5% of serial correctness at 1.4 times the speed; naive parallel passes 13%. AgentRoom (synthetic benchmark, one greenfield TypeScript task as headline, Sonnet 4.6, LLM judge): two agents 0.669 against parallel-merge 0.456 (p=0.003) and solo 0.544. Its parallel-merge baseline is a union of files where the later timestamp wins, not a git merge, so the gap says nothing about Git worktrees. It claims no semantic conflicts in its main pool. A secondary replay that executes the result (capabilities passed of 10) gives 5.06 AgentRoom, 4.91 shared workspace only, 4.70 without the MCP tools, 3.67 solo, 1.46 ChatDev-style, 1.00 parallel-merge. ATM (synthetic benchmark of 20 scenarios and 42 comparisons against the author's own frozen oracle): 42 of 42 routes right, 2 unsafe admissions and 4 unnecessary serialisations in the policy comparison; field use in the author's own projects: 37 task cards, 2 out-of-scope writes refused, 1 contention burst. CodeCRDT (synthetic, 600 trials, 6 small TypeScript tasks): no merge failures, but 5-10% semantic conflicts in a manual sample of 60.

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > CAID: one worktree per engineer. STORM: one shared workspace in a container, with per-file version counters in a mediating service. CoAgent: live system state behind middleware. AgentRoom: shared CRDT filesystem (pycrdt/Yrs). ATM: normal worktree plus a registry of atoms and declared scopes. CodeCRDT: Yjs document over a Hocuspocus relay.
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > All self-reported. STORM (synthetic benchmark): at most 8 engineers, 6.4 deployed on average. CAID (synthetic benchmark): 8 engineers tried, best result at 4 (Commit0-Lite) and 2 (PaperBench). CoAgent (synthetic): 2 agents per trial. AgentRoom (synthetic): 1 to 4 agents, the claim demonstrated at 2. CodeCRDT (synthetic): 5 agents; anything above is the author's projection. ATM (synthetic harness): 50-way contention run of the broker, not 50 coding agents; field use (production, author's own projects): 15 agent channels over time, one burst of 10 task cards. Landing throughput: none published by any paper. No controlled coding experiment above 8 agents.
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > STORM, CAID: synthetic coding benchmarks (16 repositories and 20 papers; STORM states no seeds or repeats). CoAgent: synthetic, 10 scenario pairs with 10 trials. AgentRoom: synthetic, one main task with 6 to 32 runs per condition. CodeCRDT: synthetic, 600 trials. ATM: synthetic benchmark plus the author's own field notes (about three weeks). Run by the authors in every case.
- **Coordination limit:**
  > CAID: quality falls from 4 to 8 engineers on Commit0-Lite (simpy 92.1% with 4, 44.3% with 8, several engineers having been given functions in one file); no gain beyond 2 on PaperBench. STORM: still improving at 8, but acceptance of writes falls to 67.1%, first-round overlap grows to 35.1% and only 6.4 of 8 engineers are used. AgentRoom: judged quality peaks at 2 agents (0.669), then 0.553 with 3 and 0.489 with 4, while mean tests passing peaks at 3; messages grow faster than the number of agents past 3. CodeCRDT: tested to 5; the author's projection puts the useful range at 3-5 and break-even near 20. ATM: the author says hundreds of agents could make the single broker a bottleneck. CoAgent: not studied above 2. All self-reported.

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > No paper tests merge queues or CI. CAID: each engineer tests its change before submitting, and the manager may review; with manager review on 8 repositories 60.2% in 3,689 s, self-verification 55.1% in 2,244 s, efficiency-first 54.0% in 1,909 s (self-reported, synthetic). STORM: the manager runs tests before committing. ATM: validators after the change. CoAgent, AgentRoom, CodeCRDT: evaluation oracle only.
- **Broken main recovery:**
  > None of the papers covers a broken main. CoAgent undoes misplaced writes with inverse operations; with CAID and STORM one could return to the last accepted commit (reading, not a tested feature).
- **Review model:**
  > CAID and STORM: manager agent reviews. ATM: declared validators and review by the steward. CoAgent: the agent re-judges after a notification. AgentRoom and CodeCRDT: no review of the final result. No human pull request in any of them.

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > STORM and CAID: OpenHands SDK tools. ATM: CLI and integrations for several coding tools; Node 20 or newer. AgentRoom: MCP tools (claim, release, read state, broadcast). CoAgent: middleware tool wrappers, with a tool-generating component (ToolSmith). CodeCRDT: shared document API. ATM is the only one ready to call from an existing agent through a CLI.
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > CAID: Git history per engineer. STORM: rejected edits change nothing; committed edits by Git. CoAgent: inverse operations for misplaced writes. ATM: the broker refuses before writing. AgentRoom, CodeCRDT: no undo described beyond the CRDT history.
- **Agent cost:**
  > All self-reported. CAID: 4.3 times the cost (USD 8.1 against USD 1.9) and 2.3 times the runtime (1,583 s against 693 s) of a single agent on Commit0-Lite with Sonnet 4.5; wall-clock time was not reduced. STORM: cost per score point 6.3 against 8.6 for worktrees and 3.2 for one agent; roughly the same wall-clock time as more engineers are added. CoAgent: 1.15 times the tokens of serial execution. AgentRoom (task T4): total compute 550 s solo, 1,072 s with two agents, 1,572 s with three; messages grow faster than the number of agents past 3. CodeCRDT: 82-189% more code and 13.1% longer on average. ATM: decision P95 0.024 ms and steward apply P95 about 0.3 s (synthetic harness), plus the declaration effort per task, which is not measured.
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > CAID: branch and worktree per engineer give authorship and scope. STORM: engineer ids in comments; only the manager commits; shell writes bypass mediation and are only detected afterwards. ATM: declared scope per task, with refusals recorded. AgentRoom: claims name their holder, advisory. CoAgent: order position per agent. CodeCRDT: author per edit in the CRDT. No per-agent permissions beyond these, and no prompt-level provenance.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > CAID: the manager agent merges; engineers resolve their own conflicts. STORM: the service rejects stale writes; the manager commits. CoAgent: the framework orders and notifies; agents repair. AgentRoom: the agents themselves, by claims and messages. ATM: broker and neutral steward; the agent declares and waits. CodeCRDT: the CRDT; agents claim work.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > STORM, CAID, ATM, CodeCRDT: preprints, authors' own benchmarks. CoAgent: preprint, submitted. AgentRoom: peer-reviewed NeurIPS 2026 poster, but on one main task, with LLM judging. Main weakness across all: the authors test their own system, at 2-8 agents, on tasks that fit in one container; models, judges and baselines differ, so scores cannot be compared across papers.
- **Independent reports:**
  > No independent reproduction of any of the six was found. The closest to an outside test is STORM running the worktree baseline of CAID, but it is run by the competing authors and is not in their published code (no worktree mode was found in the repository). CoAgent, AgentRoom and ATM discuss other systems in their related-work sections without re-running them.

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > Reading, not adoption: choosing between worktrees, shared workspace with checks, claims and CRDTs. CAID for splitting a build-from-scratch task, STORM for tightly coupled code in one workspace, CoAgent for agents acting on a shared live system, ATM for teams willing to declare the scope of each task.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > Common: a poor split of work into files or interfaces undoes any protocol; agents pass the check and still break behaviour. STORM: shell edits bypass the check; hot files serialise; nearly all failed runs had scope drift or two accepted edits to one file. CAID: merge conflicts arrive late; resolver fallback can keep one side; runs vary widely (two runs of the same minitorch setup gave 8.7% and 34.3%). CoAgent: correctness rests on agents judging honestly (5 of 100 trials misjudged), and it needs an order fixed at the start. AgentRoom: claims are advisory, unclaimed writes were not linked to worse quality, and a second model (Codex) got worse on one task. ATM: wrong declarations break the guarantee; single broker; single author evidence. CodeCRDT: code volume grows and average time is worse.
- **Minimum useful scale:**
  > No paper measures this against worktrees plus a merge queue. From the results: the worth of any coordination layer shows with coupled work rather than with the number of agents, from about 2 agents on tasks that share interfaces (STORM high-coupling stratum, AgentRoom at 2). Where work splits into separate files, plain worktrees are enough: in STORM's own PaperBench run worktrees are best on 6 of 20 papers, which the authors link to tasks that map to separate files, against 11 for STORM and 3 for the single agent.
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Skip as tools, copy the practices. Keep worktrees plus a merge queue as the default; have a planner give each agent whole files or modules, merge each commit as it is finished, and make the engineer resolve its own conflicts. Pilot ATM (alpha) only on a small part of the work, if enforced pre-write scopes are wanted. STORM and CAID have no licence file, and 5-30 agents on one repository lies mostly above what they tested (2-8).
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Skip. There is no controlled result above 8 coding agents; what exists at larger numbers is a 50-way synthetic contention run in ATM and projections. Central parts are the likely bottleneck: ATM itself names its single broker, CAID merges sequentially through one manager, and AgentRoom leaves open whether its single broadcast channel saturates. At hundreds of agents, the lesson is to raise the number of file boundaries, keep conflicts visible early and let a queue order the merges, not to adopt any of these protocols.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > Split work by file and interface before starting; overlap is predictable from dependency signals and gets worse with more agents. Check edits against the files an agent has read, not only the file it writes: STORM's per-file version check shows this can be done with a read snapshot. Return a useful rejection (diff plus current content) instead of only a refusal. Leave an intent comment above each block, and preserve other agents' comments; in STORM's ablation this accounts for most of the gain. Merge every finished commit rather than one batch at the end (CAID's design; no paper compares the two). Let the author of a branch resolve its conflicts. Give agents visibility of others' work (AgentRoom's execution probe suggests visibility may matter more than claims). Treat byte convergence as no proof of correct behaviour; run tests on the integrated result. For agents acting on live systems, order the actions up front and undo by inverse operation (CoAgent).

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > STORM v1, 2026-05-19, https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20563 and https://github.com/RISE-X-Lab/STORM ; CAID v2, 2026-07-08 (v1 2026-03-23), https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.21489 and https://github.com/JiayiGeng/CAID ; CoAgent/MTPO v1, 2026-06-13, https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15376 ; AgentRoom v2, 2026-11-12 (v1 2026-08-24), https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23740 ; poster listing https://neurips.cc/virtual/2026/poster/148882 ; workshop https://icml.cc/virtual/2026/78012 ; project page https://seongland.com/article/agentroom ; ATM v1, 2026-06-29, https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00041 and https://github.com/eaglhuang/AI-Atomic-Framework ; CodeCRDT v1, 2025-10-18, https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.18893 and https://github.com/spugachev/CodeCRDT ; cited inside CoAgent as background: S-Bus, https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17076 (74% of reads on a SWE-bench workload invisible to the tool surface).
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > Where the papers disagree, in short (full version in where_the_papers_disagree_and_why): (1) Worktrees: CAID finds they help (59.1 against 53.1 macro on Commit0-Lite, Sonnet 4.5), STORM finds they hurt on macro (63.8 against 66.4, Sonnet 4.6); likely because the single agent is stronger, the models differ, and STORM's authors re-ran the rival baseline with one merge at the end. On PaperBench they mostly agree that worktrees help. (2) Shared workspace: both find instruction-only sharing no clear improvement over worktrees; only STORM tests enforced write checks, so CAID does not contradict it there. (3) Cause: STORM's abstract credits write-time detection, its ablation credits intent comments for most of the gain. (4) Reject or notify: CoAgent shows that aborting whole agent transactions wastes work, STORM that refusing one edit is cheap; the unit differs. (5) Claims: ATM enforces them, AgentRoom keeps them advisory and sees no harm in unclaimed writes; they measure different outcomes. (6) Agent count: best at 4 (CAID), still rising at 8 (STORM), best at 2 (AgentRoom), 3-5 projected (CodeCRDT). Unresolved: the worktree baseline in STORM cannot be re-run from the published STORM code, and the paper says only that branches were merged after completion, which may differ from CAID's merge as each engineer finishes. Not confirmed: whether CoAgent was accepted at ATC 2026; whether STORM and CAID will get a licence; whether AgentRoom code will be published. Data flags noted in STORM: the Qwen worktree and STORM results for jinja are identical, one PaperBench row has zero cost, and the text says worktrees are worst across the board although the weighted table shows them above the single agent with Sonnet. CodeCRDT is internally unclear on conflict types (5-10% overall against 20%/80% by task kind).

#### Other info

- **Where the papers disagree and why:**
  > 1) Do isolated worktrees help? CAID says yes: +6.0 points over a single Sonnet 4.5 agent on Commit0-Lite, and worktrees beat an instruction-only shared workspace. STORM says no: worktrees score 63.8 macro against 66.4 for a single Sonnet 4.6 agent and are below the single agent on macro with all three models (on the weighted score they are above it with Sonnet, 24.6 against 20.7, and below with Qwen and DeepSeek). Likely reasons (a reading; no paper isolates them): a stronger single-agent baseline in STORM (66.4 against 53.1) leaves less to gain; other models (Sonnet 4.6, Qwen, DeepSeek against Sonnet 4.5, MiniMax, GLM); the worktree baseline in STORM was run by the authors of the competing method and, as the paper says, merged after completion, while CAID merges each engineer's commit as it is finished, and the released STORM code has no worktree mode to check this; delegation differs between runs (two CAID runs of one repository gave 8.7% and 34.3%) and neither paper reports repeated runs per cell. The judge does not explain it: Commit0-Lite is scored by pytest in both. On PaperBench (judged by Sonnet 4.6 in STORM and gpt-5-mini in CAID, so levels are not comparable) they mostly agree that worktrees beat one agent: 72.7 against 68.7 and 51.6 against 47.7 (Qwen) in STORM, 63.3 against 57.2 in CAID; DeepSeek in STORM is the exception (55.8 against 62.9). 2) Is a shared workspace enough? Neither finds instruction-only sharing clearly better than worktrees: about equal in STORM (65.4 against 63.8 macro), worse in CAID (56.1 against 59.1 on Commit0-Lite, 55.5 against 63.3 on PaperBench, where it falls below the single agent at 57.2). The real open question is whether enforced write-time checks help, and only STORM tests that. 3) Is the write check the cause of STORM's gain? STORM's abstract credits it; its ablation credits the intent comments: without them STORM drops to 70.9/26.6, so most of the gain over worktrees comes from the comments. 4) Reject or notify? STORM rejects a stale edit and lets the agent retry; CoAgent finds that aborting and rejecting wastes work (0.93 times the speed, 1.83 times the tokens for classic optimistic control) and prefers notifying and patching. The unit differs: CoAgent aborts whole agent transactions, STORM refuses one write. 5) Do enforced claims matter? ATM enforces admission and measures only its own scenarios; AgentRoom keeps claims advisory and finds that unclaimed writes went with slightly higher judged quality (r=0.28, correlation only). Different questions: ATM asks whether unsafe combinations are refused, AgentRoom whether quality rises. 6) Is a CRDT the answer? CodeCRDT shows that text converges while 5-10% semantic conflicts remain; CoAgent argues that CRDTs only fit operations that commute, and ATM that they leave semantic conflicts to later validation. AgentRoom uses a CRDT but credits the explicit signalling channel, and its two scorers disagree: by LLM judge most of the gain over a shared workspace comes with the MCP tools (0.575 shared only, 0.588 with the prompt, 0.669 with the tools, the last step not statistically separated), while the replay that executes the result shows almost no gap (4.91, 4.70, 5.06 of 10). A small comparison inside AgentRoom (2 runs against 7) says a system-level claim error works better than claims broadcast as free text. 7) How many agents? CAID best at 4, STORM still rising at 8, AgentRoom best at 2 (3 by tests passing), CodeCRDT 3-5 by projection; causes are different tasks (repository building, paper reproduction, greenfield app, small components) and, for CAID at 8, a manager that put several engineers in one file. Agreement: decomposition quality decides, text convergence is not correctness, and tests on the integrated result are needed. Short answer: the disagreement is mostly about how strong the single agent is, whether comments and visibility are counted as part of the method, and whether the conflict unit is a file, a transaction or a claim; no paper has shown that write-time coordination beats worktrees plus a queue at realistic scale.

### GitLab next-gen SCM

*Layer: C Git hosting*

#### Basics

- **Name:** GitLab next-gen SCM
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > Product page: https://about.gitlab.com/early-access-preview/next-gen-source-control/ . GitLab marketing calls it next-gen SCM or next-generation source code management. Project Switch appears as the project name in Japanese press of June 2026 and in the description of GitLab epic &21711 (title: MVCC Backend (Object Storage back-end)). The engineering design is in the GitLab handbook as Scaling Git. That the three names mean one programme is well supported (same wording, same architecture, and the push-throughput design is by user kerusu, matching blog author Kranthi Erusu) but is an inference; no GitLab page states it. The 2026-10-13 post resolves under two slugs (scaling-git-for-agentic-software-engineering and scaling-git-for-the-agentic-software-factory). Not the same thing as GitLab Orbit (context graph), GitLab Duo Agent Platform, or the separate New SCM Experience design epics.
- **Maker:**
  > GitLab Inc. Blog authors: Kranthi Erusu and Jessica Taylor (2026-08-26), Bill Staples (2026-10-13). Design documents: user pks-gitlab (Scaling Git), user kerusu (Push Throughput).
- **What it is:**
  > A rebuilt Git backend for GitLab plus agent-facing read and write interfaces. Three evidence layers, kept apart here. (1) Vendor posts: the Git protocol stays; stateless compute scales out over shared object storage reached through the S3 API; an intelligence layer does routing, caching and background work; agents read files, blame, history or a diff stat from the server instead of cloning, commit without pulling the repository, and work in isolated sessions that can be thrown away. (2) Public design documents (proposals, not committed): the repository is a set of immutable content-addressed artifacts (packs, reftables, indexes, manifests) in object storage; a numbered generation log names the active manifest; a write becomes durable by a create-only conditional write of the next generation; Gitaly nodes hold only a cache and prefetch what a manifest names. A single mutable pointer was dropped because object stores limit updates to one key (the document cites one update per second per object on Google Cloud Storage). The push-throughput proposal of 2026-11-12 adds ingest on any pod, one committer pod per repository and one generation per batch of pushes. (3) Public tracker: the work is staged (references, objects, S3 backend, clustering) and unfinished.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > Addresses, by GitLab's statement: throughput for many concurrent sessions and workspace cost (its clone tax: 5-10 GB and more than 30 seconds of setup for one agent invocation, self-reported). Partly: landing contention, only as concurrent pushes to different references of one repository. Task collision only through isolation of sessions. Does not address: textual conflicts and semantic conflicts. The posts do not describe merge or conflict handling. GitLab's topic page talks about intent-aware coordination that flags overlapping objectives before merge, but that is explainer copy for the category and is not listed as a feature of the beta. The October post itself says the bottleneck then moves to validation, security review and deployment.
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > Isolate then merge (branch or session per agent, merge request at the end). Inside the server: central compare-and-swap on a generation log, with a three-way merge of references and a union of objects.

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > Private beta, by request form; announced 2026-06-10 at GitLab Transcend London, still private beta in the 2026-10-13 post and on the product pages read 2026-11-24. No GA date, eligibility rule or named beta customer was found.
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > Vendor: GitLab.com, Self-Managed, Dedicated, private cloud and air-gapped are all named on the product page, with customer-controlled S3-compatible storage. Design document (proposal): the objects-on-local-disk stage is the last stage for non-enterprise customers and the object-storage migration is for enterprise customers only; a Gitaly work item of 2026-11-22 expects the non-EE build to reject a bucket configuration. Inference, not a vendor statement: the self-hosted object-storage form needs the Enterprise Edition; the paid tier is not stated. The Git-side storage interfaces are being upstreamed to Git.
- **Price:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] No price or tier is published for next-gen SCM. For reference (pricing page read 2026-11-24): GitLab Premium 29 USD per user per month billed annually, Ultimate by quote.
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > Design document created 2026-06-16, status ongoing, described as not yet a committed design. Tracker state on 2026-11-24: proof-of-concept epic closed 2026-07-09; references stage closed 2026-11-05 with the note that one or two repositories were ready to migrate on staging; on 2026-11-19 the objects backend for Git landed; packfile infrastructure due 2027-01-20; simple clustering and routing 2026-11-02 to 2027-02-01; S3-compatible backend 2027-01-29 to 2027-04-09. Work is active (proof-of-concept merge request updated on 2026-11-24). Gitaly itself is a mature production service; the new backend is not in general production use by any public statement.
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > The design aims at stateless nodes with no data loss when a node dies, atomic writes and point-in-time reads. Open in the documents: truncating the generation log, cold-cache latency, object-store rate limits, multi-region replication (out of scope). Failure table in the push design: if a put times out with unknown outcome, the client is told the push failed although it may be committed. A test plan of 2026-11-22 states that no job could yet verify a Gitaly change on object storage before merge. Vendor continuity risk is low (public company); the October post carries a forward-looking-statements disclaimer.
- **OS support:** n/a: server-side service; any operating system with a standard Git client or HTTP client works.

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > Git protocol and existing clients, repositories and branches kept, by GitLab's statement; the design documents confirm that Git itself runs unchanged for the client and only the storage backend differs. Not independently tested. The agent read and write APIs are an addition beside Git.
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > It is GitLab: merge requests, pipelines, protected branches, Code Owners, approvals and audit events are kept. It does not work as a backend for GitHub; a GitHub team would have to migrate (a one-way GitHub Sync is tracked separately: experiment epic closed, beta epic open, 2026-11-23). External coding agents named on the product page: Claude Code, OpenAI Codex, Google Antigravity.
- **Migration and exit:**
  > For a project already on GitLab the aim is a backend change without a repository move; the first steps are migrations between storage formats on the server. Constraint in the S3-backend epic: forks of repositories on the new backend are rejected at that stage. For a team on GitHub the cost is a full platform move. Partial adoption: only through the private beta. Exit: Git history leaves with an ordinary mirror clone; merge requests, session records and CI history stay in GitLab.

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > Vendor description: an agent works in its own session and branch; a failed attempt is discarded; a successful one becomes a merge request and passes the same approvals, pipelines and protected-branch rules as a human change. The landing step itself is therefore GitLab's existing one, optionally a merge train (Premium and Ultimate): each merge request is tested on top of the target branch plus all merge requests ahead of it, 20 pipelines in parallel by default (configurable since 19.0), no batching; a failure removes the merge request and restarts all pipelines behind it. Server design (proposal): a push runs Git on any ingest pod against one resolved manifest, uploads its artifacts, and sends a delta of references with their old values to the one committer pod of the repository; the committer checks each old value in memory, folds many deltas into one batch and publishes one generation with one conditional put. A delta whose references have not moved is accepted on a newer head; a delta that updates a moved reference is rejected. The server does not rebase or merge file contents for the writer at this layer. main is still one reference, so landings on it remain ordered.
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > Server design: at publish, at reference level. A conflict exists only when the same reference moved; a generation that moved for other references is merged automatically (three-way merge of references, union of objects). File and hunk conflicts are found where GitLab finds them today: merge request mergeability (git merge-tree) and merge train entry. Semantic conflicts: only by CI on the train. Nothing is detected before or at write.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > At push: rejection for the writer whose reference moved, with an internal retry for pushes that only raced on other references. For merge requests, today, outside next-gen SCM: a merge request with conflicts is dropped from the train with a note that names up to 10 files; the author rebases and re-adds it. New in the tracker: a GraphQL mutation that resolves merge request conflicts (epic &23712, closed 2026-11-03) and a Duo flow that uses it without a clone (epic &24374, closed 2026-11-12). Motive given there (one self-reported case): a Duo session took 24.5 minutes for one conflicted file, of which about 2 minutes was the resolution, and left the merge request conflicted. Feature-flag state and release of both were not checked.
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > An agent on its own branch is unaffected when main moves; it meets the newer base at the merge request or merge train. At storage level a stale base is the normal case: in the lab run of 2026-10-26, 91% of publishes (1,619 of 1,786) arrived with a base that was no longer the newest generation although the harness ran its branch strategy (read here as one branch per agent), and were merged by the server at reference level (GitLab internal lab, synthetic). The cost was about 0.25 s median under the per-repository lock per publish.
- **Pre write coordination:**
  > None in next-gen SCM: no claims, locks or messages before editing. Adjacent and not shipped: epic &24427 (opened for work from 2026-11-14, target 2026-12-11) proposes that Duo sessions can find each other, exchange messages and report back.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > No evidence and no claim that conflicts fall. Neither post, nor the product page, nor the design documents report a conflict rate, a rejected-push rate for real work or a merge success rate, and no independent measurement was found. More cheap parallel sessions may raise the number of branches waiting to merge (reasoning, not measured).

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > Client: no full clone for agents that use the server-side APIs (batch file read, blame, history, diff stat, last commit per path); ordinary clone and fetch otherwise. Server: immutable packs, reftables and manifests in S3-compatible object storage, local disk as cache, geometric compaction with frozen large packs. Self-reported bound: with a 1 GB freeze threshold and a ratio of 2, a warm node has to fetch at most 2 GB to catch up, whatever the repository size (design arithmetic, not a measurement). Forks are meant to share artifacts through a deduplication network.
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > 16 concurrent agents on one repository is the largest figure with a published method, all self-reported by GitLab engineers on a proof-of-concept branch, synthetic, 2-minute windows, harness strategy named branch (read here as each agent pushing its own branch). With hooks on (2026-10-26): 3.4 pushes/s on MVCC over S3, against 5.5 on the same image's file backend and 7.3 on stock GitLab 19.5.1. With hooks stubbed, one m5.2xlarge node (2026-10-30 and 31): 5.5 pushes/s at baseline and 8.9 with group commit on S3; 10.3 to 10.5 on a local-file store. Modelled, not measured: 115-155 publishes/s for one merge per push, and a batch cost extrapolated to 220 pushes/s, from a laptop microbenchmark. Thousands of agent sessions is a design goal in the posts, not a demonstration. No landing rate on main is published.
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > Three kinds. Vendor peaks without method: up to 50x faster, 2x fewer tokens, 1,000x less network traffic (early internal tests; GitLab calls them ceilings and says sustained concurrent load is still being validated). Secondary: up to 42x clone, 17x write, about 22x agent work time (Publickey via GameBusiness.jp, June 2026; not found on GitLab's pages read here). Internal synthetic lab runs in the public tracker: up to 16 agents, 2 minutes per level. No production figures and none for the beta.
- **Coordination limit:**
  > In the lab the proof of concept was flat from 4 agents upward at 3.4 to 3.9 pushes/s on one repository (2026-10-26). Two causes are documented: a per-repository publish lock with one object-store round trip per push, and pre- and post-receive hooks that cost 0.4 to 0.65 s per push and cap one repository near 5 to 7 pushes/s on every backend, stock included. The push-throughput proposal exists to lift the first; the second was still an open issue. These are not limits of the beta as shipped, which are unpublished. Stated product limit today: merge train 20 parallel pipelines by default.

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > Unchanged GitLab: merge request pipelines, merged-results pipelines, merge trains (speculative, 20 in parallel by default, no batching, no affected targets). GitLab says agent-scale CI must validate more changes with less compute and reports 40% more pipelines and 50% more pushes on GitLab.com in a year (self-reported, production); it announces no new gating mechanism for next-gen SCM.
- **Broken main recovery:**
  > None described for next-gen SCM. GitLab today: merge train prevents, manual revert of a merge request. The storage design would allow reading or restoring an older generation (point-in-time), which is a storage rollback and not an automatic revert.
- **Review model:**
  > Human merge request with approvals and Code Owners, plus optional agent review (Duo Code Review); role-scoped human-in-the-loop controls apply to agents as well. Keeping this path is the stated selling point.

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > Vendor: purpose-built read and write APIs for file, blame, history and commits without a checkout. No public reference documentation, schema or error format was found, so machine-readable errors cannot be confirmed. Related proposals in the public tracker, all still open and last touched between April and July 2026, so not evidence of shipped features: ephemeral branches under refs/ephemeral/\<owner>/ with a time limit and a promote call (&21723), standalone repositories per agent (&21717, parent &21716), repository-scoped short-lived tokens (&21719). Existing and usable without the beta: Git, glab CLI, REST and GraphQL, GitLab MCP server.
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > Discarding a session or branch, by the vendor's description; ordinary Git revert and reflog otherwise. Design only: immutable generations give point-in-time reads and rollbacks for operators, not a documented undo call for agents.
- **Agent cost:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] GitLab claims up to 2x fewer tokens and up to 1,000x less network traffic per task (peaks, early internal tests, no task mix, repository size or baseline published). No figure for setup time, push latency or retries per agent in the beta. In the lab a push took 2.08 s median in PostReceivePack on S3 at the 2026-10-26 run, against 1.20 s on the file backend.
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > Vendor claims: each agent gets the minimum visibility its task needs; agent actions run through the same project authorisation and are attributed to a workflow, a model and a token; Governance for agents (identity, policy, audit, approval) is a separate private beta. Not documented publicly: how visibility is scoped (path, project or token) and whether per-folder write permission exists. The tracker shows an agent session viewer for merge requests and commits (epic &23968, closed 2026-10-23, Enterprise Edition, behind a feature flag) that shows owner, model, cost and transcript with tiered access.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > The server orders and merges reference updates and, with merge trains, tests the combined result. The agent or its orchestrator still creates branches, opens merge requests, rebases on conflicts and re-adds them to the train.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Vendor self-report for the product; unusually good public engineering evidence for the backend (design documents, dated epics, lab numbers, failure tables). Main weakness: the headline figures have no method, and the only figures with a method are single-digit pushes per second at 16 agents on a proof of concept, below stock GitLab in one run. No independent benchmark.
- **Independent reports:**
  > No beta user report and no Hacker News thread was found (Algolia and web search, 2026-11-24). Commentary only: an analyst note on GitLab 19.5 from Efficiently Connected (undated, 2026) calls it the most architecturally significant item of the release and advises treating the peak figures as directional, not contractual; Invide Labs, 2026-11-05, lists it as private beta with a proposed architecture and gives no figures; braindetox.kr (after 2026-10-13) notes that the repository sizes and task types behind the 50x figure are not disclosed; Publickey via GameBusiness.jp, 2026-06-18, reports the launch and extra vendor peaks. All are readings of GitLab's posts, not tests.

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > Organisations already on GitLab, above all those that need self-hosted, sovereign or air-gapped Git with many read-heavy agents, and that are willing to test a private beta.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > Cannot be obtained on request; no date. The public schedule puts the S3 backend in 2027, so what beta users run is unclear. Write throughput on one hot repository is not shown to scale yet; hooks to Rails are a separate ceiling. Higher latency per request is an accepted trade-off in the design, and cold caches are an open risk. Merge trains restart every pipeline behind a failure and do not batch, so landing stays the bottleneck. Object-storage mode appears limited to paid editions. Headline numbers are easily misread as concurrent-load results.
- **Minimum useful scale:**
  > Judgement, nothing published: worth it when repeated clones dominate (repositories of several GB, dozens of short agent runs an hour). Below that, partial or shallow clones with worktrees cost less, and at 5-30 agents stock GitLab's single-repository ceiling of about 5 to 7 pushes/s (lab, hooks on) is not reached in normal work.
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Skip unless the repository is already on GitLab; if it is, request the beta only when clone cost or storage sovereignty hurts, and otherwise wait. The size of the problem does not need it: 30 agents pushing once a minute is 0.5 pushes/s (arithmetic), under the 3.4 to 7.3 measured in the lab on every backend. Conflicts and landing would be handled by ordinary merge requests and merge trains either way.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Wait, then pilot with a load test of your own. 300 agents pushing once a minute is 5 pushes/s (arithmetic), at or above what the published lab runs reached on one repository, so the push-throughput work has to land first. It also has to be paired with a landing layer that batches, which merge trains do not. Choose it over GitHub's equivalent mainly for self-hosting and air-gapped storage.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > Let agents read by API (batch file read, diff stat, last commit per path) instead of cloning. Give each agent a private, expiring reference namespace and promote only the winner. Issue short-lived tokens scoped to one repository. On object storage: immutable content-addressed artifacts plus a create-only write of the next generation; batch many pushes into one commit of the log; check old values per reference so that writers to different branches never conflict; keep the critical section to one conditional write. Measure with hooks on: a benchmark with hooks stubbed hid 0.4 to 0.65 s per push. Ask three things of any vendor number, as GitLab itself suggests: mixed concurrent load on one repository, a load generator that is not the limit, and the full latency distribution.

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > GitLab Blog, Git was built for humans - agents need an upgrade, 2026-08-26, https://about.gitlab.com/blog/gitlab-next-gen-scm/ ; GitLab Blog, Scaling Git for the agentic software factory, 2026-10-13, https://about.gitlab.com/blog/scaling-git-for-agentic-software-engineering/ ; GitLab Blog, Transcend announcements, 2026-06-10, https://about.gitlab.com/blog/gitlab-transcend-announcements/ ; press release, 2026-06-10, https://about.gitlab.com/press/releases/2026-06-10-gitlab-announces-new-capabilities-to-give-enterprises-speed-control-at-agentic-scale/ ; GitLab Handbook, Scaling Git (created 2026-06-16, ongoing), https://handbook.gitlab.com/handbook/engineering/architecture/design-documents/scaling-git/ ; Scaling Git: Push Throughput (2026-11-12, proposed), https://gitlab.com/gitlab-com/content-sites/handbook/-/blob/main/content/handbook/engineering/architecture/design-documents/scaling-git/push-throughput.md ; Gitaly issue 7518, 2026-10-29, https://gitlab.com/gitlab-org/gitaly/-/issues/7518 ; Gitaly issue 7521, 2026-10-29 to 31, https://gitlab.com/gitlab-org/gitaly/-/issues/7521 ; Gitaly issue 7581, 2026-11-22, https://gitlab.com/gitlab-org/gitaly/-/issues/7581 ; - **Uncertainty notes:**
  > The public schedule and the private beta do not fit together: the tracker has the S3 backend running to April 2027, while a private beta of an object-storage design has existed since June 2026; the beta may run the proof-of-concept branch, a hosted subset or only the APIs, and the epic on beta customers is not public. Whether the Scaling Git documents describe exactly the beta is inferred. The lab figures come from the proof of concept in late October 2026, before the push-throughput proposal of 2026-11-12; later measurements are linked from a Gitaly merge request as a shared claude.ai page that was not opened. The 50x, 2x and 1,000x figures have no published baseline; the 42x, 17x and 22x figures are known only from Japanese press. Not published: price, tier, beta terms, API reference, latency, landing rate, scoping mechanism. Tracker text can change after 2026-11-24.
