### Merge queues and stacked PRs

*Layer: E landing*

#### Basics

- **Name:** Merge queues and stacked PRs
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > GitHub merge queue: feature of github.com and GitHub Enterprise Server. GitHub stacked pull requests: feature plus CLI extension github/gh-stack (MIT). Mergify: mergify.com, CLI Mergifyio/mergify-cli; the repository Mergifyio/mergify is only an old community issue tracker. Graphite: graphite.com (formerly graphite.dev), CLI gt. Trunk: trunk.io, product Merge Queue (docs also call it Merge). Aviator: aviator.co, product MergeQueue, CLI aviator-co/av; not Aviato, which has an unrelated MCP server. Added, outside the brief: uber/submitqueue (Apache-2.0), a rewrite in the open of Uber's SubmitQueue. GitLab merge trains are covered under GitLab next-gen SCM.
- **Maker:**
  > GitHub (Microsoft): merge queue and stacked PRs; Mergify (co-founder and CEO Julien Danjou); Graphite (Cursor announced a definitive agreement to acquire it on 2025-12-19; closing not confirmed here); Trunk; Aviator; Uber (SubmitQueue). Paper: Christoph Treude and Sebastian Baltes.
- **What it is:**
  > A merge queue is a server-side landing step: a change marked ready is combined with the current main and everything queued ahead of it, CI runs on that combined result, and only a passing result reaches main. GitHub merge queue: temporary branch per entry, several builds at once, no batching of builds, no lanes. Mergify: parallel (speculative) checks on draft pull requests, batches of 1 to 128 or a dynamic range, scopes for independent areas of a monorepo, two-step CI. Graphite: queue that treats a whole stack as one unit, with parallel CI, fast-forward and batching. Trunk: single or parallel mode, where parallel mode builds independent lanes from the impacted targets uploaded for each pull request, plus batching with bisection. Aviator: parallel mode on draft pull requests, batching with bisection, dynamic queues from affected targets. SubmitQueue: speculative builds of predicted future heads with conflict analysis. Stacked PRs are a client-side shape, not a queue: an ordered chain of small pull requests, each based on the one below, that can land together.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > All queues: semantic conflicts that CI can see (each change green, jointly red), detected before main breaks; landing contention, by replacing rebase races with one ordered server-side step. Throughput: partly, through parallel builds, batching and lanes; the limit moves to CI capacity. Textual conflicts: not reduced, only detected at landing (the change is ejected). Stacked PRs: shorter review waits and dependent work by one agent; they add rebase work and do not reduce conflicts between agents. Not addressed by any: task collision, workspace cost, conflicts before writing.
- **Sync philosophy:** Isolate then merge, with a serialised or lane-parallel gate at the end.

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > GitHub merge queue: GA (announced years before this study; date not re-checked), public repositories owned by an organisation and private organisation repositories on Enterprise Cloud; any organisation repository on Enterprise Server. GitHub stacked PRs: public preview 2026-07-30 (changelog page dated July 30, slug 07-31), GA 2026-10-06 on all github.com plans, Enterprise Server later. Mergify, Graphite, Trunk, Aviator: GA SaaS. Graphite batching: private beta in its docs. Trunk self-host: private preview. SubmitQueue: source available, under active development.
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > GitHub: proprietary; SaaS and Enterprise Server. Mergify: proprietary SaaS, Mergify Enterprise Server for on-premises as an Enterprise option; CLI source on GitHub, licence not recognised by the GitHub API. Graphite: proprietary SaaS; Enterprise Server support on Enterprise. Trunk: proprietary SaaS; on-premises or own cloud on Enterprise, in private preview. Aviator: proprietary SaaS; self-hosted (VPC or on-premises) on Enterprise; av CLI MIT. gh-stack: MIT. SubmitQueue: Apache-2.0, self-host only.
- **Price:**
  > Read 2026-11-24, per user and month. GitHub merge queue: included where available, so private repositories need Enterprise Cloud (its price was not checked); CI minutes extra. GitHub stacked PRs: included on all plans. Mergify: free up to 5 users and for open source, Max 21 USD per active contributor (anyone who opened a pull request or pushed to one in 30 days), Enterprise custom. Graphite: merge queue from Team, 40 USD billed annually; Starter 20 USD without queue; Enterprise custom. Trunk: free up to 5 committers including the queue; Team is listed at 0 USD per committer on the page while its FAQ calls the plan paid after the trial, so treat the Team price as unpublished; Enterprise custom; only non-bot committers count. Aviator: Team 20 USD with MergeQueue, Scale 50 USD adds affected targets, Enterprise custom. Uber SubmitQueue: free software, own infrastructure. The larger cost is CI: one team reports about 350-450 runner-minutes per merged change (StudioOS, 2026-11-11, self-reported).
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > GitHub merge queue: production for years, used by GitHub itself (secondary: 500+ engineers, about 2,500 pull requests a month). gh-stack: v0.3.0 (2026-10-26), 1,793 stars, last push 2026-11-16, created 2026-02-06. Mergify: report covers 477 organisations and more than 200,000 merges in 90 days (self-reported); CLI release 2026.11.19. Trunk: stacked PR support 2026-07-31, latest queue change 2026-10-06. Aviator: av v0.1.48 (2026-10-09), 511 stars, names Notion, DoorDash and Amplitude as customers (self-reported). Graphite: active product page with Cursor Cloud Agents banner. SubmitQueue: 259 stars, created 2026-01-12, pushed 2026-11-24, only a pre-release asset tag found.
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > GitHub: on 2026-04-23 (16:05 to 20:43 UTC) the merge queue produced wrong squash commits for groups of more than one pull request; 2,092 pull requests in 658 repositories, later merges reverted earlier ones; no commits lost, but default branches were wrong and GitHub could not repair all of them automatically; found by customers after 3 h 33 min. Graphite: being or already acquired by Cursor (agreement 2025-12-19, closing not confirmed), which also runs its own forge (Origin); the 2025 statement was that Graphite stays independent; no later statement found. Trunk, Mergify, Aviator: independent vendors, no acquisition or shutdown found. gh-stack open issue 489 describes a case where init and submit force-push over an existing remote branch and close its pull request.
- **OS support:**
  > n/a for the queues: server-side. Clients: gh-stack needs GitHub CLI 2.0+ and Git 2.36+ (README; operating systems not listed there); Mergify CLI installs with uv or pipx. Windows support of gt, av and the Mergify CLI was not checked.

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > Full: ordinary Git and GitHub pull requests. All commercial queues in this item sit on GitHub. Stacked PRs rewrite branch history on rebase and squash, so agents must work with rewritten commits. Graphite's queue can close pull requests instead of marking them merged, which can break tools that read merge status.
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > GitHub merge queue: CI must react to the merge_group event (Actions) or to gh-readonly-queue branches (other CI). Mergify, Trunk, Aviator, Graphite: GitHub Apps that create draft pull requests or branches and read status checks, so existing CI stays; Trunk and Aviator are CI-agnostic. Trunk has ready-made target upload for Bazel and Nx, an API for others; Aviator for Bazel, Nx and directory rules. Stacked PRs: every pull request is checked against the rules of the stack base, and workflows run for each layer, so a stack multiplies CI unless reduced. GitHub stacks and GitHub's queue work together since GA; Trunk supports GitHub stacks since 2026-07-31; Mergify and Graphite have their own stacks.
- **Migration and exit:**
  > Low cost and reversible: a setting plus a CI trigger (GitHub), or an app plus a configuration file (others). Partial adoption per branch or per repository. Exit: turn off the required queue; nothing in Git changes. What is lost: queue statistics, target uploads, priority rules. Stacked PRs: existing pull requests become ordinary ones after unstack; cross-fork stacks are not supported. Parallel lanes cost more: somebody must compute impacted targets correctly.

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > GitHub merge queue: agent or human enqueues (gh pr merge, GraphQL enqueuePullRequest, async merge API); GitHub builds a temporary branch of main plus the entries ahead plus this one, dispatches up to 100 merge_group builds at once, and merges groups of 1 to 100 after their checks pass; a failure or conflict removes the entry and rebuilds the branches behind it. One queue per protected branch. Mergify: creates draft pull requests for batches, runs several in parallel, merges the originals in order (or fast-forward or merge-batch to land the tested commit); failed batch is split, the culprit leaves. Trunk: predictive branches per lane; batch of 4 by default once batching is on; failed batch is bisected with its own concurrency; optimistic merging and pending failure depth keep good work when an earlier batch fails. Aviator: bot builds draft pull requests that combine queued changes, merges the originals, splits a failed batch. Graphite: enqueues a stack, rebases and lands it atomically, speculative CI for several stacks at once, isolates a failing stack by testing subsets. SubmitQueue: validates predicted future heads in parallel, lands by cherry-pick and push. Stacked PRs on GitHub: merging the top ready pull request lands everything below it as one atomic operation; in the queue the stack is one merge group, which may exceed the group size by up to 50%; if one is ejected, all above it leave too. In every case the server integrates; the agent only prepares the branch and repairs it after ejection.
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > At landing. Textual conflicts: when the combined branch is built (line level, Git merge). Semantic conflicts: when CI fails on the combined result (whatever tests see). Lanes add a coarse prediction: two changes are treated as independent if their targets (Bazel target, Nx project, folder or scope) do not overlap, which is only as correct as the target list. Stacked PRs: at rebase of the chain, line level.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > No queue resolves content. The entry is ejected and the author (agent) has to rebase or fix and enqueue again. GitHub shows the removal reason in the timeline. Mergify exposes dequeue reason and failing check to the CLI and agent skill. Trunk's API returns failure status and what an item waits on. gh stack rebase stops with exit code 3, prints conflicted files with line numbers, and offers --continue or --abort; init enables git rerere so a resolution is reused; gh stack sync restores all branches if a conflict appears. Structured conflict payloads (both sides as data) were not found in any of the products.
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > The queue is the answer to a stale base: the entry is retested on top of the newest main plus the entries ahead, so agents need not rebase before enqueuing (turn off the require-up-to-date rule). The retry cost moves to CI: a failure in front restarts everything behind it. Numbers: 2.6 queue runs per pull request and 53% failed queue runs (38 of 72) in one agent-heavy repository (StudioOS, 24 h to 2026-11-11, self-reported, production). Model only: a batch of 10 passes 59.9% of the time at a 5% failure rate per change and 10.7% at 20% (menuagentic.com, 2026-09-23, arithmetic). Stacks: when the trunk or a lower layer moves the whole chain is rebased; GitHub does it on the server after the bottom merges, and issue reports complain that sync always rebases and loses review history (gh-stack 354).
- **Pre write coordination:**
  > None. Impacted or affected targets and scopes are declared at queue time, not before editing. Pull request dependencies (Mergify) and stack order express intent between one author's changes only.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > No evidence that queues or stacks lower the rate of textual conflicts, and none of the vendors reports a conflict rate. What is measured is broken main or failed batches. Mergify, 2026-07-27, self-reported, production, observational, 153,000 merges from about 160 teams in 90 days: share of merges in a failed batch 5.1% in private and 1.1% in open-source repositories, from 0.77% at 2-5 developers to 12.5% above 40; AI-assisted 1.9% against 4.4% for others (association, AI detected mostly from Claude Code commit signatures, and only a few hundred of the merges were authored by autonomous agents, so it says little about agent swarms). In that report broke main means that the change was in a failed batch, not that it caused it. StudioOS (one team, 2026-11-11, self-reported): text conflicts in 1 of 35 open pull requests, and of 16 red-main incidents 5 were jointly red changes, 6 wall-clock tests, 3 count baselines, 2 migration stamps; so in that repository the cost was joint failures and flaky tests rather than textual conflicts.

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > n/a: queues do not change the checkout; agents keep clones or worktrees. gh-stack supports linked worktrees and keeps its metadata in the common Git directory, shared by all worktrees of a clone.
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > No source shows hundreds of agents landing through one of these queues. Measured: Mergify customers, merges per active day, private median 3.1, P90 13, P99 32 (self-reported, production, 2026); so almost all measured use is far below agent scale, and 94% of private merges went one change at a time. StudioOS, GitHub merge queue at 5 parallel builds: 18 to 146 merged pull requests a day in November 2026, but about 92% of the last 100 pushes bypassed the queue, so the queue itself landed far fewer. Uber: SubmitQueue on Go, iOS and Android monorepos (peer-reviewed, production; the abstract read here gives no volume). Configured ceilings: GitHub 100 parallel builds and groups of up to 100, but documentation recommends 1 merged pull request a minute per repository, about 1,440 a day (arithmetic); Mergify batch up to 128, 5 parallel checks by default; Trunk presets 5, 25, 50 or custom; Aviator and Graphite user-set. Model: throughput = parallel builds x batch size / CI time, for example 5 / 44.5 min = about 160 a day and 5 / 12 min = about 600 (StudioOS estimate).
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > Mergify report: production telemetry from the vendor, 90 days, observational. Uber: production, ICSE 2025 paper. StudioOS: one team's own measurements, an issue written with Claude Code. Trunk (60-80% less test time, 3-5x more merges an hour, 50-70% fewer CI minutes) and Graphite (1.5x faster merges, up to 2.5x for stack-heavy teams): vendor claims without method. Batch and serial-queue arithmetic from blogs: model.
- **Coordination limit:**
  > The limit is CI time, CI capacity and failure rate, not agent count. Serial queue with 30 minutes of CI: 48 landings a day (arithmetic, TianPan, 2026-07-02). Hosted Actions allow 20 concurrent jobs on Free, 40 Pro, 60 Team, 500 Enterprise (docs, 2026-11-24). Batching stops paying when the failure rate per change is high (model: at 20% and a batch of 10, about 0.93 CI runs per change without bisection). Mergify's measured failed-batch share rises about 16x from the smallest to the largest teams. Without a queue every landing triggers mergeability checks for all open pull requests; with a queue and the up-to-date rule off they cover queued ones only. GitHub documents 1,000 open pull requests per target branch.

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > Pre-merge for all. GitHub: up to 100 parallel speculative builds; merge limits group landings but do not combine builds; option to let a failing entry pass if the last one in the group passes. Mergify: batching 1-128 or dynamic min-max by backlog, parallel checks, scopes with their own capacity, two-step CI (cheap on push, full in queue). Graphite: parallel CI for stacks, check every layer or only the top, batching (private beta per docs read 2026-11-24). Trunk: target batch 4, wait 5 minutes by default, off until enabled; separate bisection concurrency (advice 2x or more); lanes from impacted targets. Aviator: batch_size 1 by default, lanes from affected targets (Scale plan), optimistic validation for flaky tests. SubmitQueue: speculation, batching and conflict analysis (README).
- **Broken main recovery:**
  > Prevention instead of recovery: bisection of failed batches before landing (Mergify, Trunk, Aviator, Graphite); GitHub has no bisection. None of the pages read describes automatic revert of a landed change or a known-good pointer. Admin bypass defeats the gate: in StudioOS about 92% of recent pushes skipped the queue and main was red on 26% of pushes over 7 days. Keep a post-merge check as well, since the April 2026 incident broke main after green queue runs.
- **Review model:**
  > Queues start after approval, so the human pull request stays. Stacked PRs make the reviewed units smaller. What replaces pull request review, from Treude and Baltes (arXiv 2610.34933, preprint, 2026-10-26, 50 grey-literature documents from 31 sources): of 44 documents with a position, 12 restrict who may submit, 11 replace the pull request by another unit, 9 keep it with tooling, 9 extend it with a new artifact, 2 leave GitHub but keep pull requests, 1 drops pull requests and human review. Review prioritised by risk is the most frequent review mechanism (14 documents). Only Zed reported using a replacement in practice (Delta: 33 people, 570 changes). No document tested whether a mechanism improves review. The paper does not discuss merge queues or stacked PRs. StudioOS plans risk tiers: docs, tests and ordinary code land without a person, sensitive paths always need one (planned, not measured).

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > GitHub queue: gh pr merge (enqueues, or sets auto-merge until checks pass), GraphQL enqueuePullRequest, and the async merge API (GA 2026-10-01: PUT to submit, GET for status; the only merge API that supports stacks; enqueues too); the CLI cannot dequeue. GitHub stacks: gh stack with --json, non-interactive mode, exit codes 0-10, a REST API for stacks (stated by a GitHub employee on Hacker News, not checked in the reference), and an agent skill (gh skill install github/gh-stack). Mergify: mergify CLI with JSON queue status plus eight agent skills for Claude Code, Codex, Cursor, Cline and Copilot (queue, dequeue, explain ejection, simulate config, stacks); application key for unattended agents; no official MCP server found. Graphite: gt CLI and gt mcp (beta, 1.6.7+) for creating stacks; no queue control documented for MCP. Trunk: public queue-management API (2026-02-06), submit and status endpoints, trunk merge CLI; its MCP server covers flaky tests and CI failures, and no merge-queue tools were found, so the MCP claim in the brief is unconfirmed for the queue. Aviator: REST API, webhooks, /aviator comments including targets, av CLI and agent plugins (aviator-co/agent-plugins, MIT); no MCP found. SubmitQueue: gateway service with an API and client.
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > Dequeue, fix, enqueue again; landed changes are undone with a revert pull request. Stacks: gh stack rebase --abort and modify --abort restore the previous state; rerere keeps resolutions. Local reflog and GitHub's branch restore are the fallback; a forced push by gh stack submit can overwrite a remote branch in the reported case (issue 489, open).
- **Agent cost:**
  > Few tokens on the happy path: one enqueue call and one status poll or webhook. Cost appears on ejection: read the failing check, rebase, fix, enqueue, and wait again. Published CI cost: 113-124 runner-minutes per run and 350-450 per merged change in StudioOS (self-reported); private Mergify users wait 8 min median, 63 min P90, 19 h 53 min P99 in queue (self-reported, production). Token cost per landing is not published by anyone.
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > Branch protection and rulesets decide who may bypass; the practical rule is that agent tokens get no admin or bypass permission, so the queue is the only way to main. GitHub Apps or fine-grained tokens per agent give identity. Mergify application keys can be limited by permission and expiry. No per-folder write permission beyond Code Owners review. Attribution to model or prompt is not part of any queue; Mergify only infers AI use from commit signatures. gh-stack 371 (open) reports a squash merge that mixes the author's name with the merger's e-mail.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > The queue server orders, combines, tests and lands. The agent prepares a branch, enqueues, and repairs after ejection; with stacks it also keeps the chain rebased unless GitHub does it. An orchestrator is useful to cancel dead runs and route failures to the owning session (StudioOS plan).

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Mixed. Best: Uber's ICSE 2025 paper (production, peer-reviewed: CI resource use -53%, CPU -44%, P95 wait -37%). Mergify's report is large but vendor-run, observational and mostly human work. Documentation is reliable for limits. Comparative pages are largely written by vendors about each other. Main weakness: nothing measured with hundreds of agents on one repository.
- **Independent reports:**
  > Hacker News 2026-07-30 (783 points, 298 comments) on GitHub stacks: complaints that merging whole stacks failed and that squash needed new approvals for each layer during preview, a stack stuck in merging, and one custom-queue owner asking to land several layers at once; GitHub answered with the async API, Trunk with its integration. gh-stack issues open on 2026-11-24: 323 (cannot merge without a clear reason, 44 comments), 174, 349 (enabling a queue hides the stack view), 354, 444 (stack split over two groups closes a pull request), 489. Hacker News 2026-04-23 (121 points) on the queue incident. StudioOS epic 5600, 2026-11-11, one public repository with 0 stars: wants to stay on the native queue, sees runners and bypasses as the limit. alatas.dev, 2026-10-18: personal repository is not eligible for GitHub's queue, so the author built a one-at-a-time queue in Actions for several agents. TianPan, 2026-07-02: argues that landing became the limit (own arithmetic plus cited figures). menuagentic.com, 2026-09-23: argues that batching gets worse as agents raise the failure rate (arithmetic). Mitchell Hashimoto, quoted by Mergify on 2026-07-13: queues work for humans and not at 100x. Mostly opinion and anecdote.

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > Any team on GitHub with a test suite worth gating on and more than a few landings a day. GitHub queue: simplest, if the plan allows it. Mergify: batching, scopes and the best documented agent skills at a known price. Trunk: Bazel or Nx monorepo that can compute targets. Aviator: affected targets with self-host. Graphite: teams that already stack. SubmitQueue: an infrastructure team that will operate it. Stacks: one agent delivering a large change in reviewable layers.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > Flaky tests and high failure rates turn speculation and batching into wasted CI. Long CI makes the queue the limit. Bypassing the queue restores broken main. Wrong target lists let jointly broken changes land in parallel lanes. Queue software can itself corrupt main (GitHub, 2026-04-23). Stacks: rebase churn, stuck merges and force-push reports in gh-stack; lower-layer failure ejects everything above. Seat pricing can count agent accounts. Green CI is not correctness: no queue sees semantic conflicts that tests miss.
- **Minimum useful scale:**
  > Judgement with one data point: in Mergify's telemetry the failed-batch share passes 2% at 16-40 developers and reaches 12.5% above 40, while teams of 2-15 stay under 1%. For agents, start at roughly 10 landings a day, or as soon as two agents touch code covered by the same tests. Batching and lanes pay off when CI time x landings an hour exceeds the parallel builds you can afford.
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Use. It is the cheapest working gate and needs no change of VCS. Take GitHub's queue if the repository is eligible (public organisation repository, or private on Enterprise Cloud), otherwise Mergify or Trunk. Give agents no bypass, cut full CI below about 15 minutes, run the full suite only in the queue, and turn on batching once the failure rate per change is low. Use stacks for one agent's dependent work only, and pilot gh stack on non-critical branches because of open issues.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Use as the landing gate, but not alone, and expect to change the configuration. Arithmetic: 300 landings a day with 12-minute CI needs about 2.5 parallel builds without failures; 3,000 a day needs 25, or batches, before retries, and it exceeds GitHub's recommended 1 merge a minute. So: lanes by affected targets, batches with bisection, affected-test selection, an agent that repairs ejections, and fewer collisions by partitioning work. Pilot Trunk, Mergify scopes, Aviator or SubmitQueue against the native queue with your own failure rate. If main must take several thousand landings a day, test after landing with automatic revert (as server-side systems do) becomes the alternative; nothing here demonstrates queues at that rate.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > One enforced way onto main, without bypass for agents. Test the exact combined tree that lands. Cheap checks on push, full suite only in the queue. Fail fast and cancel orphaned runs. Bisect failed batches with separate CI capacity and reuse earlier results. Size batches by measured failure rate, not by backlog. Derive lanes from the build graph. Return the ejection reason to the owning agent as data and cap its repair attempts. Split append-only registries and ledgers into one file per entry so that changes do not collide. Remove wall-clock tests. Track queue failures per entry and runner-minutes per landed change. Verify the content of landed commits, not only their CI status.

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > GitHub Docs, Managing a merge queue, read 2026-11-24, https://docs.github.com/en/repositories/configuring-branches-and-merges-in-your-repository/configuring-pull-request-merges/managing-a-merge-queue ; - **Uncertainty notes:**
  > Three statements of the brief needed correction: stacked PRs left preview (GA 2026-10-06); Trunk's MCP server has no documented merge-queue tools; the Treude and Baltes paper does not cover queues. Sources disagree on Graphite batching (docs: private beta; 2024 post: public beta), on the Trunk Team price (page shows 0 USD and calls the plan paid; other sites name 18 USD or no public price) and on whether the April 2026 incident also hit rebase merges (status updates mention it, the final report says squash only). Not found in the pages read: conflict handling of Mergify, Trunk, Aviator and Graphite queues when the combined branch cannot be built; Graphite's numeric limits; whether vendors bill agent accounts as seats (Trunk excludes bots). Mergify's AI detection is a lower bound, and its prose and tables differ slightly (median 7 against 8 and 5 minutes). Aviator's docs name a Pro plan for parallel mode while the pricing page has Team and Scale. Not read: full text of the Uber paper, the Graphite 2026 changelog, the Base Power article (body not delivered). Plan price of GitHub Enterprise Cloud was not checked.

### Cursor Origin

*Layer: C Git hosting*

#### Basics

- **Name:** Cursor Origin
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > Docs https://cursor.com/docs/origin, product page https://cursor.com/origin, Git host origin.cursor.com, API https://api.cursor.com/v1/origin. Keep apart: (1) Origin, the forge; (2) Continuity, Cursor's Git storage layer (post of 2026-08-18), described as serving the operations that Origin performs; (3) the unnamed version control system that Cursor wrote from scratch for its research swarm (post of 2026-07-20), which nothing read here links to Origin; (4) Graphite, the review and stacking product that Cursor bought and still sells separately; (5) origin, Git's default remote name. Some Hacker News users complained about the name. One status aggregator lists it under Grok Origin.
- **Maker:**
  > Cursor (Anysphere, Inc.). SpaceX announced its purchase of Cursor in June 2026 and closed it on 2026-08-14, three days before Origin shipped (The Next Web, 2026-08-18). One of Origin's developers is Tomas Reimers, a Graphite co-founder; the Graphite acquisition was announced in December 2025.
- **What it is:**
  > A Git forge inside Cursor: repositories, browsing and search, pull requests, native stacked pull requests, rulesets, CODEOWNERS, checks reported by outside CI, apps, a REST API with webhooks, a CLI (origin) and an MCP server. A repository is either Origin-hosted (Origin is the source of truth) or mirrored from GitHub (GitHub stays the source of truth, pushes to the Origin remote go to GitHub, and Origin holds a copy for browsing, clones and agents). Cursor cloud agents and Automations can clone, branch, commit, push and open pull requests on it. The working model is the GitHub one: branch, pull request, checks, merge. Announced 2026-06-16, early beta from 2026-08-17.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > Addresses: throughput (push and clone capacity of the storage layer), landing contention in part (expected-head guards, merge when ready, stacks that land as one commit), agent operability of the pull request workflow (typed merge blockers, JSON CLI). Textual conflicts: detected and reported, not prevented. Does not address: semantic conflicts (left to CI and review), task collision (no claims, locks or messages between agents), workspace cost on the agent side (ordinary clones).
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > Isolate then merge: standard Git branches and pull requests, with expected-head (compare-and-swap) guards on API writes and optional stacking. No automatic server-side replay of stale work.

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > Usable by outsiders as an early beta since 2026-08-17 on paid plans; docs and API pages still said early beta on 2026-11-24. Not on the Free and Plus plans. On by default for paid customers; admins can disable it; teams on legacy privacy mode cannot enable it.
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > Proprietary, SaaS only. No self-hosted or single-tenant version of the forge was found. The CLI is a vendor binary.
- **Price:**
  > No separate price found. Code hosting via Origin is listed from Premium upward: Premium \$30, Super \$100, Ultra \$200 a month, Teams from \$30 per user a month, Enterprise on request; older paid plans (Start, Pro, Pro Student, Pro+, Ultra) keep access; not in Free (\$0) or Plus (\$8) (pricing page and docs read 2026-11-24). Storage quotas and overage prices were not found. CI is bought from partners; cloud agents and Automations are billed as Cursor usage. CloneKit (fast CI clones) needs an Enterprise plan.
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > Beta, about three months public. Weekly release notes from the week of 2026-08-31 to the week of 2026-11-09; API changelog to 2026-11-23, with several entries marked Breaking in October and November. App marketplace opened 2026-10-13 with more than 20 apps. CI partners: Buildkite (launch), CircleCI (2026-09-01), Depot. Launch thread on Hacker News 597 points and 454 comments. Number of users or repositories is not published; no named large customer was found. Cursor's own monorepo is used in the storage tests.
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > Cursor says a push is acknowledged only after it is persisted in an object-storage write-ahead log and that copies on local disks are caches (self-reported, 2026-08-18). No data-loss report was found. Release notes list fixed correctness bugs, for example squash merges that landed while the pull request stayed open (week of 2026-08-31) and updates of stacked pull requests that could bring back removed code (week of 2026-10-19). Incidents naming Origin on the Cursor status page in the window seen (from 2026-09-23): 2026-10-13 about 2 h 45 min (minor), 2026-10-27 45 min (no impact level), 2026-10-28 about 2 h (major), 2026-10-29 17 min (minor). On launch day a GitHub outage also affected Origin. Data terms: Origin follows the Privacy Mode of the repository owner (team or individual); with Privacy Mode on Cursor says code is not used for training, with it off Cursor may store and train on codebase data; the data processing agreement covers Teams and Enterprise only. No Origin-specific page on retention, residency, backup, deletion or service level was found. Owner changed in August 2026 (SpaceX). Moving a personal namespace into a team is not self-serve (forum, 2026-11-03).
- **OS support:**
  > Server: any system with Git over HTTPS or SSH. CLI: macOS, Linux, WSL, and native Windows through a PowerShell installer. CloneKit on Linux CI runners needs glibc on x64 or arm64; Alpine and other musl images are not supported; macOS runners are.

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > Standard Git client over HTTPS and SSH, with Git LFS (push errors point to it for files over 100 MB; on mirrors LFS depends on GitHub). No native jj protocol (users asked for it; a Cursor developer expressed interest), but commits that Origin replays keep the jj change-id header, and the local stack restack refuses such commits unless told to drop the header.
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > Two modes. Mirrored repository: GitHub pull requests, Issues and Actions stay on GitHub; agents open GitHub pull requests there; Origin shows and reviews them; the public API treats the mirror as read-only for apps and rejects merges and file commits. Origin-hosted repository: pull requests live on Origin only, GitHub Actions does not run, CI comes from Buildkite, CircleCI or Depot (which converts GitHub Actions workflows and reports actions that depend on missing GitHub APIs) or from any system that posts check runs through an Origin app. The CLI also knows repositories that Origin owns and mirrors out to GitHub, and release notes mention fixes for them, but no setup page for that direction was found.
- **Migration and exit:**
  > In: Sync from GitHub copies history, branches and tags and keeps them updated (needs GitHub admin rights). CLI helpers import labels and rulesets and verify a migration; a migration API is in preview. Detach from GitHub then makes Origin the source of truth. Issues, Actions workflows and secrets are not copied. Partial adoption is easy: keep GitHub as the source of truth and use the mirror. Out: Git history leaves with an ordinary clone and push; pull requests, reviews, tours, rulesets and stack relations can be read through the API but no export was found. Namespace names cannot be changed in the beta.

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > The agent or person commits locally (or through the API call Create Commit From Files), pushes a branch and opens a pull request. A person, an app or the CLI then asks for a merge (origin pr merge; squash or merge commit). The server builds the merge after checking expected head, rulesets, required checks, approvals and conflicts, and lands nothing if one fails. Direct push to main is possible where rules allow it. Stacks: merging pull request N lands every open member from the root to N in one commit; in squash-only repositories the children then move to the base branch (from the week of 2026-10-26). The CLI docs say Origin never rebases a branch for you: restacking happens locally, or on the server when someone asks (Restack button, origin stack restack-remote). Merge when ready lands a pull request once requirements are met. Limits for agents that should land their own work, as documented on 2026-11-24: (1) the token of a Cursor cloud agent can open and update pull requests but cannot merge (Cursor staff, 2026-10-29; a merge scope is something the team is looking at); (2) service accounts and apps acting as a user cannot turn on auto-merge; (3) the MCP merge tool is marked destructive and asks for confirmation on each call; (4) the MCP server works only in Cursor and Grok Bot; (5) mirrored repositories cannot be merged through the API. What remains for unattended agents is the CLI or API with a user credential or an app credential, or a direct push to main, which one forum user fell back to. Merge queue: the MCP merge tool and one OpenAPI description say that where a merge queue owns the base branch the pull request or the stack is queued instead of merged, but no page on enabling, batching or configuring a queue was found in the docs, the ruleset types or the release notes.
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > After push, on the server. Origin computes a test merge for a pull request version and reports prepared, merge_conflict or unknown; the merge is held while that check is pending. A preview API call, Get Pull Request Mergeability, returns typed blockers (draft, merge_conflict, required_checks, required_approvals, codeowner_approval, behind_base, needs_restack, restack_pending, conflict_check_pending and others); merge_conflict carries up to 100 conflicted paths with a truncation flag, plus flags for conflicts inherited from lower stack members. Granularity reported: file path. It is a textual merge that honours .gitattributes; no entity or semantic detection. The test merge is not refreshed when only the base moves; a preview call (Prepare Pull Request Merge Ref) rebuilds it. File commits through the API conflict at branch-head level.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > The author or the agent resolves. Local origin stack restack stops with ordinary conflict markers and offers a dry run that previews conflicts; restack-remote moves nothing on a conflict and names the stack member. The API returns a typed blocker or HTTP 409. The web page can apply a conflict resolution as a commit, with an Undo button that restores the previous head (week of 2026-10-05); what produces that resolution is not documented. Merge when ready stays on through a conflict. A Cursor developer said on 2026-08-17 that getting pull requests to a mergeable state automatically would come in the following weeks; no documentation of it was found by 2026-11-24. An AI conflict engine mentioned in press coverage goes back to a June 2026 conference demo and is not in the docs.
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > An agent on its own branch is unaffected when main moves. Stale writes fail cleanly: a non-fast-forward push is rejected; Create Commit From Files requires expectedHeadSha and answers HTTP 400 FailedPrecondition on a mismatch; a merge with an outdated expectedHeadSha answers HTTP 409 Aborted and lands nothing; stack push uses a lease. By default a pull request need not be up to date with main, so a change tested on an old base can land; a ruleset (require_branch_up_to_date) can demand it, and then each landing makes the other open pull requests stale. Stack members need a restack after their parent changes. Retry rate and cost are not published.
- **Pre write coordination:**
  > None. No claims, locks or messages before editing. Nearest features: stack commands record a branch that someone else authored as frozen and never rewrite it, and rulesets and CODEOWNERS act at push or merge time.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > No evidence and no figure. Cursor publishes no conflict rate, merge success rate or rejected-push rate for Origin, and no independent measurement was found. The one related entry is a bug fix: edits next to a line inserted on the base branch had been shown as conflicts although Git merges them (week of 2026-09-28). The widely quoted drop from more than 70,000 to under 1,000 conflicts belongs to Cursor's research swarm and its own version control system (July 2026, self-reported), not to Origin.

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > Client: ordinary Git clones; from the week of 2026-11-09 admins can hide ref patterns from clones to keep them small. CloneKit (Enterprise, per repository, aimed at CI) downloads a prebuilt pack snapshot and fetches only newer objects. The API can read files and commit files without a clone (up to 1,000 files, 8 MiB per file, 32 MiB per commit). No virtualised or copy-on-write checkout. Server, according to the Continuity post: a write-ahead log in S3 is the source of truth, bare repositories on local NVMe are warm caches, the log index moves by atomic compare-and-swap, any node can accept a push, only a primary compacts. The post implies that Origin runs on it but gives no share.
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > Not published for concurrent agents or for landings on one Origin branch. Storage layer only (self-reported, synthetic tests on Cursor's own monorepo, 2026-08-18): up to 120 pushes a second on S3 Standard and more than 300 on S3 Express One Zone, reads scaling linearly up to 100 replicas. These are pushes, not merges to main; push sizes, repository size and duration are not given. Figures such as 22.6 commits a second in one repository come from press reports of a June 2026 stage demo without a Cursor document behind them. Documented API ceilings, not achieved rates: 600 points a minute per user or service account and 3,000 per app installation, with 10 points per merge, so at most 60 or 300 merges a minute (arithmetic here).
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > Vendor synthetic test of the storage layer, N and duration not published. No production figure for landings. InfoQ (2026-09-30) quotes an outside architect noting that the figures are not independently verified.
- **Coordination limit:**
  > Not published for agents. Documented limits: 200 unmerged pull requests per stack, the API point budgets, 100 MiB per file. The Continuity post names Git compaction as the limit above 300 pushes a second.

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > Pre-merge, by outside CI: required status checks, required approvals and code owners in rulesets. A test merge ref (refs/pull/N/merge) exists for CI, and a check run can record the base commit it was evaluated against. Requiring an up-to-date branch gives test-on-latest-main at the price of repeated updates. No documentation of batching, speculation or affected targets was found. Agents can re-run failed checks through MCP (up to 20 per call).
- **Broken main recovery:**
  > Manual. Pull requests and stacks can be reverted; no detection of a broken main, bisect, automatic revert or known-good pointer is documented.
- **Review model:**
  > Risk-tiered mix. Pull request review with required approvals, a request-changes review that blocks merging, and CODEOWNERS that can be set to agentic review (2026-10-12). Agentic Owners policies let an agent approve very-low and low risk changes and hold the rest, or require a person; the policy is read from the base branch. Code Tours explain a diff; Bugbot reviews and can propose fixes. A bot approval must be on the latest version (2026-11-23).

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > CLI with JSON output, a non-interactive mode and an agent guide (origin agent prompt), plus a skill installer for Cursor, Claude Code and Codex (origin agent setup --harness). REST API with an OpenAPI file, typed merge blockers, webhooks and a Go SDK. MCP server with about 40 tools and structured errors (category, retryAfterSeconds), available only in Cursor and Grok Bot on 2026-11-24, with other harnesses announced as coming. So Claude Code and Codex agents use the CLI, plain Git and the API.
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > Git itself (reflog, branches, revert), Undo of an applied conflict resolution, force pushes recorded in the pull request activity, and origin stack get --force to reset local branches to Origin's copy. No operation log was found.
- **Agent cost:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] No figure for tokens, setup time, retries or push latency per agent is published. Known inputs only: one CLI login or API key per agent identity, app tokens of at most 15 minutes, API costs of 10 points per merge or file commit.
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > Identities: users, service accounts, Origin apps with short-lived installation tokens, and apps acting as a user. Built-in and custom access policies (custom ones in preview), branch, tag and push rulesets, secret scanning on push, inbound IP allowlist. No per-folder write permission: the documented ruleset types do not restrict paths; CODEOWNERS works at review. Provenance: the audit log names the person, app, service account, key or agent run behind a change (2026-11-02); commits made by a Cursor agent show the person with a Cursor badge; agent comments link to the run in private repositories. Squash merges drop the addresses of Cursor agents and bots from Co-authored-by trailers (week of 2026-09-21), so the landed commit does not name the agent. Model and prompt are not recorded in Git.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > Server: test merge, rule checks, the merge itself, restack on request, mirror sync. Agent: branch, fetch, rebase or restack, resolve, push, open and update pull requests. A person, an app or a user credential triggers the landing or arms auto-merge.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Vendor documentation, detailed and dated (release notes, API changelog), plus vendor posts for storage speed. Main weakness: no published number on landings, conflicts or agent counts; the beta changes weekly; launch coverage mixes demo claims with shipped features.
- **Independent reports:**
  > Appwrite engineer, hands-on, 2026-08-18 (sells a competing platform): the core works but feels early, teams that depend on GitHub governance should wait, mirror one repository first. The Next Web 2026-08-18: opt-out rollout and unpublished data terms. InfoQ 2026-08-25 and 2026-09-30: summaries, performance figures unverified. Cursor forum 2026-09-16: a team could not merge on a synced repository with required checks and fixed it by detaching from GitHub. Forum 2026-10-29: a cloud agent cannot merge, the user arms auto-merge by hand or pushes straight to main. Forum 2026-11-03: agents blocked for days after the owner of a personal namespace joined a team plan. Hacker News 2026-08-17: thin difference from GitHub, lock-in, ownership, requests for jj. No independent report of many agents landing on one Origin repository was found.

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > Teams already on a paid Cursor plan that want cloud agents, stacked pull requests and review in one place, or a mirror of a GitHub repository for agent clones and as a fallback during GitHub outages.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > Cloud agents cannot land their own work; conflict handling is ordinary textual merge with better reporting; test merges go stale when main moves; an up-to-date rule turns landings into a chain of updates, and without the rule a change tested on an old base can land; merge queue undocumented; MCP closed to other harnesses; mirrors depend on GitHub for writes; GitHub Actions, Issues and secrets do not come along; retention and residency terms unpublished; breaking API changes during the beta; one vendor for editor, agents, review and hosting.
- **Minimum useful scale:**
  > Nothing published; judgement: this is a workflow choice more than a scale threshold. The mirror pays off when agents or CI clone one GitHub repository many times a day. For landing, no advantage over worktrees plus a merge queue is shown at any agent count.
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Wait as the main forge; pilot as a mirror only if the team already pays for Cursor. Agents that should run the landing themselves meet too many limits (cloud-agent tokens cannot merge, auto-merge closed to service accounts, MCP closed to Claude Code and Codex, merge queue undocumented), and nothing in it lowers the number of conflicts. If tried natively, use the CLI and API with expectedHeadSha on every write and bring your own CI.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Wait. The storage tests suggest that branch pushes from several hundred agents would not be the limit, but they are synthetic and self-reported, and landing on one main branch is unproven: no landing rate, no queue documentation, no batching, no conflict data. Check again when three things exist: a merge scope for agents, a documented merge queue, and an MCP server open to other harnesses.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > Give agents typed merge blockers (conflicted paths, missing checks, behind base, needs restack) instead of prose. Require the expected head on every merge and file commit, so a stale writer fails without side effects. Record on each check the base commit it ran against, and refresh the test merge on demand before landing. Land a stack atomically as one commit. Mark branches an agent did not author as frozen. Offer a dry-run restack. Read owner policies from the base branch, so a change cannot loosen its own review. Keep a reserved branch namespace on a second host for use while the main host is down.

Status page, read 2026-11-24, https://status.cursor.com ; Hacker News launch thread with developer replies, 2026-08-17, https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49334209 ; Hacker News announcement thread, 2026-06-16, https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=48558605 . Secondary: The Next Web, 2026-08-18, https://thenextweb.com/news/cursor-origin-opt-out-data-terms-spacex-github-outage ; InfoQ, 2026-08-25, https://www.infoq.com/news/2026/08/cursor-origin-alternative-github ; InfoQ, 2026-09-30, https://www.infoq.com/news/2026/09/cursor-continuity-git-storage ; Appwrite, 2026-08-18, https://appwrite.io/blog/post/cursor-origin-review-an-engineers-perspective
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > Not confirmed: whether customers can enable a merge queue (named only in API and MCP descriptions and as a component by a status aggregator); who or what produces conflict resolutions applied in the web page; how mirroring from Origin out to GitHub is set up (the pull request docs say pull requests on Origin-hosted repositories are not mirrored anywhere, while release notes mention pull requests in repositories that mirror out); the share of Origin that Continuity serves; storage quotas, retention, residency, backup, deletion; number of users; cost per agent. Sources differ on whether agents can merge: MCP lists a merge tool while staff say the cloud-agent token cannot merge; both can hold, because the tool acts for the user with confirmation. Part of the Hacker News thread was searched by keyword, not read in full.

**Unverified fields (1):**

- agent_cost

### Swarm case studies and task partitioning

*Layer: G coordination*

#### Basics

- **Name:** Swarm case studies and task partitioning
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > Cases, not one product. (A) Cursor, Scaling long-running autonomous coding, 2026-01-14, https://cursor.com/blog/scaling-agents, and Towards self-driving codebases, 2026-02-05, https://cursor.com/blog/self-driving-codebases (browser project, public output wilsonzlin/fastrender). (B) Cursor, Agent swarms and the new model economics, 2026-07-20, https://cursor.com/blog/agent-swarm-model-economics (SQLite rewrite, public output cursor/minisqlite); it changes a main conclusion of A. (C) Anthropic, Building a C compiler with a team of parallel Claudes, 2026-02-05, https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-c-compiler (public output anthropics/claudes-c-compiler). (D) Ownership practice: two-slice teams (Every, 2026-02-13; Przeprogramowani video, 2026-10-26) and the Claude Code agent teams documentation. Keep apart: Cursor's swarm version control system (case B, not public) is not Cursor Origin (the forge) or Continuity (the storage layer); nothing read links them. Namesakes: other projects named minisqlite were not checked; two-slice team is Every's phrase for one person with agents, not a research term.
- **Maker:**
  > A and B: Cursor (Anysphere), author Wilson Lin. C: Anthropic, author Nicholas Carlini (Safeguards team). D: Dan Shipper (Every); Przemek Smyrdek (Przeprogramowani, sells the 10xDevs course); Anthropic documentation.
- **What it is:**
  > Vendor reports on running many coding agents on one repository, and an ownership practice that follows from them. A: first flat agents with a shared coordination file and locks, then optimistic concurrency, then planners, sub-planners and workers (plus a judge, later removed); each worker has its own copy of the repository and hands results to the planner that asked for them. B: the same planner and worker tree with a new version control system, shared design docs, a neutral agent that resolves conflicts, splitting of oversized files and stacked review. C: 16 Claude agents, each in a Docker container with a clone of one bare repository, no orchestrator and no messages; a text file in current_tasks/ claims a task and Git refuses the second claimant. D: one or two people with agents own a whole product or module, so two owners rarely edit the same files. Attribution matters: the Every article proposes one person plus agents owning a product and says nothing about modules, repositories or merge conflicts; the reading that small owners avoid conflicts comes from the Przeprogramowani video, which is opinion without measurements; the Claude Code docs give the file-ownership rule.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > A: task collision and landing contention (planner tree instead of locks, integrator removed); textual conflicts tolerated. B: throughput (new version control system), textual conflicts (neutral resolver, splitting hot files), semantic and design conflicts between planners (design docs with compile-checked references), task collision (no two subtrees decide the same question). C: task collision (claim files), then convergent work on one failing test (oracle that splits the work); textual conflicts left to the agents; semantic regressions handled late by CI. D: textual conflicts and task collision by ownership. Not addressed: A does not solve semantic conflicts beyond later repair; C does not address landing contention at higher counts or workspace cost; D gives no mechanism for changes that cross module borders, and none of the four addresses per-agent permissions.
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > A: isolate, then push to one shared branch with optimistic concurrency; accept turbulence. B: central version control system written for the swarm, with coordination and conflict arbitration inside it. C: pre-write task claim plus isolate then merge on Git. D: partition before work starts, so that there is little to merge.

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > Practices and write-ups are public. The harnesses of A, B and C are internal; the version control system of B is not published. Public outputs: wilsonzlin/fastrender, cursor/minisqlite, anthropics/claudes-c-compiler. Claude Code agent teams (D) is an experimental feature, off by default (docs read 2026-11-24).
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > Patterns, nothing to host. Output repositories on GitHub: anthropics/claudes-c-compiler CC0-1.0; wilsonzlin/fastrender and cursor/minisqlite carry no licence file (read 2026-11-24).
- **Price:**
  > Self-reported API costs: C just under \$20,000 (2 billion input and 140 million output tokens, about 2,000 sessions, two weeks). B between \$1,339 (Opus 4.8 planner with Composer 2.5 workers) and \$10,565 (GPT-5.5 for both roles) per SQLite run. A only trillions of tokens, no price. D none.
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > Research experiments, not products. anthropics/claudes-c-compiler: 2,843 stars, last push 2026-02-05. wilsonzlin/fastrender: 1,579 stars, 29,858 commits, last push 2026-01-18. cursor/minisqlite: 294 stars, 3 commits, last push 2026-07-17. Cursor says the swarm is also used internally for vulnerability fixing, test coverage and data generation (2026-07-20). Claude Code agent teams: experimental.
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > All three code outputs show no pushes after the period of their publication (last push 2026-01-18, 2026-02-05 and 2026-07-17) and are snapshots, not maintained projects. Conclusions of A were replaced in part by the same author six months later (B). Cursor has belonged to SpaceX since August 2026.
- **OS support:**
  > A: one large Linux VM per run. C: Docker containers (Linux). B: not stated. D: independent of the operating system.

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > A and C: plain Git. B: Cursor's own system replaced Git inside the swarm; the published result (cursor/minisqlite) is an ordinary Git repository with three commits. D: plain Git.
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > None needed. In A and C agents push straight to a shared branch without pull requests. C added a CI pipeline late, because new features kept breaking existing ones. A describes no pre-merge CI; The Register reported on 2026-01-22 that 88 percent of CI job runs in the public browser repository failed (figure from a consultant, Jason Gorman); Lin had stated on 2026-01-16 that build errors were resolved. B publishes no CI set-up. D assumes ordinary repositories and reviews.
- **Migration and exit:**
  > Nothing to migrate. Adopting the practices costs planning work (module borders, owners, independent tests) and may require splitting a monolith; leaving them costs nothing. The version control system and harness of A and B cannot be adopted by outsiders.

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > A: a worker changes its own copy and pushes to the shared branch; an integrator agent that all work passed through was tried and removed because hundreds of workers had one gate; Cursor proposed a separate green branch where an agent regularly snapshots and fixes before release. B: every change passes through the new version control system; when two workers collide a neutral third agent resolves for all parties, which Cursor compares to a merge queue; commits to a file flagged as oversized are blocked until an outside agent has split it. C: pull from the shared repository, merge other agents' changes, push, remove the claim file; every agent lands its own work on the same branch, with no pull request. D: the module owner lands; Claude Code agent teams share a task list under one lead session; how their work lands on a branch was not found in the page read.
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > A: at push and merge time, Git text level; build and test failures show semantic conflicts afterwards. B: in the version control system, through which every change passes; the statistics are given per file; design conflicts are found when a compile-checked reference to a decision breaks. C: task claim conflict at push of the claim file (file name level), text conflicts at merge, semantic ones by tests. D: before work, at module or file-set level; the agent teams docs warn that two teammates editing the same file overwrite each other.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > A (January 2026): workers resolve their own conflicts; the post says they were already capable of it. B (July 2026): reversed; workers are said to be bad at it and either overwrite the other change or abandon their own, so a neutral third-party agent resolves. Contradicting planners are handled by a reconciler that merges the design docs. C: the agent merges itself; the author writes that conflicts are frequent and Claude handles them. D: avoided by ownership; the rare cross-border change needs agreement between owners, with no mechanism given.
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > A: a worker pulls the newest repository and reconciles at push; under optimistic concurrency a write fails when the state changed since it was read. An earlier rule that every commit had to be fully correct stalled the whole system when one small error appeared, so a small steady error rate is accepted. B: not described beyond arbitration inside the version control system. C: pull, merge, push, retried until it works; retry counts not published. D: rarely occurs inside one owner's module.
- **Pre write coordination:**
  > A: a shared coordination file with locks failed: locks were held too long or never released, agents updated the file without the lock, and 20 agents fell to the throughput of two or three (one to three in the February post). These were locks on the coordination file that held tasks, not locks on code. Replacement: a planner owns a scope and hands narrow tasks to workers who do not talk to each other. B: planners must make design decisions themselves and no two subtrees may decide the same question; shared docs record the decisions. C: advisory claim files per task, enforced only by Git refusing the second push of the same file name; no expiry described. Own count on the public history (2026-11-24): stale-lock removals exist, for example one commit removing locks older than two hours. D: ownership is the claim; agent teams claim tasks with file locking and recommend that each teammate owns different files.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > No controlled comparison of locking and partitioning exists in these sources. Numbers: A, self-reported, no number: hundreds of workers pushing to one branch with minimal conflicts; in an interview (2026-01-23) Lin said most commits had no merge conflict because the harness divides scope. B, self-reported, one SQLite task, apparently one run per configuration, Grok 4.5, old against new harness: old run 68,000 commits in the first two hours and more than 70,000 conflicts before it was paused; hottest file 7,771 conflicts from 1,173 agents; new run fewer than 1,000 conflicts in four hours, most contested file 47. The post gives the new run's commit pace only as about one seventieth of the old one. Arithmetic here: 68,000 divided by 70 is about 970 commits in two hours, so conflicts per commit fell from roughly 1 to at most about 0.5 if the pace held, while the absolute number fell about 70 times; most of the drop comes from far fewer commits, not from each commit meeting fewer conflicts. Several things changed at once (version control system, planner prompts, conflict agent, file splitting, review), so no part can be credited on its own, and which version control system the old run used is not stated. C, self-reported, qualitative: conflicts frequent, no rate. The kernel stage is the clearest partitioning result: 16 agents all hit the same bug and overwrote each other, and a GCC oracle that gave each agent a different failing file restored parallel work; no rate before or after. Own counts on the public repositories (2026-11-24, GitHub API, regex on commit subjects, not conflict rates): claudes-c-compiler has 3,982 commits from 2026-01-23 to 2026-02-05, of which 2,633 (66 percent) are lock take or release bookkeeping, about 10 releases say the task was already fixed by another agent, and 6 are merge commits (conflicts were probably solved in rebases or squashes, so this does not measure them); fastrender has 29,858 commits, 66 subjects that mention merge conflicts or markers and 29 merge commits. D: no data.

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > A: each worker has its own copy of the repository on one large Linux VM; disk and compile output were the bottleneck, and splitting the project into self-contained crates cut the compile wait (several times more throughput, self-reported). B: not described. C: bare repository /upstream mounted into each Docker container, clone at /workspace. D: ordinary clone, worktree or one shared directory (agent teams).
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > A, self-reported, vendor research run: about 2,000 agents at peak over several machines, about 300 per machine (interview, 2026-01-23); about 1,000 commits an hour at peak and 10 million tool calls in one week (post, 2026-02-05). Own count on the public browser repository: peak 467 commits in a clock hour, peak day 6,360 on 2026-01-13; this is below the published figure, and the public repository may be a snapshot. B, self-reported: the new system peaks at about 1,000 commits a second; the SQLite runs shown are far slower (the old run averages about 9 a second over its first two hours, arithmetic here). C: 16 agents, 3,982 commits in two weeks, own count peak 74 an hour. D: not a scale claim.
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > A and B: vendor research runs on greenfield Rust projects, self-reported, one or a few runs per configuration; outputs public. C: one vendor experiment, output public, commit history can be recounted. D: opinion and documentation, no measurement. None is peer-reviewed.
- **Coordination limit:**
  > A: locking broke at 20 agents; a single integrator broke at hundreds; a rule of full correctness per commit stalled the whole system. C: 16 agents gave no gain on one indivisible task. B: the old harness spiralled on Grok 4.5 and was paused before two hours. D: agent teams docs advise 3 to 5 teammates with 5 to 6 tasks each and say returns diminish beyond that.

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > A: none described; later fix-up passes and a proposed green branch. B: not described; review agents with different views of the work, on different models, stacked. C: none at first, then a CI pipeline because new features broke existing ones, with a fast mode that runs a 1 to 10 percent sample, fixed per agent and different across machines. D: not described.
- **Broken main recovery:**
  > A: broken states are tolerated and repaired by later workers; one run recovered from a heavily broken tree in a few days; a green branch is proposed. B: an agent that breaks something outside its scope leaves a comment, and compiler errors carry the fix to the others. C: CI from the later stage. D: none.
- **Review model:**
  > A: a judge agent decided whether to continue a cycle; later removed. B: several review agents with different inputs and models (full transcript, output only, codebase only), manual review after each run. C: test suites plus special-role agents (duplicate removal, performance, design critique, documentation). D: not described for agents; owners review their own module.

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > A: an internal harness, operated over SSH in a terminal. B: internal harness, interface not described. C: Claude Code sessions in an endless loop inside Docker, with one prompt and a claim-file convention. D: Claude Code agent teams settings, with limits: teammates cannot be started from non-interactive -p mode, and task status can lag.
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > Git history in A and C; not described in B. Own count on the public history of C: stale claim files are removed by other agents, and only 2 of 3,982 subjects mention a revert.
- **Agent cost:**
  > Self-reported: C, \$20,000 for about 2,000 sessions, about \$10 a session (arithmetic here). B, workers used at least 69 percent of the tokens in every run; \$9,373 of worker cost in the GPT-5.5 run against \$411 for the Composer fleet. A: trillions of tokens, no price. Coordination overhead visible in C: two thirds of the commits are lock bookkeeping (own count); in A early locking, most agent time was waiting.
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > A: workers are unaware of the larger system and only their planner sees their report. C: one shared repository, no per-agent rights described; nearly all 3,982 public commits carry the same author name, Claude Opus 4.6, so a commit shows the model but not the agent. B: a neutral conflict agent with its own role; a licensed out-of-scope patch must carry a comment. D: ownership is a naming rule, not enforced by permissions in the docs read.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > A: the worker (own push and merge), planners decide scope; the integrator was removed. B: the version control system and a neutral agent, the workers only commit. C: the agent itself. D: the owner.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Vendor self-report and one open-source replayable history (C). Main weaknesses: no controlled ablation, one task per system, greenfield Rust projects with a known specification or a complete test suite, and the authors benefit from the result. Independent checks exist only as counts and code reading.
- **Independent reports:**
  > The Register, 2026-01-22 and 2026-01-26: consultant Jason Gorman reported that 88 percent of CI jobs in the public browser repository failed, and recent commits did not build cleanly; Lin had written on Hacker News on 2026-01-16, before the article, that build errors were resolved and that the failing CI test was not a standard check; Gorman said the CI build was still failing when asked. Hacker News (2026-01-14 and 2026-02-05 threads): one user notes that the lock files stop two agents from claiming the same task but not from converging on the same downstream failure, and that the GCC oracle was a human inventing a new harness halfway (anupamchugh, 2026-02-06); another credits small, well-written tests more than agent teams (andrewshawcare, 2026-02-06); a third reports that four parallel Claude sessions on a complex task lose coherence and that no smaller set of agents was compared (pcloadlett3r, 2026-02-06 and 07). yage.ai, 2026-07-23: the SQLite task suits agents and several variables changed at once. TechTimes, 2026-07-27: B is self-reported and not audited. Hacker News thread 48982535, 2026-07-20: nobody outside can try the harness or the version control system. D: the Przeprogramowani video names one customer rollout that was not checked here.

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > Greenfield projects with a full specification or test suite, work that splits into modules with clear borders, and teams that can give each agent an independent check. The practices fit any size; the platforms of A and B do not exist for outsiders.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > Locks held or forgotten, agents waiting on each other, agents fixing the same error in different ways, a single integrator as bottleneck, oversized files that collect conflicts, one indivisible task for many agents, tests that are too slow for the agent loop, semantic breakage hidden by late CI, code that compiles badly because nobody checks the whole, tokens spent on lock bookkeeping, and runs that spiral (old harness with Grok 4.5; GPT-5.6 Sol was dropped for runaway loops). For ownership (D): cross-module changes need an agreement process that none of the sources describes, and one person owning a whole module is a single point of failure.
- **Minimum useful scale:**
  > Nothing measured. Judgement: module ownership and independent tests pay off from a few agents, planner and worker trees from about 10 to 20 (locking failed at 20 in A), and a neutral conflict agent and file splitting only when hundreds of agents or a rising conflict count show the need.
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Use the practices, not a product. Give each agent a module or file set and a failing test of its own; let a planner (agent or script) cut the work so that no two agents decide the same question; avoid a shared status file that everyone edits; use Git-side claims only for task choice; merge through a queue with CI. Keep one owner per module. Skip the lock-file design of C as a default: it cost two thirds of its commits in bookkeeping and the same work was still done twice.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Pilot as a design, not as a product: planner tree, isolated copies, a single neutral resolver for conflicts, hot-file detection that blocks and splits, decision docs with references that break when they change, an accepted error budget with a green snapshot branch. All evidence is vendor self-report on greenfield Rust projects, so test it on one subsystem with a real conflict and rework count before trusting it at several hundred agents. The version control system of B is not available, so plan for Git or jj underneath.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > Partition by module, with one owner per module, and by test: give each agent a different failing test or a different failing file (the GCC oracle idea). Claim tasks, not code, and expire claims automatically. Do not route all work through one agent or one shared file. Let a neutral agent resolve conflicts instead of either author. Detect files that collect edits and split them before they become contested. Record design decisions once and link the code to them. Accept a small error rate inside the swarm but keep a branch that is always green. Start review agents with different inputs. Measure conflicts per commit, not only in total, or a harness that commits less will look better than it is.

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > Cursor, Scaling long-running autonomous coding, 2026-01-14, https://cursor.com/blog/scaling-agents ; Cursor, Towards self-driving codebases, 2026-02-05, https://cursor.com/blog/self-driving-codebases ; Cursor, Agent swarms and the new model economics, 2026-07-20, https://cursor.com/blog/agent-swarm-model-economics ; Simon Willison, interview with Wilson Lin, 2026-01-23, https://simonwillison.net/2026/Jan/23/fastrender/ ; Anthropic, Building a C compiler with a team of parallel Claudes, 2026-02-05, https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-c-compiler ; anthropics/claudes-c-compiler, https://github.com/anthropics/claudes-c-compiler ; wilsonzlin/fastrender, https://github.com/wilsonzlin/fastrender ; cursor/minisqlite, https://github.com/cursor/minisqlite ; Every, The Two-Slice Team, 2026-02-13, https://every.to/chain-of-thought/the-two-slice-team ; Przeprogramowani, 2026-10-26, https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TGVtRGHhruM (Polish video, transcript in the vault) ; Claude Code agent teams, read 2026-11-24, https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-teams . Independent: The Register, 2026-01-22, https://www.theregister.com/2026/01/22/cursor_ai_wrote_a_browser/ and 2026-01-26, https://www.theregister.com/2026/01/26/cursor_opinion/ ; TechTimes, 2026-07-27, https://www.techtimes.com/articles/321660/20260727/cursor-agent-swarm-worker-fleet-dropped-9373-411-sqlite-test.htm ; yage.ai, 2026-07-23, https://yage.ai/share/cursor-sqlite-harness-swarm-en-20260723.html ; Hacker News, 2026-01-14, https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=46624541 (Lin's comment 2026-01-16, https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=46650998) ; 2026-02-05, https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=46903616 ; 2026-07-20, https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=48982535 . Context on multi-agent limits (not coding): Google, Towards a science of scaling agent systems, 2026-01-28, https://research.google/blog/towards-a-science-of-scaling-agent-systems-when-and-why-agent-systems-work , paper arXiv 2512.08296
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > The outline summary says workers resolve their own conflicts; that is the January 2026 position and Cursor reversed it in July 2026. The published peak of about 1,000 commits an hour (A) and thousands an hour in the interview are above the peak of 467 found in the public repository; the repository may be incomplete, so this is unexplained. The old Grok run is described both as having 68,000 commits in two hours and as paused before its second hour. The version control system of B is not public and not linked by any source to Origin. B's per-commit conflict figure is arithmetic from a rounded ratio. Counts on public histories depend on commit-subject patterns and may miss squashed work. Not verified: the customer rollout named in the Przeprogramowani video; The Register's 88 percent figure beyond its source; the interview figures of Lin, reported by Simon Willison.

### Entire

*Layer: C Git hosting*

#### Basics

- **Name:** Entire
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > https://entire.com (entire.io redirects there), docs https://docs.entire.com, GitHub organisation entireio: entireio/cli, entireio/git-sync, entireio/forgemark, entireio/entire-graph, entireio/entire-brain, entireio/entire-rsl, entireio/skills. The Git server is called EntireDB; its source is not public. No rename found; the product scope grew from a session-capture CLI (February 2026) through a mirror network (July 2026) to a forge (October 2026), so older articles describe a different product. The name is an ordinary English word and searches return unrelated results; no unrelated version-control project named Entire was found.
- **Maker:**
  > Entire Inc.; founder and CEO Thomas Dohmke (CEO of GitHub until the end of 2025). USD 60 million seed round announced in February 2026 and a team of about 50 (company press release, 2026-10-26).
- **What it is:**
  > A hosted, Git-compatible forge for agents and people, in four parts. (1) EntireDB, a Git server built on go-git: one PostgreSQL database per cell holds refs (one row per ref, every update a compare-and-swap against the expected previous value) and a set of nodes holds loose objects and packfiles; a push becomes visible once nodes in at least two availability zones hold the validated objects and the ref update succeeds. (2) Code hosting in two forms: mirrors of a GitHub repository, where GitHub stays the source of truth, and native private repositories, with one writable home cell and read mirrors in other regions. (3) Trails, which replace the pull request: an intent, one or more changes (a branch in a repository; a trail can span repositories), agent evaluations and merge gates. (4) An MIT-licensed CLI that records agent sessions as checkpoints in Git refs of the repository itself, with search, entire-graph (code map) and entire-brain (memory). The July item in the outline (distributed network) and the November InfoQ item (forge) are the same product at two stages.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > Addressed: throughput (clone and push rates, regional reads) and review at agent speed, plus provenance of changes. Partly addressed: landing contention (server-side merge, gates, branch protection), but without a merge queue, batching or speculative testing. Not addressed: prevention of textual conflicts, semantic conflicts (beyond what review agents or CI happen to find), task collision, and workspace cost on the client.
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > Isolate then merge: one branch per agent, three-way merge at landing, compare-and-swap on each ref in the server. No server-side replay in the documented landing path.

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > Usable today by an outsider with an account, under preview terms. CLI and checkpoints since 2026-02-10; mirrors of GitHub repositories since 2026-07-08 (waitlist at first); Entire-native branches (own post 2026-09-09, already described in the July post); native repositories and Trails for every developer since 2026-10-26. The pricing page still says early access and 'during our preview' (read 2026-11-24). CI plugins are in public preview. Announced, not available: self-hosting, public native repositories, open-source EntireDB (promised on 2026-10-27 for the coming months).
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > Hosting, Trails and EntireDB: closed source, SaaS only. MIT: CLI, git-sync, ForgeMark, entire-graph, entire-brain, entire-rsl, skills. Self-hosting is announced, not available.
- **Price:**
  > Read 2026-11-24: code hosting, Trails, session storage, continuous memory, search and CLI are free during the preview. Only agents are charged, pay-as-you-go from prepaid credit at model-provider token rates (rate table dated 2026-11-23; vendor example USD 0.41 for one review run with Claude Opus 5.5 on a 4,000-line diff). A USD 100, 90-day trial credit is documented but paused for new accounts; purchased credit expires after 365 days; agents stop at a zero balance. Enterprise: contact sales.
