CI is billed by the CI partner. No price for hosting after the preview is published.
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > Native hosting was four weeks old at the time of reading. Timeline: CLI 2026-02-10, mirror network 2026-07-08, post on Entire-native branches 2026-09-09, forge 2026-10-26, Dagger and RWX CI 2026-11-04 and 2026-11-05, Reference State Log 2026-11-10, service accounts 2026-11-13. CLI v0.13.4 stable (2026-11-13) with daily nightlies. GitHub API, 2026-11-24: entireio/cli 5,353 stars and 506 forks, created 2026-01-02, pushed the same day; git-sync 525; skills 241; entire-graph 143; entire-brain 105; forgemark 60. Entire said on 2026-10-27 that most of its repositories mirror GitHub. No named customer was found; two Hacker News commenters report regular use.
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > Seed-funded start-up with a free preview, so price and terms can change. Branches under entire/unmirrored/ are not backed up by Entire and live in one region. Replication between cells is asynchronous. The status page showed 100% uptime for August to November 2026 and small incidents: 2026-08-14 (delayed metadata, India), 2026-09-25 (errors loading repositories and trails, six minutes), 2026-10-20 (Entire Gates checks added by mistake to GitHub pull requests of mirrored repositories, fixed the same day). A CLI bug in which concurrent checkpoint writes could silently lose data was reported 2026-08-07 and closed 2026-08-31 (entireio/cli issue 1917). Entire runs Antithesis fault simulation on EntireDB and reports one defect found and fixed (a fetch could return a half-written packfile). SOC 2 Type II audit in progress (homepage). No acquisition found.
- **OS support:**
  > CLI: macOS, Linux and Windows (Homebrew, Scoop, PowerShell installer; Git 2.25 or later). The remote helper git-remote-entire is published for linux, darwin and windows on amd64 and arm64. WSL is not named in the pages read. The CI set-up action does not run on a Windows runner; the docs give a manual token exchange. Hosting is a service.

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > Git client and protocol kept: entire:// remotes through the helper git-remote-entire, or smart HTTP. Repositories stay ordinary Git, with session data in extra refs. Git LFS is not supported on mirrors. A server-side Git API (trees, merges, rebases, reverts) exists in the OpenAPI file alongside the protocol.
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > GitHub mirror: GitHub stays the origin; clones and fetches come from Entire; every push is forwarded and accepted only if GitHub accepts it; CI and the merge go through a linked GitHub pull request, GitHub branch rules still apply, and the Checks gate reads GitHub checks. Branches under entire/unmirrored/ skip GitHub until they are pushed again under a normal name. Native repository: GitHub is out of the loop; issues, pull requests, releases and permissions do not migrate and GitHub Actions does not run there. CI comes from plugins in public preview (Buildkite hosted agents, Depot, Dagger, RWX) that report into gates; a Jenkins branch-source plugin repository exists; bk pipeline convert and depot ci migrate translate GitHub Actions workflows.
- **Migration and exit:**
  > Three levels of adoption. (1) CLI only: capture sessions in a repository hosted anywhere; leaving means uninstalling, and the checkpoint refs stay in the repository. (2) Mirror: install the GitHub App and add a remote; leaving means removing the mirror, after pushing any entire/unmirrored/ branches to GitHub. (3) Native: pause pushes, copy refs and switch the remote, or detach an existing mirror (about one minute with writes frozen, after which GitHub is no longer synchronised). LFS payloads and submodule repositories need separate handling. Git history and checkpoint refs leave with a clone at any time; trails, findings, evaluations, approvals and hosted memory stay on the platform, and no export for them was found in the documentation.

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > Native repository. (1) The agent commits locally and pushes its own branch. (2) The branch is linked to a trail as a change. (3) Configured review agents run in cloud sandboxes, on each push if the push trigger is on, and write scores and findings. (4) Optional gates are evaluated: up to date, human approvals, findings above a severity, CI plugin results, base-branch CI. Gates are off until a repository enables them. (5) A person, a service account or another authorised caller merges through the web interface, `entire trail merge`, or POST /repos/{repo_id}/changes/{number}/merge with expectedHeadSha; the server performs the merge. Each change in a trail merges on its own. Bypassing gates needs --force and permission and is recorded. Direct pushes to main work as in plain Git unless branch protection forbids them; the level 'server-side merge only' makes main move only through merges that Entire performs. The OpenAPI file also lists low-level calls: merges (merge, noFastForward, fastForwardOnly, squash; expectedBaseSHA required, that is, a compare-and-swap), rebases, reverts, and a preview of each. These calls have no documentation page. No merge queue, batching, automatic rebase, speculative build or automatic retry was found in the docs. GitHub mirror: the merge happens on GitHub through the linked pull request, so any merge queue would be GitHub's. Serialisation: one writable cell per native repository, one row per ref, so main advances one compare-and-swap at a time; other regions forward pushes to the home cell.
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > Ref level, at push: every ref update is a compare-and-swap, so a push to a branch that moved is refused. Content level, at landing or on request: a three-way merge of head and base; the mergeability call returns conflict_status clean, conflicting or unknown plus behind_by, and `entire trail merge --dry-run` reports a refusal without merging. The OpenAPI schema returns conflictedPaths (may be truncated) and one of four reasons (treeType, treeMode, blobOverlap, blobBinary); a rebase preview says per commit whether it would conflict. So the report is per path. Whether blobOverlap is judged per line or per hunk is not stated in docs or schema. Nothing is detected before or during editing, between two open branches, or after the merge; semantic conflicts are left to review agents and CI.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > Rejection. The docs say that a conflicting change is never merged and must be resolved first. The agent or developer resolves it locally with Git and pushes again. Through the low-level API the caller gets outcome conflict, a reason code and a list of conflicted paths, which is machine-readable but contains no hunks or markers. No automatic replay, conflict-as-data or resolving agent is documented. Across regions there is nothing to resolve because only the home cell accepts writes; Entire says that several writable cells would need a conflict-resolution model it does not provide (2026-10-27).
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > Depends on the Up to Date gate. Off, or set to allow clean merges while behind: a change that merges cleanly lands against the newer main without a new test of the combined result. On and strict: the agent must update the branch and push, which starts CI again, reruns any review agent with a push trigger (billed), can make earlier findings stale and, if so configured, resets approvals. With many agents landing on one main and no queue, the strict setting makes every landing invalidate the others; this is reasoning from the documented rules, not a measurement. Compare-and-swap failures return an error code (409, 412, 422 and 429 are listed); retry semantics are not described.
- **Pre write coordination:**
  > None found: no claims, locks, leases or file reservations. A trail can record intent and assignees before code exists, and search, entire-graph and entire-brain let an agent read earlier decisions, but nothing warns about live edits by another agent. Advisory at best.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > No evidence, and no vendor claim with numbers. A vendor post (2026-07-06, modified 2026-08-26) says that agents can work in parallel without overwriting or colliding once session memory is in the repository; no measurement supports it (marketing claim). ForgeMark cannot show an effect: in its default branch strategy every agent force-pushes its own ref, so there is no ref contention and nothing is merged. No conflict rate, refused-merge rate or landing success rate was found from Entire or from anyone else. Review agents and gates act after the code is written, so a lower conflict rate would not be expected from the design (inference).

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > Client: ordinary Git clone, full or shallow, from a nearby region; `entire trail checkout --worktree` creates a worktree. No virtualised, sparse-by-default or copy-on-write checkout found. Server: refs in PostgreSQL per cell; full copies of objects and packfiles on the nodes assigned by repository ID; a push is acknowledged with copies in two availability zones, the other nodes catch up asynchronously. Regional read mirrors are complete copies updated asynchronously with the open-source git-sync. Regions: US, EU, India and Australia at launch; UK, Singapore and Brazil added by 2026-11-24 according to the docs.
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > Concurrent agents or writers on one repository: not published. Throughput, all self-reported synthetic benchmarks of branch pushes: October 2026 with ForgeMark, 418 pushes per second for Entire against 65 Cursor Origin, 15 Pierre, 10 GitLab and 4.7 GitHub (418 / 4.7 = 89 times and 418 / 65 = 6.4 times, arithmetic here); agent count, duration, region, repository size and result files are not published. July 2026, 586 pushes per second, about 570,000 clones per hour and about 470 mixed clone-and-push operations per second at 50-60 ms median latency (press reports). The July post, as modified 2026-08-28, says that entire/unmirrored/ branches, which are not forwarded to GitHub, power its ForgeMark numbers; which branch type the October run used is not stated. ForgeMark's branch strategy pushes to refs/forgemark/, outside refs/heads, which avoids CI, search and webhooks. Landings per second on one main: not published, so 418 is not a landing rate.
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > Vendor synthetic benchmark with an open-source tool but unpublished run conditions; N and duration not given. No independent run and no production figure for concurrent agents found by 2026-11-24.
- **Coordination limit:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] Not measured by anyone. Documented limits that would bind first: one writable home cell per native repository, at most 5 service accounts per organisation or account, pushes through a mirror only as fast as GitHub accepts them, no queue in front of main, per-push cost of review agents.

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > Pre-merge, optional. Builds start on push through Buildkite, Depot, Dagger or RWX and report into a gate per plugin. With an empty Required checks list a failed build blocks the merge, but a build that has not reported does not. A gate can also look at the CI of the base branch. On mirrors, the Checks gate uses GitHub checks. Review agents have path filters for monorepos, and their score shows the last five runs. No batching, speculation, affected-target selection or test of the merged result is documented; the Up to Date gate is the only way to test against the latest base.
- **Broken main recovery:**
  > No bisect, automatic revert or known-good pointer documented. A base-branch CI gate can stop further merges while main is red, and the OpenAPI file has a server-side revert with preview (no documentation page).
- **Review model:**
  > Agent review first: scored evaluations and findings on a push, optional human approvals as a gate, a Findings gate with a severity threshold (default high). Merging without reading the code is an explicit aim when evaluations are stable. Not tied to risk tiers beyond path filters and thresholds.

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > CLI with --json on many commands, `entire agent-help --json` for usage that matches the installed version, `entire trail create / merge --dry-run / watch --json / finding apply`, and `entire api`. REST API with OpenAPI files, webhooks, Go SDK, Agent Skills, and hooks for Claude Code, Codex, Cursor, Copilot CLI, OpenCode and others. The MCP server (`entire mcp`) is read-only with two tools, so landing goes through CLI or REST. Non-interactive sign-in through service accounts with OIDC or a secret. Gap: issue 2924 (open, 2026-10-27) says no CLI command explains why a gate check failed.
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > Ordinary Git, plus for native repositories the Reference State Log: an append-only signed record of ref updates (gittuf) that keeps earlier positions traceable after force pushes and deletions. Checkpoints let an agent inspect or resume an earlier session; an open issue (2604, 2026-09-28) says that session resume can overwrite ignored untracked files.
- **Agent cost:**
  > Set-up: CLI, remote helper, login, hooks. No coordination messages. Hosting is free during the preview. Every push that triggers review agents costs tokens (vendor example USD 0.41 per run), so updating a stale branch is paid again. Vendor claims, not verified: up to 71% fewer tokens with entire-graph, and 81 against 70 of 90 history questions answered at under half the tokens with search.
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > Access: reader, writer and admin per repository or project; service accounts (since 2026-11-13) for native projects only, with OIDC or a secret, at most 5 per organisation or account, so one identity per agent is not possible at 30 or 300 agents. On mirrors, access is copied from GitHub. No per-folder permissions found. Provenance: each commit made in a captured session carries an Entire-Checkpoint trailer that points to prompt, transcript and tool calls in refs/entire/checkpoints/ (since CLI 0.10.0). Attribution is per commit; blame and why are labs commands. Squash merges can drop trailers. Anyone who can read the repository can read its checkpoints; redaction is best effort, and local shadow branches hold raw snapshots. Review agents run in Daytona or E2B sandboxes.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > The agent: it fetches, rebases or merges, resolves conflicts and pushes. The server serialises ref updates, performs the final merge and enforces gates; a person or authorised account triggers the merge.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Vendor self-report throughout. The architecture post is concrete and states its own limits; the benchmark is marketing until run conditions are published. InfoQ (2026-11-08) restates the announcement without a test. Main weakness: nothing at all on conflicts, landing rate or contended refs.
- **Independent reports:**
  > Stackness (late October 2026, read 2026-11-24): no outside reproduction; reads GitHub's 4.7 as most likely a rate limit, because ForgeMark's README warns about github.com limits and recommends low concurrency (its inference); notes the lead over Cursor Origin went from about 26 times in July to 6.4 times in October; advises skipping Entire if open source, self-hosting or LFS is needed. Hacker News, forge launch (2026-10-26, 57 points, 40 comments): doubts about the value of session transcripts, about a venture-backed 'rebellion' and about a high-trust review view; two positive users, one on a large main project for a month; several replies are from Entire staff. Hacker News, February launch: 611 points, 577 comments. Chiri (read 2026-11-24): no independent test; one user removed the CLI because of conflicts with semantic-release and git-cz. SoftTechHub (2026-11-11): no independent benchmark. GitHub issues: 1349 (lefthook pre-push conflict, open since 2026-06-03), 340 (transcripts on a Git branch as a privacy problem, open since 2026-02-15). Nesbitt's weekly (2026-10-28) repeats the claim and lists Enroute's competing claim of more than 7,600 pushes per second into one repository (vendor claim).

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > Teams on GitHub that want session provenance and agent review without moving the repository; teams throttled on clones or branch pushes; teams that need code hosted in a given region.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > Reading the 89-times figure as faster landing, when it is uncontended branch pushes. Main still moves one change at a time with manual conflict repair and no queue. A strict Up to Date gate reruns CI and paid reviews for every waiting agent. Permissive setting: untested combinations reach main. A missing build does not block when Required checks is empty. Unmirrored branches can be lost and a removed GitHub user may push to them until access is synchronised. Transcripts may expose secrets to all repository readers. Five service accounts. Native hosting gives up GitHub Actions, issues, public repositories and LFS on the mirror path. Price after the preview unknown. Merging on evaluation scores rests on review agents whose accuracy nobody has measured independently.
- **Minimum useful scale:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] No data. Judgement: hosting pays only when clone or push limits already hurt, which 5-30 agents on separate branches usually do not reach; below that, worktrees plus GitHub's merge queue do more for landing. Checkpoints are useful from one agent, for provenance rather than scale.
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Pilot the parts that leave the repository where it is: the CLI for checkpoints, and a GitHub mirror if clones are throttled. Wait on moving the main repository: conflicts are handled no better than on GitHub and there is no merge queue, native hosting is four weeks old, push speed is not the bottleneck at this size, and five service accounts do not give each agent its own identity.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Wait, then pilot as the Git host behind an own landing queue. Several hundred agents do need fast branch pushes and regional clones, and Entire is available now. Missing: landing queue or server-side replay, evidence of landings per second on one main, pre-write coordination, more than five service accounts, backup of unmirrored branches, self-hosting, a published price. If adopted, protect main with server-side merge only, put an external queue in front that uses expectedHeadSha and the preview calls, and keep the strict Up to Date gate for high-risk paths only.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > Store the agent session beside the commit, in separate refs linked by a commit trailer. Use a mergeability or rebase preview that returns conflicted paths and a reason code before spending an agent run on a rebase. Make every merge conditional on the expected head and base. Allow a clean merge while behind for low-risk paths, demand up-to-date for the rest. Push throwaway agent branches to a ref namespace that triggers no CI or mirroring. Look at several evaluation runs, not one. Benchmark your own forge with ForgeMark where load tests are allowed, and add a contended-ref and a landing test, which its default strategy leaves out.

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > Entire press release, 2026-10-26, https://entire.com/news/entire-launches-an-independent-git-forge-for-agent-human-collaboration ; The Git Forge for Agent-Human Collaboration, 2026-10-26, https://entire.com/blog/the-git-forge-for-agent-human-collaboration ; A Git Forge Without a Center (architecture), 2026-10-27, https://entire.com/blog/a-git-forge-without-a-center ; An Entirely New Git Hosting Network, 2026-07-08 (modified 2026-08-28), https://entire.com/blog/an-entirely-new-git-hosting-network ; How Version Control Will Evolve for the Agent Boom, 2026-07-06 (modified 2026-08-26), https://entire.com/blog/how-version-control-will-evolve-for-the-agent-boom ; Pricing, rates dated 2026-11-23, https://entire.com/pricing ; Docs, read 2026-11-24, https://docs.entire.com (guides/trails/overview, guides/trails/gates, guides/repositories/repository, guides/repositories/mirrors, guides/repositories/entire-native-branches, guides/repositories/reference-state-log, guides/ci/checks, guides/configuration/service-accounts, cli-reference/trail, cli-reference/mcp, billing, security, migration/migrate-from-github) ; OpenAPI, https://docs.entire.com/openapi/generated/trails.json ; ForgeMark, https://github.com/entireio/forgemark ; CLI and issues 340, 1349, 1917, 2604, 2924, https://github.com/entireio/cli ; Status, https://status.entire.io (all read 2026-11-24). Secondary: InfoQ, 2026-11-08, https://www.infoq.com/news/2026/11/entire-git-forge-coding-agents/ ; The New Stack, July 2026, https://thenewstack.io/entire-git-for-agents/ ; The Next Web, July 2026, https://thenextweb.com/news/entire-distributed-git-network-agents ; Stackness, https://stackness.dev/blog/entire-git-forge-trail-vs-pull-request-what-changes-and-what-stays ; Hacker News, 2026-10-26, https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50247453 and February 2026, https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=46961345 ; Chiri, https://chiri.ai/chiri-atlas/entire ; SoftTechHub, 2026-11-11, https://softtechhub.us/2026/11/11/git-forge-entire-coding-agents/ ; Nesbitt, 2026-10-28, https://nesbitt.io/2026/10/28/this-week-in-source-control.html
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > Not published: conditions of the 418 and 4.7 figures, landing rate on one branch, agents per repository, hosting price after the preview, retry semantics of the merge calls. Server-side merge, rebase and revert calls are in the OpenAPI file only (no hit in the docs text), so whether outsiders may rely on them is unknown; they were not called here. The OpenAPI file names refs/heads/entire/native/ as the Entire-owned namespace, docs and blog name entire/unmirrored/. Region lists differ: press release four regions, docs seven, status page four. The mirror docs give two intervals for access synchronisation (hourly, and at least daily). July figures come from press reports because the post shows a chart. The USD 300 million valuation appears only in secondary sources. 'No merge queue found' is absence in the docs, not a statement by Entire.

**Unverified fields (2):**

- coordination_limit
- minimum_useful_scale

### Conflict-rate studies of agent PRs

*Layer: H evidence*

#### Basics

- **Name:** Conflict-rate studies of agent PRs
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > AgenticFlict: arXiv 2604.03551, DOI 10.1145/3805760.3814923, code unlv-evol/AgenticFlict, data Zenodo 10.5281/zenodo.19396916. Xu et al., AI Agent Pull Requests on GitHub: Frequency, Structure, and Merge Conflict Rates: arXiv 2607.04697, Quantum535/concurrent-agentic-prs-replication, Zenodo 10.5281/zenodo.21186464. MergeGym: arXiv 2610.04779, OSF 10.17605/OSF.IO/38SKH (same three authors as 2607.04697). SBES 2026, How AI Coding Agents Resolve Merge Conflicts (Campos Junior and Murta). Repository effect: arXiv 2606.28235 (renamed between versions: Govern the Repository, Not the Agent; For Coding Agents, the Repository Matters). Rejection reasons: arXiv 2601.15195 and 2509.14745. Pre-PR coordination: arXiv 2606.19616 with tool neul-labs/grite. Shared base: AIDev, arXiv 2507.15003 and 2602.09185. Namesake risk: 'AIDev' means the full set (932,791 PRs) in some papers and the curated subset AIDev-pop (33,596 PRs) in others.
- **Maker:**
  > AgenticFlict: Daniel Ogenrwot and John Businge, University of Nevada Las Vegas (NSF funded). 2607.04697 and MergeGym: Xu, Subramanian, Karthik (Harvard Medical School and MGH, MIT CSAIL, DevRev AI). SBES 2026: Campos Junior and Murta (Fluminense Federal University, UFRJ). 2606.19616: Dipankar Sarkar, Arizona State University, who also wrote the tool it evaluates. AIDev: Li et al.
- **What it is:**
  > A group of papers that measure textual merge conflicts in pull requests written by coding agents, plus one simulation of coordination before the PR. AgenticFlict: replays `git merge --no-commit --no-ff` of 107,026 agent PRs that were open or closed without merging against their base; 29,609 conflict (27.67%). Xu et al.: finds PRs open at the same time in one repository (co-active) and replays `git merge-tree` on one sampled pair per repository, PR against PR. MergeGym: turns that replay into a benchmark (forecast overlap, resolve conflicts, schedule work) and corrects its reading. SBES 2026: looks inside agent PRs of all states for merge commits made while the PR was open, whether they conflicted and who resolved them. 2606.28235: joins AgenticFlict labels to AIDev-pop and asks how much of the variation sits in the repository. 2606.19616: seeded scripted agents (no LLM) on an abstract task pool with no coordination, locks only, or locks plus shared state (grite). Answer to the overlap question: the observational papers share one data source, AIDev, so they are one body of evidence, not four independent ones (see evidence_quality).
- **Problem addressed:**
  > Textual conflicts: AgenticFlict, 2607.04697, MergeGym, SBES 2026. Task collision: 2606.19616 (simulated), 2601.15195 (duplicate PRs), MergeGym (near-duplicate titles). Landing contention: only as simulated scheduling in MergeGym. Not addressed by any: semantic conflicts, throughput of a Git host, workspace cost, and conflicts in private repositories or with several hundred agents on one repository.
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > Studied practice is isolate then merge (branch and PR). Proposed remedies: verify each pair by replay and schedule (MergeGym), keep changes small (AgenticFlict), pre-write leases plus shared state in Git refs (2606.19616).

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > AgenticFlict: paper (v1 2026-04-04, v2 2026-05-12; AIware 2026, 6-7 July), data and code public. 2607.04697: preprint (v1 2026-07-06, v2 2026-07-07), replication package public. MergeGym: preprint (2026-10-03), artifact on OSF. SBES 2026: conference paper (8-11 September 2026), open PDF. 2606.28235: preprint (v2 2026-10-20). 2606.19616: preprint (2026-06-17), tool public. Data sets were not downloaded or re-run for this note.
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > AgenticFlict: code MIT, data CC BY 4.0 (Zenodo, 148 MB). 2607.04697: replication repository has no licence file. grite: MIT, self-hosted, state in refs/grite/\* of the Git repository. MergeGym and SBES: licence of artifacts not checked.
- **Price:**
  > n/a: papers and free data sets. Cost hint from MergeGym: one `git merge-tree` replay takes a median of 0.02 s and 0.50 s at the 95th percentile (authors' measurement), so checking pairs is cheap.
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > AgenticFlict: peer reviewed, 8 citations (Semantic Scholar, 2026-11-24), repository 2 stars, pushed 2026-07-29. 2607.04697: 7 pages, replication repository 0 stars, pushed 2026-07-04. MergeGym: seven weeks old. SBES 2026: peer reviewed. 2606.19616: 9 pages, 0 citations; grite 25 stars, created 2026-01-13, pushed 2026-07-02. Data cut-off behind the observational papers is 2025-08-01 (PRs from December 2024 to July 2025), so agents, models and workflows are 16 months old.
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > n/a for papers; data is archived on Zenodo and OSF. Caveat: 35,626 of 142,652 eligible AgenticFlict PRs (25%) could not be replayed because commits or repositories had disappeared or were not accessible, so a later re-run will lose more. grite is a one-author project without pushes since 2026-07-02.
- **OS support:** n/a: papers. The replays use plain Git (merge, merge-tree). grite platforms not checked.

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > All observational work runs on ordinary Git histories from GitHub. Squash merges and rebases hide conflicts: SBES sees only merge commits inside PRs, AgenticFlict excludes merged PRs because the state before the merge is not preserved. grite keeps its log in Git refs.
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > All data comes from public GitHub pull requests through the GitHub API. CI results are not joined to conflicts in any of the conflict papers; 2607.04697 only hypothesises CI cost. 2601.15195 counts CI or test failure as the reason for 99 rejected agent PRs (17% of 600 sampled).
- **Migration and exit:**
  > n/a: papers. To reuse the method on an own repository: list open agent branches, run `git merge-tree` per branch against main and per pair of branches, and log size, age, behind-count and conflicting paths. No tool adoption needed.

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > n/a: not a product. What the studies assume: AgenticFlict: each PR merges alone into its base (current base for open PRs, reconstructed base at closing time for closed PRs). 2607.04697 and MergeGym: two PRs merged with each other, not with main, so part of the measured conflict can be staleness between different bases; MergeGym says this explicitly. SBES 2026: the branch is kept current by merging main into it while the PR is open; 12,299 of 210,902 PRs (5.8%) contain such an internal merge. MergeGym scheduling track (simulation, 97 repositories, 19,397 PRs): all scope collisions disappear if PRs are serialised first-in-first-out, at +167.3% median makespan; a trained gate removes 91.7% at +65.0%; an oracle removes 100% at +33.4%. Scope collision there means a shared authored file, not a replayed conflict.
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > AgenticFlict: at (simulated) landing, textual, per PR, file and conflict region; mean 4.36 conflicting files (median 2), 11.36 regions and 540.42 lines per conflicting PR, 336,380 regions in total. 2607.04697: pair of open PRs, textual, per file and conflict type (content 57.6%, modify/delete 26.8%, add/add 15.1%). MergeGym: same pairs plus file-overlap, lineage and hunk level. SBES 2026: internal merge commits inside the PR, chunk level, median 2 chunks. 2606.19616: cross-actor overwrites of tracker state in a simulation, not code. None detects semantic conflicts.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > SBES 2026 (independent, observational): people commit 96.1% of the conflicting internal merges (14,381 of 14,960 in the results table), agents 462 (3.1%), agent-assisted 117. The share resolved by the agent itself differs: Devin 29.4%, Claude Code 5.5% plus 24.1% assisted, Codex 0.5%. 7,291 of 121,599 chunks (6.0%) were committed with conflict markers still in them. MergeGym (162-conflict pilot, 1,469 locatable hunks): taking the later side reproduces the final repository state for 49.6% of hunks, the earlier side 28.4%, union 12.8%; the best of the three per hunk, chosen with hindsight, 76.2%. No learned resolver was run. AgenticFlict reports no resolutions because it has no merged PRs.
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > Indirect evidence only. MergeGym: features of the common base and branch lineage (branch age, commits behind base, base churn, whether the other lineage touched an authored path) predict a conflict much better than authored-file overlap: AUROC 0.877 for a single lineage-union rule and 0.873 for lineage features alone, against 0.694-0.702 for file overlap. 79 of 167 conflicts (47.3%) had no authored file in common; 48 of the 79 could be rebuilt on one common base and all 48 became clean, the other 31 were inconclusive, so 28.7% of the conflicts in that sample (48/167, arithmetic here) vanished once the two authored patches stood on the same base. That points to diverged bases rather than clashing edits; the authors do not claim to have separated the two, and the sample is enriched with cross-agent pairs. 2607.04697 notes that stale pairs are possible because unresolved PRs stay open until the cut-off. Retry cost was not measured by anyone.
- **Pre write coordination:**
  > 2606.19616 only: advisory leases with a time-to-live plus an append-only log in Git refs (grite), in a simulation with scripted agents. Finding: leases alone are not enough; duplicate work at 32 agents is 0.78 without coordination, 0.64 with locks only and 0.00 with locks plus shared done-state. MergeGym's forecast track shows how hard it is to know scope ahead: from title, body and timing at open time the best model reaches AUC 0.740 for 'will these PRs share a file' (zero-shot LLM 0.704, title overlap 0.629), useful for planning, not for a guarantee.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > Rates are not comparable because the units differ; do not average them. (1) AgenticFlict, PR against its base, only open or unmerged PRs: 27.67% (29,609 of 107,026), independent, observational. Per agent (Table 2): Copilot 15.24%, Cursor 19.75%, Devin 22.85%, Claude Code 25.93% (779 PRs), Codex 31.85% (73,856 PRs, 69% of the sample). The text of the same paper gives 15.43, 20.06, 23.04, 26.86 and 32.31%; clean (75,924) plus conflicting PRs make 105,533, not 107,026, and over 105,533 the rate is 28.06% (arithmetic here). Merged PRs are missing, so 27.67% is not the rate of all agent PRs; 29,609 of 932,791 gives a floor of 3.2% (arithmetic here). (2) 2607.04697, PR against PR, one random co-active pair per repository: same agent 19.8% (119/601, CI 16.8-23.2), different agents 41.7% (48/115, CI 33.1-50.9). MergeGym weakens the second figure: in the 60 repositories with both kinds, 28.3% against 43.3%, McNemar p = 0.078, with 37.4% expected from the mix of agents; the authors do not claim a cross-vendor penalty. (3) SBES 2026, merge commits inside PRs: 14,960 of 50,700 (29.5%) conflict; 7,268 of 210,902 PRs (3.4%) have at least one. (4) MergeGym T2, pairs that were both merged and share a source file: 1,252 of 5,936 (21.1%). Human reference, different unit and period: 11.9% of 960,366 Java merges (Ghiotto, as cited by SBES 2026) and nearly 20% in AgenticFlict's citation of the same study. No like-for-like human baseline exists, so 'agents conflict more than people' is not shown. DRIVERS. Size, supported but uncontrolled: AgenticFlict about 9.9% at a median churn of 2 lines, nearly 30% at 25 lines, plateau of 32-33% at 46-185 lines, slightly lower above; MergeGym 10.3% up to 1,000 modified lines, 34.3% above 20,000. Duration, not measured directly: the closest are the lineage features above, and hours of overlap barely predict a shared file (AUC 0.570). Hot files, partly: source files are 84.4% of 1,646 conflicting files, manifests and lock files 3.9% (but present in 19.2% of conflicting pairs); 7.38% of 577,045 co-active pairs share an authored file, README.md and package.json being the commonest names; conflict yield rises from 5.0% with one shared source file to 24.0% with six or more. No paper measures concentration on hot files within one repository. Repository: between-repository share of conflict incidence (ICC) 0.18 after covariates; size and age of the code base remove at most 11.5% of it (2606.28235). Agent brand: differences are confounded with task mix and repository. Interventions: none tested on real agents. 2606.19616 (simulation, N = 32): 'conflicting edits' 410 without coordination, 138 with locks, 48 with locks plus state, where the term means overwritten tracker state, not code merges. Production anecdote: the Organ project (self-reported, one repository, 2026-10-13) had 38 of 62 open agent PRs in conflict (61%), 26 of them at least a week old, with one shared stylesheet as the hot file.

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > n/a: papers. Replays use full clones; 2607.04697 and MergeGym use `git merge-tree`, which needs no working tree, so pair checks do not need a checkout per pair.
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > No study observes hundreds of agents on one repository. 2607.04697: 40.2% of 2,807 repositories and 79.4% of 33,596 PRs had at least one other agent PR open at the same time (53.4% and 95.0% within seven days); 580,913 co-active pairs, of which the top ten repositories hold 91.4%; only 0.50% of pairs mix agents, in 122 repositories. 2606.19616: 32 scripted agents in a simulation. Landing throughput not measured.
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > AgenticFlict: offline replay of production PRs, N = 107,026 from up to 59,412 repositories. 2607.04697: offline replay, N = 716 evaluable pairs of 747 sampled. MergeGym: offline replay (715 complete pairs, 5,936 both-merged pairs) plus simulation of scheduling. SBES 2026: mining of 50,700 production merge commits. 2606.19616: simulation, 2 to 32 scripted agents. All by the authors, no replication by others.
- **Coordination limit:**
  > Not measured in production. 2606.19616 (simulation): at 32 agents, locks without shared state still give 0.64 duplicate work, and lock starvation appears (128 cases in a separate run, against 12 with state). Arithmetic, not a measurement: the number of open pairs grows as k(k-1)/2, that is 435 for 30 agents and 44,850 for 300; at the median of 0.02 s per merge-tree check that is about 9 seconds and 15 minutes of single-core time (the mean is higher: 95th percentile 0.50 s).

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > Not studied. Relevant neighbours: 2601.15195 finds CI or test failure behind 99 rejected agent PRs (17% of 600 sampled, of which 562 were still accessible). Organ (self-reported) names CI of about 11 minutes per run as the reason that rebasing every PR consumed the landing budget.
- **Broken main recovery:** n/a: none of the papers follows main after the merge.
- **Review model:**
  > Conventional PR review in public repositories. 2606.28235: 57.7% of merged agent PRs were merged within six minutes, so a large part of AIDev has no real waiting time in which conflicts could arise. 2601.15195: reviewer abandonment explains 228 of the rejected PRs (38%), more than any technical reason.

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > n/a for the observational papers. grite (2606.19616) is a CLI with a Git-backed log. AgenticFlict, 2607.04697 and MergeGym publish research scripts, not a product interface.
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > n/a: not studied. Warning sign from SBES 2026: 6.0% of conflict chunks were committed with markers, so a check for conflict markers before push is a cheap safeguard.
- **Agent cost:**
  > Not measured: no paper reports tokens, reruns or time spent on conflicts; 2607.04697 only hypothesises CI and token cost. Cost of the remedy: MergeGym replay median 0.02 s per pair; serialising work +167.3% makespan, a predictive gate +65.0% (simulation).
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > Attribution of a PR to an agent is inherited from AIDev's detection rules, which were not re-checked here; attribution is per PR, so human commits inside a PR count with the agent unless a study separates them (SBES 2026 does). 2606.28235 notes that one agent product can run many instances through one account, so the number of agents is undercounted.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > In the observed PRs mostly people: 96.1% of conflicting internal merges are committed by a human (SBES 2026). In the simulation the scripted agents coordinate through the shared log.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Independence verdict: one observational source. AIDev (five agents, public GitHub, December 2024 to July 2025, dominated by Codex) feeds AgenticFlict (its unmerged PRs), 2607.04697 and MergeGym (its curated subset, same authors and the same 747-pair replay), SBES 2026 (full set, internal merges) and 2606.28235 (reuses AgenticFlict labels). The evaluated agents, period and repositories therefore coincide; only unit and filter differ. At PR level some subsets are disjoint by construction: AgenticFlict keeps only unmerged PRs while MergeGym T2 keeps only pairs in which both PRs were merged (inference from the filters). Measured overlap: 9,582 of the 33,596 curated PRs (28.5%) are in AgenticFlict (2606.28235). PR-level overlap of the 747 pairs or of the 12,299 SBES PRs with AgenticFlict is not published. The replays are separate measurements on one population, not independent samples; agreement near 30% between AgenticFlict (27.67%) and SBES 2026 (29.5%) is therefore no confirmation, the more so because the units differ. Peer reviewed: AgenticFlict, SBES 2026, AIDev. Preprints: 2607.04697, MergeGym, 2606.28235, 2606.19616. The last is a simulation by the tool's author and says nothing about code conflicts. Outside AIDev: constructed benchmarks that are covered in other items, 2609.25396 (Django PR pairs, semantic) and 2611.00314 (two agents with contradictory or complementary tasks; not a rate of production PRs), and the Organ anecdote. Main weakness: unmerged PRs only in the headline figure, textual conflicts only, no controls, 2025 data.
- **Independent reports:**
  > Cross-criticism inside the group: MergeGym (same authors) withdraws the cross-agent reading of 2607.04697; 2607.04697 describes AgenticFlict as covering every agent PR, which is not so (unmerged only). 2606.28235 uses AgenticFlict labels and finds no difference between repositories with several agents and with one (30.8% against 31.2%). Rejection studies put conflicts far lower as a stated reason: 2509.14745 finds one such case among rejected Claude Code PRs (1.1%), and 2601.15195 has no conflict category at all, while duplicate PRs are 142 of 600 rejections (23%). Field report: Organ (dev.to, 2026-10-13, posted under an AI 'CMO agent', self-reported): 61% of 62 open PRs in conflict, 8 of 8 approved PRs already conflicted; merges per week went from 68 to 112 after changes but the backlog did not shrink. Synthetic benchmark outside AIDev: 2611.00314 reports that in 58% of failed contradictory task pairs each agent's half passes its own tests but no combination passes, and 98% of those give a Git merge conflict (Claude Opus 4.8; share of failures, not of PRs). NP-Bench (2610.07261): scope-disjoint planning raised clean integration from 1 to 9 of 9 small synthetic scenarios. Searches for production data from Mergify, Graphite, GitClear, Cursor or Anthropic found none; no independent re-run of AgenticFlict was found.

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > Deciding whether conflicts deserve attention, choosing limits on PR size, designing a pre-merge pair check, and arguing that file-disjoint task splits are not a guarantee.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > Quoting 27.67% as the conflict rate of agent PRs (it excludes merged ones, and unmerged PRs are larger). Counting the four AIDev studies as four confirmations. Averaging PR-against-base, PR-against-PR and internal-merge rates. Quoting 41.7% against 19.8% without p = 0.078. Reading size as cause: uncontrolled, and the curve flattens after about 50 lines. Ranking agents: Codex is 69% of the sample and Claude Code has 779 PRs. Treating file-disjoint scopes as safe: 47.3% of textual conflicts had no shared authored file. Citing grite's numbers as fewer code conflicts. Assuming textual success means correctness. Carrying 2025 open-source numbers over to a private repository with hundreds of agents.
- **Minimum useful scale:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] No study gives a threshold. Judgement: from two agents with overlapping lifetimes a `git merge-tree` check against main and against open branches is worth running, since it costs a fraction of a second; shared done-state is worth having as soon as agents pick tasks themselves.
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Use the findings now (nothing to install): keep PRs small, land quickly, run merge-tree checks per branch and per pair, rebase only on a real conflict, and publish what is in progress and done so that agents do not duplicate work. Skip grite as a tool for now: 25 stars, one author, simulation only. Do not use 27.67% or 41.7% as a forecast; log the own rate for two weeks instead.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Use with caution, as hypotheses: nothing here was measured above 32 simulated agents or on one repository with hundreds of writers. The safest carry-over is structural: pairs grow quadratically, lineage (stale base) predicts conflict better than file overlap, full serialisation is too slow (+167% in simulation) and a predictive gate (91.7% of scope collisions removed at +65.0% makespan, simulation) is the better compromise. Pilot: replay all open pairs with merge-tree, since that is cheap enough, use the predictor only to decide which work to delay, and measure the rate per 100 landings by size, age and path. The 81-83% recall at a one-third replay budget in MergeGym comes from an enriched test set and the paper claims no saving over replaying everything.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > (1) Run `git merge-tree` for each open branch against main and against other open branches; cheap enough to check everything. (2) Limit PR size, with the largest gain below about 25 changed lines (descriptive). (3) Keep branches fresh and keep the merge base available (no shallow fetch that cuts it), because stale lineage predicts conflict best. (4) Use file overlap for planning only, never as proof. (5) Give shared append-only files and manifests an owner or split them (package.json, README.md, shared stylesheet). (6) Keep shared done-state, not only locks. (7) Reject commits that contain conflict markers. (8) Merge when merely behind; rebase only on a real conflict (Organ). (9) Report conflict rates with unit, population and denominator.

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > AgenticFlict, v1 2026-04-04, v2 2026-05-12, https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.03551 ; data, https://zenodo.org/records/20118379 ; code, https://github.com/unlv-evol/AgenticFlict ; Xu, Subramanian, Karthik, v2 2026-07-07, https://arxiv.org/html/2607.04697v2 ; replication, https://github.com/Quantum535/concurrent-agentic-prs-replication ; MergeGym, 2026-10-03, https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04779 ; SBES 2026, September 2026, https://cbsoft.sbc.org.br/2026/data/papers/sbes/How%20AI%20Coding%20Agents%20Resolve%20Merge%20Conflicts%20An%20Empirical%20Study.pdf ; 2606.28235, v2 2026-10-20, https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.28235 ; Before the Pull Request, 2026-06-17, https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19616 ; grite, https://github.com/neul-labs/grite ; AIDev, 2507.15003 and https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.09185 (MSR 2026) ; rejection reasons, https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.15195 (MSR 2026 challenge) and https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14745 ; Passes Alone, Fails Together, 2026-09-21, https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25396 ; NP-Bench, 2026-10-05, https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07261 ; Decentralized Multi-Agent Coordination and Communication in Shared Codebases, November 2026, https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00314 (all read 2026-11-24). Secondary: Organ field report, 2026-10-13, https://dev.to/itaisagi/ai-agent-pull-requests-stuck-in-merge-conflicts-what-broke-in-our-merge-path-and-what-we-changed-4ni9
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > AgenticFlict's table and text give different per-agent rates and its totals differ by 1,493 PRs; the reason (probably another denominator) is an inference. SBES 2026 gives 14,386 human resolutions in the abstract and 14,381 in the results. PR-level overlap between the AIDev studies is published only for AIDev-pop against AgenticFlict (28.5%); the rest could be computed from the public data, which was not downloaded here. Whether older base commits or true edit clashes explain the remaining 31 zero-overlap conflicts is open. Citation counts for 2607.04697 and 2610.04779 were not obtained (rate limit). No evidence on duration as a controlled variable, hot-file concentration, semantic conflicts at scale, human baseline for PRs, or agents newer than July 2025.

**Unverified fields (1):**

- minimum_useful_scale

### Cloudflare Artifacts

*Layer: C Git hosting*

#### Basics

- **Name:** Cloudflare Artifacts
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > Product: https://www.cloudflare.com/products/artifacts/ ; docs: https://developers.cloudflare.com/artifacts/ . The service itself has no public repository. Related open-source parts: cloudflare/artifact-fs (FUSE driver, works with any Git remote) and cloudflare/ci (CI SDK). Namesakes that are unrelated: Claude Artifacts (Anthropic), GitHub Actions and GitLab build artifacts, JFrog Artifactory. Searches for the bare name mostly return those.
- **Maker:**
  > Cloudflare. Launch post by Dillon Mulroy, Matt Carey and Matt Silverlock (2026-04-16); open-beta post by Dina Kozlov, Dillon Mulroy and Zebulon Piasecki (2026-10-01).
- **What it is:**
  > Hosted, versioned storage that speaks the Git smart HTTP protocol and is created and controlled by API. A namespace holds any number of repositories; each repository is one isolated Git service with its own remote URL, read and write tokens, refs and lifecycle. Built on Durable Objects: a Worker front end handles authentication and routing, each repository is a single logical Durable Object instance that stores objects in its SQLite database, and Cloudflare's own Git engine (written in Zig, compiled to about 100 KB of WebAssembly) runs inside it. R2 holds snapshots and KV holds tokens (launch post; the engine has to work within roughly 128 MB of memory per object). Three interfaces reach the same repository: a Workers binding, a REST API and any Git client. The binding and REST create, import, fork, list, delete, mint tokens and read files, trees, commits and logs; commits and ref updates go through the Git protocol. The intended pattern is one repository per agent, session or task, forked from a reviewed baseline. It is a storage primitive: Cloudflare says the coordination, review and merge layer is left for others to build, and held a competition for exactly that layer.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > Addresses: workspace cost on the server side (cheap programmatic repositories and forks), throughput across many repositories, isolation and per-agent credentials. Partly addresses: clone time for large repositories through ArtifactFS. Does not address: landing contention on one main, textual conflicts, semantic conflicts, task collision. Forking each agent's work only moves the conflict to the moment somebody merges the forks, and Artifacts documents no merge operation.
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > Isolate then merge, taken to one repository (fork) per agent; the merge itself is outside the product.

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > Open beta since 2026-10-01 (confirmed in Cloudflare's changelog), for any account on the Workers Paid plan; not available on Workers Free. Private beta 2026-04-16; the public beta had been announced for early May and slipped about five months. No GA date found. A third-party tutorial updated 2026-11-17 still says access must be enabled on the account; the official docs do not say so.
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > Proprietary SaaS on Cloudflare; no self-hosted version. A local simulator (Wrangler 4.163.0 or later, Git 2.32 or later, announced 2026-11-16) is for development only and does not sync with deployed repositories. Data can be pinned to the EU or US per namespace at creation (2026-08-13). cloudflare/artifact-fs and cloudflare/ci are Apache-2.0.
- **Price:**
  > As of 2026-11-24 (pricing page last modified 2026-11-19): needs Workers Paid (minimum USD 5 per month per account). Operations: 10,000 per month included, then USD 0.15 per 1,000; a push, pull, clone, fetch, create, fork or delete is 1 operation, 100 content reads count as 1, 5 token operations count as 1. Storage: 1 GB-month included, then USD 0.50 per GB-month. Announced but not active: USD 0.01 per repository above 100,000. Billing start has moved three times: 2026-10-14 (changelog; the blog post of the same day says 10-15), then 2026-11-12 (docs change 2026-10-28), then 2026-11-19 (docs change 2026-11-16). The page still uses the future tense, so whether billing has actually begun is not confirmed. The brief's mid-October date is out of date. Arithmetic here, not a quote: 300 agents that each push and fetch once every 5 minutes, 8 hours a day, 22 days, make about 1.27 million operations, about USD 189 per month plus storage.
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > About 7 months since private beta, under 2 months of open beta. Steady releases in 2026: event subscriptions and Wrangler commands (May), dashboard management (June), CI on push (August 4), data localisation (August 13), 1 GB repository limit (September 29), Workers Builds integration (October 1), push limits documented (October 28), local development (November 16). Docs still drift from production: an open, unmerged docs pull request of 2026-11-10 (cloudflare-docs 35104) lists token format, error codes and features missing from the docs. artifact-fs: Go, 1,228 stars, created 2026-03-29, last push 2026-11-13, latest release 1.0.0-rc.10 (2026-08-12), README calls it beta. cloudflare/ci: 620 stars, created 2026-07-30, v0.2.0 (2026-09-14) (GitHub API, 2026-11-24). Known users: Cloudflare's own internal agents (self-reported); no named external customer found. A tutorial author relays a Cloudflare employee's figure of nearly 50,000 new repositories per day (secondhand vendor claim, not verified).
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > Stated by Cloudflare: synchronous replication across several data centres, plus asynchronous copies to object storage and snapshots. No SLA, recovery objective or independent durability test found for the beta. One concrete defect report: a user (artifact-fs issue 49, 2026-08-31, still open) says a repository seeded by a shallow (depth 1) push stays shallow for ever, that later pushes of the missing ancestry succeed but the objects are absent afterwards, and that no client-side action repairs it; repositories seeded with full history are said to behave correctly. No outage or data-loss incident specific to Artifacts found. Vendor continuity risk is low; beta terms, price and limits have already changed (per-repository limit cut from 10 GB to 1 GB for new repositories, billing date moved, a per-repository charge announced).
- **OS support:**
  > Server: n/a, any operating system with a Git client works over HTTPS, including native Windows. ArtifactFS: Linux (fuse3) and macOS (macFUSE) only, needs Go 1.26 or later; no Windows build documented (WSL should count as Linux, not tested here).

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > Standard Git client kept, over smart HTTP only. Clone and fetch support protocol v1 and v2, including shallow and deepen; push uses v1 receive-pack and v2 push is not supported. The docs list v1 `filter` (partial clone) and `include-tag` as unsupported; whether `filter` works over v2 is not stated, while the ArtifactFS guide describes a blobless clone of an Artifacts remote. SHA-1 object IDs. Git notes supported. Documented limits: 1 GB per repository created on or after 2026-09-29 (10 GB before), 64 MiB and 50,000 objects per push, 32 MiB per file, 100,000 entries per folder. SSH and LFS are mentioned only as absent from the local simulator; hosted support is not documented on the pages read.
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > Replaces GitHub storage rather than plugging into it. Import takes a public HTTPS remote (a one-time copy of one branch, optionally shallow); continuous mirroring in either direction is not documented, and importing a private repository with credentials is not documented. No pull requests, issues, branch protection or required checks. CI: push events (`cf.artifacts.repo.pushed`) can start a Cloudflare Workflow that runs the @cloudflare/ci SDK in sandboxes, per repository or for a whole namespace; Workers Builds deploys the `main` branch (the only production branch supported) and builds previews for other branches. GitHub Actions and other existing CI need a custom bridge through the event queue.
- **Migration and exit:**
  > Entry: a normal `git push --mirror` or the import API, if the repository fits under 1 GB and every file under 32 MiB; large repositories or binaries rule it out. A full-history import is safer than a shallow one (see the shallow-boundary report). Partial adoption is the natural way: keep GitHub as the source of truth and use Artifacts only for scratch forks and session history, with your own bridge that pushes selected branches back. Exit: `git clone --mirror` of each repository; what is lost is tokens, event subscriptions, the Workflows CI definition and anything built on the Workers binding. Git notes sit on separate refs and must be fetched explicitly. Repositories stay stored and billed until deleted.

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > Not provided. Agents commit in their own clone and push to their own fork or branch. To reach main, something else has to fetch the fork, merge or rebase it in a Git client (sandbox, container, CI job, or isomorphic-git in a Worker for small repositories) and push to the baseline. The REST API and the Workers binding are read-only for content: the published reference has no merge, pull request, commit-write, ref-update or diff call (docs read 2026-11-24; a docs change describing a binding method `repo.diff` was drafted 2026-11-02 and closed without merging). The April launch post says the REST and Workers APIs create commits, and the product page says commit, diff and search can be done programmatically; the current API reference does not contain them. Cloudflare's guidance is explicit that branches in one repository are for collaborators who share a lifecycle and that one shared repository should not serve as a queue for many autonomous agents. A third-party skill file (tpscloud/flared on the Tessl registry) recommends a trusted integrator or a queue that serialises merges per target repository.
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > At push, at reference level, by ordinary Git rules: a push that is not a fast-forward is refused (stated by the third-party skill file and expected from Git; Cloudflare's own pages do not describe concurrent pushes). Between forks there is no detection at all until somebody merges them. File, hunk and semantic conflicts appear in the integrator's Git client or in CI.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > Left to the integrator or the agent: Git's own conflict markers in a local clone. No structured conflict payload, no server-side replay and no conflict-as-data. An unmerged docs change (cloudflare-docs 35104) says production also answers 503 with Retry-After when a repository is busy with another write.
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > A fork diverges from the baseline at once and is not updated; there is no upstream-sync call in the published API, so an agent that needs newer code must add the baseline as a second remote, fetch and rebase with a read token. Cost per retry: one fetch plus one push (2 billed operations) and the agent's own rebase work. No retry figures published.
- **Pre write coordination:**
  > None built in. Event subscriptions (push, clone, fetch, fork, create, delete, token events through Queues) could feed a claim or notification service that you write; Durable Objects would be the natural place for it. The push event carries ref, before, after and commit messages, not changed paths.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > No evidence and no claim with numbers. Cloudflare says one repository per agent avoids a hot spot for conflicts and accidental overwrites; that is design advice, true while the work stays apart, and it says nothing about merging the results into one repository. No measurement by Cloudflare or anyone else was found.

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > Client: full or shallow clone, or ArtifactFS: a blobless clone mounted through FUSE, where the tree appears at once and file contents are fetched on demand, with writes in a copy-on-write overlay and normal commit and push. Self-reported by Cloudflare: a 2.4 GB framework repository takes close to 2 minutes to clone and the aim is about 10-15 seconds to start; no independent benchmark. ArtifactFS works with GitHub and GitLab remotes too, and partial-clone filtering must be advertised by the remote or Git downloads the blobs eagerly. Server: objects and deltas chunked into the repository's Durable Object SQLite store. Whether a fork shares storage with its parent, and is billed for it, is not documented; the pricing page says only that storage is summed over all repositories and replicas are free.
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > Not demonstrated on one repository. The documented ceiling is a rate limit, not a result: 2,000 Git requests per 10 seconds per repository and 2,000 control-plane requests per 10 seconds per namespace. Repository count is unlimited, 1 TB per account. Landing throughput on one ref: not published. The large numbers in Cloudflare's material (10,000 forks, tens of millions of repositories) count repositories, not writers on one repository. Because a repository is one Durable Object, writes to it are handled by one instance.
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > Vendor design claims and documented limits; one secondhand usage figure (about 50,000 new repositories per day, production, self-reported). No benchmark, N or duration published.
- **Coordination limit:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] No load at which one repository degrades is published. The docs advise against a shared repository for many agents, without a number, and 429-type rejections are exposed as a `rateLimited` metric.

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > Post-push, not pre-merge: push events start a CI Workflow with parallel sandboxed steps, dependency caching keyed on lockfiles and retries; a failed step stops deployment but does not stop the commit from being on the branch. No required checks, batching, speculation, merge queue or affected-target selection. A gate is possible only if your integrator waits for CI on the fork before it pushes to the baseline.
- **Broken main recovery:**
  > None built in: no bisect, automatic revert or known-good pointer. A failing CI Workflow or Workers Build blocks deployment, so the last deployed Worker keeps serving; the branch itself stays broken until someone reverts it.
- **Review model:**
  > None built in. Cloudflare shows push events starting a review agent that you write. Read-only tokens and read-only repositories support reviewers that must not push.

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > Git CLI with a bearer token in http.extraHeader; REST API with JSON envelopes and numeric error codes; Workers binding with TypeScript types; Wrangler commands for namespaces, repositories and tokens; GraphQL analytics with operation counts, errors and latency percentiles. All non-interactive. The docs offer a setup prompt for Claude, Codex, Cursor and OpenCode, and Markdown versions of every page. No Artifacts-specific MCP server was found on the pages read. SDKs for TypeScript, Go and Python were announced in April; none is listed in the current docs index.
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > Ordinary Git on the client (revert, reset, reflog) plus cheap isolation: throw the fork away and fork again. No documented server-side reflog, snapshot restore or undelete; the delete call is permanent as far as the docs say.
- **Agent cost:**
  > No extra tokens for learning a tool, because agents use plain Git. Setup per agent: one fork call, one token, one clone (3 billable actions, the token at one fifth). The merge work that the product leaves out is the real cost, and it falls on the integrator. No measured figures published.
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > Strong at repository level: each token belongs to one repository, is read or write, and lives from 60 seconds to 1 year; docs advise short-lived tokens per session, and token creation and revocation emit events. Because each agent has its own fork, the token identifies the agent. No per-folder or per-branch permission inside a repository and no branch protection documented. Provenance: Git notes are recommended for prompts, model output and run IDs; they are conventions, not signed or enforced, and commit author fields are whatever the client sets.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > The server only stores and orders ref updates. The agent pushes to its fork; an integrator, orchestrator or human that you provide fetches, merges, tests and pushes to main.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Vendor documentation and blog posts; detailed on API and limits, silent on concurrency. Main weakness: no benchmark, no named customer and nothing about many writers on one repository.
- **Independent reports:**
  > Thin. Hacker News 2026-04-16 (217 points, 26 comments, at private beta): doubts about who needs it over GitHub branches and worktrees (gavinray, 2026-04-17), operation price against S3 (tln), missing list, commit and merge calls (tln; a Cloudflare engineer answered that more was coming), concern over the 128 MB object memory during delta resolution (nulltrace), and praise for lazy hydration. artifact-fs issue 49 (2026-08-31): permanent shallow boundary, the only concrete server-side failure report found. artifact-fs issues 65 (2026-11-16, FUSE, git status and refresh bugs with failing tests) and 48 (2026-08-25, stale mount): driver defects, open on 2026-11-24. Flavio Copes, tutorial updated 2026-11-17: positive walk-through, not a test at scale. Forkast, 2026-10-07: written by an AI reporter from Cloudflare's announcements, no testing. tpscloud/flared skill: a practitioner's list of what the API lacks. No independent benchmark or production report found up to 2026-11-24; no announced winner of Cloudflare's Git platform competition found either.

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > Platforms that need thousands of small, separate repositories (one per user project, session or sandbox), teams already on Workers, and durable per-session history for agents.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > Wrong shape for the scenario: many agents on one repository is what the docs tell you not to do. Merge debt: every fork has to be integrated by a component you build. 1 GB repository, 32 MiB file and 64 MiB push limits. No branch protection, so one leaked write token can rewrite main of that repository. Shallow-seeded repositories reported as unrepairable. FUSE driver is a release candidate with open consistency bugs and no Windows support. Beta: price, limits and docs still move. Lock-in is in the Workers code around it rather than in the Git data. Marketing text describes API commits and diffs (the April launch post only commits) that the current reference does not contain.
- **Minimum useful scale:**
  > Judgement, nothing published: useful when you need hundreds or thousands of separate repositories or GitHub repository-creation limits hurt. For one repository with 5-30 agents, worktrees plus a merge queue already cover what it offers.
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Skip as the main repository; optionally pilot as a scratch and session-history store. It adds a second remote and an integrator to write, has no pull request, queue or protection, and does nothing about conflicts on main. ArtifactFS is worth a separate try on Linux or macOS if clones of a large repository are slow, because it works with GitHub remotes.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Wait, or use it only as a building block. The request limits look sufficient on paper (300 agents pushing and fetching every 5 minutes is about 2 Git operations per second against a ceiling of 200 requests per second per repository; arithmetic here, each operation is several requests), and forks with per-agent tokens are a good isolation layer. But the landing layer, gating and review would be your own software, the repository must stay under 1 GB, and no one has shown many writers on one repository. Choose it only if you intend to build a forge.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > Give each agent its own fork and a short-lived token that cannot push to the baseline; let a single trusted integrator own that right. Fork from a reviewed, pinned baseline. Use names built from stable agent and session IDs. Keep prompts and run IDs in Git notes and remember to push refs/notes. Trigger CI and review agents from push events instead of polling. Persist each session's file state in Git separately from the real repository, so that work can be resumed or handed over. Use blobless clones or a lazy mount for agent start-up on any Git host. Seed repositories with full history, not a shallow push.

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > Cloudflare changelog, Artifacts open beta, 2026-10-01, https://developers.cloudflare.com/changelog/post/2026-10-01-artifacts-open-beta/ ; private beta, 2026-04-16, https://developers.cloudflare.com/changelog/post/2026-04-16-artifacts-now-in-beta/ ; 1 GB limit, 2026-09-29, https://developers.cloudflare.com/changelog/post/2026-09-29-repository-size-limit/ ; all entries, https://developers.cloudflare.com/changelog/product/artifacts/ ; Cloudflare Blog, launch post, 2026-04-16, https://blog.cloudflare.com/artifacts-git-for-agents-beta/ ; open beta and competition, 2026-10-01, https://blog.cloudflare.com/next-git-platform-on-cloudflare/ ; Docs read 2026-11-24: pricing (modified 2026-11-19) https://developers.cloudflare.com/artifacts/platform/pricing/ ; limits (modified 2026-10-28) https://developers.cloudflare.com/artifacts/platform/limits/ ; how it works https://developers.cloudflare.com/artifacts/concepts/how-artifacts-works/ ; best practices https://developers.cloudflare.com/artifacts/concepts/best-practices/ ; Git protocol https://developers.cloudflare.com/artifacts/api/git-protocol/ ; Workers binding https://developers.cloudflare.com/artifacts/api/workers-binding/ ; REST API https://developers.cloudflare.com/artifacts/api/rest-api/ ; events https://developers.cloudflare.com/artifacts/guides/event-subscriptions/ ; build on push https://developers.cloudflare.com/artifacts/guides/build-and-deploy-on-push/ ; ArtifactFS https://developers.cloudflare.com/artifacts/guides/artifact-fs/ ; local development https://developers.cloudflare.com/artifacts/guides/local-development/ ; Workers Builds https://developers.cloudflare.com/workers/ci-cd/builds/git-integration/artifacts-integration/ ; Workers pricing https://developers.cloudflare.com/workers/platform/pricing/ ; Docs history: billing date to 2026-11-12 (2026-10-28) https://github.com/cloudflare/cloudflare-docs/pull/34695 ; to 2026-11-19 plus repository price (2026-11-16) https://github.com/cloudflare/cloudflare-docs/pull/35273 ; unmerged corrections (2026-11-10) https://github.com/cloudflare/cloudflare-docs/pull/35104 ; closed diff method (2026-11-02) https://github.com/cloudflare/cloudflare-docs/pull/34863 ; Repositories: https://github.com/cloudflare/artifact-fs (issue 49: https://github.com/cloudflare/artifact-fs/issues/49 ), https://github.com/cloudflare/ci . Secondary: Hacker News 2026-04-16 https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=47792374 ; Flavio Copes, updated 2026-11-17, https://flaviocopes.com/cloudflare-artifacts/ ; tpscloud/flared skill https://tessl.io/registry/tpscloud/flared/files/skills/cloudflare-artifacts/references/patterns.md ; Forkast 2026-10-07 https://forkast.news/cloudflare-ships-git-for-agents-artifacts-turns-version-control-into-infrastructure/
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > Not confirmed: whether billing began on 2026-11-19; how concurrent pushes to one ref are handled and at what rate; whether forks share or duplicate billed storage; partial-clone `filter` over protocol v2; hosted SSH and LFS support; import of private repositories; continuous mirroring; undelete; an MCP server. Sources disagree: billing date (changelog 10-14, blog 10-15, docs 11-12 then 11-19); API commits (product page and April post yes, API reference no) and diff (product page yes, API reference no); account enablement (tutorial yes, docs no). The research brief's open beta date and 1 GB limit are confirmed, its mid-October billing date is superseded. Nothing was run or measured for this note.

**Unverified fields (1):**

- coordination_limit

### Coordination and merge-resolution benchmarks

*Layer: H evidence*

#### Basics

- **Name:** Coordination and merge-resolution benchmarks
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > CooperBench: arXiv 2601.13295 (v2 2026-01-26), cooperbench/CooperBench, https://cooperbench.com ; ParallACT: arXiv 2611.00314 (v1 2026-10-30), rm206/ParallACT_data_and_code; Passes Alone, Fails Together (the benchmark is called STALE): arXiv 2609.25396 (v1 2026-09-21), DOI 10.1145/3842650.3843171, illinoisdata/STALE-bench; SWE-Git-Bench: NeurIPS 2026 Evaluations and Datasets Track poster 139812, OpenReview 37pMYoL9Iz, zwpride/SWE-Git-Bench, data doi:10.7910/DVN/FSFEZX; Merge-Bench: arXiv 2605.25890 (2026-05-25), benedikt-schesch/Merge-Bench. Namesakes: uiuctml/MergeBench (merging of language models), MergeMedBench and Bench4Merge are unrelated to Merge-Bench; SWE-Git-Bench is a different thing from GitGoodBench (arXiv 2505.22583) and from SWE-Bench.
- **Maker:**
  > CooperBench: Stanford University and SAP Labs US (Khatua, Zhu and others, Diyi Yang); ParallACT: UT Austin and NVIDIA (Mediratta, Xiao, Stone, Biswas, Stengel-Eskin); STALE: UIUC and Columbia (Xia, Wu, Park); SWE-Git-Bench: Beihang University, IQuest Research and University of Manchester (Wei Zhang, Jian Yang, Jiajun Wu, Zidan Tang, Siwei Wu); Merge-Bench: Benedikt Schesch (Amazon) and Michael Ernst (University of Washington).
- **What it is:**
  > Five 2026 papers that measure two different things. Coordination (do agents that work at the same time produce a correct whole): CooperBench: 652 tasks from 12 open-source libraries in Python, TypeScript, Go and Rust; two agents in separate containers each build one feature, may message each other, the patches are merged and both features' tests must pass; 77.3% of tasks have conflicting human solutions, because pairs are drawn from overlapping features. ParallACT: 155 instances (85 contradictory, 70 complementary) built from SWE-Bench Pro tasks in 11 repositories; two agents each receive an independently motivated requirement (audited so that the pair does not read as two halves of one decomposed task), and the two either contradict or complement each other at a shared point, and up to 19 scenarios are compared, from one agent doing both to synchronised message channels. STALE: pairs of changes where each passes its own tests and the textual merge is clean, checked on real reviewed pull requests and on constructed pairs. Merge resolution (can a model resolve a conflict that already exists): SWE-Git-Bench: 135 real conflicts over 185 files in 23 repositories, answer compared with the developer's merged file; Merge-Bench: 7,938 conflict hunks from 1,439 repositories in 11 languages, answer compared with the developer's resolution. Only the first three run tests; the two resolution benchmarks compare text with the maintainer's merge.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > CooperBench: textual conflicts, semantic conflicts and task collision, in pairs; ParallACT: textual and semantic conflicts and the value of hand-off, messages and shared state; STALE: semantic conflicts behind a clean merge; SWE-Git-Bench and Merge-Bench: resolution of textual conflicts. Not addressed by any: throughput, landing contention on one main, workspace cost, more than four agents, long-running work, and how often conflicts occur in production.
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > n/a as tools; what they test: CooperBench: isolate then merge, with messaging; ParallACT: isolate then merge against sequential hand-off and shared state; STALE: isolate then merge with a message about the other change; SWE-Git-Bench and Merge-Bench: after-the-fact conflict resolution.

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > Papers and data are public. CooperBench: usable package and dataset, v0.0.29 (2026-08-14); ParallACT: code and data posted 2026-10-27; STALE: code since 2026-06-06; SWE-Git-Bench: data released 2026-10-19, poster scheduled 2026-12-10, the OpenReview PDF refused download (HTTP 403) so only the abstract, reviews, rebuttal and dataset record were read; Merge-Bench: data and code since 2025-07-12, preprint 2026-05-25.
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > Self-run benchmarks. CooperBench: README badge says MIT, GitHub detects no licence file; ParallACT: no licence stated; STALE: no licence stated; SWE-Git-Bench: CC BY 4.0 for data and, per README, the repository; Merge-Bench: MIT.
- **Price:**
  > Free data and code; the cost is model calls. Reported by the authors or by the one replication: ParallACT (Opus 4.8): parallel conditions cost 1.14-1.63 times, Redis-synchronised 1.39-2.23 and the communication oracle 1.31-2.74 times the effective tokens of one agent doing both requirements; dev.to replication of CooperBench (gpt-5.4, independent): about USD 3.60 solo, 7.10 sequential and 9.73 integrator per run.
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > All first published in 2026, all small communities (GitHub API, 2026-11-24). CooperBench: 38 stars, created 2026-01-18, last push 2026-11-20, active; ICLR 2026 workshop poster, COLM 2026 acceptance seen only on a third-party page. ParallACT: 6 stars, last push 2026-11-03, preprint. STALE: 0 stars, last push 2026-08-11, 6-page EXPRESS '26 workshop paper. SWE-Git-Bench: 1 star, last push 2026-10-08, accepted as a NeurIPS 2026 poster. Merge-Bench: 5 stars, last push 2026-11-21, preprint; the ICPR 2026 venue is inferred from a file name.
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > Research artefacts with no support promise. CooperBench is the only one with releases and a public leaderboard; its changelog of 2026-08 admits that 24 of 199 features could not be graded properly, so scores before and after v0.0.29 should not be compared. Model results age fast: Merge-Bench and the CooperBench paper tested 2025 models.
- **OS support:**
  > n/a: benchmark harnesses; CooperBench and STALE run containers (Docker), which means Linux, macOS or WSL. Not tested here.

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > All use plain Git patches and merges. CooperBench merges by naive git merge, union merge or a small trained resolver; ParallACT accepts concatenation, git merge or the CooperBench resolver; SWE-Git-Bench and Merge-Bench take conflicts produced by Git merge commits.
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > n/a: benchmarks; tasks come from GitHub repositories and pull requests but nothing is meant to run in a project's CI.
- **Migration and exit:** n/a: nothing to adopt; the reusable part is the method (see transferable_practice).

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > n/a as a product. What each assumes about landing: CooperBench: both agents finish, an external step merges the two patches once and runs both test suites; no rebase, retry or queue. Its later ablation (2026-05) uses another rule, naive merge and then fall back to the lead agent's patch alone. ParallACT: both finish and an attempt passes if any of three merge strategies works; in the sequential conditions the second agent starts from the first agent's result, which is the closest to a serialised landing queue. STALE: textual merge of two independent patches, then the combined tests. SWE-Git-Bench and Merge-Bench: only the moment of resolution inside one merge. None models the situation of the scenario, a main branch that moves many times per hour while hundreds of agents rebase and retry.
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > CooperBench and ParallACT: after both agents finish, textual (git merge) and by tests; STALE: after a clean textual merge, by running combined tests, at function level; SWE-Git-Bench and Merge-Bench: given textual conflict hunks or files. None tests detection before or during writing.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > How well models resolve conflicts that exist. All figures are authors' measurements on synthetic benchmarks built from real merges, scored against the developer's own merge, which is a lower bound for correctness because a different answer can also be right.
