Merge-Bench (all languages, match / different code / conflict left in place): Gemini 2.5 Pro 52.6/42.1/5.3%; o3 Pro 45.1/40.9/14.1; Claude Opus 4 44.8/34.5/20.4; R1-0528 36.5/26.3/36.9; Grok 4 31.7/20.9/47.3; Qwen3 235B 30.6/32.0/37.3. Java only: Gemini 2.5 Pro 62.5%, fine-tuned 14B LLMergeJ 58.9, o3 Pro 54.3, Opus 4 51.2. Hunks are at most 20 lines, and the authors say leaving out longer ones may make the results an over-estimate. They note that if a wrong resolution costs twice what a right one saves, every model is net negative. SWE-Git-Bench (whole-file exact match, 36 endpoints, April 2026): best single model 26.5%; 41.6% if the best of all models could be picked per file; a Claude Code agent 31.0% whole file and 37.0% conflict region (authors' rebuttal). Prompting with only the conflict block raises text similarity by 19.2 points and exact match by -0.1. In a hand audit of 80 outputs that were at least 99% similar to the truth, 40 were judged likely unsafe. CooperBench: end-to-end pass rate by merge method (Table 6), average without messages 8.64% naive, 16.87% union, 17.64% model resolver; with messages 10.71, 15.97, 17.14 (pass rate of the pair, not resolution rate). ParallACT: with Opus 4.8, unaware agents on contradictory instances pass 10% by concatenation, 14% by git merge, 16% by a reconciler; open-weight models 9% git, 19% reconciler; for the open-weight models the best parallel condition with reconciliation reaches 28% on contradictory instances against 60% for a single hand-off; with Opus the model reconciler lifts Redis-synchronised runs from 22 to 32% (not significant).
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > Not measured directly. ParallACT's sequential and re-entrant conditions are the nearest: the second agent works on the first agent's result, and this recovers most of the loss (54 and 77% against 62 and 86% for one agent doing both, Opus). But 23% of contradictory and 5% of complementary hand-offs overwrite the first agent's work; with the edit history visible, overwrites rose from 15 to 30% of hand-offs. Retry cost under a moving main: not measured by any of the five.
- **Pre write coordination:**
  > All figures here are authors' own measurements on synthetic benchmarks, except the dev.to replication, which is independent but small. Messaging: CooperBench agents spend up to 20% of their steps on messages; messaging clearly lowers textual conflicts but does not change success significantly. A plan in the first message nearly halves the conflict rate (29.4% against 51.5%). ParallACT, Opus, joint pass rate on contradictory / complementary instances: unaware 16/57%, aware that a peer exists 26/60 (not significant), free messages 20/63, synchronised messages 22/71, Redis-synchronised 32/77; given the partner's requirement word for word (communication oracle) 56/83. Open-weight mean: unaware 19/53, aware 19/58, messages 24/66, synchronised 27/64, communication oracle 48/71. About a third of messages are never read and only 21-23% are read before the first edit. In the oracle runs 85% of contradictory conversations assign an owner to the contested code and textual conflicts fall from 67% to 18% of runs (main text; 65 to 15% in the appendix); runs with an owner pass at 61%, without one at 29% (association, not a controlled comparison). STALE: a perfect message about the other change, of about 130 tokens, cuts mean interference from 2.50 to 0.04 on the synthetic set and recovers 89 of 108 constructed cases; whether agents can write such messages themselves was not tested. Ownership (dev.to replication): enforced file ownership reduced conflicts to 0 of 19 but success rose only to 0-14%, because failures became semantic. Locks and expiring claims: tested by none of the five.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > All figures in this field are authors' own measurements on synthetic or mined benchmarks unless marked independent; none is production. How often semantic interference happens, by source. None of the five measures its prevalence in real parallel agent work, and they disagree because they answer different questions. (1) STALE mined tier (real): 685 Django pull requests gave 4,262 candidate pairs sharing a file, 417 valid after pre-filtering (in a representative batch 36% were dropped for textual conflict, 33% for an invalid base, 17% for not applying, 14% accepted), plus SymPy 11, xarray 12, seaborn 7 and 23 disjoint control pairs. Across 834 agent runs on the 417 Django pairs 1 showed interference (one failing test): 0.12%, about 0.67% as a 95% upper bound (arithmetic here). Both figures assume all 834 runs were eligible; only observable pairs are, so the bound is optimistic. On the most strongly coupled mined pairs GPT-5.5 showed none in 72 runs; the authors say development and review had probably already made the patches compatible. These are reviewed, merged pull requests pre-filtered to merge cleanly, so this is a floor, not an estimate for agents. A pair counts only when each patch passes its own tests on the combined suite; how many runs that left is not reported. (2) STALE constructed tier: 12 real Django helpers, 3 mechanisms, 3 trials: 105 of 108 (97%) interfere although the text merges cleanly; a synthetic tier shows the same for renames and required arguments. These are designed to interfere, so they show the mechanism, not the rate. (3) CooperBench: 652 tasks built from potentially conflicting features, 77.3% with conflicting human solutions: agents lose about half of their solo success in pairs (Claude Sonnet 4.5 47.1 to 25.9%, GPT-5 48.31 to 27.95%, pooled 31.9 to 17.1% over 3,260 trials each, arithmetic here). Conflicts in the human solutions are a design choice, so this is not a conflict rate. (4) ParallACT: Opus 4.8 on 50 contradictory and 35 complementary instances (the 85 instances run with Opus): unaware parallel 16% and 57%, against 62% and 86% for one agent doing both; 58% of contradictory failures are integration failures and 98% of those show a textual conflict, while 53% of complementary failures merge cleanly and the two changes diverge in names, signatures or data shape. (5) Evidence outside this item: a study of 33,596 agent pull requests (arXiv 2607.04697, covered by another item) found textual conflicts in 19.8% of replayed co-active pairs from the same agent and 41.7% across different agents (one pair per repository, 716 of 747 evaluable), and left build and semantic conflicts unmeasured. What lowers loss, by measured size: one agent or a sequential hand-off instead of parallel writing (ParallACT 54/77% sequential against 16/57 unaware; dev.to replication 35 against 7%); knowing the other change or requirement in full (STALE oracle message; ParallACT communication oracle 56/83); assigning an owner to contested code (ParallACT oracle runs: 61% pass with an owner against 29% without, an association). More agents lower success (CooperBench, 46 tasks in 3 sets: 68.6% with 2 agents, 46.5% with 3, 30.0% with 4).

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:** n/a: benchmarks; CooperBench and ParallACT give each agent its own container or clone.
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > Four agents, in CooperBench (46 tasks), where success falls to 30%. ParallACT and STALE use two agents; the resolution benchmarks use one model on an existing conflict. No result for 5-30 agents, let alone hundreds.
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > Synthetic benchmarks by the authors, with agents on reconstructed or mined tasks; STALE's mined tier uses real pull requests; no production measurement. N: CooperBench 652 tasks and 3,260 runs per condition pooled; ParallACT 155 instances, 19 scenarios, 7 model configurations; STALE 834 mined and 108 constructed runs; SWE-Git-Bench 135 conflicts and 36 endpoints; Merge-Bench 7,938 hunks. Independent replication: one blog post with 19 pairs.
- **Coordination limit:**
  > CooperBench: success falls from 68.6% (2 agents) to 46.5% (3) and 30.0% (4), so about a third of the remaining success is lost per added agent on 46 tasks (small experiment, self-reported); the authors blame coordination overhead, meaning more context to track and more chances for inconsistent plans. The other four papers use at most two parties (the resolution benchmarks use none). Extrapolating to 30 or 300 agents is not supported.

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > n/a as a feature. The studies imply that tests of the individual change are not enough: in STALE every interfering patch passed its own tests, and the remedy is a run of the combined tests on the merged tree.
- **Broken main recovery:** n/a: not covered by any of the five.
- **Review model:**
  > n/a: no review is modelled. STALE's mined pairs had passed human review, which is one reason interference is rare there.

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > n/a: harnesses; agents used: CooperBench: OpenHands, Mini-SWE-Agent, Codex, Claude Code and others; ParallACT: Claude Code and Codex; STALE: mini-swe-agent and OpenHands; SWE-Git-Bench: 36 model endpoints plus a Claude Code baseline.
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > n/a: not covered; CooperBench's blog notes force pushes and overwrites when agents were given shared git access.
- **Agent cost:**
  > ParallACT (Opus 4.8), effective tokens relative to one agent doing both requirements: sequential 1.18 (contradictory) and 1.12 (complementary); unaware parallel 1.17 and 1.14; messages 1.42 and 1.43; synchronised messages 1.63 and 1.43; re-entrant 1.88 and 1.49; synchronised through Redis 2.23 and 1.39; communication oracle 2.74 and 1.31. CooperBench: up to 20% of steps spent on messages. Replication: integrator about 2.7 times the solo cost (USD 9.73 against 3.60 per run). CooperBench and ParallACT figures are the authors' own; the replication is one blogger's.
- **Access scope and provenance:** n/a: not covered; the benchmarks identify agents by container only.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > CooperBench: agents message each other and the evaluation harness merges; ParallACT: an orchestrator, the agents, or a synchronisation layer depending on condition; STALE, SWE-Git-Bench and Merge-Bench: the harness or one model at resolution time. Nothing is left to a server or a queue.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Mixed: SWE-Git-Bench accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (poster) but read only through abstract, reviews and rebuttal; STALE a peer-reviewed 6-page workshop paper; CooperBench an ICLR 2026 workshop poster with a preprint; ParallACT and Merge-Bench preprints. All self-reported by the authors. Main weaknesses: two agents only, scoring by tests or by text match with one reference, no prevalence measurement, author-run models; CooperBench had a grading defect (24 of 199 features not gradeable, found in August 2026) and inconsistent headline numbers; SWE-Git-Bench reviewers called it small, single-reference and SWE-bench-derived (ratings 5, 4 and 4) and the rebuttal says contamination is not excluded and exact match is not semantic correctness.
- **Independent reports:**
  > Javier Aguilar (dev.to, posted 12 Jul, edited 20 Oct, presumably 2026) replicated CooperBench on 19 pairs from 5 repositories with gpt-5.4-mini and gpt-5.4 (1-3 seeds): solo 14% and 30%; coop 7% and 7%; coop with fair conflict resolution 7% and 9%; team modes 0%; sequential 26% and 35%; integrator 23% and 33%; file ownership 0% and 14%. 14-15 of the 19 coop failures were plain merge conflicts, roughly half of 29 hand-triaged conflicts were not real logic collisions, and enforced ownership moved the failures to semantic ones. Single author, small sample. Stanford HAI news 2026-06-01 on CooperBench is institutional publicity. Hacker News posts of January 2026 had 1 point each. CooperBench's own later reports (team_harness_ablation 2026-05-19, BENCHMARK_RESULTS 2026-05-21) are by the same authors and are not independent: coop with messages 26% (13/50), solo 48% (24/50), coop with git 56% (28/50), team with all features 62% (31/50), without protocol 70%, without task list 40%, without scratchpad 30%, all n 50, one seed, gpt-5.5, flash subset. No independent replication of ParallACT, STALE, SWE-Git-Bench or Merge-Bench was found up to 2026-11-24.

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > Reading for a decision on whether agents should share work at all (CooperBench, ParallACT), on whether a clean merge can be trusted (STALE), and on how much a merge resolver can be trusted (SWE-Git-Bench, Merge-Bench).
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > Misreading: quoting 97% or 'half of success lost' as a rate for real projects, or 1 in 834 as proof that semantic conflicts are rare for agents. Grading defects and changing rules make CooperBench versions hard to compare. Agents' failure patterns reported: work overlap 33.2% and divergent architecture 29.7% of CooperBench symptoms, with expectation 42%, commitment 32% and communication 26% as causes in 50 traced runs; agents miss about a third of messages (ParallACT); overwriting the partner's work in hand-off (ParallACT 23%); near-identical but unsafe resolutions (SWE-Git-Bench 40 of 80); tests of the single change pass while the merged tree fails (STALE). Over-trusting model merge resolution is the largest practical risk: best exact match is 26-53% depending on benchmark.
- **Minimum useful scale:** n/a: benchmarks; the effects appear with two agents already.
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Use as evidence, not as a tool: at 2-4 agents the measured loss is already large for tasks that overlap, so split work so that agents do not touch the same symbol, run the combined tests on the merged result and hand overlapping work over in sequence. If the merged tree is not tested, STALE suggests individual test passes are not enough. Pilot: run the CooperBench or ParallACT task format on your own repository to get a local rate.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Wait: no study covers more than 4 agents, so nothing here proves or denies that a design works at several hundred. ParallACT's authors call taking turns infeasible with hundreds of agents on one codebase, so the sequential hand-off that recovers most of the loss can only be used for contested symbols, not everywhere. The direction is against uncoordinated parallel writing: loss grows with agent count (CooperBench) and messages help little. Evidence for a server-side queue with merged-tree tests and for pre-write ownership is indirect.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > Test pairs of changes on the merged tree with the union of both test suites, not only each change alone (STALE). Before agents write, publish the final names, signatures and return shapes of changed interfaces to every agent that calls them; STALE's 130-token perfect message removed 98% of interference on its synthetic set. Ask for a short plan with files and line ranges in the first message (CooperBench: conflict rate 29.4% against 51.5%). Assign one owner per contested symbol, or hand off in sequence; sequential beats parallel in ParallACT and in the replication. Make agents re-read messages before the first edit (ParallACT: only 21-23% of messages are read by then). Give the resolver the common ancestor (SWE-Git-Bench: five-model mean exact match 22.0 to 25.3%), tell it to leave the conflict in place when unsure, and run tests afterwards. Do not judge a resolution by text similarity. Measure your own rate: log conflicts that surface after clean merges, and do not import a rate from these papers.

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > CooperBench, arXiv 2601.13295, v2 2026-01-26, https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.13295 ; code https://github.com/cooperbench/CooperBench (changelog v0.0.28-v0.0.29, 2026-08; docs/team_harness_ablation_report.html, 2026-05-19; docs/BENCHMARK_RESULTS.md, 2026-05-21); dataset https://huggingface.co/datasets/CodeConflict/cooperbench-dataset ; ParallACT, arXiv 2611.00314, v1 2026-10-30, https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00314 ; code https://github.com/rm206/ParallACT_data_and_code ; page https://lingyunxiao18.github.io/ParallACT/ ; Passes Alone, Fails Together, arXiv 2609.25396, v1 2026-09-21, https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25396 ; DOI https://doi.org/10.1145/3842650.3843171 ; code https://github.com/illinoisdata/STALE-bench ; SWE-Git-Bench, OpenReview https://openreview.net/forum?id=37pMYoL9Iz ; code https://github.com/zwpride/SWE-Git-Bench ; data https://doi.org/10.7910/DVN/FSFEZX (2026-10-19); Merge-Bench, arXiv 2605.25890, 2026-05-25, https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25890 ; code https://github.com/benedikt-schesch/Merge-Bench ; Independent: dev.to, https://dev.to/javieraguilarai/coding-agents-and-teamwork-social-skills-or-structure-jm6 (posted 12 Jul, edited 20 Oct); Stanford HAI 2026-06-01, https://hai.stanford.edu/news/ai-coding-agents-fail-at-teamwork . Related, not part of this item: arXiv 2607.04697.
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > CooperBench headline loss is stated as 30%, about 50% and 41% in the same paper. Its January scores were not re-run after the August grading fixes. Its resolver is described as 1.5B in the text and 0.5B in the appendix. The COLM 2026 acceptance is from a third-party page only. ParallACT's conflict reduction is 67 to 18% in the main text and 65 to 15% in the appendix; the 85 instances with Opus are 50 contradictory and 35 complementary, so do not write '85 contradictory'. STALE's pre-filtered pairs make 1/834 a floor. SWE-Git-Bench's PDF was not accessible (HTTP 403) and its abstract, reviews and dataset record were used; the title variant 'A Focused Worktree-Level Benchmark' appears in the search index. Merge-Bench's ICPR 2026 venue is inferred from a file name. The post date of the dev.to replication has no year in the page. No prevalence figure for real agents was found in any of the five.

#### Other info

- **Where the papers disagree and why:**
  > Rate: 1 in 834 (STALE, reviewed human pull requests) against 97% (STALE, constructed) and 'about half of success lost' (CooperBench, selected for conflict): different populations, not a contradiction. CooperBench itself: abstract says success falls by 30%, the body says about 50%, the appendix 41%; arithmetic on the pooled table gives 46%. Git access: CooperBench's February 2026 blog finds +1.4 to +2.9 points, not significant; its May 2026 ablation finds coop with git 56% against solo 48% (n 50, one seed, a lead-alone fallback that changes the score); the independent dev.to replication finds team modes at 0%. Messages: CooperBench finds no significant success change; ParallACT finds small gains; STALE finds a near-complete fix, but from a perfect message. ParallACT's related work says STALE finds interference in most tasks; STALE does not say that. Merge resolution: SWE-Git-Bench 26.5% and Merge-Bench 52.6% measure whole files against short hunks.

### ERSC

*Layer: C Git hosting*

#### Basics

- **Name:** ERSC (East River Source Control; first product: ERSC Storage)
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > https://ersc.io/, East River Source Control Inc., New York. A GitHub organisation named east-river-source-control (created 2025-06-02, 0 public repositories, read 2026-11-24) is presumably theirs; ownership not confirmed. No rename found. Not to be confused with jj itself (jj-vcs/jj, a community project that ERSC staff say the company does not own) or with GitButler, whose April 2026 post about raising USD 17M 'to build what comes after Git' has a nearly identical slogan.
- **Maker:**
  > East River Source Control Inc., launched 2025, backed by Amplify Partners (company boilerplate, 2026-09-01). Eleven people on the team page (2026-11-24). Co-founders Benjamin Brittain (CEO) and David Barsky; Steve Klabnik (CPO); Martin von Zweigbergk, creator of jj, as CTO; jj maintainers Austin Seipp and Ilya Grigoriev on staff. The CTO appointment was announced on 2026-09-01, but he had joined almost two months earlier (his Bluesky post, 2026-09-01).
- **What it is:**
  > An enterprise source-control storage server, not a public forge. A normal Git client talks the Git protocol to a 'Git bridge'; behind it there is no Git repository but a custom, horizontally scalable storage engine, plus a GraphQL API. Each customer gets a separate deployment instead of a share of one global platform. Planned next is a second bridge that speaks a documented non-Git protocol to the same engine, with jj as the client; the post states this is future work, that upstream has no 'jj native' protocol and that ERSC does not claim to be building one. Other products are meant as separate building blocks: code review with stacked diffs is announced as coming, and CI and the rest are bring-your-own. Testing uses Antithesis, and Rust is the company's default language outside the web front end (so probably the engine's too). The storage design itself is not published.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > Stated targets: throughput (horizontal scale, 'land thousands of commits per second' as a goal, May 2026), landing contention (the September post names contention on merging new work as an effect of agents), workspace cost (fast clones, 'instant checkouts' as a goal, large files) and access control inside a monorepo. How contention is reduced is not described. Textual conflicts: not reduced by any published mechanism; the May post only says the model has jj-style first-class conflicts. Not addressed in any public text: semantic conflicts and task collision.
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > Not published for the server. Today it is Git semantics at the edge (isolate on a branch, push, merge elsewhere); the stated direction is a jj-based change model with conflicts as data, plus a cloud-workspace workflow in the style of Google's CitC or Meta's Commit Cloud (Klabnik, Hacker News, 2026-09-12).

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > Not usable by an outsider on 2026-11-24. Trials with early partners reported on 2026-05-04; ERSC Storage was said on 2026-09-01 to enter private beta later that month; the 2026-09-10 post says none of it is available just yet. No announcement that the beta started was found, but the site navigation now says 'Storage Beta' and links to an enterprise-only application form (company, commits in the repository, how often code lands). No self-service onboarding at first (Klabnik, 2026-09-01).
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > Proprietary; no source published. Deployment as bring-your-own-cloud (BYOC) and on-premises, both covered by the SOC 2 Type I audit of August 2026, and hosted by ERSC on Google Cloud in the United States (compliance page, updated 2026-11-04). Klabnik on 2026-10-13: BYOC and on-premises first, 'possibly' a single-tenant SaaS. ERSC promises that the future protocol will be documented and that client changes for it will be open source.
- **Price:** Not published (2026-11-24); enterprise sales through an application form.
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > Company about 18 months old, mostly in stealth until September 2026 (Klabnik). No version number, public documentation, status page or public repository was found. Signs of a running system: ERSC lists its own ERSC Storage among the infrastructure of its site (colophon), the SOC 2 Type I audit was finished in August 2026 and Type II is in its observation period, and prospective customers go through a proof-of-concept process (Klabnik, 2026-09-12). Latest posts: 2026-10-06 (randomised testing). No named customer or user was found.
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > Young venture-backed company; the size of its funding is not public. Nothing is published about replication, backup, recovery objectives or consistency of the storage engine, although the team says it fuzzes storage and data structures with Antithesis. Positive for continuity: the Git protocol keeps ordinary clones outside the server, BYOC and on-premises keep data in the customer's account, and jj stays a separate Apache-2.0 project (Google holds the jj trademark, Klabnik, 2026-09-03). ERSC says it does not modify jj.
- **OS support:**
  > Client: any Git client, so native Windows, WSL, Linux and macOS follow from Git, and jj works today as a Git client. Server platforms for BYOC and on-premises are not published.

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > Git protocol and normal Git client kept; the server does not store Git repositories and does not use Git's code base. jj can be used now through the Git protocol. A native protocol is future work. ERSC states that features beyond what Git can express will need the non-Git protocol. Which Git protocol features work (partial clone, shallow clone, LFS, hooks, signed commits) is not published.
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > Replaces the Git server, not the whole forge. The product is sold as building blocks to combine with the customer's other tools. CI is bring-your-own: ERSC uses Buildkite and expects to share that integration first (Klabnik, 2026-09-03). Code review with stacked diffs was 'coming soon' on 2026-05-04. No GitHub mirroring, pull-request bridge or GitHub Actions support is described in public, so a GitHub-based team cannot judge the fit from public sources.
- **Migration and exit:**
  > Designed for gradual adoption: move the remote, keep Git, let individual developers switch to jj, and only later use the native protocol. Because clients stay Git clones, leaving while on the Git protocol should cost little beyond moving the remote; this follows from the design and is not documented. Work that depends on later non-Git features (directory permissions, native protocol) would be lost or need conversion. Importers, export tools, history-rewrite guarantees and the cost of moving reviews, permissions and CI triggers are not published.

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > Not published. In today's Git mode nothing public suggests a change from standard Git: the agent commits and rebases in its clone and pushes; main moves by a push or through whatever review and CI tool the customer adds. Stated intent only: a distributed system that can land thousands of commits per second (goal from May 2026, no test), and stacked-diff review to come. No description exists of server-side rebase, replay, compare-and-swap granularity, queueing or batching. Klabnik calls Anthropic's Scion post a validation of ERSC's thesis (2026-10-13), but that is a statement about moving off Git at scale, not evidence that ERSC lands changes the same way.
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > Not published. With a Git client it is the usual point: a rejected non-fast-forward push, or a line-level conflict in the client's merge or rebase. The May post says the version-control model is based on Jujutsu with first-class conflicts; on the jj client that means conflicts are recorded in commits instead of stopping the operation. Whether the server stores or detects conflicts is not stated.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > Not published for the server. With Git: the agent resolves in its clone. With jj over Git: conflicts are data in the local repository, and the agent can resolve later. Note the limit described by an ERSC engineer: Git has no way to store a conflict inside a commit, and jj keeps the structure that describes it outside what Git understands (Seipp, Hacker News, 2026-09-01). Inference: conflicts as data will not work end to end over the Git bridge, which is one reason a native protocol would matter.
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > Not published. By Git protocol rules a push built on an old head is rejected and the agent fetches, rebases and pushes again; nothing says ERSC replays it on the server. Retry cost is unknown.
- **Pre write coordination:** None described: no claims, locks, reservations or messaging between agents in any public text.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > No evidence and no claim with a number. ERSC names merge contention as a problem caused by agents but publishes no conflict rate, rejected-push rate, landing rate or customer result, and no independent measurement exists. Its investor Amplify argues that conflicts as data make automatic rebasing sturdier (2026-05-20, disclosed as an investor); that is an argument about jj, available without ERSC.

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > Server: custom non-Git storage engine, horizontally scalable, separate per customer; internals unpublished. Client today: ordinary Git or jj clone. Stated goals: instant checkouts, files of any size, and something along the lines of CitC or Commit Cloud (cloud-backed workspaces). None of these is shown in public.
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > None demonstrated. 'Thousands of commits per second' (2026-05-04) is a vendor design goal, not a measurement, and it does not say whether the commits go to one branch. The application form asks whether a customer lands code daily, hourly, every minute or every second, which shows the intended market.
- **Scale evidence type:** None: vendor statement of intent only; no benchmark, simulation, paper or production report.
- **Coordination limit:** Unknown; nothing published.

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > Not part of the product at first: bring your own CI (Buildkite integration expected first). No affected-target selection, batching, speculation or merge queue is described.
- **Broken main recovery:** Nothing published.
- **Review model:**
  > First-party code review with stacked diffs announced as coming soon (2026-05-04, repeated 2026-09-01); not released. Until then the customer's own review tool. No position on agent review or review without humans was found.

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > Git CLI over the Git protocol, jj CLI as a Git client, and a GraphQL API that is mentioned once without a schema. No MCP server, agent skill, instruction file, structured error format or public documentation was found. ERSC presents itself as a platform for 'humans and machines'.
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > Server side: nothing published. Client side: Git reflog, or jj's operation log and undo if the agent uses jj.
- **Agent cost:**
  > No ERSC-specific figure. In Git mode an agent pays the same clone, rebase and retry cost as with any Git server; faster clones are promised but not measured.
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > Stated intent: fine-grained access control, with per-directory read and write permissions named as a reason to leave Git (2026-05-04; Klabnik, 2026-09-01 and 2026-09-11). Whether it works over the Git bridge is not stated, and Klabnik ties features Git cannot express to the later protocol. No per-agent identity, sandboxing or attribution of changes to agent, model or prompt is described. If ERSC hosts the software, telemetry with user and repository identifiers, file paths and branch names goes to Honeycomb, and Claude reads that telemetry (compliance page, 2026-11-04); in BYOC and on-premises mode ERSC says it receives no data unless the customer sends it.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > As far as is public: the agent itself, with Git or jj. The server only serves and accepts pushes. No server-side landing, orchestrator or queue is described.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Vendor statements only (blog, compliance page, staff comments), strong on people and weak on product: no design, API, benchmark, price or customer is public. Main weakness: every technical claim is a goal or a diagram. The team's credentials (jj creator, jj maintainers, Meta and Google source-control experience) are verifiable and are the main reason to watch it.
- **Independent reports:**
  > No hands-on report by a user was found. Hacker News on 'What comes after git' (2026-09-11, 77 points, 31 comments) was mostly critical: commenters called the post vague about what is wrong with Git and which problem ERSC solves; nrr, with experience in the field, called it thin on technical substance, while rbsmith (formerly of BitKeeper) defended the idea of staying mappable to Git. Klabnik answered that it was a teaser and technical posts would follow. The CTO news drew 265 points and 268 comments (2026-09-01), mostly about jj and its governance. One commenter wrote on 2026-10-19 that ERSC had not got back to him.

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > Larger organisations with a big private monorepo (the form starts at enterprise scale) that want to leave Git storage gradually, keep the Git protocol, run the server in their own cloud or on-premises, and already like jj.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > Product risk: no public product, price, design or customer; availability statements have moved (2026-04-28 launch called imminent, 2026-05-04 said to be adoptable now, 2026-09-10 said to be not available yet). Technical risk: the benefits beyond storage depend on a protocol that does not exist yet, and conflicts as data are unlikely to work over the Git bridge (inference). Scope risk: no landing queue, CI or agent coordination, so conflict and landing problems stay with the customer. Vendor risk: small start-up with a proprietary engine. Data risk in hosted mode: telemetry with file paths and branch names leaves for third parties.
- **Minimum useful scale:**
  > Not stated by ERSC; the 'enterprise scale' wording and the form (millions of commits, landing every second) suggest far above 5-30 agents. With ordinary Git hosting working, there is no reason to change below the point where clone time or push throughput becomes the bottleneck.
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Skip. It cannot be bought or tried without acceptance as an enterprise beta customer, it publishes no mechanism against conflicts or landing contention, and 5-30 agents do not need a new storage engine. Use jj on top of an existing Git host to get the part of the idea that works today.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Wait. The architecture (Git protocol kept, separate deployment per customer, BYOC, later a richer protocol) is one of the more conservative routes to several hundred agents in the Git-compatible variant, and the team is credible. Apply to the beta only if storage or clone throughput is a proven bottleneck. Ask first for: landing rate on one branch, behaviour on a stale base, server-side rebase, how conflicts are represented, permissions over Git, export, and price.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > Put a protocol bridge in front of the storage and keep Git at the edge, so storage and clients can change at different times. Adopt jj per developer or per agent while the server stays Git. Isolate each team's source-control load (own deployment or own remote) so that other users cannot slow it down. Compose storage, review and CI from separate parts. Test a storage engine with deterministic fault injection before trusting it with source code.

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > ERSC, What comes after git, 2026-09-10, https://ersc.io/blog/what-comes-after-git ; ERSC, East River Source Control Names Jujutsu Creator Martin von Zweigbergk Chief Technology Officer, 2026-09-01, https://ersc.io/blog/martin-joins-ersc ; ERSC, An update on ERSC availability, 2026-05-04, https://ersc.io/blog/ersc-availability ; ERSC, When random is not actually random enough, 2026-10-06, https://ersc.io/blog/when-random-isnt-random-enough ; ERSC application form, https://ersc.io/apply , compliance page (updated 2026-11-04), https://ersc.io/compliance , team page, https://ersc.io/team , colophon, https://ersc.io/colophon , all read 2026-11-24; Steve Klabnik on Hacker News: 2026-09-01, https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49526190 , 2026-09-03 (CI), https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49546387 , 2026-09-12 (CitC, proof of concept), https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49668677 , 2026-10-13 (Scion, BYOC), https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50075405 ; Austin Seipp on Hacker News, 2026-09-01, https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49526366 ; Martin von Zweigbergk and ERSC on Bluesky, 2026-09-01 and 2026-04-28, https://bsky.app/profile/ersc.io . Secondary: Amplify Partners (investor), Will agents like Git any more than we do (linked by ERSC on 2026-05-20), https://www.amplifypartners.com/blog-posts/will-agents-like-git-any-more-than-we-do ; Hacker News threads, https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49652985 and https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49525297
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > Not confirmed: whether the private beta started, and who uses it. The May post says the engine speaks Git and 'jj' and can be adopted today; the September post says the native protocol is future work and nothing is available yet; the later text is used here. The brief's 'native jj protocol planned' is narrower in the source: a future native protocol for a jj client, explicitly not called 'jj native'. The brief's CTO date is the announcement date. A HackerNoon article of 2026-10-14, bylined to an AI agent, says ERSC keeps Git as its storage; that contradicts ERSC's own diagram and was not used. PitchBook and The Consensus returned HTTP 403; RuntimeWire returned a paywall page; Lobsters blocked the request. Nine JJ-Con 2026 talks are on YouTube (conference co-hosted by ERSC); two involve ERSC staff (Nick Gerace, How to Accidentally Create a Graph-based Version Control System, sE8Vd9OZCps; David Barsky in the jj project update, pwgLGt76CCQ). Their titles are not about ERSC Storage and they were not watched, so 'not published' here means not in ERSC's written sources and staff comments.

### Structural merge drivers Weave and Mergiraf

*Layer: F merge algorithm*

#### Basics

- **Name:** Structural merge drivers: Weave (Ataraxy-Labs/weave) and Mergiraf
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > Weave: https://github.com/Ataraxy-Labs/weave, site https://ataraxy-labs.github.io/weave/ ; sibling Ataraxy-Labs/sem (entity-level diff, blame and impact, the parser Weave uses). Namesakes: Weaveworks Weave (networking), Weights & Biases Weave (LLM tracing), the arXiv benchmark WEAVE (2610.22039), and the weave data structure used by SCCS and BitKeeper. Mergiraf: https://codeberg.org/mergiraf/mergiraf (canonical home is Codeberg, not GitHub), site https://mergiraf.org/ . Not the same: MergirafSemi (a research tool built on Mergiraf 0.12.1) and mergerfs (a file system). No renames found.
- **Maker:**
  > Weave: Ataraxy Labs Inc., by its own description a two-person company (Rohan Sharma, M Palanikannan; founded 2025). Mergiraf: community project started by Antonin Delpeuch, with Ada Alakbarova as the other most active contributor; governance file and Liberapay donations, no company.
- **What it is:**
  > Both are Git merge drivers in Rust that parse files with tree-sitter and replace the content-merge step when Git merges, rebases or cherry-picks; they run locally and write an ordinary file. Mergiraf: tries the line merge first and returns it if clean (except when that would create duplicate keys); otherwise it matches syntax-tree nodes (GumTree) across base, left and right, merges them, and unites insertions only where a language profile says order does not matter ('commutative parents'). Weave: splits each version into entities (functions, classes, keys), matches them by name, type and scope, takes one-sided changes, merges inside an entity when both sides changed it, and otherwise conflicts with a line naming the guard that refused. Weave adds a CLI around it: preview, explain, check (looks for lost lines, duplicates, dangling names, wrong argument counts), patch, land, an MCP server and an experimental CRDT claim layer. Sem gives agents entity-level diffs and impact; it does not merge.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > Addressed: textual conflicts that are false, that is, independent edits that happen to touch neighbouring lines of one file. Partly addressed, Weave only: some cross-file breakage after a merge (weave check reports dangling names and wrong argument counts), and a small local landing step (weave land --onto). Not addressed by either: throughput, landing contention, task collision (Weave's claims are advisory and experimental), workspace cost, and semantic conflicts in behaviour.
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > Isolate then merge, with a better merge algorithm at the end (syntax-aware three-way merge). Weave also ships a separate, experimental pre-write claim layer on a CRDT that the merge itself never uses.

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > Both usable today by anyone. Weave: v0.7.1, 2026-11-02, Homebrew (core), npm, GitHub binaries, source. Mergiraf: v0.20.0, 2026-09-26, many package managers, Codeberg binaries, crates.io. Both are pre-1.0.
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > Weave: MIT or Apache-2.0 (README and licence files; GitHub shows Apache-2.0, and the company's own weave.md page still says MIT). Sem: GitHub shows Apache-2.0. Mergiraf: GPLv3; as a separate program called by Git that does not affect your code, but GitButler's developers say it stops them from embedding it (2026-02-14). Both run locally; nothing to host. Ataraxy's separate paid product 'land' (useland.dev) uses Weave inside a hosted test-and-merge service and is out of scope here.
- **Price:**
  > Both free (2026-11-24). Cost is local CPU time at merge. Ataraxy's hosted product land is priced per minute, with two different rate tables on its own pages on the same day, so no figure is given here.
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > Weave: created 2026-02-06, v0.7.1 on 2026-11-02, 1,365 stars, 49 forks, last push 2026-11-21, 1 open issue besides open pull requests (read 2026-11-24); nearly all commits by the two founders; Homebrew installs 334 in 30 days. Martin von Zweigbergk counted 9 public jj configurations using it (2026-08-09). Replit's Agent 4 paper (CAIS '26, 2026-05-26) lists Weave in its references. Sem: 3,459 stars, v0.30.1 on 2026-11-10. Mergiraf: repository created 2024-10-31, v0.20.0 on 2026-09-26, 482 stars on Codeberg, 92 forks, 87 open issues, last commit 2026-11-21; its Linux x64 binary of v0.20.0 was downloaded 177,534 times (probably mostly automated), Homebrew installs 145 in 30 days and 3,308 in a year. Mergiraf is built into jj as a merge tool; Weave's request to be added (jj PR 8833) is open since February 2026.
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > No stored data in either tool: the result is a normal Git commit, so removing the driver loses nothing. Weave: two-person start-up that is now building a commercial product (land) on it; fast, breaking changes in behaviour (0.5.3 changed verdicts on purpose); crates.io lags GitHub (0.7.0 against 0.7.1). Mergiraf: volunteer project with two main people, two years of steady releases. Data-loss caveat for both: a wrong silent merge is the failure that costs code (see failure_modes_and_risks).
- **OS support:**
  > Weave: Linux (x86-64, arm64), macOS, native Windows binaries (msvc), CI on Linux and Windows; older Linux builds needed GLIBC 2.39 (issue 153, closed). Mergiraf: Linux (gnu and musl), macOS, native Windows (gnu) binaries. WSL works as Linux for both.

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > Full: both plug into Git's own merge-driver interface through .gitattributes; history, objects and protocol unchanged. Mergiraf recommends Git 2.44 or newer. jj: Mergiraf is a built-in tool and Weave needs a config block, but in jj both run only on 'jj resolve', not automatically. GitButler uses configured merge drivers (its developer Byron, 2026-02-08).
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > The driver runs only where it is installed and configured, so merges computed on a hosting service do not use it. GitLab states that custom merge drivers are not supported on GitLab.com or GitLab Dedicated (self-managed administrators can configure them). For GitHub no documentation was found; its users have asked since 2021 for even the built-in merge=union attribute to be honoured on pull requests (community discussion 9288), so it is an inference that the merge button, conflict check and merge queue ignore Weave and Mergiraf. Practical consequence: a pull request can show a conflict that the driver would resolve locally, and the agent or a landing script has to rebase or merge locally and push. Branch protection, reviews and CI then work unchanged on the result. Weave also has a hosted pull-request comment integration (weave-github).
- **Migration and exit:**
  > Cheapest adoption of all the layers: install a binary, add config, done per machine or per repository ('weave setup', 'weave setup --off'; a few lines of Git config for Mergiraf, 'mergiraf=0' to disable for one command). Every agent machine or container needs the binary and the config; with attributes committed but no driver installed, Git falls back to its normal text merge. Partial adoption by file extension is possible. Exit loses nothing. Cost: a check of merges made while it was on, if a silent error is suspected.

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > Unchanged Git: whoever runs git merge or git rebase (agent, script, integrator) gets the driver's result and pushes it; main moves through the usual push, pull request or queue. Neither tool is a landing system. Mergiraf has nothing beyond the driver and 'mergiraf solve' for files that already contain conflict markers. Weave adds 'weave land': it resolves the merge in progress, can call a resolver command (for example an LLM) with the merge inputs as JSON, labels every file PROVEN, VERIFIED or REFUSED, writes only accepted files, and returns exit code 0 accepted, 1 refused, 2 error. With '--onto origin/main --verify-cmd \<tests>' it merges the target, runs the checks on the merged tree and publishes fast-forward only, retrying against the new tip if the target moved; '--queue' is marked experimental. The retry is per client with no shared queue, so it does not remove contention on main when many agents land at once. Its README warns that the labels are merge checks and do not prove that the program works.
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > After the work, at merge or rebase time, per file. Mergiraf: syntax-tree node, and only for files where the line merge conflicts (or would duplicate a key); clean line merges pass through untouched. Weave: entity (function, class, key) with statement-level merge inside; it also judges files the line merge would accept and can refuse them. Neither driver sees other files while merging. Weave's separate command 'weave check' (and the MCP tool) adds a cross-file pass by name: callers of a renamed or deleted entity, calls with the wrong number of arguments. Languages: Weave 38 formats, including TypeScript, Python, Go, Rust, Java, C, C++, C#, SQL, Markdown, with Vue, Svelte, ERB and Haskell deliberately left to the line merge; Mergiraf about 30 languages plus formats such as JSON, YAML, TOML, XML, HTML, go.mod, requirements.txt, including Haskell, Solidity, SystemVerilog and Starlark but not SQL or Markdown. Files Mergiraf cannot parse, and for Weave files over 1 MB, binaries and unknown types, fall back to the line merge.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > Resolved automatically and deterministically, with no model call, when the tool judges the edits independent; otherwise conflict markers. Mergiraf leaves diff3-style markers aligned to lines (narrower ones with --compact), prints how many conflicts it solved and offers 'mergiraf review \<id>' to inspect them. Weave's markers name the entity, the type, a confidence and a 'refused_by:' line; 'weave explain --json' gives per-hunk detail and 'weave check' verifies the agent's own resolution (exit 1 on findings, 2 if it could not verify). Mergiraf's usage page describes no machine-readable output.
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > As in Git: the agent rebases or merges the new main and the driver runs again for each file both sides changed. The gain is that fewer rebases stop for help. weave land --onto retries by itself when the target moves. The test run after each rebase is not avoided.
- **Pre write coordination:**
  > Mergiraf: none. Weave: experimental entity claims through a CRDT file in the working tree ('weave experimental claim', MCP tools only with WEAVE_EXPERIMENTAL=1); advisory, no expiry and no cleanup after a crashed agent, as its README states. Weave's edit command adds compare-and-swap on one entity ('expect') and one lock per repository for batches, which helps agents that share a checkout.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > Weave, claim: '\~95% reduction vs. line-based merge' (GitHub description and the company's llms.txt); no dataset or method for it was found in the README, the benchmark page or the repository files read. Weave, own replay (self-reported, real human merge commits, engine 0.5.3, 2026-09-01, five repositories, 4,971 file merges where both sides changed the file): Git's line merge conflicted in 1,052; Weave resolved 344 and still conflicted in 708, so it removed 32.7% of the conflicts (own calculation), from 14% on TypeScript to 47% on Flask (67 files). The page also counts 86 regressions and is inconsistent about them: its definition says Git merged cleanly and Weave's output differs from the human result, while its 0.5.3 note and per-repository text describe Weave conflicting or declining to auto-merge. If all 86 are refusals, the net number of conflicted files fell from 1,052 to 794, about 24.5% (own calculation); if some are silent different output, they are wrong or altered merges rather than extra conflicts. Of the 344, between 33% and 72% per repository match the human result byte for byte apart from whitespace. Earlier author figure: 39 of 310 conflicts on git/git, 13% (2026-02-15). Synthetic: 31 hand-made cases, Weave 31, Mergiraf 0.16.3 28, Git 17 correct (self-reported, small, chosen by the author). Mergiraf, independent: 84.1% of 21,615 real human merge scenarios in 513 repositories and five languages came out conflict-free (version 0.12.1, SBES 2026 paper; the paper gives no Git rate for the same full set in its text, so this is not a reduction). LWN replay of the Linux kernel, 2025-10-31: 428 of 7,415 conflicting merges fully resolved, about 6%, many more partly. One user reported about 70% of his own conflicts solved over four months (paulirish, Hacker News, 2025-11-13, anecdote). Agent merges: no measurement found for either tool; nine 2026 papers on agent conflicts and coordination were searched and none mentions Weave or Mergiraf.

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > n/a: a merge driver does not manage checkouts or storage. It works in any clone or worktree that has the binary and the config.
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > Merging: none shown; drivers run one merge at a time in one clone. Weave's edit lock: 40 concurrent edits to different functions of one file in one checkout all kept, against 7 without the lock (self-reported test, changelog 0.6.0, not a landing figure). No landing throughput for either tool.
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > Weave: self-reported replay of 4,971 historical human file merges plus 31 synthetic cases and 20,000 generated merges; no production data published by a user. Mergiraf: peer-reviewed and preprint replays of historical human merges (21,615 and 5,229 scenarios) by independent researchers, plus one LWN replay. No agent workload for either.
- **Coordination limit:**
  > Not measured. By design the benefit ends where agents edit the same function, the same ordered list or the same behaviour; those conflicts stay, and their share grows as more agents work on the same hot code.

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > None built into the drivers, and needed more with them: every automatic resolution should be tested on the merged tree before it lands. Weave offers --verify-cmd and --check sem in weave land (any command; a failed check refuses publication). No batching, speculation or affected-target selection.
- **Broken main recovery:**
  > None. Use the surrounding queue or CI. mergiraf review and weave check help to find out afterwards what was merged automatically.
- **Review model:**
  > Unchanged; the tools add no review step. Mergiraf asks the user to review solved conflicts; with agents nobody does that unless a step is added. Weave's labels (PROVEN, VERIFIED, REFUSED) can serve as a filter for which merges need a second look.

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > Weave is made for agents: CLI with --json and defined exit codes, SKILL.md with a resolve loop, MCP server with six tools (preview, edit, explain, check, land, patch), works non-interactively. Mergiraf: non-interactive driver and solve command, plain text output, no MCP and no agent instructions; agents use it without knowing it is there. Both need no prompt changes for the basic merge. Sem adds an MCP server for entity diffs and impact.
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > git merge --abort, git rebase --abort, reset or reflog, as always; both tools can be switched off and the merge repeated. Mergiraf keeps merge ids for review and bug reports. 'weave edit --revert' puts one entity back.
- **Agent cost:**
  > Low. No tokens for automatic merges, and fewer conflict-resolution rounds for the agent (users on Hacker News say agents can resolve conflicts but spend extra rounds and tokens on it; not measured). Runtime: Mergiraf median 57.3 ms per merge scenario (independent, v0.12.1), with outliers above 10 seconds; Weave 65 to 374 microseconds per synthetic merge (self-reported), while 'weave check' took 42 s on the largest vscode merges (self-reported). Setup: one binary per machine or container image.
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > n/a: no identities, permissions or sandbox. Provenance is whatever Git records; automatic merges are not marked in history unless you add it. Weave land can write a certificate file of the merge checks.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > The agent or script that runs git merge or rebase; the driver acts inside that command. The agent is left with the conflicts the tool refuses, the test run and the push.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Mergiraf: good for human merges, with two independent studies (MergirafSemi, SBES 2026; LastMerge, arXiv 2507.19687, 2025) and an LWN test; weakness: old versions (0.12.1) and no agent merges. Weave: vendor self-report with unusually open notes on its own regressions, Lean proofs of a model of its merge rule with 20,000 generated merges agreeing on 0.7.0 (renames and merges inside a function are not modelled), and a headline number (95%) its own tables do not support; benchmark page, site and README disagree with each other and with the current version. No independent evaluation of Weave was found.
- **Independent reports:**
  > Mergiraf, positive: paulirish, about 70% of conflicts solved in four months and none contested (2025-11-13); mtndew4brkfst, frequent time-saver for TOML and Rust (2026-03-09); indentit, the opposite: so many odd problems that he uninstalled it (2025-11-13). Weave, negative: igravious reported three corruption bugs in Python merges in April 2026 (57, 58, 60; fixed within days) and in June wrote that the tool does not work for automated merges and that a merge tool cannot have silent failures (2026-06-14 and 2026-06-17); nullbio ran it for weeks, noticed code disappearing and stopped (2026-06-14). Weave, mixed: csomar, who runs a bot over new GitHub conflicts, found no case outside TypeScript where it beats Mergiraf (2026-06-14); slarse (GitButler discussion, says he built the Spork merge tool) liked the idea but called 31 cases too small and pointed to a signature case it failed (2026-02-14); nshcr found a duplicated enum on his first test, fixed in 0.1.2 (2026-02-12); jj maintainer ilyagr hit invalid markers and a lost impl block, fixed (March 2026). Weave, positive: igravious on 2026-03-05, one merge of a modified kimi-cli worked well. Elijah Newren's and Taylor Blau's praise is reported by the author from private e-mail. Replit: a citation in its paper is confirmed; that Weave powers all Agent 4 merges in production is only the Weave author's statement (2026-08-08), not confirmed by Replit in anything read.

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > Teams that keep Git and have many agents adding separate functions, methods, imports, keys or tests to the same files, with a good test suite that runs on the merged result. Mergiraf for the more conservative choice (clean line merges untouched, longer history); Weave when agents should get structured refusals and an MCP or JSON interface.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > 1. Silent wrong merges, the worst case for unattended agents. Independent study (Mergiraf 0.12.1, 21,615 human merges): structured merging reports fewer conflicts but lets more real ones through; Mergiraf had 143 added false negatives on Go, 90 on Python and 81 on Rust against its semistructured variant, and 377 (Python) and 432 (Rust) against the plain line merge; a false negative here is a conflict-free result that differs from the human merge and fails to build or fails tests. LastMerge study (Java, 5,229 scenarios): Mergiraf did better than the Java-only tool Spork on this point, 62 against 107, but raised more false conflicts, 290 against 150. Open Mergiraf issues on 0.19 and 0.20: argument inserted in the wrong place (868, 2026-11-23), edit applied to the wrong property with exit 0 (829), duplicated TOML tables with exit 0 (853). Weave, all closed: unparseable Python (57), truncated import (58), dropped assignment (60), dropped import (95), same function added twice with exit 0 (107), dropped top-level code (148, fixed 2026-09-01). 2. Semantic conflicts are outside the method: in a 2026 benchmark, constructed pairs of agent patches that merged cleanly as text failed together in 97% of runs, though only 1 of 834 runs on mined real pairs did (arXiv 2609.25396). Order matters in ways a tree does not show (decorators, statements). 3. Weave does worse than Git on some merges Git accepts (86 of 4,971 in its own test); its page is inconsistent on whether these are refusals or different output. 4. Not applied on the hosting side, so agents and pull requests can see different results. 5. C and C++ with macros and files with syntax errors parse badly. 6. Generated files and lockfiles are better regenerated than merged. 7. Mixed versions of the driver among agents give different results for the same merge (inference).
- **Minimum useful scale:**
  > Pays off from a handful of agents as soon as false conflicts in shared files cost agent time; nearly free to try. No published threshold.
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Pilot. Cheap, reversible and Git-compatible, so it belongs on top of worktrees and a merge queue rather than instead of them. Start with Mergiraf (or Weave if structured output for agents matters) in the container image of the agents, limited to source languages you test well, with the full test run on the merged tree as the gate, and log every automatic resolution for a sample review. Measure your own conflict rate before and after; Weave's own replay of human merges suggests a quarter to a third fewer conflicts, not 95%; nobody has published the figure for agent merges.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Pilot as a component, not as the answer. At several hundred agents landing contention, test capacity and semantic conflicts dominate and neither tool touches them. A server that lands changes itself can use a syntax-aware merge as its replay step, which also puts one version of the driver in one place and solves the hosting gap; with line merge kept as the reference and every structural resolution tested before it reaches main. Weave's per-client retry in weave land is not a queue.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > Merge by structure only where order carries no meaning, and say so per file or container (Mergiraf's commutative parents, Weave's weave-set attribute). Refuse rather than guess on ordered things. Tell the agent why a merge was refused and exactly which lines collide. After any merge run a cheap structural check (parses, no duplicates, no lost lines, no dangling names, right argument counts), then tests on the merged tree. Make append-only files conflict-free by layout (one file per entry for changelogs, tests and registrations). Replay your own last 500 merges with a candidate driver before turning it on, and count both the conflicts removed and the clean merges broken.

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > Weave README at commit e52ba820 (2026-11-21), https://github.com/Ataraxy-Labs/weave ; Weave changelog, https://github.com/Ataraxy-Labs/weave/blob/main/CHANGELOG.md ; Weave benchmark page (0.5.3, 2026-09-01), https://ataraxy-labs.github.io/weave/benchmarks.html ; Weave issues 57, 58, 60, 95, 107, 148, https://github.com/Ataraxy-Labs/weave/issues ; merge-laws (Lean proofs), https://github.com/Ataraxy-Labs/merge-laws ; Ataraxy Labs, What if Merges Understood Your Code?, February 2026, https://ataraxy-labs.com/blogs/what-if-merges-understood-code ; llms.txt with the 95% claim, https://ataraxy-labs.com/llms.txt ; Sem, https://github.com/Ataraxy-Labs/sem ; Mergiraf documentation, https://mergiraf.org/ (usage, conflicts, languages), releases and issues 725, 829, 853, 868, https://codeberg.org/mergiraf/mergiraf ; Lopes et al., MergirafSemi, arXiv 2608.11345, 2026-08-11 (SBES 2026), https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11345 ; LastMerge, arXiv 2507.19687, 2025-07-25, https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.19687 ; LWN, Daroc Alden, 2025-10-31, https://lwn.net/Articles/1042355/ ; GitButler discussion 12274, February 2026, https://github.com/gitbutlerapp/gitbutler/discussions/12274 ; jj pull request 8833, https://github.com/jj-vcs/jj/pull/8833 ; Hacker News, 2026-03-04, https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=47241976 , 2026-06-14, https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=48523705 , and Mergiraf 2025-11, https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=45799664 ; Replit, Agent 4: Teamwork and Collaboration for Vibe-Coding, CAIS '26, 2026-05-26, https://doi.org/10.1145/3786335.3813211 (reference list through Crossref) ; GitLab docs, Git attributes, https://docs.gitlab.com/user/project/repository/files/git_attributes ; GitHub community discussion 9288, https://github.com/orgs/community/discussions/9288 ; Passes Alone, Fails Together, arXiv 2609.25396, 2026-09-21, https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25396 . All read 2026-11-24.
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > The origin of the 95% figure was not found; a code search of the repository returned nothing before it timed out and the benchmark tables give about a third. Weave's numbers were measured on 0.5.3 and not repeated on 0.6 or 0.7; its baseline line merge is the diffy library, which differs from git merge-file in a few cases. The definition of a regression differs between README (Weave conflicts where the line merge is clean) and benchmark page (Weave's output differs from the human's); the net calculation here uses the README's. The page still shows older figures (83 wins, 0 regressions) and lists decorator cases as auto-resolved that the README now says must be refused. The Mergiraf comparison uses 0.16.3, and its maintainer opened an issue to check those cases (725, open). The LWN and LastMerge figures were read through page summaries. The text of Replit's paper could not be opened (ACM returned HTTP 403, no repository copy); its abstract describes forked copies per task, validation and an incremental rebasing strategy and does not name Weave, which appears in the reference list. GitHub's handling of custom merge drivers is an inference. Papers searched by name for both tools, none found: arXiv 2601.13295, 2603.21489, 2604.03551, 2605.25890, 2606.19616, 2607.04697, 2609.25396, 2610.07261, 2611.00314. Weave's 86 regressions: the benchmark page defines them as different output but describes them as refusals (re-read 2026-11-25); not resolved.

### Jujutsu

*Layer: D VCS or client*

#### Basics

- **Name:** Jujutsu (jj)
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > jj-vcs/jj on GitHub, documentation at https://docs.jj-vcs.dev/ . Formerly martinvonz/jj; moved to the jj-vcs organisation in December 2024 (README); docs moved from jj-vcs.github.io/jj in v0.36.0 (2025-12-03). Binary and Homebrew formula: jj; crate: jj-cli; some Linux packages: jujutsu. Unrelated or third-party names: Canonical's Juju (juju/juju, deployment orchestration), tidwall/jj (a JSON editor), and the agentic-jujutsu crate/npm package (a third-party wrapper whose '23x faster' style figures have no published method and were not used). Anthropic's Scion and ERSC Storage are separate projects.
- **Maker:**
  > Community open-source project started by Martin von Zweigbergk as a hobby in late 2019 and later his full-time work at Google; he has been CTO of East River Source Control (ERSC) since summer 2026 (announced 2026-09-01). The project's own list of paying employers (v0.47.1) names Alphabet/Google (42 contributors), ERSC (8, including von Zweigbergk and Steve Klabnik) and IMC Trading; it lists contributing employees whether or not jj is part of their job. The README still says Google employment and adds that jj is 'not a supported Google product'.
- **What it is:**
  > A distributed version control client in Rust that uses a normal Git repository as its storage backend (the Git backend is the only production-ready one). Differences from Git that matter for agents: (1) the working copy is a commit, so nearly every jj command snapshots the files automatically and there is no staging area or stash; (2) changes keep a stable change ID when they are rewritten; (3) a conflict is stored as data in the commit, so a rebase or merge always completes and is resolved later; (4) descendants of a rewritten commit are rebased automatically; (5) every repository operation goes into an operation log that can be undone, reverted or restored; (6) several workspaces (working copies) can share one store. Since v0.46.0 (2026-10-07) a workspace can also be created as a colocated Git worktree.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > Partly: textual conflicts (does not prevent them; stores them and stops them from blocking a rebase), workspace cost (several working copies on one object store, sparse patterns; still a full checkout of the selected files), and rebase or stack maintenance after main moves. Not addressed: throughput (the server is whatever Git host you use), landing contention (a push is a compare-and-swap on the remote bookmark, like git push --force-with-lease), semantic conflicts, and task collision between agents.
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > Isolate then merge, with conflicts as data and automatic rebase of descendants; locally, lock-free optimistic concurrency through the operation log (divergent operations are merged afterwards). No write-time detection and no pre-write claims.

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > Usable today by anyone. Latest release v0.47.1 (changelog date 2026-11-04, GitHub release 2026-11-05 UTC). The README still labels it an experimental version control system with stable Git compatibility; no 1.0 and format or workflow changes are announced as possible before 1.0.
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > Apache-2.0. Local client, no server of its own; works with any Git remote (GitHub, GitLab, Gitea, Gerrit through 'jj gerrit upload'). The open-source cloud server and daemon are only on the roadmap, without a date; Google runs an internal one.
- **Price:** Free (2026-11-24).
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > v0.47.1, monthly releases. Repository created 2020-12-18; 32,627 stars, 1,298 forks, 1,234 open issues and pull requests, about 406 contributors, last push 2026-11-24 (GitHub API, 2026-11-24). Homebrew: 4,495 installs in 30 days, 47,141 in 365 days (read 2026-11-24). Known users: Google internally (a team member's design document describes daily use on Google's monorepo through custom backends; user numbers not found), ERSC, and all core developers on jj itself.
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > Data sits in an ordinary Git repository, so the content survives without jj; the operation log, bookmarks' jj state and stored conflicts do not. Documented caveats at v0.47.1: the Git backend is not entirely lock-free, so corruption is possible when a repository is shared over NFS, Dropbox or rsync (issue 2193, open since 2023; 'jj debug reindex' usually recovers it); the docs say colocated repositories are less tested in that setup. Edits are captured only when a jj command runs, so work written after the last snapshot can be lost to 'git reset --hard' or 'git clean' (practitioner report, 2026-10-21). 'jj workspace remove' snapshots tracked files but deletes ignored ones. A corruption of Git trees by external 'git add' (issues 8884, 9711) was fixed in 0.45.0, without repair of already damaged repositories. Continuity is good: many contributors, two main employers, no single vendor.
- **OS support:**
  > Native Windows (x86_64 and aarch64 binaries in the release), Linux (musl), macOS (both architectures); WSL works, best with the clone on the Linux file system. Windows caveats in the docs or issue tracker: its own line-ending setting instead of core.autocrlf or .gitattributes, symlinks only with Developer Mode, '@' must be quoted in PowerShell, unsigned binaries (issue 3163), and an open bug where a directory symlink or junction (as made by pnpm or npm link) makes checkout fail with 'Access is denied' and leaves the workspace stale until the link is deleted by hand (issue 10521, reproduced on 0.47.1). Fixed recently: hang when ssh prompts (0.46.0). Downloads of the v0.47.1 x86_64 Windows archive: 4,565 against 37,174 for x86_64 Linux (2026-11-24, includes CI).

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > Git protocol and Git storage kept; jj is a client on top. From the v0.47.1 compatibility page: submodules no, Git LFS no, hooks no (including pre-commit), .gitattributes no, partial clones no, shallow clones 'kind of' (cannot be deepened), Git configuration partial, annotated tags cannot be created, signed commits yes, Git's own sparse checkout no (own 'jj sparse'). The page still says 'git-worktree: No', but since 0.46.0 'jj workspace add --colocate' creates a Git worktree next to the jj workspace. Partial clone: a promisor clone fails later with 'object could not be found' (issue 10091, 2026-08-31); pull request 6451 that would fetch missing objects has been open since 2025-05-05, and 10317 (2026-10) only explains the error.
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > Works with GitHub and existing CI through normal branches: a bookmark is pushed as a Git branch ('jj git push -c' creates one per change). Change IDs travel in a commit header that GitHub and other large forges mostly preserve. No built-in pull-request command ('jj github submit' is on the roadmap); community tools such as jj-stack (listed in the docs) create stacked pull requests. CI, merge queues and branch protection stay as they are, because the server sees plain Git. Local Git hooks do not run; 'jj fix' (formatters) and 'jj run' (a command over a set of revisions, in isolated working copies) are the substitutes.
- **Migration and exit:**
  > Cheap and partial: 'jj git init' inside an existing clone makes it colocated, one developer or one agent can use it while the rest use Git, and deleting .jj returns to plain Git. Lost on exit: operation log, evolution history, anonymous unpushed changes without a bookmark, and unresolved conflicts (in Git they appear as .jjconflict-\* directories). Real adoption cost is in blockers (LFS, submodules, partial clones, hooks, .gitattributes) and in agent habits: instructions or a skill are needed, because agents fall back to git.

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > jj has no landing server; the agent lands through the Git host. Typical loop: 'jj git fetch', 'jj rebase -d main@origin' (never stops on conflicts; all descendants and their bookmarks move along), resolve conflicted commits, then either move main and push, or push a bookmark and open a pull request for a merge queue. 'jj git push' contacts the remote and updates a bookmark only if it is where jj last saw it; otherwise it refuses and the agent must fetch, and a diverged bookmark becomes a 'conflicted bookmark' that has to be resolved first. Commits with conflicts are not pushed unless '--allow-conflicts' is given (added in 0.44.0), and commits without a description are refused, which protects main. A coordinating agent or human can rebase the work of several agents in one command because all workspaces share one store; each affected workspace is then stale and needs 'jj workspace update-stale' (or snapshot.auto-update-stale = true). With Gerrit, 'jj gerrit upload' hands landing to Gerrit. Nothing queues, batches or tests.
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > At rebase or merge time in the client, not before or at write. Granularity: line hunks by default, optional word-level merging (merge.hunk-level = "word", since 0.34.0), three-way merge generalised to many sides. A syntax-aware resolver can be plugged in ('jj resolve --tool mergiraf'; Mergiraf is in the built-in tool list) but is run on demand, not as an automatic merge driver. Separately jj detects repository-level conflicts: divergent changes (one change ID, several visible commits), conflicted bookmarks, and divergent operations. No semantic detection.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > Conflict as data: the operation succeeds, the commit is marked conflicted, 'jj resolve --list' and templates show it, and the agent resolves by editing the markers in the working copy (or with 'jj resolve' and a tool) and squashing the result into the conflicted commit; descendants pick up the resolution automatically. Resolution can be postponed or handed to another agent on the same machine. Limits: conflicts between a file and a directory or symlink have no good tool (working-copy documentation), conflicts cannot usefully be shared through a Git remote, and Git tools see the stored form of a conflicted commit. Divergent changes can be repaired with 'jj converge' (since 0.45.0), which has '--no-interactive' and gives up if its heuristics fail.
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > A push on an outdated remote position is refused; the agent fetches and rebases. The rebase itself cannot fail halfway, so there is no interrupted state to clean up, which is the main saving over Git. Cost per retry: one fetch, one rebase, resolution of any conflicted commits, tests again. Extra local cost: rewriting a commit that another workspace sits on makes that workspace stale, and its next command errors until it is updated. A pull request by von Zweigbergk (10710, 2026-11-17, open) proposes to start new workspaces on the closest immutable ancestor so that workspaces do not share mutable commits.
- **Pre write coordination:**
  > None. No claims, locks or messages. 'Immutable' commit sets protect published history from rewriting, and an open pull request (10640, 2026-11-07) would also make other workspaces' working-copy commits immutable. A coordinator can see what every workspace currently contains with 'jj log' and 'jj workspace list', which gives visibility, not reservation.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > No evidence that jj lowers the number of conflicts; no study or vendor figure was found, and the design changes when a conflict blocks, not how often it occurs. Word-level merging could remove some line-level false conflicts (no numbers found). Repository-level conflicts are added by concurrency: the 'independent workspaces' design document by a Google engineer (pull request 10123, 2026-09-05, under review) says divergence risk grows with the number of workspaces, that 'jj sync --all' at Google frequently leads to trouble, and that reconciling concurrent operations can create divergent commits. No rate is given.

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > One full Git object store per repository (no partial clone), shared by all workspaces; each workspace is a real checkout of all files, or of a subset with 'jj workspace add --sparse-patterns'. Dependencies and other ignored files are not shared between workspaces. A virtual file system and better large-file storage are roadmap items. Operations and views are content-addressed files; 'jj util gc' prunes old operations (two weeks by default, after 'jj op abandon'). Refs: colocated repositories with very many refs get slower because every command imports them.
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > No published load test. Largest described single-repository use found: five parallel agents on workspaces, one practitioner (minikin.me, 2026-10-21, jj 0.46; anecdotal, no measurements). A third-party tool (Isolate) states 8-12 agents as its design target (search summary, not opened). Google runs jj with a server, daemon and commit cloud that are not open source; its concurrency is not published. Landing throughput is that of the Git host.
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > Anecdote (N=1 user, 5 agents, 2026-10). Single-agent tool benchmarks exist (vc-bench and jj-benchmark), both synthetic and without concurrency.
- **Coordination limit:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] Not measured. Documented limits are qualitative: shared operation log, undo and 'op restore' across workspaces, divergence risk growing with the number of workspaces, and every fetch or rebase by one agent changing the view of all. Judging from these, trouble starts with a handful of agents that rewrite shared commits, and is mild when each agent works on its own stack from main.

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > n/a as a feature: jj is a client and runs no pre- or post-merge tests; gating stays with the Git host's CI or merge queue. Locally, 'jj run -j N -r \<revset> -- \<command>' tests a whole stack in isolated working copies, and 'jj fix' runs formatters over a stack.
- **Broken main recovery:**
  > No detection and no automatic revert. 'jj bisect run' (with consistency checks since 0.46.0) finds the first bad revision, 'jj revert' makes the reverting commit.
- **Review model:**
  > n/a: review stays with the forge (GitHub pull requests, Gerrit). Small stacked changes are easier to produce, but stacked pull requests need a community tool.

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > CLI, fully scriptable except for hunk selection. Machine-readable output through templates, including json() on commits, operations and workspaces; '--no-pager', '--quiet', '--ignore-working-copy', '--at-op', '--no-integrate-operation'. No official MCP server, no official skill: the project declined to ship one (pull request 9755 closed 2026-10-31) and points to a wiki page. Third-party: danverbraganza/jujutsu-skill (84 stars, MIT, updated 2026-11-13), onevcat/skills (used by vc-bench), laulauland/jj-hunk (non-interactive hunk split), keanemind/jj-mcp-server (21 stars, last push 2025-05-12, stalled) and a few MCP servers with under 5 stars. Gaps: 'split', 'squash -i', 'diffedit' and 'restore -i' open a TUI (request 9814 merged into 8218), 'describe' and others open an editor without -m, and a central non-interactive switch (ui.interactive, pull request 10440, 2026-10-21) is still open. Harness fit: Claude Code creates Git worktrees by itself; a jj workspace needs WorktreeCreate and WorktreeRemove hooks (third-party examples: muloka/claude-plugins, msmorgan/jj-workflow). Since 0.46.0 a colocated workspace is a real Git worktree, so read-only git commands of the harness work in it.
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > Strongest point of jj. 'jj undo', 'jj redo', 'jj op revert \<id>', 'jj op restore \<id>', 'jj evolog' per change, and '--at-op' to look at any past state; every jj command also snapshots the files. Limit with several agents: the operation log is shared. Until 0.46.0 'jj undo' in one workspace could silently undo another agent's snapshots (issue 10181, real incident, recovered); it now refuses without '--allow-cross-workspace', which means an agent whose last operation is no longer on top must find it and use 'jj op revert' (issue 10476, open).
