'jj op restore' by one agent still wipes the others' operations from the current view (user report, 2026-11-02).
- **Agent cost:**
  > Setup: one binary, a skill or AGENTS.md text, one workspace checkout per agent plus dependency installation. Time, synthetic benchmark by GitButler, an interested party (vc-bench, 2026-07-20, jj 0.42.0 plus a skill, 6 history-editing tasks, k=10, single agent): Claude 145.7 s mean with jj against 98.4 s with git (paired difference +47.2 s, 95% CI 6.6 to 87.8), Codex 109.9 s against 92.2 s (interval crosses zero); version-control commands 15.0 against 21.6 (Claude) and 21.0 against 20.4 (Codex); pass rate 119/120 against 120/120. Tokens and cost were not measured. Conflict and rebase tasks across agents were not part of it.
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > Author and committer as in Git, optional commit signing; operations record user, host, time, command line and (since 0.40.0, 2026-04-01) the workspace name, so a local audit trail per agent workspace exists in 'jj op log', but it is not pushed. No per-agent identity, per-folder permission or sandbox: every workspace needs write access to the shared .jj/repo and .git, so one agent can damage all. The independent-workspaces document names per-workspace write ACLs as future work that today's open-source storage cannot support. No model, session or prompt attribution.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > The agent itself (fetch, rebase, resolve, push), or one coordinator that rebases all workspaces in the shared store; the Git host only checks the ref. Snapshotting and rebasing of descendants are automatic.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Primary documentation, changelog and issue tracker are candid and current; that part is strong. Evidence on agents is weak: one vendor-run benchmark (GitButler, competitor), one model leaderboard (TabbyML), blog posts by individuals. No peer-reviewed or multi-agent measurement found.
- **Independent reports:**
  > Positive: Oleksandr Prokhorenko (2026-10-21) runs five agents on workspaces, blocks agents from pushing with a hook, and uses one command to rebase all of them after main moves; he also documents stale workspaces and missing ignored files. TabbyML jj-benchmark (last run 2026-06-16, 52 tasks, jj version not stated): best models pass 94-98%, older ones 85-90%, so models can use jj without a git baseline to compare with. Mixed: Wavect guide (2026-07-22, updated 2026-11-11, consultancy, no measurements) advises Git worktrees with a shared cache first, and jj where rebases, stacks and undo cost operators time. Negative: 'Frequent errors: The working copy is stale' (issue 7538, 22 comments, open; reproduced in seconds with watchman enabled, 2026-10-04); user on Hacker News who returned to Git because agents fall back to git and tools expect worktrees (Okkef, 2026-02-01); another on divergence caused by an editor plugin and an agent in one workspace (KingMob, 2026-02-02); several agents in plain Git worktrees, one ran 'jj git init --colocate' and 'jj rebase', which moved other agents' branches (issue 10741, 2026-11-22, open).

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > Teams on plain GitHub or GitLab, without LFS, submodules or partial clone, whose agents produce stacks of small changes and lose time on interrupted rebases, dirty trees and lost work; a coordinator that integrates several local agents' work.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > Stale workspaces and divergent changes when agents rewrite shared commits or when file watchers race; shared operation log (undo or restore by one agent affects others); mixing jj with mutating git commands (several races fixed only in 0.46-0.47, for example 7349, 10478 and lost Git index changes); agents silently using git and bypassing jj; interactive commands hanging an agent; no hooks, so local pre-commit checks disappear; a forgotten conflict stored in a commit (push guard helps); full clone required; no protection against semantic conflicts; pre-1.0 changes in commands and formats, so agent instructions must be pinned to a version.
- **Minimum useful scale:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] No threshold published. One agent already benefits from undo and automatic snapshots; the gain over worktrees plus a merge queue comes from stacks and frequent rebases, plausibly from about 3-5 agents on one machine, if the blockers do not apply.
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Pilot. It is free, reversible and works against the existing Git host, so test it on a few agents: current version (0.47 or later), one colocated workspace per agent started from main, a skill plus a hook that blocks mutating git, 'jj undo' allowed but 'jj op restore' forbidden, snapshot.auto-update-stale on, watchman off, and landing left to a merge queue. Expect less rebase friction and easy recovery, not fewer conflicts. Use a separate clone per agent instead of workspaces if agents interfere through the operation log. Skip if the repository needs LFS, submodules, hooks or partial clone.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Wait as the shared-store model: no load evidence, shared operation log and no isolation between agents, and the maintainers themselves are still designing independent workspaces for concurrent agents. As a per-agent client in separate clones or sandboxes it can scale with the Git host, but then it solves only each agent's local rebase and undo, and the full clone per agent becomes the cost. Watch independent workspaces (10123), immutable working copies (10640), partial clone (6451) and a server (ERSC, or the roadmap's open-source one).
- **Transferable practice:**
  > Let a rebase always finish and treat conflicts as work items instead of a blocked state; snapshot automatically before every tool call; keep an operation log and make undo scoped to the agent; start every agent from an immutable published commit so that agents never share mutable history; refuse to push conflicted or undescribed commits; compare-and-swap on push; give each change a stable ID that survives rebases. In Git: worktree per agent from origin/main, commit per tool call, 'git rebase' with rerere, 'git push --force-with-lease'.

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > jj-vcs/jj at tag v0.47.1: README, CHANGELOG (0.36.0 to 0.47.1), docs/git-compatibility.md, docs/technical/concurrency.md, docs/working-copy.md, docs/windows.md, docs/bookmarks.md, docs/conflicts.md, docs/FAQ.md, docs/roadmap.md, docs/paid_contributors.md, docs/community_tools.md, CLI reference, https://github.com/jj-vcs/jj/tree/v0.47.1 (release 2026-11-05); issues and pull requests (read 2026-11-24) 2193, 6451, 7349, 7530, 7538, 8780, 9755, 9814, 10091, 10123, 10181, 10317, 10440, 10476, 10478, 10483, 10521, 10640, 10710, 10741, https://github.com/jj-vcs/jj/issues ; wiki page AI-Agent-Skills, https://github.com/jj-vcs/jj/wiki/AI-Agent-Skills ; GitHub API and Homebrew API, 2026-11-24; vc-bench results 2026-07-20 (GitButler), https://github.com/gitbutlerapp/version-control-bench/blob/main/docs/results/full-k10-2026-07-20.md ; TabbyML jj-benchmark, https://tabbyml.github.io/jj-benchmark/ ; Claude Code worktree documentation, https://code.claude.com/docs/en/worktrees ; O. Prokhorenko, 2026-10-21, https://minikin.me/blog/jj-workspaces-for-parallel-agents/ ; Wavect, updated 2026-11-11, https://wavect.io/blog/git-worktrees-vs-jujutsu-ai-coding-agents/ ; Hacker News comments 46846034 (2026-02-01) and 46852960 (2026-02-02), https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=46846034 .
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > jj was not installed or load-tested here; all behaviour is from documentation, changelog and issues. Release date differs by time zone (changelog 2026-11-04, GitHub 2026-11-05 UTC). The compatibility page's 'git-worktree: No' and 'no garbage collection of jj data yet' lag behind 0.46.0 and behind issue 12 (closed 2025-12-12). vc-bench is run by a competitor and its web page pools agents; the figures here come from its repository file; jj 0.42.0 is five releases old. Star and commit figures for third-party skills are small, so popularity says little. The blog posts by Prokhorenko and Wavect were read through page summaries. Google-internal scale of jj and any multi-agent load data were not found; JJ Con 2026 talks were not watched.

**Unverified fields (2):**

- coordination_limit
- minimum_useful_scale

### Workspace isolation worktrees LayerFS BranchFS

*Layer: W workspace isolation*

#### Basics

- **Name:** Workspace isolation: Git worktrees, LayerFS, BranchFS
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > Git worktrees: the git-worktree command, https://git-scm.com/docs/git-worktree ; use in an agent harness: https://code.claude.com/docs/en/worktrees . LayerFS: Ephemeral-AI-Lab/layerfs, homepage https://layerfs.ai/ ; sibling repositories Ephemeral-AI-Lab/dsh-layerfs (plugin for DeepSeek Harness) and ephemeral-sandbox. Not container-image layer filesystems or overlayfs. The tag issue334-diagnostic-evidence-20261016 is an evidence bundle of a demo, not a version. BranchFS: multikernel/branchfs, paper arXiv 2602.08199 ('Fork, Explore, Commit'). Related, same makers: multikernel/branching (Python library BranchContext), multikernel/sandlock (sandbox, BranchFS as optional backend).
- **Maker:**
  > Git worktrees: the Git project; harness integration by Anthropic (Claude Code), Cursor and others. LayerFS: Ephemeral-AI-Lab (GitHub organisation created 2026-07-10); about 4,133 of about 4,180 commits are by one account, yifanxuaaa. BranchFS: Multikernel Technologies; paper by Cong Wang and Yusheng Zheng.
- **What it is:**
  > Worktrees: one repository, several working directories, each with its own HEAD, index and files; objects, refs and configuration are shared. A harness makes one per agent (Claude Code: `claude --worktree`, or `isolation: worktree` for a subagent, under .claude/worktrees on branch worktree-\<name>). LayerFS: a workspace store for agents. One SQLite file per project holds Branches, Commits and Layers as deduplicated, compressed chunks. Each Docker Sandbox runs a daemon; a Workspace is a FUSE view of a committed tree plus an overlay of changes, up to 16 per Sandbox. The whole tree is versioned, including .git, ignored files and node_modules. File-tool calls commit straight to the store; a Bash call can mount, run, commit and unmount. BranchFS: a FUSE filesystem over an existing directory. A branch is a copy-on-write view, files are copied whole when first written, and a leaf branch either commits into its parent or is aborted. Built for best-of-N exploration. The branch() system call in the paper is a design proposal, not shipped. The five asked points. Setup time: worktrees 100 ms-1 s in a paper's table (authors' figure), 9-10 s for 10,000 files on Windows with default settings (own synthetic test), about 50 s with npm ci on a 6 GB repository (one user, independent, production use); LayerFS publishes no measured workspace start time; BranchFS about 0.3 ms (self-reported synthetic microbenchmark, paper's version). Disk: worktrees a full set of files plus dependencies each; LayerFS a deduplicated store that is never collected; BranchFS whole copies of changed files only. Merge-back: worktrees ordinary Git merge, rebase or pull request; LayerFS three-way merge with refusal on overlap, but publishing to main hides Layers it did not merge; BranchFS one winner per parent, no merging. Durability: worktrees that of Git once committed, but harness cleanup has deleted ignored or uncommitted work; LayerFS none claimed against power loss or OS crash; BranchFS no history after commit, and a data-loss bug fixed only after the last release. Windows: worktrees native; LayerFS through WSL2 and Docker; BranchFS none.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > All three: workspace cost, in the sense of isolation between running agents. Worktrees: keep agents from overwriting each other's files while working; they do not lower workspace cost, they are the cost being compared. Textual conflicts are postponed to the merge. Not addressed: throughput, landing contention, textual and semantic conflicts at landing, task collision, shared ports, databases and caches. LayerFS: workspace cost, and detection of textual conflicts at commit or merge with a structured refusal. Not addressed: throughput of landing on GitHub, landing contention beyond a short serial publish step, semantic conflicts, task collision. BranchFS: workspace cost and discarding failed attempts. Not addressed: combining several agents' results (by design), so throughput, landing contention, textual and semantic conflicts and task collision stay open.
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > Worktrees: isolate, then merge. LayerFS: isolate per Branch, three-way merge at commit, serial publish that is refused if main moves during the call. BranchFS: isolate, then first commit wins; siblings forked earlier can no longer commit.

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > Worktrees: GA in every current Git (manual read for 2.56.0, 2026-09-28) and in Claude Code; the manual still calls multiple checkout experimental and advises against it for superprojects. LayerFS: public source release v1.1.0 (2026-10-24); 0 release assets and npm packages marked private, so it is built from source. BranchFS: public source, v0.1.1 (2026-05-14); research prototype.
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > Worktrees: GPLv2 (Git), local. LayerFS: MIT, self-hosted on one machine with Docker. BranchFS: MIT, local; its Python library installs with pip.
- **Price:**
  > Worktrees, LayerFS, BranchFS: free (2026-11-24). The real cost is disk, dependency installation and operator time.
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > Worktrees: part of Git for about ten years; documented isolation mode of Claude Code and other harnesses. Claude Code 2.1.331 was current on 2026-11-24 and its changelog shows worktree fixes throughout 2026. LayerFS: created 2026-08-10; v0.1.6 2026-09-16, v1.0.0 2026-10-21, v1.1.0 2026-10-24; 2,866 stars, 164 forks, 62 open issues and pull requests, pushed 2026-11-24 (GitHub API). v1.1.0 broke 1.0.0 SDK code three days after 1.0.0, under an explicit waiver. A 2.0 line with PostgreSQL stores is in development. No named production user found. BranchFS: created 2026-02-02; 113 stars, 17 forks, 5 open issues and pull requests; last push 2026-05-23, six months ago; community pull requests 37 (2026-08-07) and 38 (2026-10-09) unanswered. The author is now active on multikernel/sandlock (633 stars). No production user found.
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > Worktrees: committed work is as safe as the repository; untracked and ignored files are not (data-loss reports under failure_modes_and_risks). LayerFS: only the Disposable profile (SQLite write-ahead log, synchronous=OFF). The documentation says durability is not claimed, that a power loss or OS crash can lose recent history, and that the store should not hold the only copy of data that cannot be rebuilt. No garbage collection, no migration between store formats; uncommitted Workspace changes die with the container. One dominant author. BranchFS: possibly stalled (inference from maturity_and_activity). Issue 29, commit not atomic and able to drop data silently, was fixed on 2026-05-23, after v0.1.1, so the last release still has it. fsync on a branch does nothing; the paper says durability is enforced at commit.
- **OS support:**
  > Worktrees: native Windows, WSL, Linux, macOS; on Windows expect slower checkouts, NTFS junction hazards and locked files on removal. LayerFS: Linux natively (store on ext4, xfs or btrfs; NFS and virtiofs refused; all agents on one kernel); macOS through a Linux worker container; Windows through a WSL2 worker with its own Docker Engine since v1.1.0; native Windows workspaces are only a proposal (issue 454). Needs Docker and Node 20 or later (package.json at v1.1.0); the DeepSeek Harness plugin needs Node 22.19 or later. BranchFS: Linux with FUSE 3, no root; macOS through macFUSE with some features off; no Windows build. WSL2 should work as Linux, but the project does not say so.

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > Worktrees: plain Git, same client and protocol; a branch can be checked out in only one worktree unless forced; submodule support incomplete; Claude Code leaves LFS files as pointers since 2.1.247. LayerFS: not Git, and by its own help page not a replacement. A .git directory is files in the tree and Git runs inside a Workspace; Branches and Commits are a separate history with no export to Git found. BranchFS: unaware of Git; a .git under the base directory is branched as ordinary files.
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > Worktrees: unchanged: push a branch, open a pull request, existing CI and merge queue. LayerFS: none of its own; landing on GitHub means committing and pushing with Git from inside a Workspace. No GitHub, pull request or CI connector found. BranchFS: none; after the winning branch is committed to the base directory, someone commits and pushes with Git.
- **Migration and exit:**
  > Worktrees: no migration; leave by removing the directories. The adoption cost is a setup script for dependencies, ports and environment files. LayerFS: Init reads the source tree once (default limit 300 s), then agents work through its CLI or SDK in Docker. Partial adoption is possible for disposable tasks next to Git. Leaving means copying files out or pushing with Git from a Workspace; Commit history, blame and Layers stay behind. Opening a 1.0.0 store with 1.1.0 was not tested by the project. BranchFS: mount over a directory, unmount to leave; unmounting removes all branches, and after a commit only the files remain, without history.

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > Worktrees: the agent commits on its own branch. Neither Git nor Claude Code merges anything by itself: the agent or a human rebases, pushes and opens a pull request, or merges locally from the main checkout; a merge queue can serialise the result. Refs are shared, so a branch made or moved in one worktree is at once visible in all. LayerFS: three steps. (1) Commit to a Branch: `workspace.commit`, or any fs write, merges with the head it meets; changes to different files land on top, overlapping changes are refused and nothing is committed. Many agents may write to one Branch; publication takes turns and reads do not wait. (2) `branch.merge(a, b, name)` creates a new Branch or refuses; inputs do not change. (3) `branch.publish` makes a Branch the next Layer of main and replaces main's view: Layers published after the Branch forked are skipped and only listed in `overwrote`. The documented routine is therefore to merge main into a new Branch, test it and publish that. If main moves during the call, publish is refused with HeadMoved. Nothing is retried automatically, and nothing reaches Git or GitHub by itself. BranchFS: the agent calls commit on a leaf branch; the change goes into the parent, at the top straight into the base directory. The code compares the parent's commit count with the count stored at fork and returns a Conflict if any sibling has committed since, even when the files do not overlap. The loser has to create a new branch and redo the work. The paper says losing siblings are invalidated (-ESTALE); the README at head says a sibling stays readable.
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > Worktrees: at merge, rebase or pull request, at hunk level; nothing is detected while two agents edit the same file. LayerFS: at commit and at Branch merge. The v1.1.0 skill reference and the concepts page describe a three-way text line merge with conflicts on overlapping lines, plus add/add, modify/delete, binary, mode and type conflicts; the README, the help page and other passages say any change to the same path conflicts. Publish detects only a move of main during the call. No semantic check. BranchFS: at commit, on the whole parent branch: any earlier sibling commit blocks it. No file, line or semantic comparison.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > Worktrees: Git conflict markers; the agent, a human or a merge driver resolves, and rerere can repeat an earlier resolution. LayerFS: structured refusal (`Conflict` with head, paths, kinds and hunks). After a Workspace Commit conflict the markers are written into that Workspace, which then follows the new head, so the agent edits and commits again; after a refused fs write it re-reads and redoes; after a refused Branch merge nothing is written and it resolves on forks of both inputs. A `/.lww` rule lets the side with the newer modification time win for named paths and drops the other; a Workspace commit can also be forced. BranchFS: rejection only; the loser keeps a readable branch and replays the work by hand on a new one.
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > Worktrees: nothing happens until the agent rebases or merges. Claude Code starts from the remote default branch, fetching if the last fetch is older than 24 hours (capped at 5 s); `worktree.baseRef` can select the local head. Retry cost: a rebase plus a test run. LayerFS: a commit to a moved Branch merges if nothing overlaps. A publish from a stale fork is accepted and hides the newer Layers, a lost-update hazard for agents that skip the merge or ignore `overwrote` (the older Layers stay in history). BranchFS: the branch keeps seeing the parent as it was at fork; once a sibling has committed, the whole commit is refused and the retry cost is the task or a manual replay.
- **Pre write coordination:**
  > Worktrees: none, apart from refusing to check out one branch twice; `git worktree lock` protects a directory against removal and is not an edit lock. LayerFS: none. BranchFS: none; races are settled by who commits first.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > None of the three is shown to lower the conflict rate at landing. Worktrees: conflicts are moved to the merge. Indirect evidence, self-reported by authors, synthetic benchmarks, figures as read in the sibling item Multi-agent_coordination_research: CAID (arXiv 2603.21489; one worktree per engineer, 4 engineers, Sonnet 4.5) 59.1 against 56.1 for an instruction-only shared workspace on Commit0-Lite, and 63.3 against 55.5 on PaperBench; STORM (arXiv 2605.20563; competing authors who re-ran the worktree baseline, Sonnet 4.6, macro score on Commit0-Lite) 63.8 worktrees, 66.4 single agent, 82.5 write-time checking. These are task scores, not conflict counts. Counts of textual conflicts in agent pull requests come from simulated merges, not from worktree runs, and are in the sibling item Conflict-rate_studies_of_agent_PRs. LayerFS: no measurement of conflicts, merges or rework found; its benchmarks cover file operations, storage and filesystem correctness. BranchFS: no measurement; by design one sibling per parent lands, so with N agents on one parent N-1 results are refused in each round (reasoning from the code, not measured).

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > Worktrees: full checkout of tracked files per agent over one shared object store; relief from sparse checkout, `--no-checkout`, symlinked directories and a shared package store. Dependencies and build output are not shared. Independent single-user reports, production use: a 6 GB repository, 3.9 GB and about 50 s per full worktree with npm ci, against 170 MB and about 4 s with sparse checkout plus copy-on-write cloning, and three .NET worktrees of 68 GB apparent size using about 3.76 GB through ReFS block cloning (Dedini, 2026-06-11); ten worktrees of two applications 9.9 GB in total, about 880 MB of dependencies per worktree, 2,833 MB recovered by hard-link deduplication on ext4, which has no reflink (Geniar, 2026-07-24); two Cursor worktrees 9.82 GB after 20 minutes on a 2 GB repository (forum user on Windows, read through a search summary). Own test, synthetic, 2026-11-24 (Windows 11, NTFS, Git 2.54, 10,000 random 2 KB files, no dependencies, one machine): sequential `git worktree add` 8.8-10.5 s (N=5), with checkout.workers=8 2.4-2.6 s (N=3), without checkout 0.2 s (N=1), ten at once 19.3 s in total without failure; 42 MB on disk per worktree for 20.5 MB of data. Paper table (DeltaBox, arXiv 2605.22781): worktree as a checkpoint 100 ms-1 s, restore 100 ms-1 s, writes proportional to the number of files; authors' figure. LayerFS: one SQLite store with content-defined chunks, zstd and deltas, FUSE view on top, nothing copied at fork, never collected. Self-reported synthetic benchmark on 1.0.0, one sample, arm64 Mac with Docker Desktop: 157 snapshots of one project, 4.94 GB of trees, in 100.1 MB against 1,994.6 MB for Btrfs with zstd (19.9 times). No benchmark against Git worktrees. BranchFS: base directory plus whole-file copies of changed files; no deduplication. Since pull request 32 (2026-05-19) creating a branch also snapshots the parent's visible tree, so the paper's constant creation time may no longer hold (inference, not measured).
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > Worktrees: no controlled load test on one shared repository found. One Hacker News user says he runs up to about 100 worktrees (2026-10-21, independent, unverified). A Claude Code issue reports 13 parallel subagents, of which 5 committed and 8 failed on lock contention at commit (55724, 2026-05-03, one user, unverified). Own synthetic test: ten `git worktree add` at once, all succeeded (2026-11-24). In the sibling item Swarm_case_studies_and_task_partitioning two swarms avoided worktrees on one .git: 16 agents with a clone per container, and one vendor research run with an own copy of the repository per worker (both self-reported); the other cases are not described or mixed. Landing throughput is that of the Git host and merge queue. LayerFS: a recorded demo with a lead and five agents on own Branches (self-reported); fsstress with four processes (synthetic); documented limits of 16 Workspaces per Sandbox and six calls at once per process and per Sandbox. No landing throughput published. BranchFS: the paper measures single operations, not concurrent agents; no demonstration with many agents found.
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > Worktrees: independent single-user reports and one own synthetic test; no controlled study. LayerFS: self-reported synthetic benchmarks, one sample each, 1.0.0, one laptop, and a recorded demo. BranchFS: self-reported preliminary microbenchmark, median of 10, one laptop (Ryzen 5 5500U, 8 GB, Linux 6.17), preprint.
- **Coordination limit:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] No published threshold for any of the three. Worktrees, user reports on Claude Code, not reproduced here: creation races on .git/config.lock from 3 parallel subagents (issues 34645, 47266); a different fault, lock failures at commit, intermittent at 5 agents and near-certain at 10 or more, 8 of 13 failed in one run (issue 55724, the reporter's own diagnosis). The own test of ten parallel `git worktree add` had no failure but configured no upstream tracking and made no commits, so it exercises neither fault. One machine's disk and the shared refs bound the rest; no curve measured. LayerFS: one kernel and one publication lane per store, no load data. BranchFS: one landing per parent and round.

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > Worktrees: none of their own; existing pre-merge checks and merge queue. Local test runs need own ports, databases and caches per worktree. LayerFS: none enforced; the agent runs tests inside a Sandbox before publishing. BranchFS: none; a caller can test in a branch and commit only the passing one.
- **Broken main recovery:**
  > Worktrees: Git bisect and revert, as usual. LayerFS: every Commit and Layer stays readable and blame names the Commit and tool call per line; repair by forking from an earlier state and publishing it. No automatic detection. BranchFS: none after a commit to the base; before commit, abort.
- **Review model:**
  > Worktrees: human or agent pull request, unchanged. LayerFS: no review step; Branch and file diffs, and a live history view in the DeepSeek Harness plugin. BranchFS: none; the caller picks the winning branch.

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > Worktrees: Git CLI, non-interactive, `git worktree list --porcelain` for machine-readable output; in Claude Code a flag, a subagent setting, and WorktreeCreate and WorktreeRemove hooks that can replace the Git logic with another tool; `.worktreeinclude` copies chosen ignored files. LayerFS: a `layerfs` command with one SDK call per command and JSON results, three JavaScript packages, agent skills in the repository, typed refusals (Conflict, HeadMoved and others). Works today in DeepSeek Harness through dsh-layerfs; Pi is announced. No Claude Code or Codex integration and no MCP server found. BranchFS: CLI, a control file (.branchfs_ctl) and ioctls, `@branch` virtual paths for bind mounts, Python library multikernel/branching. No MCP server found.
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > Worktrees: commits, reflog and stash cover committed or stashed work; nothing covers deleted untracked or ignored files. `git worktree repair` and `prune` fix metadata. LayerFS: fork from any earlier Commit; nothing is rewritten. Uncommitted Workspace changes are lost with the container. BranchFS: abort before commit; no undo after commit; unmount drops all branches.
- **Agent cost:**
  > Worktrees: no extra tokens; seconds to about a minute of setup, dependency installation, disk. LayerFS: a Docker Sandbox and daemon, a FUSE layer, and a skill plus explicit merge-before-publish steps in the agent's context (token cost not published). Self-reported synthetic file benchmarks, 1.0.0, one sample, against a FUSE passthrough baseline: small files 1.27-10.71 times as fast, but fio sequential write 0.31, sequential read 0.81 and random write 0.40 times as fast. BranchFS, all figures from the makers' synthetic microbenchmark, self-reported: 0.29-0.32 ms to create and 0.32-2.1 ms to commit (internal daemon time without the CLI); sequential read 1,655 MB/s through FUSE, 19% of native, and 7,236 MB/s, 82%, with passthrough. A lost race costs the whole task.
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > Worktrees: no security boundary; a directory with the user's rights, and the shared .git is writable by all. Claude Code blocks edits, commands and Git redirection into the main checkout, but its documentation says it does not track every shell write. Attribution is the Git author and message; no per-folder rights. LayerFS: a container per Sandbox; blame can name the Commit and tool call. No per-agent access control found. BranchFS: files only; network and IPC effects are not rolled back, and absolute symlinks lead outside the branch (paper). No attribution. Sandlock, by the same makers, adds process confinement.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > Worktrees: the agent or a human through Git, plus a merge queue if there is one; the harness creates and removes directories. LayerFS: the agent (fork, commit, merge main, test, publish); no orchestrator included. BranchFS: the calling program or orchestrator, which picks the winner.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Worktrees: official documentation plus independent user reports without controlled measurements. LayerFS: vendor self-report; no independent benchmark or production report found. Main weakness: single samples on one laptop, for 1.0.0 and not 1.1.0, none against worktrees. BranchFS: preprint by the makers with a preliminary microbenchmark; no independent reproduction found. Main weakness: no concurrent agents, and the paper describes older code than the current head.
- **Independent reports:**
  > Worktrees, with remedies: Isaac Dedini (2026-06-11), sparse checkout plus copy-on-write cloning on macOS and ReFS; his first Windows run still used 22 GB per worktree because Git Bash cp bypassed block cloning. Mattias Geniar (2026-07-24), ten worktrees filled a 28 GB volume in about a day; he later moved them to btrfs. Negative: Claude Code issues 34645 and 47266 (parallel creation failing on .git/config.lock; closed as not planned), 55724 (13 parallel agents on macOS, 8 failed at commit on lock contention, then cleanup removed the uncommitted work; closed as duplicate), 75275 (Windows cleanup followed NTFS junctions, about 800 GB lost; fixed in 2.1.205), 48927 (.git destroyed by parallel cleanup; closed as not planned), 98186 (open since 2026-09-29: forced removal of an idle worktree deleted ignored files holding about three days of paid model output), 104311 (open since 2026-10-22: directories left on disk on Windows). Cursor forum thread 146189: disk use on Windows. Wavect (2026-07-22, updated 2026-11-11, consultancy, no measurements): worktrees isolate files, not meaning. LayerFS: Hacker News 50179857 (2026-10-21, 76 points, 48 comments): doubts about a 1.0 without track record and code thought to be mostly written by models, SQLite without sync, name confusion, comparison with Btrfs and prior art; a user with up to about 100 worktrees finds the storage problem real. A byteiota.com article (2026-10-23) has a bot byline and repeats site figures, so it is not counted. BranchFS: Hacker News 46875857 (2026-02-03, 1 point, 1 comment); repository issue 36 (2026-05-29) asks why not Git, the author answers that merging is avoided by design, and a commenter replies that a sandbox plus Git refs covers it. DeltaBox (arXiv 2605.22781) describes it as isolating only filesystem state, without measuring it.

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > Worktrees: everyday parallel agents on one machine whose work lands through pull requests. LayerFS: disposable experiments on large dependency trees where every tool call should be versioned. BranchFS: best-of-N attempts at one task, all but one thrown away.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > Worktrees: config.lock race when several are created at once (34645, 47266); lock failures when many agents commit at once (55724, user diagnosis), made worse when the harness then removes the uncommitted worktree; harness cleanup deleting ignored files (98186) or following junctions (75275, fixed in 2.1.205); disk use and stale directories; shared ports, databases and caches; branch changes visible in all worktrees; gaps with submodules and LFS. LayerFS: no crash durability; publish hides newer Layers unless main is merged first; `.lww` and forced commits drop one side; no garbage collection; store format not migrated; FUSE and Docker dependency; one host; SDK broken between 1.0 and 1.1; for v1.1.0 the Linux FUSE mount gate and the real-store and Docker tests were recorded as not run, and no GitHub Actions gate exists; open defects in the 2.0 line (issues 747, 748, 749, 776); one dominant author; documentation inconsistent on conflict granularity. BranchFS: every agent after the first loses; data-loss fix (issue 29) unreleased; concurrent writes across sibling mounts misrouted or dropped in some runs (issue 28, closed as not reproducible); hard links lose their link, special files unsupported, absolute symlinks escape; fsync without effect; quota drift (issue 31, open); no activity for six months.
- **Minimum useful scale:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] No threshold published. Worktrees are the baseline and pay off from two concurrent agents. LayerFS and BranchFS could pay off only where checkouts with dependencies make setup or disk the bottleneck; at 5-30 agents a shared package store, sparse checkout and reflink copies usually address that first (own judgement).
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Worktrees: use, as the default. They keep Git, GitHub, CI and review; the costs are setup and disk, and both can be reduced: create them one at a time under a lock, set checkout.workers, use sparse checkout, share the package store or make reflink copies of dependencies, give each worktree its own ports and database, retry commits that fail on a lock, never remove a worktree whose commit failed, commit and push before any cleanup, keep valuable ignored files outside, and land through a merge queue. LayerFS: wait; at most a small pilot for disposable experiments with the source kept in Git. No crash durability, no measured gain over worktrees, landing in Git stays manual, and the API changed three days after 1.0. BranchFS: skip for work that has to land together, because only one sibling wins; pilot only as a best-of-N tool, from the head rather than v0.1.1, with no irreplaceable data.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Worktrees: pilot only as a part of the design, not as the whole: several hundred on one shared .git and one disk would meet lock, checkout and disk limits and raise cleanup risk (reasoning, not measured here). Give agents separate sandboxes with a partial clone or a copy-on-write snapshot of a prepared checkout, and keep worktrees for the few agents inside one sandbox. LayerFS: wait. Deduplicated trees with cheap forks fit the target, but the implementation serves one kernel, publishes in one lane and has no load evidence. Re-check at 2.0 for shared stores, a durable profile and a published load test. BranchFS: skip. One winner per parent does not suit hundreds of results that must all land, and the project looks stalled.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > Prepare one base checkout with dependencies and copy it with reflink (btrfs, XFS, APFS, ReFS) instead of installing per agent; check that the copy tool really clones blocks (Git Bash cp did not). Share package stores (pnpm). Create worktrees under a lock or with jitter, and retry a commit that fails on a lock with backoff; set checkout.workers; use sparse checkout. Commit and push often, so a lost workspace costs one step. Make a merge refusal structured (head, paths, kinds) and do not retry silently. Never let a stale publish replace newer state. Make commit or discard an explicit call. Never delete untracked or ignored files automatically, and never follow links or junctions when removing a worktree. Use copy-on-write for best-of-N attempts and Git for landing. Isolate ports, databases and caches as well as files.

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > Git, git-worktree manual for 2.56.0, 2026-09-28, https://git-scm.com/docs/git-worktree ; Anthropic, Claude Code worktrees documentation, read 2026-11-24, https://code.claude.com/docs/en/worktrees ; Claude Code changelog to 2.1.331, read 2026-11-24, https://github.com/anthropics/claude-code/blob/main/CHANGELOG.md ; Claude Code issues 34645 (2026-03-15), 47266 (2026-04-13), 48927 (2026-04-16), 55724 (2026-05-03), 75275 (2026-07-07), 98186 (2026-09-29), 104311 (2026-10-22), each at https://github.com/anthropics/claude-code/issues/\<number> ; LayerFS repository and README at v1.1.0, 2026-10-24, https://github.com/Ephemeral-AI-Lab/layerfs ; LayerFS release v1.1.0, 2026-10-24, https://github.com/Ephemeral-AI-Lab/layerfs/releases/tag/v1.1.0 ; LayerFS skill references at v1.1.0, read 2026-11-25, https://github.com/Ephemeral-AI-Lab/layerfs/blob/v1.1.0/.agents/skills/layerfs-js-sdk/references/methods/branch.md and https://github.com/Ephemeral-AI-Lab/layerfs/blob/v1.1.0/.agents/skills/layerfs-js-sdk/references/rules/conflicts.md ; LayerFS documentation and benchmarks, read 2026-11-24 and 2026-11-25, https://layerfs.ai/docs/concepts/ , https://layerfs.ai/docs/help/ , https://layerfs.ai/docs/core-ideas/ , https://layerfs.ai/benchmarks/ ; LayerFS plugin for DeepSeek Harness, read 2026-11-24, https://github.com/Ephemeral-AI-Lab/dsh-layerfs ; LayerFS issues 454, 747, 748, 749, 776, read 2026-11-24, https://github.com/Ephemeral-AI-Lab/layerfs/issues ; BranchFS repository, README and src/branch.rs at the head of 2026-05-23, https://github.com/multikernel/branchfs ; BranchFS issues 28, 29, 30, 31, 33, 36 and pull requests 26, 32, 34, 35, 37, 38, https://github.com/multikernel/branchfs/issues ; Wang and Zheng, Fork, Explore, Commit: OS Primitives for Agentic Exploration, v1 2026-02-09, v2 2026-03-19, https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08199 ; BranchContext library, https://github.com/multikernel/branching ; Sandlock paper, 2026-05-25, https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26298 ; DeltaBox, 2026-05-21, https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22781 ; Isaac Dedini, Sparse worktrees on a 6 GB repo, 2026-06-11, https://vivecuervo7.github.io/dev-blog/p/sparse-worktrees/ ; Mattias Geniar on worktrees and jdupes, 2026-07-24, https://ma.ttias.be/deduplicating-git-worktrees-ext4-jdupes/ ; Cursor forum thread 146189, https://forum.cursor.com/t/windows-request-to-disable-automatic-worktree-creation-critical-disk-space-issue/146189 ; pnpm, Git worktrees, https://pnpm.io/git-worktrees ; Hacker News, 2026-10-21, https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50179857 ; Hacker News, 2026-02-03, https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=46875857 ; CAID, https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.21489 , and STORM, https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20563 (figures as read in the sibling item Multi-agent_coordination_research) ; Own measurement of `git worktree add`, 2026-11-24, method in checkout_and_storage_model.
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > LayerFS and BranchFS were not installed or load-tested; their behaviour is from documentation, code and issues. The own worktree test is synthetic, one Windows machine, no dependencies, and exercises neither the config.lock race of issue 34645 (no upstream tracking was configured) nor the commit-time lock failures of issue 55724 (no commits were made). Issue reports are user statements and were not reproduced. LayerFS documentation gives both line-level and path-level conflicts; the v1.1.0 conflict reference was taken as closer to the code. A mount time of 5-20 ms appears on its site, but the page says those card numbers are illustrative, so it was not used. Its benchmarks are for 1.0.0, not 1.1.0. Whether BranchFS creation is still constant-time after pull request 32 was not measured, and 'stalled' is an inference. The Cursor figure is from a search summary; a New Stack article on LayerFS returned HTTP 403. No controlled comparison of the three was found.

**Unverified fields (2):**

- coordination_limit
- minimum_useful_scale

### GitButler

*Layer: D VCS or client*

#### Basics

- **Name:** GitButler (but CLI and desktop client)
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > gitbutlerapp/gitbutler, https://gitbutler.com, docs https://docs.gitbutler.com; the CLI binary is called but. No rename found. Related but separate: gitbutlerapp/grit (Grit, GitButler's MIT reimplementation of Git as a Rust library, announced 2026-06-09, about 600 stars, pushed 2026-11-24). It has nothing to do with rtk-ai/grit (agent locks), GritQL, or mojombo/grit (old Ruby library). gitbutlerapp/version-control-bench is the source of vcbench.dev. The bare word but cannot be searched for; other products called Butler are unrelated.
- **Maker:**
  > GitButler Inc; co-founders include Scott Chacon (also a GitHub co-founder) and Kiril Videlov. Active maintainers seen in issues: Byron (author of gitoxide), krlvi, mtsgrd, slarse, davidpdrsn. Series A of USD 17M led by a16z with Fly Ventures and A Capital, announced 2026-04-08 (vendor blog; one aggregator says USD 22M).
- **What it is:**
  > Git client (desktop app, terminal UI and but CLI, Rust core) that keeps several branches applied in one working directory at the same time. The directory holds the combined changes of all applied branches; behind it GitButler keeps a merge commit on a branch called gitbutler/workspace. An agent edits files in the shared directory and then commits chosen files or hunks, by short ID, to its own branch; each branch is pushed and gets its own pull request. Branches can be parallel or stacked. Since 0.23 (release 2026-10-27, post 2026-10-28) a repository with one applied branch stays a normal checkout, the workspace commit appears only when a second branch is applied, GitButler no longer installs its own pre-commit and post-checkout hooks, and linked worktrees are supported next to parallel branches (but worktree new, list, archive, recycle, remove; .worktreeinclude; copy-on-write copies where the file system allows). History editing commands (absorb, move, split, squash, reorder) rebase dependent commits automatically, and operations are recorded in an operation log.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > Addressed: workspace cost (several tasks share one checkout, one dependency install and one running app) and the version-control labour of an agent (selective commits, splitting, amending into the right commit, stacked pull requests). Partly: textual conflicts (overlapping changes meet in one directory while work is in progress, and conflicted commits are stored and resolved later). Not addressed: throughput of landings and landing contention (no queue), semantic conflicts, task collision (no claims or locks). It also does not give runtime isolation, which the docs say explicitly.
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > One shared working directory with the merge kept live (virtual branches), followed by ordinary Git branches and pull requests; optional isolate-then-merge through linked worktrees. Not pre-write claims, not central compare-and-swap, not CRDT.

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > Usable today by anyone. Desktop app and CLI are public, pre-1.0 (0.23.5, 2026-11-20) with nightly builds. CLI introduced 2026-02-05, Linux CLI announced 2026-03-20 (blog feed dates). A new desktop client built on gpui is an early look (0.23 post).
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > Functional Source Licence with MIT as the future licence (FSL-1.1-MIT, Fair Source): any use except a competing product or service; each version becomes MIT two years after its release. The GitHub API reports NOASSERTION. Not OSI open source until conversion. Runs locally, nothing to host; code hosting stays with GitHub, GitLab or others.
- **Price:**
  > Free to download and use (2026-11-24). The docs state that GitButler does not charge for agent use; model usage is billed by the agent vendor. Paid tiers: not confirmed. gitbutler.com/pricing redirects to the home page and lists no price; the docs page for early supporters offers a voluntary monthly contribution and promises locked-in pricing however the company later charges, which suggests no final price list. Toolradar lists Free, Basic USD 10 and Pro USD 25 a month, citing gitbutler.com/pricing read on 2026-09-28; this could not be confirmed on the vendor's site.
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > Repository created 2023-01-31; 22,015 stars, 1,057 forks, 454 open issues including pull requests, pushed 2026-11-24, not archived (GitHub API, 2026-11-24). Releases: 0.20.0 2026-06-03, 0.21.0 07-06, 0.22.0 07-30, 0.22.3 08-29, 0.23.0 10-27, 0.23.1 10-28, 0.23.2 11-02, 0.23.3 11-10, 0.23.4 11-11, 0.23.5 11-20; nightly daily. Homebrew cask installs: 472 in 30 days, 728 in 90 days, 3,326 in 365 days (Homebrew analytics, independent counter; other channels not counted). Known users: Trigger.dev described using it (2026-04-16). Interfaces still move: hunk assignment was removed from the CLI in 0.22.0, but land became but merge, the ID of the uncommitted area changed from zz to @ in 0.23, and the in-app Claude Code tab was removed in 0.20.0 in favour of the skill.
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > Funded, very active, not archived, no acquisition found. Work is stored as ordinary Git objects and branches, so losing the vendor does not strand code. Data-loss caveats, all user reports: one same-day nightly report (issue 17461, 2026-11-24, open, single reporter) says but pull rebased a linked-worktree branch onto an empty tree and silently dropped files from its commits; issue 14848 (2026-07-17, open, 0.21.0) reports an amend that dropped an unrelated stack and a pull that left refs and workspace commit inconsistent with several agents and 9 to 10 stacks. Release notes of 0.23.3 to 0.23.5 fix failed checkouts that lost uncommitted edits, undo problems and a Windows amend regression.
- **OS support:**
  > Native Windows x86_64 (msi; CLI comes with the app), macOS x64 and arm64 (dmg, Homebrew cask), Linux x86_64 (deb, rpm, AppImage, Flathub, standalone CLI binary). The install.sh CLI installer covers macOS and Linux only. WSL: not documented separately; the Linux CLI is the obvious route, not tested.

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > Full Git repository, Git remotes and Git objects; commits carry a change ID header compatible with Jujutsu's. Plain git commands keep working for reading, but agents are told to use but for writes, because raw git writes while several branches are applied act on the generated workspace commit. Git commit hooks: run again by but commit in the main checkout since 0.23.3; commits in linked worktrees still skip hooks (issue 17363, open). Git merge drivers configured in the repository are honoured (maintainer Byron, 2026-02-08).
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > Works with what exists. but pr new opens pull requests on GitHub, GitLab, Bitbucket and others, with a Gerrit mode; native GitHub stacked pull requests since 0.22.0 (2026-07-30). CI, branch protection, reviews and merge queue stay on the forge; GitButler runs no CI. Direct landing with but merge is refused by protected branches.
- **Migration and exit:**
  > Adoption is cheap: run but in an existing repository; but agent setup writes a skill plus instruction files (AGENTS.md, \~/.claude/rules, \~/.codex) after showing them; one developer or one agent can use it alone. Exit: leave the workspace with normal Git, branches are ordinary; but agent uninstall removes the agent files. Lost on exit: the operation log, workspace metadata about stacks and, if used, GitButler's worktree bookkeeping. The running cost is in agent instructions, since agents must not mix raw git writes with but.

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > Agents commit, GitButler rebases locally, and the change reaches main in one of two ways. (1) Normal: each branch or stack is pushed (but push, with a dry run that exits non-zero when a push would be refused, including conflicted commits) and opened as a pull request (but pr new); review, CI and merge happen on the forge, including its merge queue if one exists. (2) Direct: but merge \<branch> (formerly but land) lands on the target by fast-forward or merge commit (--ff-only, --whole-stack, --yes), then reconciles the other applied branches; protected branches reject it. Updating: but pull fetches and rebases all applied branches and linked worktrees onto the new target; --check previews, --update limits it to the workspace or to worktrees. There is no server component, no queue, no batching and no serialisation beyond the local repository; two machines or two checkouts contend on the remote exactly as with plain Git. Inside one checkout several but processes write the same project state, see coordination_limit.
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > Three points, all at text hunk level. While editing: nothing is detected or blocked; two agents writing the same file simply write it, and a stale whole-file write can overwrite the other's edit. At commit: a hunk that depends on lines committed in another applied branch cannot be put on an unrelated branch; the command refuses and names the branch, and since 0.22.0 adjacent edits are treated as conflicts as in Git. At update or landing: but pull and but merge rebase and mark conflicted commits; but pull --check and but push --dry-run show it earlier. No symbol, entity or semantic detection.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > Conflicts are data: a rebase always completes and conflicting commits are stored as conflicted. The agent lists them with but resolve conflicts \<branch> and resolves with but resolve apply \<path>:\<N> (ours, theirs, AI, or text on stdin); in edit mode zdiff3 markers are used. Pushing conflicted commits is refused. A commit that crosses branches is answered by a text refusal naming the blocking branch, and the remedy is to stack the branch or keep the hunk uncommitted until that branch lands. A conflict with uncommitted files is written as markers into the file. No automatic replay of the agent's work and no server side resolver.
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > The agent runs but pull, which rebases every applied branch at once, so the cost of an update grows with the number of applied branches and one bad operation touches all of them (issue 14848: a crashed pull rebased nine refs but not the workspace commit). Conflicts do not stop the pull, they are parked. Retry cost for an agent is one command plus resolution of the marked commits; all other agents in the checkout see files change under them. Hunk and commit IDs are snapshots and go stale when another agent writes; the agent must re-read status before acting (issue 16224, and maintainer comment in 13028).
- **Pre write coordination:**
  > None. No claims, locks, leases, messaging or write-time checks. The skill file only tells agents that others may share the checkout and that they must commit, amend or discard only their own paths; a bare but commit, but absorb or discard of everything includes other agents' uncommitted work. Branch rules (path or content regex that assigns changes to a branch) are documented for the desktop app, while hunk assignment was removed from the CLI in 0.22.0; the substitute suggested by a maintainer (2026-08-26) is an early draft commit per branch. Early conflict hints between teammates and agents are a vision in the funding post, not a shipped feature.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > No measurement found that GitButler lowers the conflict rate of parallel agents, from the vendor or anyone else. What exists: (a) vcbench.dev, vendor self-report, synthetic benchmark, one agent at a time. The blog post (2026-07-07) describes five scenarios; the report as read (data dated 2026-07-20) has six: 360 runs = 6 scenarios x 3 tools (git, Jujutsu 0.42.0 with a third-party skill, GitButler) x 2 agents (Claude Code with claude-opus-4-8, Codex with gpt-5.5) x 10. Means: 92.2 s git, 110 s jj, 34.0 s GitButler; commands 20.4, 21.0, 4.5; 359 of 360 passed grading. In the GitButler and jj runs raw git writes were blocked. It measures the time of version-control chores, not parallelism and not conflicts. (b) The vendor claim of about 60% faster and 80% fewer commands follows from (a). (c) Plausible mechanism, unmeasured: overlapping edits surface in the shared directory earlier than at pull-request merge, which may lower late textual conflicts but raises the risk of silent overwrites while editing.

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > Ordinary full clone. Default: one working directory for all applied branches, so one dependency install and one build cache, with no isolation of files, ports, database or generated artefacts. Optional linked worktrees under \~/.gitbutler-worktrees, with ignored files copied through .worktreeinclude and copy-on-write where supported; the 0.23 post shows a warm Rust worktree building in 0.22 s against 2.6 s cold (vendor demo, one small project). Local state in a project database (SQLite) plus Git refs. No server storage.
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > Not demonstrated at scale. Largest user report: one checkout with more than 20 applied branches and several agents committing at the same time on 0.23.4 Linux (issue 17258, 2026-11-13, production use, self-reported by the user; the 117 in that issue counts agent sessions over time that used but, not concurrent writers). Another: 9 to 10 applied stacks with several agents on a private monorepo (issue 14848). Exact numbers of simultaneous writers are not stated. No landing throughput figure exists. The vendor benchmark uses a single agent.
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > User issue reports (production, self-reported, a handful) and one vendor benchmark (synthetic, single agent, 360 runs, 2026-07). No simulation or paper.
- **Coordination limit:**
  > Degradation reported with only a few concurrent writers. Issue 13028 (2026-03-25, 3 parallel but processes): SQLite database is locked, and a commit that timed out although it was created; concurrent commit fixed in 0.19.7 (2026-04-02) and race tests added, but a maintainer said on 2026-04-01 that commit is multi-process safe and every other mutation would need the same work. Whether all mutations are serialised by now was not confirmed; the issue is still open. Issue 17258: of 117 sessions, 5 hit a cross-branch commit refusal and none resolved it well; 3 made throwaway commits. The reports suggest a practical ceiling of a handful of agents per checkout (my reading).

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > None of its own. Tests run through existing Git hooks, the agent, and the forge's CI. Because the working directory is the sum of all applied branches, a green local test run proves the combination, not any single branch; each branch must be tested again in CI (also the point of the Wavect article, 2026-10-08). No batching, speculation or affected targets.
- **Broken main recovery:**
  > n/a: GitButler does not own main; detection, revert and bisect come from the forge and Git. Locally, undo and revert are available.
- **Review model:**
  > Human or agent review on the forge's pull requests; stacked pull requests supported. The hidden MCP server has tools to show review cards and to mark a review ready. No risk tiers, no invariant checks.

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > CLI first. but takes --json (standard since 0.22.0), --status-after, short IDs, non-interactive flags, and dry runs on absorb, pull and push; errors are not consistently JSON (issue 17258: --json not applied to errors). Instructions: a SKILL.md of about 34 KB installed with but skill install or the but agent setup wizard for Claude Code, Codex, Cursor, Copilot, Windsurf, OpenCode and others. MCP: a hidden command but mcp serve offers six tools, workspace, commit details, branch details, review card, refresh reviews (read-only) and mark review ready; nothing to commit or branch; the current documentation does not mention MCP. The December 2025 blog described an MCP server whose flag was removed in 0.19.10 (2026-05-08). Hooks: the 2025 Claude Code hooks (post 2025-07-22) put each session on its own branch automatically; no such command was found in the current argument definitions and the docs describe only skill plus CLI. Whether any hook command still works in 0.23 was not confirmed. A hidden but agentlog command records agent logs (experiment).
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > Strong in the single-writer case: every operation writes a snapshot, including uncommitted changes; but undo, but redo, but oplog restore, manual but oplog snapshot. Limits: discarding changes in a linked worktree cannot be undone (0.23.0 notes, although the 0.23 post says worktrees support undo), undo refuses operations it cannot safely reverse on branches checked out in worktrees (0.23.3), and in issue 14848 status, unapply and oplog restore all failed, so the user repaired the repository with Git plumbing.
- **Agent cost:**
  > Setup: minutes (install, but agent setup). Per session: the skill file, about 34 KB (roughly 8,000 tokens at 4 characters per token, my estimate), plus status output. Vendor benchmark: 4.5 commands and 13.3 KB of output against 20.4 commands and 31.7 KB with git (synthetic, single agent, self-reported). The blog claims fewer tokens, but the report publishes no token or cost figures. No coordination messages; agents must re-read status after others write.
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > No per-agent identity, no per-folder permissions, no sandbox: any agent can read or overwrite anything in the checkout. Attribution is by branch name and commit author; the skill makes agents commit to their own branch, and commits carry change IDs. Session, model and prompt are not recorded by default; but agentlog is hidden and experimental. The docs say the instructions steer behaviour and are not access controls, so protection must come from branch protection and the agent's own permission system.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > The agent, through but commands (commit, pull, push, pr, resolve); GitButler does the rebases and dependency tracking; a human or coordinator normally merges on the forge. Left for the agent: choosing files and hunks, keeping to its own paths, resolving conflicted commits, re-reading IDs, running tests.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Product docs, source and issues are first-hand; the only numbers are a vendor self-report on a synthetic single-agent benchmark. User reports are real but few. Main weakness: no test of many agents in one checkout by anyone, and no conflict-rate data.
- **Independent reports:**
  > Trigger.dev CTO Eric Allam (https://trigger.dev/blog/parallel-agents-gitbutler, 2026-04-16): replaced worktrees in a full-stack app because ports, databases and disk made worktrees heavy; one Claude Code session split its work into branches; found the hooks too rigid and same-file edits messy. Wavect, Kevin Riedl (2026-10-08, updated 2026-11-11): shared edits are not locked, a stale full-file replacement can overwrite a peer, plain but commit takes every uncommitted change, a green combined build does not prove each branch; advises one coordinator and testing branch, combined and integration states; no measured speedup. Hacker News thread 47712656 (2026-04-10, 337 points): mostly sceptical; complaints about hook takeover (dropped in 0.23.0) and odd stuck states; some users prefer it to worktrees (mgambati, 2026-06-02) or like testing branches together (treeblah). GitHub: issues 13028, 14848, 16224, 17258 and 17461 (see failure_modes_and_risks). Maintainer Byron in discussion 12228 (2026-02-05) confirms there is no isolation and that overlapping areas can cause races and interference.

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > A small group of agents (about 2 to 5, my judgement) working on mostly separate files of one application that is costly to run twice (ports, database, heavy install), or one agent whose session must be split into several pull requests. Also a better history-editing client for any agent in its own worktree.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > Silent overwrite of a peer's edit, because no locks exist. Commits or amendments that take another agent's uncommitted work when no path is given. Stale IDs between status and action (16224). Cross-branch commit refusal that agents handle badly (17258). Database locked or lost-but-created commits with parallel processes (13028; fix confirmed only for commit). Corrupted workspace after a crashed pull, and dropped stacks (14848). Possible silent data loss with pull and linked worktrees on a nightly (17461, one same-day report, unconfirmed). Agents sharing one runtime: dependency installs, generated files and servers interfere; a green combined build hides branch dependencies; generated and lock files conflict constantly. Hooks skipped in linked worktrees. Repositories far behind upstream used gigabytes of memory until 0.23.5. A fast-moving interface breaks prompts and skills.
- **Minimum useful scale:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] No measured break-even found. Judgement: it pays off from about two parallel tasks when starting another runtime is expensive; where worktrees are cheap, the benefit over worktrees plus a merge queue is mainly ergonomic (stacked pull requests and history editing).
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Pilot. Use it for one or two agents per checkout, or a small group of about 2 to 5 with clearly separate file ownership and one coordinator, on a project where a second runtime is expensive, with ordinary branches, CI and a merge queue doing the landing. Prefer linked worktrees or separate clones when agents need different dependencies, ports or competing attempts. Pin a release, not nightly; do not combine but pull with linked worktrees on old bases until issue 17461 is settled; take manual oplog snapshots before history edits; commit by path only. For 10 to 30 agents in one directory: skip, because the reports of locked state, stale IDs and corrupted workspaces come from fewer agents than that.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Skip as the synchronisation layer. One directory, one local SQLite-backed state, one workspace merge commit, a pull that rebases every applied branch, and no landing queue, claims or server side replay do not scale to several hundred writers; nothing published shows more than tens of branches. As a per-agent client inside worktrees or sandboxes it can still be used, where its value is history editing and stacked pull requests, not synchronisation. Wait if the company ships the multi-agent awareness it describes.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > Give agents one clear rule: commit, amend and discard only by explicit path or ID, never everything. Make a conflict a stored state (conflicted commit plus a resolve command) instead of a stop. Offer a dry run with a non-zero exit code for pull, push and absorb, and machine-readable output including errors. Re-read state after every write, because IDs are snapshots. Record an operation log that includes uncommitted work and take a snapshot before each history edit. Refuse at commit time when a hunk depends on another branch's lines, and say which branch. Test each branch, the combination and the integration state separately. Keep one task per branch even when the directory is shared, and route changes to branches by path rule.

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > gitbutlerapp/gitbutler repository, releases and issues, https://github.com/gitbutlerapp/gitbutler (API read 2026-11-24; release 0.23.5, 2026-11-20); LICENSE.md, FSL-1.1-MIT, https://github.com/gitbutlerapp/gitbutler/blob/master/LICENSE.md; docs https://docs.gitbutler.com/llms-full.txt (parallel branches, worktrees, AI agents, getting started, rules, early supporters); GitButler 0.23 post, https://blog.gitbutler.com/gitbutler-0-23 (2026-10-28); Series A, https://blog.gitbutler.com/series-a (2026-04-08); Introducing the CLI (2026-02-05) and Agent-safe Git (2026-04-16), https://blog.gitbutler.com; Claude Code hooks, https://blog.gitbutler.com/parallel-claude-code (2025-07-22); MCP post, https://blog.gitbutler.com/using-gb-mcp (2025-12-30); Agents tab, https://blog.gitbutler.com/gitbutler-agent-assist (2026-01-09); Agentic benchmarks, https://blog.gitbutler.com/vcbench (2026-07-07), report https://vcbench.dev (data 2026-07-20), source https://github.com/gitbutlerapp/version-control-bench; CLI argument definitions, MCP module and SKILL.md in the repository (files read 2026-11-24); issues https://github.com/gitbutlerapp/gitbutler/issues/13028, 14848, 14831, 15210, 15540, 16224, 17258, 17363, 17461; discussion 12228; PR 13079; Trigger.dev, https://trigger.dev/blog/parallel-agents-gitbutler (2026-04-16); Wavect, https://wavect.io/blog/gitbutler-parallel-ai-agents-one-workspace/ (2026-10-08, updated 2026-11-11); Hacker News https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=47712656 (2026-04-10); Toolradar comparison, https://toolradar.com/compare/gitbutler-vs-nori (updated 2026-11-20); gitbutlerapp/grit, https://github.com/gitbutlerapp/grit and https://blog.gitbutler.com/true-grit (2026-06-09). All read 2026-11-24.
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > Paid tiers could not be confirmed and the sources disagree (docs: later pricing undecided; Toolradar: Basic and Pro with monthly prices). Whether the 2025 Claude Code and Cursor hook commands still exist in 0.23 was not confirmed; the negative finding rests on reading the CLI argument definitions and docs, not on running the binary. Whether all but mutations are now multi-process safe was not confirmed. Issue 17461 was filed on the day of reading (nightly 0.5.2291), one reporter, not reproduced. vcbench numbers come from the vendor; the five-versus-six scenario difference is probably a later extension of the report (inference). Nothing was installed or run, and the Windows CLI was not tested. The claim that the 0.23 post and 0.23.0 notes differ about undo in worktrees rests on those two texts.

**Unverified fields (1):**

- minimum_useful_scale

### Agent claims and locks Agent Mail and Grit

*Layer: G coordination*

#### Basics

- **Name:** Agent claims and locks: Agent Mail (Dicklesworthstone/mcp_agent_mail_rust) and Grit (rtk-ai/grit)
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > Agent Mail: https://github.com/Dicklesworthstone/mcp_agent_mail_rust (Rust, current). Predecessor: Dicklesworthstone/mcp_agent_mail (Python), marked deprecated and superseded by the Rust version. Namesakes: agentmail.to (hosted email for agents), mcp-mail on PyPI, and forks or repacks (jleechanorg/mcp_agent_mail_archived and others). Grit: https://github.com/rtk-ai/grit (moved from pszymkowiak/grit; pszymkowiak/grit-samples is the author's demo). Namesakes, all unrelated: gitbutlerapp/grit (GitButler's Git-in-Rust library, announced 2026-06-09, same month as the last Grit release), GritQL from getgrit, mojombo/grit (old Ruby library), grite (neul-labs), grit-control/grit, X-Scale-AI/GRITS.
- **Maker:**
  > Agent Mail: Jeffrey Emanuel (Dicklesworthstone), one human author; commits credited to him about 6,500, to AI accounts a handful; outside contributions are not merged as a rule. Grit: Patrick Szymkowiak (pszymkowiak), RTK AI, sole human contributor (44 commits).
- **What it is:**
  > Agent Mail: a coordination server for coding agents working in one shared checkout. Agents register an identity, send threaded messages with acknowledgements, and take advisory reservations on file globs (exclusive or shared, with expiry). A guard in Git hooks refuses commits that touch files reserved by others. Live state in SQLite (its own engine, FrankenSQLite), durable ledger in a Git archive; 47 MCP tools (the repository description says 45), 25 resources, a robot CLI, terminal and web UI. It deliberately avoids worktrees. Grit: a CLI that indexes a repository with tree-sitter, lets an agent claim functions, classes or methods, gives each agent its own worktree, and on grit done commits, rebases and merges that branch into the main checkout under one lock. State in SQLite, or in Azure Blob, S3 or R2 for several machines. 13 languages. Both are pre-write claim systems; Agent Mail adds messaging and no landing, Grit adds worktrees and landing and no messaging.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > Agent Mail: task collision and textual conflicts in a shared directory (advisory), visibility between agents. Workspace cost is avoided by sharing one checkout. Not addressed: throughput, landing contention, semantic conflicts, and it enforces nothing until commit. Grit: task collision at symbol level, textual conflicts between agents, landing contention through a serial merge lock, workspace isolation (worktrees, so workspace cost is paid). Not addressed: throughput beyond one merge at a time, semantic conflicts, anything outside indexed symbols (new functions, imports, configuration, documents).
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > Agent Mail: pre-write claim (advisory lease with expiry) in one shared space, checked at commit. Grit: pre-write claim at AST-symbol level, then isolate then merge with a serialised merge.

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > Agent Mail: usable today, v0.3.41 (2026-11-21), pre-1.0. Grit: usable today, v0.4.0 (2026-06-14), no push since; Cargo metadata calls it experimental.
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > Agent Mail: MIT text plus a rider, shown by GitHub as NOASSERTION, not OSI open source. The rider grants no rights to OpenAI, Anthropic, their affiliates or anyone acting on their behalf or under their direction (including contractors, agents and service providers), forbids giving those parties access to the software or derivatives, counts executing, benchmarking, analysing and indexing as use, must travel with every copy, and ends the licence automatically on breach. Whether using it with Claude Code or Codex, whose models see its output or source, is affected is a legal question with no answer in the repository; have counsel read it before any commercial use. Self-hosted only. Grit: Apache-2.0 (history: MIT, then source-available on 2026-03-23, then Apache-2.0 on 2026-03-25). Self-hosted CLI; lock store local or in your Azure, S3 or R2 account.
- **Price:**
  > Both free (2026-11-24). Agent Mail: local server only. Grit: local free; Azure, S3 or R2 storage charges apply for the shared backend.
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > Agent Mail (Rust): created 2026-02-05, 282 stars, 50 forks, 17 open issues of 376 in total, pushed 2026-11-24; v0.3.38 10-08, v0.3.39 10-13, v0.3.40 11-01, v0.3.41 11-21. Release 0.3.41 downloads: 477 Linux musl, 81 Windows, 71 Linux gnu, 59 macOS arm, 55 Linux arm. Python predecessor: 2,245 stars, created 2025-10-23, last v0.3.5 2026-11-22, deprecated. Known users: the author's own agent setup and issue reporters. Grit: created 2026-03-17, 127 stars, 15 forks, v0.3.0 2026-04-06, v0.4.0 and last push 2026-06-14, issues filed since September unanswered, pull request 32 (2026-06-15, hardening) open; v0.4.0 downloads 31 Linux, 15 macOS arm, 4 macOS x64. No known users. Counts from the GitHub API, 2026-11-24.
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > Agent Mail: very active, one maintainer, no company. Data-loss history is serious: acknowledged writes lost after registration (issue 213, v0.3.21, 2026-07-28, closed 2026-08-19); repeated SQLite corruption under swarm load (issue 278, several reporters from 2026-08-30 to 2026-10-02, closed 2026-11-04 after engine fixes, no later recurrence reported there; issues 152 and 257 earlier); rebuilding from the archive loses read and acknowledgement state (issue 404, 2026-11-22, open). Live state depends on a SQLite engine written by the same author. Whether corruption is gone was not confirmed. Grit: stalled, no push since 2026-06-14, maintainer active elsewhere (rtk-ai/rtk). Past bug: grit done flipped core.bare, skipped the merge and lost the branch reference (issue 21, 2026-06-11, fixed in v0.4.0). Lock loss is limited to metadata; worktrees and branches stay in Git.
- **OS support:**
  > Agent Mail: Linux x86_64 and aarch64, macOS x64 and arm64, Windows x86_64 (native zip and PowerShell installer); signatures are checked by default; some open Windows reports (issue 393, 2026-11-17; issue 352 about the installer). Grit: Linux and macOS only, no Windows build (Unix sockets); WSL should work, not tested.

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > Agent Mail: keeps ordinary Git, adds hooks (pre-commit, optional pre-push, post-commit receipts); ledger in a separate Git archive. Documented incompatibility: Git 2.51.0 index race, with an optional lock to serialise Git calls. Grit: ordinary Git repository with worktrees and branches named agent/\<id>; needs the main checkout clean at merge time.
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > Agent Mail: no pull-request or CI features; works beside them, the guard can also run at push. It is commonly paired with the author's Beads task tools. Grit: grit session start, pr and end can open a pull request from the session; otherwise merges directly into the current local branch; no CI integration and no test step.
- **Migration and exit:**
  > Agent Mail: install the binary, run the server, register agents through MCP or CLI, install the guard in each clone, write instructions into agent files; 100 or more environment variables and 47 tools make it heavy. Leave by removing hooks and server; loses mail history and reservations, code is untouched. Partial adoption possible (reservations only). Grit: grit init plus a prompt snippet; leave by deleting .grit and the worktrees, code and branches remain. Partial adoption is possible for a few symbols, but agents that do not use it bypass it.

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > Agent Mail: none. Agents edit the same checkout and commit with Git themselves, or a human does; the guard only blocks commits touching other agents' reservations. No merge, queue, rebase or tests; build slots (optional) coordinate heavy builds. Landing on main follows whatever the team uses. Grit: the agent runs grit done. The tool stages everything in the agent's worktree (git add -A), commits, takes a file lock (merge.lock), refuses if the main checkout has tracked changes, rebases the agent branch inside its worktree, and merges with --no-ff into the checked-out branch of the main checkout, then releases the agent's symbol locks. If the rebase conflicts it aborts and tries a plain merge; if that fails it aborts, returns an error and keeps branch and worktree; locks are released either way. Landing is single-file, one merge at a time, with a 200 x 50 ms wait for the lock (issue 38), so about 10 seconds and then a hard failure, and no queue order. Optional grit session pr pushes a pull request. Before v0.4.0 the rebase ran in the wrong place and silently did nothing (comment in source).
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > Agent Mail: at reservation (path or glob overlap between active leases, symmetric matching, re-checked inside the database transaction), and at commit through the guard (path); pre-push optional. Nothing at the write itself. Granularity: file or glob. Grit: at claim, per indexed AST symbol (read or write mode); then ordinary Git line conflict at grit done. It does not check that the diff stays inside the claimed symbols (no diff code in the source read, and it adds all changes), so the claim is only a promise; two agents editing different functions but adjacent lines, imports or a shared new function still meet at Git's line merge.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > Agent Mail: the request returns a structured answer, granted paths plus conflicts, each naming holder, pattern, exclusivity and expiry; conflicting paths are not granted, partial grants are possible, there is no waiting or queue, and the agent decides (message, wait, work elsewhere). Error envelopes fail closed with a do-not-edit flag and recovery hints. A stale holder can be force-released by tool, and expired leases lapse. Merge conflicts are ordinary Git. Grit: claim reports blocked by whom in text; --queue or --wait available; on a merge conflict the command fails, keeps the branch, releases the locks, and leaves resolution to the agent in the worktree. No automatic replay, no resolver.
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > Agent Mail: none; all agents share the working files, so there is no stale base, only stale reads; a reservation can be renewed and its expiry is shown. Retry cost: re-read the file. Grit: the worktree starts from the current branch; at done it rebases onto the branch tip (since v0.4.0) and merges; a claim that expires while work continues, without a heartbeat from the agent, can be taken by another agent.
- **Pre write coordination:**
  > Agent Mail: leases on globs, exclusive or shared, expiry at least 60 s (examples use 3600 s), advisory; messaging, threads, acknowledgements, contact rules; a read-only conflict check; force release; a background watcher for dead agents and stale leases that runs in shadow mode by default since v0.3.31. Agents can edit without a lease; enforcement only at commit and push, and it can be skipped with the hook bypass flag, and when no mailbox archive is found the guard warns and allows the commit (files under .beads are always exempt). Grit: claim of symbols in write or read mode, --with-deps, --queue, --wait, expiry 600 s by default and heartbeat; advisory because diffs are not checked; a claim of several symbols is not atomic and can leak part of the grant (issue 35), status has shown expired for live locks (issue 34).
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > Agent Mail: no measurement found that it reduces conflicts, by the author or others; the benefit is asserted. Benchmarks measure server latency only (self-reported, synthetic). Grit, self-reported synthetic benchmark (scripted sed edits by shell loops, not agents), read in the repository: the README claims raw git loses 70 to 90% of work and Grit 0%, and zero conflicts with any number of agents.
