Reading scripts/synthetic/bench.sh and the published assets/bench_data.json (2026-04-06, v0.3.0 era) shows why this is not usable as evidence: (1) The git arm prepends a header line to every touched file, and the script comment says this is done to guarantee conflicts; the Grit arm does not. In the git arm each agent branch that conflicts is simply abandoned, with no rebase or resolution. (2) Grit's failures are not measured: the summary prints Merges FAILED and failure rate for Grit as fixed text 0 and 0%, claim and done output is discarded, and a refused claim exits silently. Conflicts are counted with git status after Grit has already aborted the merge, so the count can only be non-zero if that abort failed. (3) The same README's Azure table (self-reported, synthetic) shows 20 merges at 10 agents, 40 at 20, 54 at both 30 and 50, and 76 at 50 on 44 symbols (pi-calc). Two commits per landing (source: one for the agent's changes, one merge) make that 10, 20, 27, 27 and 38 agents landed. ts-api has about 27 claimable functions (my estimate from the slope of the git arm in the data below), so agents beyond that had nothing to claim and the first four rows are consistent with every claimant landing; the pi-calc row leaves 6 of 44 unexplained. (4) assets/bench_data.json tells a different story (own derivation, not re-run; generating script not stated). Its grit_ok is not a count of agents: it counts log lines containing grit:, and one agent in three rounds gives 6, so half of it is landings. A script that counts merge lines only (tests/bench_sweep.sh) would give 3, and runs five rounds on one project, so it is not the source. Landings of agent runs, Grit vs git. ts-api: 2 agents 3 vs 3 of 6; 5: 3 vs 3 of 15; 10: 7 vs 7 of 30; 15: 15 vs 15 of 45; 20: 15 vs 15 of 60; 25: 15 vs 15 of 75. pi-calc: 10: 5 vs 6 of 30; 20: 11 vs 12 of 60; 25: 26 vs 25 of 75. rust-service: 10: 8 vs 6 of 30; 20: 14 vs 15 of 60; 25: 15 vs 15 of 75. Under this reading Grit lands about as many as the rigged git arm, and its ceiling is 5 landings per round in ts-api and in rust-service, each of which has exactly five source files, which looks like one landing per file rather than per function (inference, cause not established; 9 per round in pi-calc, 14 files). Whatever the counting, these data do not show 0% of work lost. At 30 agents and more the comparison is invalid in the other direction, because the git arm counts an agent with nothing to edit as a successful merge (so its success rises to 84 of 150 at 50 agents in ts-api). The two result sets of the README and JSON were not reconciled. A real-agent script exists but publishes no results.

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > Agent Mail: one shared ordinary checkout, no copies; state in SQLite plus a Git archive of every message and lease under the storage root, which grows (user report of 1.09 million archive files, issue 382). Grit: full worktree per agent under .grit/worktrees, shared object store; locks in SQLite or cloud object store.
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > Agent Mail: 60 agents over 480 s in three projects with a server kill midway (author's release test, self-reported synthetic; reservation latency missed its 5 s p99 target in v0.3.38 and v0.3.39); about 26 Claude Code and Codex agents in user production with corruption reported (issue 278, 2026-10-02, independent user, self-reported). Cumulative counts such as 12,236 identities and 203,498 messages (issue 382) are not concurrency. Landing throughput: none, it does not land. Grit: README claims 50 agents in parallel on Azure (self-reported, synthetic; 27 of about 27 possible claimants in ts-api and 38 of 44 in pi-calc landed by my two-commits count); the test projects have 27 (estimated) to 44 symbols, so fewer distinct writers than agents. Landing throughput not published; merges are serial.
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > Agent Mail: author's stress tests (synthetic, 30 to 60 agents), plus user issue reports (production, small n). Grit: author's synthetic shell benchmark. No independent benchmark, no paper for either.
- **Coordination limit:**
  > Agent Mail: single machine or shared file system by design, README limitation. Author's release tests: at 60 agents the reservation p99 was about 25 s in v0.3.38 and about 15 s in v0.3.39, and 0.9 to 2.2 s in three runs before v0.3.40 (measured on an earlier commit than the release); issues 344 and 345 remain open. User corruption reports at about 10 or more concurrent writers (issue 152) and 26 (issue 278), on older versions. Latency grows with archive size (issue 382: inbox 10 to 22 s, start 228 s). Grit: merge lock fails after about 10 s with no queue, so it degrades when merges pile up; agents beyond the number of symbols cannot claim anything (44 symbols in the README's 50-agent pi-calc run).

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > None in either. Agent Mail: guard before commit and optionally before push, not tests; build slots can serialise heavy builds. Grit: no test step before the merge into the main checkout.
- **Broken main recovery:**
  > Agent Mail: n/a: it does not land or watch main. Grit: none; a bad merge is a normal Git merge commit to revert, and main can take it without tests.
- **Review model:**
  > Agent Mail: none of its own; messages and acknowledgements can carry review requests between agents, and the archive gives an audit trail. Grit: none; optional pull request through grit session pr.

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > Agent Mail: 47 MCP tools over HTTP (127.0.0.1:8765) or stdio, plus a robot CLI with JSON and token-efficient output, and a structured error envelope; idempotency key on reservation requests; installable instruction files. Non-interactive. Grit: CLI with human text only, no JSON flag, no MCP; a prompt snippet shows Claude Code how to use it; errors are text on standard error.
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > Agent Mail: leases expire, can be released or force-released; every message and reservation is in a Git archive, but rebuilding from it loses read and acknowledgement state (issue 404). File edits are recovered with Git only. Grit: failed merges keep branch and worktree; claims released on done and after expiry; v0.4.0 preserves the agent branch when a merge is refused.
- **Agent cost:**
  > Agent Mail: 47 tool descriptions in context, registration, reservation and release calls, message reading, plus token use for mail threads; no token figure published. Grit: claim and done commands per task, one worktree per agent (disk and dependency install), no message cost; cost of re-running an agent after failed done not published.
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > Agent Mail: named agent identity with registration token (rotate, revoke), optional session-bound identity, HTTP bearer or JWT; with no token set, localhost needs no authentication; no per-folder permission and no sandbox; the Git archive ties messages, leases and acknowledgements to agents, with optional commit receipts. Grit: agent name is a command argument, not verified; no permissions, no sandbox; provenance is the commit message and agent/\<id> branch; the Azure key is stored as plain text (issue 36).
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > Agent Mail: the agent asks, waits or messages by itself; server only records and answers. Grit: the agent runs claim and done; the tool merges. Both need a planner to divide work; neither orchestrates agents.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Author self-report in both cases. Agent Mail: honest limitations in README and detailed release notes, no outcome evidence. Grit: benchmark favours itself by construction (see conflict_rate_effect). Main weakness: no independent measurement of either.
- **Independent reports:**
  > Agent Mail: corruption and latency reports by users (issues 213, 278, 344, 345, 382, 404, 2026-07 to 2026-11); Hacker News commenter jauntywundrkind (47146975, 2026-02-25) calls it early and great; jimmySixDOF (47340141, 2026-03-11) praises the author's related setup. Praise quoted in the README is not a review. No independent measured review found. Grit: only issue filers, fglogan (issue 21, 2026-06-11), brcourt (among 34, 35, 36, 38; filed since September 2026), kocaemre; no reviews or Hacker News discussion found.

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > Agent Mail: a small team on one machine or shared file system where agents must share one checkout, and where advance notice and messages between agents matter more than enforcement. Grit: a research or demo setting of a few agents on a repository with well separated functions on Linux or macOS.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > Agent Mail: no write enforcement, so any agent that skips the reservation, uses the bypass flag, or works before the guard is installed can overwrite others; glob reservations that are too wide serialise the team; lease expiry during a long edit; corruption and lost acknowledgements under swarm load in past versions; growth of archive and latency; one maintainer, custom database engine; licence rider; 47 tools and 100 settings to learn. Grit: stalled project; advisory claims not checked against the diff; multi-symbol claims not atomic; locks released even when the merge failed, so others may take the symbol while the branch is unmerged; merge lock without order; claim only what is indexed, so new code, imports, config and tests are unprotected; rebases on a dirty or wrong state hidden until v0.4.0; no tests before merge; plaintext cloud key; no Windows. Both: claims do not catch semantic conflicts, and both fail if agents ignore them.
- **Minimum useful scale:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] No published break-even. Reasoning only: claims pay off when several agents often want the same files, and cost more than they save when a planner can already divide work by file; Agent Mail is aimed at roughly 5 to 30 agents on one machine (my reading), Grit at fewer than its symbol count.
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Agent Mail: Pilot only, after a licence review, for a team that keeps one shared checkout and wants leases plus messages; start with reservations and the guard, and measure blocked commits, waiting time and server latency; keep backups because of the corruption history. If worktrees or separate clones are already used, skip it and copy the lease protocol. Grit: Skip. The project is stalled, its benchmark is not evidence, claims are not enforced, it runs no tests before merging, and it is Linux and macOS only. Try symbol-level claims inside another tool if you want them.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Agent Mail: Skip. Single machine by design, reservation p99 of seconds at 60 agents in the author's own tests, corruption history, advisory only, no landing. Reuse the ideas in a server. Grit: Skip. One serial merge lock with a 10 s timeout, symbols limit the number of writers, stalled, no tests, no queue.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > Return holder, pattern and expiry in every refusal, so an agent can wait or ask instead of retrying blindly. Make leases expire (60 s minimum, renewable) and let expired ones be taken. Offer a read-only conflict check and idempotent requests. Check again at commit and before push, because a claim is only a promise, and verify that the diff stays inside the claim, which Grit omits. Make multi-item claims all or nothing. Reserve narrow paths, not broad globs, and no broadcast messages. Put a fair queue (FIFO) in front of the merge, test the merged tree, and do not release claims when the merge failed. Plan work so that claims rarely collide; a sibling result on swarm case studies reports that splitting work beat locking. Measure blocked claims, waits and rework before judging.

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > Agent Mail: https://github.com/Dicklesworthstone/mcp_agent_mail_rust (README, LICENSE with rider, reservation source, releases v0.3.38 to v0.3.41, read 2026-11-24; issues https://github.com/Dicklesworthstone/mcp_agent_mail_rust/issues/152, 213, 257, 278, 344, 345, 351, 352, 354, 362, 382, 393, 404, 407); predecessor https://github.com/Dicklesworthstone/mcp_agent_mail (deprecated); Grit: https://github.com/rtk-ai/grit (README, CHANGELOG, src/git/mod.rs, src/cli/mod.rs, scripts/synthetic/bench.sh, scripts/sweep/bench.sh, scripts/lib/common.sh, scripts/throughput/bench.sh, assets/bench_data.json of 2026-04-06, releases v0.3.0 2026-04-06 and v0.4.0 2026-06-14, read 2026-11-24; issues 21, 34, 35, 36, 38; pull request 32); namesake https://github.com/gitbutlerapp/grit; Hacker News comments https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=47146975 and https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=47340141 (2026-02-25, 2026-03-11); other lock servers, GitHub API 2026-11-24: asynkor/asynkor (52 stars, Apache-2.0, last push 2026-05-03, file leases over MCP), clash-sh/clash (66, MIT, 2026-07-17, conflict radar across worktrees), amaar-mc/wit (46, MIT, 2026-03-27, symbol locks as warnings plus intents), prakrititz/relayBrain (34, MIT, 2026-10-18), nybarius/SVRF (2), kigster/agent-lock (1, MIT), wcook04/one-checkout (0, MIT, 2026-11-17). All read 2026-11-24; no tool installed or run.
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > Agent Mail: no outcome measurement; whether database corruption is gone was not confirmed, and the latency figures of v0.3.40 were measured on an earlier commit; applicability of the rider to users of Claude or Codex is open; the tool count differs between sources (47 in the README, 45 in the repository description). Grit: the landings comparison is my own arithmetic from the published JSON, checked against the source (two commits per landing, one agent in three rounds gives 6 and README Azure table gives 20 for 10 agents) but not re-run, and the cause of the plateau is inferred; the README Azure table and the JSON (committed 2026-04-06) disagree on landed agents (the first lands all 10 and 20 agents, the second 7 of 30 at 10 agents in ts-api), which may reflect different versions, backends or a scripting fault. Grit's current HEAD is the v0.4.0 state. Other lock servers were only listed from the API, not read.

**Unverified fields (1):**

- minimum_useful_scale

### Oak

*Layer: D VCS or client*

#### Basics

- **Name:** Oak
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > Canonical home is https://oak.space/oak/oak (Oak is hosted on itself); public mirror oakdotspace/oak on GitHub (the old address oakvcs/oak redirects there); crates oakvcs-cli and oakvcs-core on crates.io. The GitHub copy is a snapshot mirror and lags the Oak-hosted repository by about a week (checked 2026-11-24). Unrelated namesakes: oak-ci (code-quality tool on PyPI), a student project 'Oak, a version control system' from IEEE-NITK, Deno's oak web framework, Apache Jackrabbit Oak and the Oak programming languages.
- **Maker:**
  > Oakspace, Inc., Seattle. Founder Zach Geier (zdgeier), who earlier built the JamHub/JamSync version-control system, and designer Adam Morse (mrmrs). The repository says that most of the code is written by AI under human supervision. Two people appear as committers.
- **What it is:**
  > A version-control system written in Rust (client library, CLI and a hosted service at oak.space) with its own data model and protocol. It is not a Git wrapper. Content is hashed with BLAKE3, split by content-defined chunking and kept in a local SQLite store under .oak/; the server keeps metadata in PostgreSQL and content in object storage. The unit of work is one branch per agent session. Commits are checkpoints without messages, the branch carries one description, and the server squashes the branch into a single commit on main with that description. main exists only on the server and cannot be pushed to. There is no staging area, stash, review system, CI system or merge queue, by stated design. oak start creates a copy-on-write worktree and branch for each task; oak finish describes, checkpoints, pushes and removes the worktree. Warning about secondary coverage: the launch design of June 2026 (virtual lazy mounts over FSKit, FUSE or ProjFS) was removed in 0.115.0 in November 2026.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > Workspace cost: yes, through shallow and sparse clones and copy-on-write worktrees. Throughput: a stated goal and some client-side speed work, but no landing-throughput evidence. Landing contention: only a compare-and-swap on main with a retryable refusal; the merge queue was removed in 0.113.0. Textual conflicts: normal line-level three-way merge, surfaced on the branch, with extra safeguards against silent deletions. Semantic conflicts: not addressed (no tests or analysis on the server). Task collision: not addressed (no claims, locks or overlap warnings).
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > Isolate then merge, with a server-side squash merge guarded by a compare-and-swap on main's head. Trunk-based, short-lived branches, checks after landing.

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > Public beta, usable by anyone after free sign-up at oak.space (checked 2026-11-24). Current CLI is v0.125.0, so the v0.99.0 quoted in most articles (released 2026-06-21) is five months old. The terms of 2026-10-30 call the service experimental and advise against production-critical use.
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > CLI and core library: Apache-2.0. Hosting: SaaS at oak.space. Self-hosting: none on offer today. oak serve, the local server, was removed in 0.113.0. The GitHub README of 2026-11-17 describes server code under AGPL-3.0, but no server directory is in the GitHub mirror or in the current oak.space tree (both checked 2026-11-24), and the current README names only Apache-2.0. The CLI keeps a --server option.
- **Price:**
  > Free during the beta, read 2026-11-24. Per organisation: 100 GiB storage, 2 million files, 1,000 repositories, unlimited members (10 GiB and 200,000 files in the first 7 days). Paid plans are announced for after the beta without figures. A USD 10 per month plan quoted in June 2026 articles is no longer listed.
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > Public since about 2026-06-01 (GitHub repository and crates created 2026-05-31 and 06-01); Show HN on 2026-06-22. Versions: 0.95.0 on 2026-06-01, 0.99.0 on 2026-06-21, 0.109.1 on 2026-11-03, 0.117.0 by 2026-11-17 and 0.125.0 by 2026-11-24. Activity is very high: eight minor versions in the last week, main updated 2026-11-24. Adoption signals are small: 86 GitHub stars, 4 forks, one listed contributor, 853 downloads across GitHub release assets, and 171 total downloads of the CLI crate (platform counters, read 2026-11-24). One article's figure of 1,500 stars is wrong. Known user: Oak's own team, which keeps Oak in Oak (self-reported). No outside production user was found.
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > The terms (2026-10-30) warn that data can be lost, that users should keep independent backups and that the service can end without notice. Encrypted paths have had no outside security review. Two-person company; funding not published. The larger risk is churn: between about 2026-11-08 and 11-24 the project removed the merge queue, oak serve, oak watch, release tags, virtual mounts, its CI and workflow system, git-backed repositories, stacked branches and the stdio MCP server, and renamed JSON fields in 0.122. Release notes 0.102 record four branch recreations while a merge bug that could drop files existing only on main was fixed.
- **OS support:**
  > macOS on Apple Silicon and Intel, and Linux x86_64, through the installer; Linux ARM64 as a manual download. Install docs state that there is no Windows build at the moment and that older Windows binaries get no updates (read 2026-11-24). WSL is not documented; it was not tested for this report.

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > Own protocol and store; the server does not speak Git, so Git clients cannot clone from or push to Oak. The CLI converts: import by oak clone \<git-url>, oak init --import-git or the resumable oak import; export by oak export, which gives back the original commit ids for imported history; ongoing two-way bridging by oak bridge push, pull and branches. Not carried over on import: submodules, tags, Git LFS content (pointers only), branches other than the checked-out one, and the third and later parents of octopus merges. Earlier reports of read-only clone over Git smart HTTP are superseded.
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > New in November 2026: a hosted 'Sync with GitHub' setting through Oak's GitHub App, with one-hour tokens. Either GitHub leads main (merging an Oak branch merges its pull request on GitHub, so GitHub checks and branch rules apply, and direct changes to Oak's main are refused) or Oak leads (main is pushed to GitHub, and commits pushed straight to GitHub stop the sync). Open branches and pull requests travel both ways; pull requests from forks are skipped; branches changed on both sides in the same file are left alone. Both repositories must have the same visibility, and path rules that hide folders block the hosted sync. Existing CI therefore keeps working only in the GitHub-led mode or through webhooks. Oak itself runs no code: its CI and workflow feature was removed in 0.115.0. The bridge was first tried against GitHub on 2026-11-22 according to the project's own notes, so it is days old.
- **Migration and exit:**
  > Entry is cheap for small repositories: one command replays the Git history, and the .git directory can stay beside it. Large histories are slow: the project's Linux kernel import (1,507,461 commits, self-reported, single run) took 25 min 47 s as a dry run, and the real upload ran at about 34 commits/s, an estimated 12 hours that had not finished when the note was written; 33,152 commits (2.2 %) were dropped with octopus parents, and the older local-conversion path produced a store of 67 GB against a 6.5 GB Git pack. Partial adoption is possible through the GitHub-led sync, which keeps GitHub as the system of record. Exit: oak export writes a normal Git repository with authors, messages and timestamps; the agent's checkpoint history inside a squashed branch, marks, path rules and Oak permissions do not fit into Git. Every agent must also learn a CLI that no model was trained on.

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > 1. The agent runs oak start with a summary. It gets a new copy-on-write worktree on a new branch cut from main's current head on the server, with the summary as description, already pushed. 2. The agent edits and runs oak commit, which makes local checkpoints without messages. 3. While working it can run oak pull, which merges current main into the branch; conflicts therefore appear on the branch. 4. oak finish checks that it can publish, sets the final description, checkpoints, pushes and removes the worktree. 5. A person, or an agent that was told to, runs oak merge or uses the Merge button. The server merges the branch's tree with main and squashes it into one commit whose message is the branch description, and whose metadata points at the last checkpoint. 'Merge selected' on the Branches page lands several branches one after another. 6. If main moved during the merge, the server answers 409 main_moved, nothing lands and the caller retries. There is no queue, no batching, no speculative build and no test run by the server; the earlier oak queue was removed in 0.113.0. Who may land: the bundled skill tells agents not to merge unless asked, and the docs admit that the server does not enforce this for ordinary credentials. It is enforced for agent API keys, which can push but can never merge or approve. For the 'agents run the synchronisation themselves' scenario this matters: full self-landing needs member credentials, or a server process that merges for the agents. In the GitHub-led sync mode step 5 becomes a merge of the corresponding GitHub pull request. Whether that mode works with a GitHub merge queue is not stated in any source read.
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > At oak pull, at oak merge, and after a push (branch pages list conflicted files; oak branch wait returns exit code 5 when main moves and the branch no longer merges cleanly). oak merge --dry-run checks main's head on the server and labels its prediction certified or uncertified. Nothing is detected before or at write time. Granularity: manifests first (path level), then a line-level three-way content merge with diff3 information; a conflict means that both sides changed the same lines. A separate merge-safety check compares four trees to refuse merges that would silently delete or revert files that exist only on main; it was added after previews had called such merges clean. Semantic conflicts are not detected.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > The agent resolves in its own branch. The server never resolves; it refuses the merge with 409 and a list of conflict_paths. Locally, oak conflict status and oak conflict show --json give the state per file, markers wrap only the differing lines, oak conflict take --ours or --theirs picks a side, and oak pull --continue or --abort finishes or backs out. Exit code 5 and recommended_next_commands tell an agent the next step. The web page offers a ready prompt for handing the conflict to an agent. No automatic replay and no structural merge driver.
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > A new branch always starts from main's head as reported by the server. If main moves later, a clean merge needs no action from the agent because the server merges the branch tree with current main. If lines overlap, the agent runs oak pull, resolves, tests and pushes again. A race during the merge call returns the retryable main_moved, and nothing lands. Retry cost is one merge call when there is no conflict; the number of retries under many concurrent landings is not published. A clean textual merge onto a moved main is not retested by Oak.
- **Pre write coordination:**
  > None between agents: no file or task claims, no messaging and no overlap warnings were found in the docs. What exists is access control: path rules, enforced by the server, can limit readers and writers of a folder or require approval from an owner group, which can serve as a static partition. A branch can be checked out in only one worktree at a time, and a local lock protects the working directory.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > No evidence. No source gives a conflict rate, a conflict count or a comparison with Git for real agents or for a simulated workload, and Oak's own benchmark compares speed, not conflicts. The design gives no reason to expect fewer textual conflicts than Git: the merge is also a line-level three-way merge. What could change is the moment of discovery: branch wait and the certified dry run show a conflict before the landing attempt. The benchmark's push test shows fewer rejected pushes (Git had 6 to 20 push failures per window at 4 or more clients, Oak none; self-reported, synthetic benchmark, 2026-10-28), but each Oak client pushed to its own branch, so this is not a measurement of landing on one main. Vendor launch claims of 50 % fewer version-control tokens and up to 90 or 95 % faster operations (June 2026) come without a published method and do not concern conflicts.

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > Shallow clone, sparse clone by path, and by default only the top level checked out for repositories of 100,000 files or more. Local worktrees are copy-on-write clones on APFS, btrfs and XFS that reuse what the first clone already downloaded. Files can be listed, read and searched on the server without a checkout. Content is stored as deduplicated chunks; the server keeps metadata in PostgreSQL and content in object storage. Limits: 50,000 file versions and 25 GiB per push, 2 million open branches per repository, 4,096 simultaneous requests per account, no rate limit on pushes, pulls and merges (docs, read 2026-11-24). The virtual mount of the launch version is gone.
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > 8 concurrent clients, each pushing its own branch: 19.3 operations/s against 37.8 for Git on the ripgrep corpus, and 28.5 against 37.3 for clones (self-reported, synthetic benchmark of 2026-10-28, one Apple M5 Max, local oak serve against git daemon). An earlier log of 2026-06-10 reports 8 workers committing into one shared checkout at 346.8 commits/s without lost updates (self-reported, synthetic, local). Concurrent landing on one main: nothing published. The benchmarked server, oak serve, no longer ships, so the hosted service that an outsider would use is unmeasured. The README's 1 million open branches, more than 1,000 merges/s and 1 PB per repository are goals which it says Oak does not meet today.
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > Synthetic benchmarks by the vendor on one laptop (8 clients, short windows) plus one unfinished import of the Linux history. No production figures, no simulation of agents, no independent run.
- **Coordination limit:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] Not published. In the vendor's local test Oak was already slower than Git at 8 concurrent pushing clients (19.3 against 37.8 operations/s, self-reported, synthetic benchmark). Landing on one main is a single compare-and-swap without batching, so retries must grow with the landing rate, but no load figure exists.

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > None before landing on Oak itself. The pattern it offers is post-merge: a mark is a named pointer on main for one deployable thing, with a list of folders. A machine you run follows the mark (oak mark follow --check --run); when those folders change it checks out main's head, runs the check, moves the mark if the check passes, and deploys. If several changes land during a run, only the newest is checked next, which acts as implicit batching and leaves the changes in between untested one by one. Webhooks (push, merge, branch created, mark moved, mark checked) can drive external CI. In the GitHub-led sync mode GitHub's required checks run before the merge. Path rules can require human approval, not tests.
- **Broken main recovery:**
  > Known-good pointer: yes, the mark stays at the last commit that passed, and a failed deploy rolls the mark back automatically. Revert: a button or API call lands an inverse commit, refused if a touched file changed since. No bisect and no automatic revert of main; the --good option of oak start, which began work from a known-good commit, was removed in 0.115.0.
- **Review model:**
  > No built-in review, comments or approval flow beyond path-rule approvals by a human owner group (agents cannot approve). Branch triage (oak branch triage --remote --json) scores open branches and recommends an action; the web page shows the diff and the Merge button. In GitHub-led mode the GitHub pull request is the review.

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > Strong for its age. The CLI takes --json on the commands an agent needs, with schema versions, fixed error codes, exit codes by class (3 locked, 4 dirty, 5 conflict, 6 network or sign-in) and recommended_next_commands that name the exact next call. oak agent state gives one compact summary; oak diff accepts a byte budget; oak docs and oak skill install put the documentation in front of the agent; oak init can write AGENTS.md. Unattended use by API key. HTTP API and webhooks. A remote MCP server at https://oak.space/mcp (OAuth) covers reading and limited writes such as descriptions and path rules, but not push or merge; the former stdio MCP server was removed in 0.118. Weekly changes to commands and JSON fields are the cost.
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > oak reset, oak restore from an earlier commit, oak split, oak log; interrupted pulls resume or abort with receipts; closed branches can be reopened. If a push was sent but not answered, the CLI reports it as unconfirmed, gives no retry command and asks the agent to compare the server head first. There is no operation log with a general undo.
- **Agent cost:**
  > No token or time measurement by an outsider. Costs visible in the design: the skill text in context, a CLI unknown to models (commenters on Hacker News made the same point), one push at start and one at finish. Copy-on-write worktrees keep setup time and disk low. The vendor's claim of 50 % fewer version-control tokens is unverified.
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > Agent API keys are tied to one organisation, limited to chosen groups, can expire, and cannot merge, approve or change settings; organisation agents have their own name and an audit log; scopes separate read, write and release. Path rules are enforced by the server and stored outside the repository, so a branch cannot loosen its own rules. 0.125 added signed commits and encrypted paths (the latter unaudited). A change is attributed to the agent identity and described by the branch description; no model, prompt or session transcript is captured. Oak supplies no process sandbox.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > The agent runs start, commit, pull, conflict resolution and finish itself through the CLI. Landing is left to a person or to a process with member credentials, because agent keys cannot merge. The server only merges and refuses.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Vendor self-report: docs, release notes and benchmark reports, unusually candid about limits and slower results. Main weaknesses: no independent use or benchmark, results from a server that no longer ships, and docs that go out of date within weeks.
- **Independent reports:**
  > Show HN, 2026-06-22 (216 points): mostly sceptical. Commenters doubted that Git's speed is the bottleneck for agents, noted that models already know Git, worried about mounts with builds and search tools, and questioned unverified numbers; some shared the pain with worktrees. HN 'Oak: Git for Agents', 2026-07-03 (21 points): criticism of inaccurate statements about Git. elasticdotventures/_b00t_ issue 818 (2026-07-14): desk evaluation of 0.101.0, closed, no hands-on result. nguyenvanduocit/concord landscape note (November 2026): early, no public benchmarks, still describing the removed mounts. MAHDTech/agent-skills added an Oak skill on 2026-10-18, which shows some outside interest. No hands-on review was found.

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > Trying out an agent-first CLI and the 'one branch, one description, squash on the server' workflow on a side project, and for very large repositories with binary assets where partial checkout matters and beta risk is acceptable.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > Breaking change every few days and whole features withdrawn (queue, mounts, CI, stacks). Data-loss and discontinuation clauses in the terms, with no self-hosted fallback. No test gate before main when Oak leads, so a clean textual merge can break main; checks come after. No queue or batching, so many simultaneous landings compete for one compare-and-swap. Past merge-safety bug that could drop files present only on main, now guarded. GitHub sync only days old. Submodules, tags and LFS content do not survive import. Agents may fall back to Git commands, which their training favours, in a repository that has no .git (inference; the bundled skill warns against it). Secondary articles describe a product that no longer exists in that form.
- **Minimum useful scale:**
  > No measured break-even. Over worktrees plus a merge queue it offers cheaper worktrees and better machine-readable output, but takes away the queue and the test gate, so there is no landing volume at which the present version pays off; it would need an ordered landing service first.
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Skip for the real repository. At 5 to 30 agents Git worktrees plus a merge queue already cover the load and test before landing, while Oak leaves out the queue and the gate, changes weekly, is run by a two-person company and tells users to keep their own backups. The one contained experiment worth considering is a copy of the repository in GitHub-led sync mode, which keeps GitHub as the system of record and its checks in force, to learn from the agent CLI. The missing Windows build played no part in this verdict.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Wait. The storage and branch limits are generous on paper and the agent CLI is among the best designed, but nothing shows concurrent landing on one main: the largest test is 8 clients pushing separate branches to a local server that has since been removed, and it was slower than Git there. Hundreds of agents would need batching or a queue, a pre-merge gate and an agent credential that may land; a queue and a CI system were built and then removed. Reconsider after six months without removals, a published landing benchmark on the hosted service and one named outside production user.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > Give each agent task one branch with one description written for the reviewer and squash on landing, so that checkpoints stay cheap and main stays readable. Return exact next commands, fixed error codes and exit codes per class from any wrapper around Git. Issue agent credentials that can push but never merge or approve. Keep folder rules outside the repository so that a branch cannot change its own permissions. Certify a merge prediction against the server's current main and say so when it could not be certified. Refuse a merge that would delete or revert files the branch never touched (a four-tree check). Let an agent wait on its branch and wake it only when main moves into conflict. Treat a push without reply as unknown: read the server head before retrying. Keep a known-good pointer per deployable that a follower moves after checks, and start work from it.

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > https://oak.space/oak/oak README, notes and release notes (main read 2026-11-24, head updated the same day); docs read 2026-11-24: https://oak.space/oak/oak/docs/merging.md, https://oak.space/oak/oak/docs/agents.md, https://oak.space/oak/oak/docs/git.md, https://oak.space/oak/oak/docs/marks.md, https://oak.space/oak/oak/docs/limits.md, https://oak.space/oak/oak/docs/install.md; notes/git-bridge.md and notes/git-import-at-scale.md in the same repository (November 2026); notes/release-0.102 to release-0.125; scripts/bench-vs-git/REPORT.md (2026-10-28) and BENCHLOG.md (2026-06-10); https://oak.space/api/releases/latest (v0.125.0 on 2026-11-24); oak.space terms (updated 2026-10-30); https://github.com/oakdotspace/oak (mirror, last push 2026-11-17, GitHub API read 2026-11-24); https://crates.io/crates/oakvcs-cli (read 2026-11-24). Independent: https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=48631726 (2026-06-22); https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=48779321 (2026-07-03); https://github.com/elasticdotventures/_b00t_/issues/818 (2026-07-14); https://github.com/nguyenvanduocit/concord docs/research/landscape-2026-11.md; https://github.com/MAHDTech/agent-skills (Oak skill, 2026-10-18).
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > Oak was not installed or run for this report. Sources disagree on version (articles say 0.99.0, the service says 0.125.0), on stars (an article says 1,500, GitHub says 86), on Git access (articles say read-only smart HTTP, the docs say the server does not talk to Git), and on the server licence (AGPL-3.0 in the mirror's README of 2026-11-17, no server source visible). The README philosophy implies that agents merge on their own, while the docs say that the agent publishes and a person merges and that agent keys cannot merge. Not confirmed: a build that works on Windows or under WSL, real retry rates under concurrent landing, the hosting location and funding, and whether GitHub's merge queue works with the GitHub-led sync. Exact dates of versions 0.113 to 0.125 are bracketed by two observations (11-17 and 11-24), not read from a changelog. Reddit was not searched.

**Unverified fields (1):**

- coordination_limit

### Atomic

*Layer: D VCS or client*

#### Basics

- **Name:** Atomic
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > atomicdotdev/atomic on GitHub (https://github.com/atomicdotdev/atomic), site https://atomic.dev, docs https://docs.atomic.dev (source atomicdotdev/atomic-docs), hosted service https://atomic.storage. Sibling repositories hold the agent plugins (atomic-claude, atomic-codex, atomic-opencode and others) and Circuit Breaker (pipelines). Predecessor: castingclouds/atomic, a Pijul-derived version that is no longer on GitHub. Theory ancestor: Pijul (https://pijul.org, nest.pijul.com/pijul/pijul). Unrelated namesakes: Atomic Data, danielgonzagat/atomic-os, and the many tools called atomic commit, Atomic Agents or Project Atomic.
- **Maker:**
  > Atomic Software, Co.; founder and CEO Lee Faus (earlier GitHub, GitLab, Red Hat). A USD 2.5 million pre-seed round is reported in an interview published by one of its investors, Vermilion Cliffs Ventures (2026-01-28). Four people write most of the code (GitHub contributor list, read 2026-11-24). Pijul: Pierre-Etienne Meunier and Florent Becker, operated by Pijul OU (Estonia).
- **What it is:**
  > A distributed version-control system in Rust built on patch theory. A file is a graph; a change adds or removes vertices and edges and is named by its content hash. Changes that do not depend on each other commute, so their order does not affect the result. A second layer models file, line and token (called trunk, branch and leaf) for finer merges. One canonical graph holds everything; a view is a filter that selects a set of changes, either draft (one agent's work) or shared, with a parent chain. Promoting work means inserting changes into another view, not merging snapshots. Around this sit copy-on-write sandboxes, hooks that record every file-changing agent turn as a change, a provenance graph, session attestations (model, tokens, cost), a vault for goals and intents, and signed triage reviews. Storage is an embedded redb database (.atomic/atomic.redb), optionally served by a local daemon on a Unix socket. The theory comes from Pijul and Darcs: in Pijul any two changes either commute or one depends on the other, a conflict is a legal repository state between two changes, and its resolution is itself a change that stays valid wherever the same two changes meet. Atomic's own comparison document says the first code base evolved from Pijul and that the current core is a clean-room rewrite on a different store, so Pijul's guarantees are background and not proof about Atomic's code.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > Textual conflicts: yes, this is the centre of the design (commuting changes, token-level merge, conflicts kept as data). Workspace cost: yes, through copy-on-write sandboxes over one graph. Landing contention: partly; inserts are metadata operations, but the recommended practice is serial integration by one integrator and there is no queue. Throughput: one-machine claims only. Task collision: advice on ownership, intents and view access grants, with no claim or lock before editing in the general case. Semantic conflicts: not addressed; the docs send the user to integration tests.
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > Patch theory: isolate in draft views and sandboxes, then insert commuting changes into a shared view, with remaining conflicts stored as data. Pijul uses the same principle with channels and line-level changes.

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > Open source and usable today, pre-1.0: v0.22.1 of 2026-11-13, with prebuilt binaries. The hosted service Atomic Storage (v1.4.0 on its start page) lets anyone register an identity and push. Enterprise features (Reactor, Sherpa, Outposts) are limited to a founding-teams pilot programme (read 2026-11-24). Pijul: public, still in beta, 1.0.0-beta.26 of 2026-10-30.
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > Atomic client and libraries: Apache-2.0. Hosting: Atomic Storage SaaS; a self-hosted server package is offered only to founding teams (docs, 2026-10-26). Without it, the Git projection on an existing Git host or a peer over ssh serves as the shared remote. Pijul: GPL-2.0-or-later, hosting at nest.pijul.com or any ssh host.
- **Price:**
  > Client free. No public price for Atomic Storage or the enterprise features was found: atomic.dev/pricing shows the founding-teams page (six months of free early access) and atomic.storage lists no plans (read 2026-11-24). Pijul: free.
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > Repository created 2026-02-23; releases from v0.8.0 (2026-06-24) to v0.22.1 (2026-11-13), lately one every one to two weeks. 123 stars, 16 forks, 19 open issues and pull requests; last commit on the default branch dev on 2026-11-15, last push on any branch 2026-11-24 (GitHub API, 2026-11-24). Main contributors: leefaus 131 commits, vinceblock99 79, geekgonecrazy 74, graywolf336 60. 1,391 downloads over all release assets, a few dozen for v0.22.1. Packaged in the aqua registry since 2026-09-25. Users: the company itself (self-reported) and a handful of outside issue reporters; no named production user. Pijul for comparison: crate first published 2016-02-02, 194,789 downloads in total and 2,314 recent, eight betas between 2026-07-14 and 2026-10-30 (crates.io, read 2026-11-24).
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > Issue 264 (2026-10-16) confirmed a storage bug in which one backend bypassed the lock of the newer database library (redb 4.3/4.4), so that independent processes could lose writes or leave an unreadable database; closed by pull request 269 on 2026-10-17, the day 0.20.0 was released (that 0.20.0 contains the fix is inferred from the dates). Issue 291 (open since 2026-10-27) reports rejected records and inconsistent derived indexes. An internal analysis of 2026-05-15 lists nine merge defects found one by one, including a file whose content was duplicated from 236 to 454 lines. Store formats changed several times (0.20 needed a migration and old processes stopped first). Funding is one small pre-seed round. Positive: Apache-2.0 code and a Git projection limit lock-in.
- **OS support:**
  > Binaries for macOS (arm64, x86_64), Linux (x86_64 and aarch64, gnu and musl) and Windows x86_64 (zip on the release page; installer documented for macOS, Linux and WSL2). The Git shim that keeps a Git repository in step is supported on Linux and macOS only; Windows is deferred, while open pull requests of 2026-11-24 repair Windows tests. Copy-on-write clones use APFS, btrfs, XFS or ReFS and fall back to copies elsewhere.

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > Own model, with a Git projection. atomic git import converts Git history (first-parent stream, tree-verified); atomic git push exports. The colocated shim (since 0.20.0, opt-in) keeps the Atomic graph as canonical store, Git objects and refs as a projection, and one working copy for both, with signed bindings between Git and Atomic states under refs/atomic, plus reconcile, verify, status, switch and publish commands. Squash merges and rebases on the Git side are tracked by bindings and not by commit id. Not handled: linked Git worktrees (hooks pass through and record nothing), adopting a foreign Git state, full Git status semantics, exporting a view with open conflicts (refused by default). Pijul: no Git protocol; the released crate contains a one-way importer from Git (default feature based on git2, read in source).
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > With the shim, teammates and CI see ordinary Git commits and pull requests on GitHub or GitLab; an agent's draft view is published as a Git branch on request (atomic git shim publish) and never implicitly. Local hooks are advisory. atomic git shim verify-receive can run as a pre-receive hook or required CI check to enforce provenance. Without the shim Atomic replaces the pull request with triage reviews on views and needs its own checks. Issue 330 (2026-11-11, fix pending in pull request 331) reports that the provenance gate of atomic git push refuses every change recorded by the hooks, which, if it holds generally, blocks the path that matters most for agents; it is one open report, not a confirmed fault.
- **Migration and exit:**
  > Entry: import, create a signing key, enable the shim, reconcile, verify. Import keeps authors, times, messages and commit ids but cannot recover agent attribution that Git never stored; submodule history is not imported. The shim documentation of 2026-10-28 states that record and reconcile still take minutes on repositories with a few hundred files, and that the ordinary importer takes hours on a thousand-commit history (the new streaming import is faster). Partial adoption is the intended route: keep the Git host and use Atomic locally, or use only provenance and sandboxes. Exit: the Git projection is a complete Git history of the published state; atomic git shim disable and cutover --rollback are documented. Lost on exit: views, unpublished drafts, conflict states, provenance graph, attestations and vault content, unless exported. Never delete .atomic or .vault to fix a failure, the docs warn, because they can hold the only copy of pending work.

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > 1. Each writing agent gets a sandbox: atomic sandbox create NAME --from dev makes a copy-on-write working tree and a new draft view parented on dev; with hooks enabled, a session can also get its own draft view automatically. 2. Hooks record each file-changing agent turn as a change with session metadata, or the agent runs atomic record. Records from several processes go to the same canonical graph and are serialised by the database lock. 3. A reviewer (person, orchestrator or agent) lists what a view would contribute (atomic triage candidates and review --into TARGET), and previews the insertion (atomic insert preview). 4. Landing is atomic insert of selected changes, or atomic insert view SOURCE --to TARGET. It adds the changes and their dependency closure to the target view; it is described as a metadata operation per change, not a content merge, and changes keep their identity. 5. Conflicts, if any, become visible in the target view and are resolved by a further change. 6. The result is pushed to Atomic Storage, or published to a Git branch and merged there by pull request. The vendor's own guide for several agents (2026-09-03) is strict: integrate one reviewed view at a time, in dependency order, switch to the target, check conflicts and status after each insertion, preview the next view again and run tests. So landing on the shared view is serial and run by a client, not by a server queue; there is no batching, speculation or automatic retry service. This sits uneasily with an investor's blog on the founder interview, which says in its own words that the model avoids serialised workflows. Concurrent pushes to the remote are described as free of push races, because a remote accepts independent changes from several sources; no test of this was found. Pijul lands the same way in principle (push or pull of changes into a channel) without views, triage or hooks.
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > Detected when changes become visible together in one view: at insert, pull or view switch, or earlier by insert preview. Not at write time. Level: lines in the graph, and tokens in the semantic layer, so two edits to different tokens of one line can combine. Kinds reported: order, cyclic, zombie (edit against deletion) and name conflicts. Binary files conflict as a whole. Known gap: two different renames of one file resolve to the last writer without a conflict, which drops one name silently. The documents disagree on maturity: a planning paper lists token merge as not started, while the later merge rubric (2026-08-11), concept page (2026-08-10) and test harness treat it as working. Semantic conflicts are not detected, and the guide warns that nearby edits may commute although behaviour does not. Pijul: two kinds inside a file (insertions at the same place, insertion into deleted text) and name conflicts between files, at line level.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > Conflict as data. The view stays usable; both sides are kept in the graph and shown on disk as markers (opened with the opposite sign from Git's). atomic conflicts --short lists path, line and kind for an agent; atomic record refuses files that still hold markers unless told otherwise; the resolution is a normal change that sticks when the same changes meet again. Export to Git with open conflicts is refused by default. The agent, a reviewer or a human decides; there is no built-in model-based resolver. Invariants stated by the project for a correct merge: no lost edit, no silent duplication, no false conflict, and markers on disk exactly when status reports a conflict. Pijul originated this treatment: changes can be applied to a conflicted repository, and the resolution can wait.
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > There is no rebase. A change records its own context and dependencies, so it stays valid when the parent view moves, and its identifier does not change. The draft sees new parent changes through the view chain. The retry cost is a fresh preview before insertion, plus resolving any conflict that has appeared; tests must be run again by the integrator. In shim mode, commits that arrive from Git must be reconciled first, which is reported as slow (minutes on a few hundred files, docs 2026-10-28).
- **Pre write coordination:**
  > No locks or claims on files for agents in general. The guide asks an orchestrator to assign files or entities, with a single owner for lockfiles, migrations, generated files and shared interfaces. Vault intents describe planned work, and remote sandboxes hold time-limited grants to one view with explicit capabilities. One plugin goes further: the OpenCode integration queues mutating tools of one workspace (30-second acquisition limit), tracks which session changed which file, and refuses to publish when a foreign session rewrote a file (docs, 2026-09-17). That is enforced inside one OpenCode instance and not across independent processes.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > No measurement. No source gives a conflict rate with Atomic against Git for the same tasks. The theory gives a reason to expect fewer textual conflicts (independent changes commute; token-level merge), and Pijul's manual shows a case where Git merges to a silently wrong place without a conflict while Pijul keeps the order of lines, so the correct comparison is fewer wrong merges as well as fewer conflicts. But the project's own analysis of nine cross-view merge defects (2026-05-15) shows that implementation bugs produced false, duplicated or lost content despite the theory, and the merge rubric and invariant tests of 2026-08-11 are its reaction. The vendor's 90-run blind-scored benchmark on atomic.dev (accepted-change rate 90 % against 60 %, cost per accepted change USD 0.15 against 0.22, 44 % faster, 82 % less context; self-reported, synthetic) concerns context from the knowledge graph, not conflicts between parallel agents. The guide itself says that no version-control system can guarantee zero conflicts when agents make incompatible edits.

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > Copy-on-write clones of the working tree by filesystem reflink, all pointing to one canonical .atomic graph, with a full copy as fallback. A view by itself does not isolate files. Sandboxes can be packaged as a layered or a sealed OCI image, opened remotely with a time limit, and materialised for one path prefix. Claim: five agents on a 200 MB base cost a few MB (self-reported design statement, 2026-06-24, not measured). Local store redb, single writer at a time; Atomic Storage holds the server copy. Pijul: full local repository with partial clones made possible by commutation.
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > 8 independent sessions recording at the same time, in a vendor test: after two fixes in September 2026, 156 of 156 turn-end recordings and 6,240 of 6,240 command-line reads succeeded over 18 runs with 1, 4 and 8 sessions, 16 tool events per turn, three repetitions (self-reported, synthetic replay of real hook processes without model calls, one shared view, 2026-09-11 and 09-15). Before the first fix only 40 of 100 recordings succeeded. A live test on 2026-09-16 ran one OpenCode parent with two overlapping subagents and produced three separate, correctly attributed changes (self-reported). These measure recording and checkpointing, not landing. Concurrent insertion into one shared view, pushes from many clients and throughput of the hosted service: nothing published. Single-writer figure: about 1,680 to 1,690 synthetic changes/s over a chain of 100,000 (self-reported, January 2026, sequential, hardware and raw data not published).
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > Vendor synthetic tests (1 to 8 sessions, three local repetitions) and one sequential stress test; no production data, no independent run. The docs state that no public benchmark with concurrent agents exists and that 100 or 1,000 agents should not be inferred (2026-09-03).
- **Coordination limit:**
  > Recording is serialised by one lock per repository. In the vendor's September 2026 test the 95th percentile of turn-end recording rose from about 0.05 to 0.12 s with 1 session to about 0.8 to 1.3 s with 8 (self-reported, synthetic), while reads stayed under 0.1 s after the fix. Acquisition waits are bounded (10 s for turn ends and reads in that test, 30 s default for the general lock wait), so heavier load turns into timeouts and retries. The September tests used redb 4.2, while the lock bypass reported in October 2026 (issue 264) names redb 4.3/4.4, so they probably do not cover the current storage code (inference from the issue title and the test notes). Behaviour above 8 sessions is unmeasured, and the sandbox document of 2026-06-24 says simultaneous writers had not been load-tested.

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > No CI and no merge queue. The guide asks the integrator to test after each insertion. Sandboxes can be staged as images for a CI runner, and the sister project Circuit Breaker runs small pipelines per change. In shim mode the Git host's checks gate the pull request, and verify-receive can refuse commits without valid provenance. No batching, speculation or affected-target selection.
- **Broken main recovery:**
  > Any change can be removed from a view (atomic unrecord) without rewriting later independent changes, or undone through the operation journal; tags and state hashes give a fixed pointer. No bisect command in the CLI reference, no automatic revert and no known-good pointer that a check moves.
- **Review model:**
  > Triage of views or features before promotion: candidates, a walkthrough, a verdict and an optional signed attestation, with publication rules checked by SHACL shapes. Human or agent reviewer. In shim mode the usual pull request stays.

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > CLI with --json or --short on many commands (status, conflicts, operation log, query). Hook integrations for Claude Code, Codex, OpenCode, Cursor, Copilot, Cline, Devin, Grok, Kilo, Kiro, Pi and Antigravity, installed by atomic agent enable; skills; a VS Code extension and Node bindings. Automatic recording means that the agent needs few version-control commands, which also means it can fail unnoticed. Natural-language query needs an API key. No MCP server was found in the code or docs trees (names searched, 2026-11-24). Hooks write into .claude/settings.json and similar files. Issue 290 (Grok hooks) was open with a fix committed on 2026-11-13.
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > Extensive on paper: an immutable operation journal (atomic op log, show, undo, restore with --json), atomic unrecord and revise, stash, restore, a doctor command, journaled moves of Git refs, and a documented rollback of the shim. The undo appends an inverse and never rewrites the journal. Caveats: a record can save its change and then report failure in legacy shadow mode, and the docs say to inspect before retrying.
- **Agent cost:**
  > Recording is automatic, so the token cost of version control is low (no measurement published). Wall-clock cost can be high: the Git shim documentation says that record and reconcile take minutes on repositories with a few hundred files (2026-10-28), and several sessions wait on one lock. Setting up a sandbox is a reflink clone. Optional context and provenance features add tokens or save them; the vendor's 82 % reduction of context is unverified.
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > The strongest part. Each turn is stored as a change with model, provider, session, tokens and cost where the agent reports them, and a provenance graph links goal, exploration, commitment and verification. Sessions produce signed attestations; agents have delegated Ed25519 identities and DIDs; attestations can be exported in DSSE form, and signed bindings tie Git commits to Atomic states. Remote sandboxes hold a grant to one view with a lifetime and capabilities. No per-folder permissions were found. Attribution depends on the hook: in a shared workspace a session could once record a sibling's file, and only the OpenCode plugin with explicit file lists fixes that; separate sandboxes avoid it.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > Recording: hooks, without agent effort. Insertion, conflict handling and tests: the agent, an orchestrator or a person through the CLI, one view at a time. Push to Git or Atomic Storage: the client. No server process lands work.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Vendor self-report with a high degree of candour: docs state that concurrency is unproven, defects are analysed in public, and the September 2026 test results include failures, commands and archives. Main weakness: no outside user or benchmark, and marketing statements on 100 or more agents that the documentation contradicts. Pijul's theory rests on published research, but Atomic's implementation is separate.
- **Independent reports:**
  > craigcode/kranz (review of 2026-08-08, v0.14.0): borrowed the attestation and intent-gate ideas, kept Git worktrees and a gated merge, and put replacing Git out of scope. The-AIE/the-gibson issue 505 (2026-10-20, v0.20.0, open): planned trial; risks listed are pre-1.0 software, a Unix-only temporary Git bridge, hooks that write agent settings and duplication of its own vault; its notes add that linked worktrees are unmanaged and that published improvements were not reproduced. nguyenvanduocit/concord (November 2026): no concurrent or tail-latency benchmark; advises use only where cryptographic per-turn attestations are a hard requirement. Issue reporters outside the company (October and November 2026) reported failed imports, rejected records and a refusing push gate; which of the storage reports came from outsiders was not checked one by one. Hacker News: no launch thread found, one passing recommendation (2026-08-18). One commenter in the Oak thread (2026-06-22) says plain Pijul worked well for several agents at once (anecdote). The interview by an investor and one article written by the company are not independent.

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > Teams that need a signed record of what each agent did per turn, on Linux or macOS, and want to try patch-based promotion beside an existing Git host. Pijul itself suits readers who want the theory or a small, human-paced repository without Git tooling.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > Pre-1.0 storage and merge engine with recent data-integrity bugs (264, 291, the nine-defect analysis). Push gate reported to refuse hook-recorded changes (330, open). Slow shim on ordinary repositories, and no support for linked Git worktrees, which many agent harnesses rely on. One writer lock per repository; contention beyond 8 sessions unknown. Serial integration moves the bottleneck to one integrator and the tests after each step. Silent loss of one name when two renames meet. Two documents disagree on what disabling the shim leaves behind. Automatic hooks may record the wrong session's file in a shared directory. No public server to self-host, unclear price. Agent integrations break with each vendor release. Anyone copying the theory should note that a sound theory did not prevent implementation defects.
- **Minimum useful scale:**
  > No measured break-even. The provenance part pays off at any size where per-turn audit is required. The conflict part pays off only if textual conflicts between overlapping edits dominate and the integration step is still serial; with disjoint work at 5 to 30 agents, worktrees plus a merge queue do the same for less.
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Skip for the real repository; pilot only on a disposable copy, to learn from sandboxes, provenance and conflict-as-data. The workflow fits (one sandbox and one draft view per agent, preview, insert) and the Git projection allows a way out, so a trial is cheap to stop. It is not ready to carry the real repository: recording is serialised and tested only to 8 sessions, the shim is slow on a few hundred files and does not manage linked worktrees, the push gate was reported on 2026-11-11 to refuse hook-recorded changes (fix still an open pull request), and a lost-write bug was confirmed a month earlier. The absence of a Windows shim played no part in this verdict.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Wait. A single writable database per canonical repository, a documented one-view-at-a-time integration procedure and no concurrent test beyond 8 sessions leave no evidence for several hundred agents, and the docs refuse to claim it. The theory is attractive at this size, since commuting changes need no rebase and conflicts can wait as data, so reconsider when there is a published concurrent insertion and push benchmark, a server that lands changes, the open record and push issues closed, and a release that has run for months without a storage fix. Pijul as a stand-alone alternative: skip, no agent tooling, no Git protocol, still beta.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > One copy-on-write worktree per agent over a shared store, with views or branches only as the logical layer. Conflict as data with four invariants to test in any merge tool: no lost edit, no silent duplication, no false conflict, markers exactly when status says conflict. Preview before every insertion, integrate in dependency order (contracts first, lockfiles and generated files last) and test after each. One owner for shared files such as migrations and lockfiles. Record attribution per agent turn, outside branch history, so that it survives squash and rebase, and bind Git commits to it by signature. An operation journal where undo is another entry. Bounded waits with typed errors instead of immediate failure when a store is busy, and stress tests that kill a process while it holds the lock. From Pijul: make a resolution a change of its own, so the same conflict is not solved twice; git rerere with a shared cache is the closest substitute.

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > https://github.com/atomicdotdev/atomic README and releases (v0.22.1, 2026-11-13; API read 2026-11-24); docs in that repository: docs/CONCURRENT-AGENT-SANDBOXES.md (2026-06-24), docs/CROSS_VIEW_MERGE_RCA.md (2026-05-15), docs/MERGE-CONFLICT-RUBRIC.md (2026-08-11), docs/COMPARISON.md, docs/THEORY.md, docs/SEMANTIC-MERGE.md, docs/concurrent-stop-publication.md and docs/ordinary-read-lock-wait.md and docs/subagent-file-ownership.md (2026-09-17); issues https://github.com/atomicdotdev/atomic/issues/264 (2026-10-16), /279, /290, /291 (2026-10-27), /329, /330 (2026-11-11). Documentation site source https://github.com/atomicdotdev/atomic-docs: docs/guides/multiple-ai-agents-without-merge-conflicts.md (2026-09-03), docs/concepts/performance-at-scale.md (2026-09-03), docs/concepts/merging-and-conflicts.md (2026-08-10), docs/getting-started/git-shim.md (2026-10-28), docs/commands/git-shim.md, docs/commands/sandbox.md (2026-11-05), docs/commands/op.md, docs/teams/self-hosting.md (2026-10-26), docs/getting-started/installation.md (2026-09-17). https://atomic.dev, https://atomic.dev/founding/ and https://atomic.storage (read 2026-11-24). https://vermilioncliffs.substack.com/p/hello-atomic-goodbye-git-rebuilding (2026-01-28, investor interview); https://allthingsopen.org/articles/version-control-agentic-ai-git-limits (2026-01-22). Pijul: https://pijul.org/manual/why_pijul.html, https://pijul.org/manual/conflicts.html, https://pijul.org/manual/theory.html, https://crates.io/crates/pijul (1.0.0-beta.26, 2026-10-30) and its source at docs.rs (read 2026-11-24). Independent: craigcode/kranz docs/reviews/atomic.md (2026-08-08); https://github.com/The-AIE/the-gibson/issues/505 (2026-10-20); https://github.com/nguyenvanduocit/concord docs/research/landscape-2026-11.md; https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49352009 (2026-08-18).
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > Atomic was not installed or run for this report. Sources disagree: an investor's description that the model avoids serialised workflows against the docs' serial integration procedure; older marketing of 100 or more agents against the docs' refusal to claim it; a planning document that calls token merge not started against later documents that describe it as working; the two shim documents on whether disable removes the hooks (getting-started says dispatchers are removed, the command page says hooks and checkpoints stay). The January 2026 speed test is internally inconsistent (about 592 microseconds per change derived, 500 stated) and lacks hardware and raw data. The benchmark on atomic.dev was not checked at source. Server behaviour of Atomic Storage under concurrent pushes, its price and its data location are unknown. The fix for the push gate (issue 330) was an open pull request on 2026-11-25 (331). That the September 2026 recording and read-wait fixes are part of v0.22.1 was not confirmed. Pijul's Git importer was read in source, not run. The predecessor repository castingclouds/atomic could not be opened.

### Perforce P4

*Layer: D VCS or client*

#### Basics

- **Name:** Perforce P4
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > Product page https://www.perforce.com/products/p4, docs https://help.perforce.com/helix-core/, GitHub organisation perforce (server is not open source; perforce/p4mcp-server and the API wrappers are). Renamed: Perforce, then Helix Core, now P4; documentation URLs and many pages still say Helix Core, and Helix Swarm is now P4 Code Review. Keep apart: (1) P4 One, a separate local-first client; (2) P4 Plan (formerly Hansoft) and P4 DAM, sibling products; (3) P4 the network data-plane language (p4lang/p4c), unrelated; (4) microsoft/p4vfs and salesforce/p4-fusion, third-party tools for P4; (5) third-party Perforce MCP servers with similar names (for example iprabhu and dristic listings), not from Perforce.
- **Maker:**
  > Perforce Software, Inc. (Minneapolis), owned by Clearlake Capital and Francisco Partners (Octus, 2026-04-06).
- **What it is:**
  > A proprietary centralised version control server (p4d) with a thin client (p4). The server holds all history and metadata, knows which files each workspace has synced and which files are open for edit, and gives every submitted changelist a global number. A person or agent syncs files, opens them with p4 edit (files are read-only until then), changes them, and submits a changelist that is all-or-nothing. Branching is done with streams (parent and child lines with a defined flow of change), task and sparse streams, or with shelves, which park uncommitted changes on the server. Around it: P4 Code Review, triggers, proxies, edge and replica servers, a REST API, and since September 2025 an MCP server. Perforce's argument for agents (blog by Jeff Anton, 2026-05-28) is mainly in-flight visibility (p4 opened -a run as a pre-edit hook), per-file conflict detection at submit, and integration records that clients cannot rewrite. It is not an argument from exclusive locks: locking appears as a row in a table and an FAQ line, and the post itself says no version control system catches semantic conflicts.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > Addresses: textual conflicts (detected per file at submit; avoidable on files marked exclusive-open), task collision in part (server-side list of open files with change descriptions, advisory), landing contention in part (a submit only needs the files it contains to be current, so unrelated landings do not force a rebase), workspace cost in part for large binary repositories (partial views, sparse streams, proxies). Throughput: proven with human and automation load, not with agents. Does not address: semantic conflicts (the vendor says so), duplicate work that touches different paths unless agents read each other's descriptions, cheap throw-away workspaces for text repositories.
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > Centralised check-out, edit, submit. Optimistic by default (several people may open one file, later submitters resolve per file), with optional pre-write claims: enforced exclusive open (+l file type) or advisory visibility (p4 opened -a).

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > GA for decades. Current server release 2026.2 (2026.2/3151160, 2026-11-18). P4 REST API promoted from technology preview in 2026.2. P4 MCP server public since 2025-09-24, latest v2026.4.3126709 (2026-11-02), community supported, meaning Perforce states it is not officially supported.
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > Proprietary server and client. Self-hosted (Linux, Windows, macOS, cloud templates) or P4 Cloud (single-tenant SaaS from Perforce); larger managed hosting through resellers such as Assembla. The MCP server is MIT. perforce/perforce-agentic-gateway (local MCP gateway) is public but GitHub reports no recognised licence.
- **Price:**
  > Read 2026-11-24, page undated: Free \$0 for up to 5 users and 20 workspaces, self-hosted (above either limit the server is restricted to 1,000 files in total); Cloud \$39 per user per month, up to 100 users, 64 GiB included, unlimited workspaces; Scale and Platform on request. Agents: the pricing page does not mention bots or agents. The P4 Supplemental Terms of 2026-02-26 do: a non-human operated device that can access P4 counts as an authorised user; a Bot User License covers only background jobs (automated tasks through Perforce client programs or API) with no GUI; one bot licence may run several jobs, but the customer needs as many bot licences as unique background users. So one identity per agent means one licence per agent; a shared bot identity is cheaper but loses per-agent attribution. The price of a bot licence was not found, and the terms do not name AI agents.
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > Very mature. Releases twice a year: 2026.1 (2026-05-19) and 2026.2 (2026-11-18). Vendor page shows logos of NVIDIA, Porsche, Epic Games, CD Projekt Red and DNEG. MCP server: 127 stars, 8 open issues, at least 8 releases since 2025-09, last push 2026-11-02 (GitHub API, 2026-11-24). Agent-related additions: secure-by-default settings and branch-on-submit for sparse streams (2026.1); per-group rate limits on command cost and concurrent commands, OpenID Connect login and REST API tokens (2026.2). Perforce announced MCP servers across its portfolio and a local Agentic Gateway (repository created 2026-06-10).
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > Server data model is long proven; submitted history is rewritten only by administrators. Vendor finances are strained: Octus (2026-04-06) reports about \$1.44 billion of first-lien debt that traded in distressed territory early in 2026, linked to fears about AI, and about \$297 million of new notes to refinance a \$300 million second-lien loan due in 2027; Bloomberg (2026-04-08, headline and snippet only) calls it a rare swap on risky debt. Security: CVE-2026-6043 (published 2026-04-24) covers insecure default settings before 2026.1; a researcher (SecurityWeek, 2026-04-21) found 6,122 exposed servers in spring 2025, 72% with unauthenticated read access to source, and about 54% of the 2,826 still reachable on recheck. 2026.1 changed the defaults. A competitor for the game market, Epic's open-source Lore (EpicGames/lore, MIT, June 2026), appeared in the same year. Downgrading from 2026.2 is not possible.
- **OS support:**
  > Server and CLI 2026.2: Windows 11 and Windows Server 2016-2025 x64, Linux x86_64 and aarch64, macOS 12+ (Intel and Apple silicon). MCP server: Windows 10+, macOS 12+, Linux with glibc 2.34+; its Docker image on macOS and Linux only. P4VFS virtual sync: Windows only. Native Windows is a first-class platform.

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > None for the main depot: own protocol, client and history. The P4 Git Connector can store and serve Git repositories (graph depots; 3 Git repositories on the Free tier) next to P4 files, and a Git-only licence exists. Import and export with git p4, or salesforce/p4-fusion (BSD-3-Clause, last push 2026-09-21) for fast conversion to Git.
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > Replaces GitHub and pull requests: review happens in P4 Code Review on shelved changes, before the submit. CI goes through Jenkins (perforce/p4-jenkins, active), other CI plugins or triggers; there is a community GitHub Action (perforce/setup-p4, last push 2024-02). Agent harnesses: Claude Code has CLAUDE_CODE_PERFORCE_MODE=1, which makes Edit, Write and NotebookEdit fail with a p4 edit hint on files that are still read-only, and its worktree isolation uses Git unless WorktreeCreate and WorktreeRemove hooks are written for Perforce (Claude Code docs, read 2026-11-25). Most agent tooling that assumes Git branches, diffs and pull requests does not apply.
- **Migration and exit:**
  > Adoption is a full change of version control, review and CI for a Git team; partial adoption is possible only by keeping some depots in P4 or bridging with the Git Connector. Costs: server administration, licences for people and bot identities, a workspace definition per agent, retraining agents on sync, edit, resolve and submit. Exit: convert history to Git (git p4, p4-fusion). Integration records, per-revision attributes, shelves, open-file state, reviews and file-level permissions have no Git equivalent and stay behind.

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > Each agent lands its own work. It syncs its workspace, runs p4 edit on the files it will touch, edits, and runs p4 submit on a changelist. The server checks every file in the changelist against the head revision: a file that moved on since the last sync must be synced and resolved before the submit passes. The submit is atomic for the changelist; during it the server briefly locks the files. Files outside the changelist are not checked, so there is no whole-repository rebase and no merge queue; two agents that touch different files never block each other at landing. Server triggers (change-submit before transfer, change-content after transfer and before commit, change-commit after) can reject or react. Alternatives: submit to a child or task stream and merge up later (p4 merge, p4 copy), or shelve the change and let P4 Code Review approve and commit it. There is no batching, speculation or server-side rebase of stale work. Through the MCP server an agent can create and submit changelists, edit, sync and resolve.
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > At sync or submit, per file (head revision against the synced revision). Within a file, a three-way text merge at line level during p4 resolve. Before write only if the file type has the exclusive-open modifier (+l: the second p4 edit on the file is refused) or if the agent reads p4 opened -a first. p4 lock on a normal file does not stop others editing it; it stops others submitting it. Cross-file and semantic conflicts are never detected; because only submitted files must be current, the tree that lands may never have existed in the agent's workspace.
- **Conflict resolution:**
