  > The agent resolves it. The failed submit names the files that need resolving and leaves them open in a numbered pending changelist; the agent syncs and runs p4 resolve. Modes: -am merges automatically when both sides changed without overlapping and skips files with real conflicts; accept yours; accept theirs; or write the merged file by hand. The MCP tool modify_files offers resolve with auto, safe, force, preview, theirs and yours. One issue for agents: accept-yours and accept-theirs silently drop one side. No structured conflict object and no automatic replay.
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > Only staleness of the files in the changelist matters. Retry cost is sync plus resolve for those files plus whatever tests the agent chooses to rerun. Trap for unattended agents: after a failed submit the files stay locked by default unless the configurable submit.unlocklocked is set, so one failed agent can block other agents' submits until it resubmits, runs p4 unlock, or an administrator does. The other side of this cheapness is that nothing forces the agent to test on the current head.
- **Pre write coordination:**
  > Three levels. (1) Advisory: p4 opened -a lists every open file with user and changelist; pending change descriptions can carry the task. The vendor proposes a pre-edit hook in the agent harness; it is a pattern, not a shipped feature. (2) Enforced: +l file type (exclusive open), set per file or by typemap for a path or the whole depot; the typemap affects new files only, existing files must be reopened with the new type. Intended by the docs mostly for files that cannot be merged. (3) Submit lock: p4 lock. No expiry was found in the pages read; a lock ends on submit, revert, unlock by the changelist owner, or an administrator's forced unlock, so a dead agent leaves it behind. Gap in the MCP server (source read 2026-11-24): modify_files has add, edit, delete, move, revert, reconcile, resolve and sync only; no lock or unlock, and no call to p4 opened -a. The central argument of the vendor blog therefore needs the p4 command line, not the MCP server.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > No evidence found. Neither the vendor blog nor the product page gives a conflict count, rate or comparison; the only case is an unnamed CTO's LinkedIn post, and the figure of hundreds of history rewrites a day for ten agents is the author's own estimate about Git, without a method. By design, exclusive open removes textual conflicts on the marked files by making the other agents wait, and per-file checks remove rebase churn on unrelated files; both are properties, not measurements. Nobody was found who measured waiting time, lock contention on hot text files or semantic breakage for agents on P4.

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > Full or partial: a workspace view or stream maps only the paths an agent needs, and files are copied to disk at sync with server-side have-records per workspace. No copy-on-write workspaces in the product. Cheaper variants: sparse streams (metadata-only branch; files branch on submit since 2026.1; a reseller talk claims under one minute for projects with millions of files), proxies and edge servers, parallel sync, alternative sync agents (P4ALTSYNC, with a hydrated-hotfiles option in the release notes), and Microsoft's P4VFS (MIT, Windows, files downloaded on first read). Server keeps metadata in its own database and file archives on disk.
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > Agents: none published. People and automation: Google's main Perforce server served more than 12,000 users, 10-12 million commands and about 10,000 submits a day with about 10 million changelists on one 16-CPU, 256 GB machine (Dan Bloch, Google, 2011; historical, human and tool load, self-reported by the operator at the vendor's conference). The same slides describe the limit: server-side locks made long reads and submits block other commands. Google later replaced it with Piper. A reseller talk in 2026 speaks of thousands of concurrent users; unverified.
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > Production, human and automation load, 2011, operator's own report. For agents: vendor marketing only, no benchmark, N = 0.
- **Coordination limit:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] Unknown for agents. Known mechanisms: hot files serialise (exclusive files completely, normal files through repeated resolve); the vendor warns that a hundred separate p4 edit calls put a hundred sequential locks on the working table and should be batched; 2026.2 added per-user limits on command cost and concurrency, which is needed when agents poll. With p4 opened -a as a hook, the list each agent must read grows with the number of agents.

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > Optional and self-built. Pre-submit: shelve, run CI on the shelf (P4 Code Review, Jenkins), or a change-content trigger that rejects. Post-submit: ordinary CI on the mainline. No merge queue, batching, speculation or affected-target selection in P4 itself. Since a submit does not require a whole-tree update, pre-submit results can be out of date at landing.
- **Broken main recovery:**
  > Manual: p4 undo opens files that reverse a submitted change or a range while keeping history; no automatic bisect, revert or known-good pointer. Labels or a release stream are used by convention.
- **Review model:**
  > Human or AI review in P4 Code Review on shelved changes, with rules that can block the commit. The AI checklist review is off by default and supports several model providers (reseller session report, July 2026). Perforce's own agent pipeline keeps human review gates (blog, 2026-08-20).

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > Full non-interactive CLI with JSON output (p4 -Mj -ztag), REST API (GA in 2026.2, some user and group endpoints still preview), API wrappers (Python, Go, Java and others), and the MCP server: queries and changes for files, changelists, shelves, workspaces, streams, jobs and reviews; read-only by default; confirmation prompts for deletes; six bundled skills (changelists, code review, DAM, file operations, streams, workspace set-up); stdio or HTTP transport, where HTTP has no authentication. Independent hands-on (Classmethod, 2026-07-18): queries and simple writes worked from Claude Code; the deletion prompt needed a manual key press, which matters for unattended runs; one 1 MB binary overflowed the context. Lock, submit and resolve through MCP were not tested there.
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > Before submit: p4 revert, and shelves as server-side backups. After submit: p4 undo. Revert discards local edits without an undo log, so unshelved work of a dead agent is recoverable only from its disk.
- **Agent cost:**
  > Per agent: one workspace definition, an initial sync of the mapped files, p4 edit before each write (or reconcile afterwards), possibly a p4 opened -a read per edit, a licence if it has its own identity, and a login ticket. Token cost of the MCP tools not measured; tool responses are unbounded for large binaries unless limited.
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > Strong. Protections per user, group and path, for read, open, write and more; the MCP server acts with the user's rights and administrators can switch MCP, toolsets and write access on or off by group through server properties. Every change records user and workspace; p4 attribute can store model, version, prompt context or agent identity on a file revision (the vendor's suggestion; nothing sets it automatically). Full audit log. The price of per-agent identity is per-identity licensing.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > The agent: sync, edit, resolve and submit are its own commands. The server arbitrates per file. Nothing schedules, rebases or retries for the agent.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Product mechanics: official documentation and release notes, strong. Fit for agents: vendor marketing without data. Independent evidence on agents is one hands-on of the MCP server by a consultancy on a small test server. Main weakness: no measurement of conflicts, waiting or throughput with agents.
- **Independent reports:**
  > Classmethod hands-on, 2026-07-18: MCP server with Claude Code works for history, changelists and shelves; binaries out of reach; community support is a production concern. Classmethod report of a CEDEC talk, 2026-07-22: the speaker is from Toyo Corporation, a reseller, so not independent. Amplify Partners essay, 2026-05-20 (investor): file locks were the simplest answer to coordination, but people forgot to release them and a whole file is too coarse a unit; it does not tie Perforce to agents. Assembla and Sourcegraph comparisons come from companies with products to sell. Hacker News and Reddit threads about running parallel agents on P4 were searched for and not found, nor was the CTO post cited by the vendor.

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > Teams already on P4 (games, chips, film), repositories dominated by large binary files that cannot be merged, and organisations that need per-path permissions and an immutable audit trail.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > Forgotten exclusive locks from dead agents; failed submits that leave files locked by default; hot text files serialised; agents that ignore p4 edit and write around read-only files (Claude Code has a guard, other harnesses may not); silent loss through accept-yours or accept-theirs; untested combinations on the mainline because only submitted files must be current; MCP server unsupported and without lock or cross-workspace open-file calls; unauthenticated HTTP transport if exposed; licence count growing with agent identities; vendor debt; leaving the Git ecosystem.
- **Minimum useful scale:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] Not a matter of agent count: it pays off when large binaries or an existing P4 installation are present. For a text repository on Git no threshold with evidence was found. The free tier stops at 5 users and 20 workspaces, so about 20 agents if each has its own workspace and identities are shared (inference).
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Skip for a Git code repository: it replaces Git, pull requests and the agent tooling, and the two ideas that matter (see what others have open, claim before editing) can be copied without it. Use it if the repository is already in P4: give each agent its own workspace, run p4 opened -a through the CLI before edits, set submit.unlocklocked, batch p4 edit, keep exclusive open for unmergeable files only. A pilot is free up to 5 users and 20 workspaces.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Wait. The server has carried thousands of users, and per-file landing avoids the rebase race, which is a real advantage at that scale. But there is no published run with agents, nothing protects the mainline from semantic breakage without a self-built gate, hot files would queue, and licensing per bot identity is unpriced. Reasonable only for organisations already running P4 at scale.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > Check only the files a change touches at landing, not the whole tree, and gate the tree separately. Make work in progress visible on a server before the first write: files, agent, one-line intent; have the harness read it in a pre-edit hook. Use hard locks only for files that cannot be merged. Make write access itself the claim (read-only until opened). Record agent, model and prompt as metadata on the revision. Make failed landings release their locks. Rate-limit agents on the server.

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > Perforce, P4 vs Git for AI coding agents, 2026-05-28, https://www.perforce.com/blog/vcs/p4-vs-git-for-ai-coding-agents ; Perforce, AI agent orchestrator, 2026-08-20, https://www.perforce.com/blog/vcs/ai-agent-orchestrator ; Why P4 for AI-powered development (Japanese page, undated), https://www.perforce.com/ja/node/4402 ; pricing (undated, read 2026-11-24), https://www.perforce.com/resources/vcs/helix-core-pricing ; P4 Supplemental Terms, 2026-02-26, https://www.perforce.com/system/files/2026-02/2026.02.26%20Perforce%20P4%20Supplemental%20Terms.pdf ; P4 2026.2 release notes, 2026-11-18, https://ftp.perforce.com/perforce/r26.2/doc/user/relnotes.txt ; 2026.1 release notes, https://ftp.perforce.com/perforce/r26.1/doc/user/relnotes.txt ; secure by default, 2026-04-28, https://www.perforce.com/blog/vcs/p4-secure-by-default ; CLI reference 2026.2 (p4 lock, unlock, submit, opened, resolve, undo, file types), https://help.perforce.com/helix-core/server-apps/cmdref/current/Content/CmdRef/p4_submit.html ; exclusive locking with typemap, https://help.perforce.com/helix-core/server-apps/p4sag/current/Content/P4SAG/superuser.basic.typemap_locking.html ; MCP server, https://github.com/perforce/p4mcp-server ; MCP press release (undated, 2026 footer), https://www.perforce.com/press-releases/MCP-Enablement ; Claude Code environment variables and worktrees, https://code.claude.com/docs/en/env-vars and https://code.claude.com/docs/en/worktrees ; Google, Still All on One Server, 2011, https://research.google/pubs/still-all-on-one-server-perforce-at-scale/ and slides https://www.slideshare.net/slideshow/still-all-on-one-server-perforce-at-scale-8139217/8139217 . Independent: Classmethod, 2026-07-18, https://dev.classmethod.jp/en/articles/perforce-p4-mcp-claude-code/ ; Classmethod, 2026-07-22, https://dev.classmethod.jp/en/articles/cedec-2026-perforce-p4-ai/ ; Amplify Partners, 2026-05-20, https://amplifypartners.com/blog-posts/will-agents-like-git-any-more-than-we-do ; SecurityWeek, 2026-04-21, https://www.securityweek.com/unsecured-perforce-servers-expose-sensitive-data-from-major-orgs/ ; Octus, 2026-04-06, https://octus.com/resources/articles/perforce-software-raises-about-297m-pari-1l-notes-to-refi-2l-loan-existing-lenders-roll/ ; Bloomberg, 2026-04-08 (headline and snippet only), https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-04-08/shaken-by-ai-threats-perforce-software-inks-rare-swap-on-risky-debt ; microsoft/p4vfs, https://github.com/microsoft/p4vfs
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > Not confirmed: whether Perforce counts an AI agent as a background user, and the price of a bot licence; that no lock expiry exists (none found in the pages read); the LinkedIn post behind the vendor's anecdote. Sources disagree on two points: the vendor blog calls attribution changelist-level in its table and file-revision-level in its text (the command reference says per revision), and it stresses file-locked work in its conclusion while its argument rests on visibility and lineage. Google's figures are from 2011 and from slides; the server now has lockless reads (the db.peeking setting in the 2026.2 release notes), so the blocking described there should not be read as today's limit. The Free tier is described as 20 workspaces on Perforce pages; a reseller mentions a 1 GB limit that was not confirmed.

**Unverified fields (2):**

- coordination_limit
- minimum_useful_scale

### Gas Town and Gas City

*Layer: G coordination*

#### Basics

- **Name:** Gas Town and Gas City
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > Gas Town: gastownhall/gastown (moved from steveyegge/gastown, which redirects; command gt). Gas City: gastownhall/gascity (command gc, docs https://docs.gascity.com), with its Refinery role in gastownhall/gascity-packs (directory gastown). Shared base: gastownhall/beads (bd), the task ledger. Keep apart: (1) Wheelhouse, Steve Yegge's closed-source successor for his own use, not public; (2) the Wasteland (gastownhall/wasteland), a federation layer, retired according to Yegge; (3) a hosted cloud version of Gas Town reported by The New Stack (page not readable, not assessed); (4) many forks of gascity; (5) the gc alias of Oh My Zsh's git plugin, which hides the Gas City command.
- **Maker:**
  > Gas Town: Steve Yegge and contributors. Gas City: built by Julian Knutsen and Chris Sells, stewarded by Gas City, Inc., a company Yegge advises.
- **What it is:**
  > Orchestrators that run many terminal coding agents (Claude Code, Codex, Gemini and others) in tmux against ordinary Git repositories, with work tracked as beads in a Dolt database. Gas Town (Go, MIT, January 2026) hardcodes roles: Mayor (coordinator), Polecats (workers in Git worktrees), Witness (per-repository monitor), Deacon and Dogs (watchdogs) and the Refinery (per-repository merge processor). Gas City (Go, MIT, first commit February 2026, 1.x in 2026) extracts the machinery into a configurable platform without built-in roles; formulas are executed as graphs by a controller, and the Gas Town roles, Refinery included, are one optional pack. Neither is a version control system: both add task dispatch and serialised landing on top of Git. Yegge's site says Gas Town shipped at v1.0 in 2026 and is now retired, with Gas City as successor.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > Addresses: landing contention (one merge processor per repository instead of every agent pushing), task collision at task level (a bead is assigned to one agent; dependencies order the work), textual conflicts after the fact (found at landing, sent back as work), agent supervision. Partly: broken main (tests before push, with a documented exception for failures judged pre-existing). Does not address: file-level overlap before writing, semantic conflicts beyond the configured tests, workspace or storage cost beyond Git worktrees, server throughput.
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > Isolate then merge: one worktree and branch per worker, then a serial merge processor. Task-level claims before work; no file-level claims.

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > Both are public and installable today. Gas Town v1.2.1 (2026-06-06) is the last release; its author calls it retired, its repository does not. Gas City v1.5.1 (2026-10-24) is the current stable release. Beads Team Server (commercial) is offered to design partners; Gasworks is marked coming soon (gascity.com, read 2026-11-24).
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > MIT for Gas Town, Gas City and Beads; self-hosted on the user's machine, a server, Docker (Gas Town) or Kubernetes sessions (Gas City runtime option). Commercial: Beads Team Server as managed SaaS or in the customer's VPC.
- **Price:**
  > Software free. The cost is model usage. Reports, all self-reported by users: about \$100 for one 60-minute session (Tim Sehn, DoltHub, 2026-01-15); about \$3,000 for one week at 6-8 hours a day, about \$100 an hour (Sehn, 2026-03-24); about \$6,000 of Claude API calls for about two weeks with up to 13 workers (Neil Macneale, DoltHub, 2026-04-16). Gas City: about a billion tokens a day for a setup of roughly 100 agents merging about 50 pull requests a day (Julian Knutsen, as relayed by Every, 2026-05-19). Wheelhouse, not these tools: Yegge reports 69 billion tokens in July 2026, about \$87,000 at API list price or about \$2,800 a month on subscription accounts.
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > Gas Town: 18,886 stars, created 2025-12-16; commits per month May 401, June 153, July 149, August 0, September 0, October 8, November 5 (to the 24th), the autumn ones mostly security and dependency updates by Julian Knutsen; last commit on main 2026-11-12; 399 open issues and 135 open pull requests. So: maintenance mode in practice. Gas City: 1,580 stars, created 2026-02-22; commits per month June 866, August 499, September 542, October 1,081, November 521 (to the 24th); 1,064 open issues and 786 open pull requests (GitHub API, 2026-11-24). The project dashboard of 2026-11-22 (self-reported) shows 125 pull requests merged in 7 days, 754 open, 501 of them stale, 0% of agent pull requests reviewed by a human in 30 days, and 99.2% CI success. Star counts do not show use: no named production user outside the maintainers, DoltHub's experiments and workshop participants was found. Beads: 29,018 stars, active.
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > Work state lives in Dolt through Beads; Git history is ordinary Git. Risks: Gas Town retired by its author; Gas City depends on one young company and exact Beads and Dolt versions, with one-time migrations at upgrades (v1.5.1 repairs a storage migration from 1.5.0). Users report frequent Beads and Dolt faults (Hacker News thread, April 2026), and Yegge himself calls Beads still a bit janky (August 2026). Does the author still use them? Timeline: May 2026, author's note on yegge.ai says he moved all workflows from Gas Town to Gas City; August 2026, his essay says Gas Town fell apart with Opus 4.7, that he has given up on reusable harnesses, and that he now runs Wheelhouse; the same essay mentions Gas City only as another Beads machine. His last commits: Gas Town 2026-06-06, Gas City 2026-06-01 (5 in total), Beads 2026-10-19. So he does not use Gas Town; that he has stopped using Gas City is implied, not stated anywhere read.
- **OS support:**
  > Gas Town: macOS and Linux; Windows binaries exist (v1.2.1 windows_amd64), but the README calls native Windows minimal, CLI-only, and points to WSL because the roles need tmux; issue 3538 (2026-04) documents a failed native attempt. Gas City: release builds for macOS and Linux only (v1.5.1); WSL works as Linux. A native Windows port is in progress: reference pull request 8559 (open, 955 files) split into parts, of which the runtime core, process and supervisor support were merged to main between 2026-11-09 and 2026-11-20; not in a release.

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > Full: ordinary Git repositories, worktrees, branches and pushes. Gas Town keeps a shared bare repository per project with a worktree per worker.
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > Works with GitHub (Gas Town's code also has a Bitbucket pull-request provider) in two modes. Direct (default in both formulas): the Refinery merges and pushes to the target branch itself, so branch protection must allow it. Pull-request mode: it opens a pull request and lets the forge merge. Telling detail: the gascity repository lands its own main through a pull request rule plus GitHub's merge queue (up to 4 entries built and 5 merged together, squash; repository ruleset, read 2026-11-24), not through a Refinery push. Pull-request mode has open faults: with strict up-to-date protection green pull requests that fall behind are not rebased and the queue can deadlock (gascity issue 8709, 2026-11-02, one user).
- **Migration and exit:**
  > Adding a repository is one command and the Git history stays plain, so leaving is easy: branches and commits remain. What is lost is the task ledger (exportable from Beads), formulas, prompts and agent identities. Costs: tmux, Dolt and Beads operations, learning a large vocabulary (Every's tester needed a full day with instructors), and watching the agents. Gas Town to Gas City has a migration guide and command map.

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > Common idea: workers never push to main; each hands a pushed branch to a Refinery, an LLM agent session that lands branches for one repository. Gas Town (formula mol-refinery-patrol v16): the worker runs gt done, which pushes the branch and files a merge request bead; the Refinery takes the next branch, rehearses a merge with the target without rewriting the submitted head, runs the configured set-up, type-check, lint, build and test commands (all empty by default, meaning skipped), then merges with a merge commit and pushes, or opens a pull request; it verifies the push and cleans up. One branch at a time. The README, the 0.9.0 changelog and the architecture document promise a Bors-style queue that batches, tests the tip and bisects; the code exists (internal/refinery/batch.go, default batch of 5, off unless configured) but on 2026-11-24 no caller outside tests was found (code search, plus reading refinery.go, mq.go and engineer.go), the architecture document lists it as blocked by an earlier phase, and the role prompt and formula say one at a time. So bisecting is documented and coded but was not found in the running path; the code was read, not run. Gas City (gastown pack): the worker sets branch and target on the work bead and assigns it to the Refinery; the Refinery skips or performs a mechanical rebase, runs the checks (falling back to the commands in CLAUDE.md or AGENTS.md when none are set), then pushes directly or hands over a pull request. No batching or bisecting in its formula. The default Gas City formulas (build-basic) do not use a Refinery: they implement in parallel worktrees, review, and open a pull request.
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > At landing, when the Refinery merges or rebases the branch: Git's line-level textual conflicts. Semantic breakage only through the configured checks. Nothing before or during writing except that two agents should not hold the same task. A reported blind spot: a branch that carries an older version of a file can silently revert landed work without a conflict and with green tests (gastown pull request 4840, 2026-09-10, two occurrences on one project; detector proposed, still open).
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > Gas Town: the formula aborts the merge, files a conflict-resolution bead with the two commits and instructions, leaves the branch and merge request open and moves on; another worker resolves by merging the target into the branch. The role prompt says something else: fix trivial conflicts in place, otherwise report to the Witness. Gas City: genuine rebase conflicts are aborted, the bead returns to the worker pool with a rejection reason and the kept branch; a new worker rebases and resubmits. Test failures: Gas Town sends FIX_NEEDED to the worker (formula) or MERGE_FAILED to the Witness (prompt); Gas City reopens the bead and deletes the branch. The summary that failed merges are fixed inline or re-dispatched comes from the README; the Gas City formula forbids the Refinery to write code to fix failures and the Gas Town role prompt forbids application code, although both role prompts allow trivial conflict fixes.
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > The Refinery re-merges or rebases each branch on the current target when its turn comes and runs the checks there, so a stale base costs nothing to the worker unless it conflicts. Cost is serial time: one check run per branch. A conflict costs a new task, a new agent context and another place in the queue. Gas City skips the rebase when the target is already an ancestor.
- **Pre write coordination:**
  > Task level: a bead is claimed by or assigned to one agent, dependencies and convoys order related work, integration branches collect an epic, messages go through beads mail. Enforced by the ledger, not by the repository. No file reservations, locks or overlap checks were found in the core of either tool (code search for such terms found only unrelated or add-on files). A gastown guide mentions file reservations only as part of a third-party toolkit (Agent Mail). Optional packs go further, as instructions in a prompt and not enforced: the compound-engineering ce-work skill compares the planned file lists of the units before dispatch and falls back to serial work on overlap when worktrees are missing, and only logs the expected conflict when they exist; a bmad planning skill reviews epics for shared core files.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > No evidence found. No report gives conflicts per merge, before and after, or against plain worktrees with a merge queue. The formula asks the Refinery to count conflicting branches per cycle, but no published counts were found. Related but not conflict data: four phantom hand-offs (work marked done with no branch on the remote) in 13 slots, one user's city with a local model (gascity issue 6148, 2026-09-08); 47 of 1,422 parity tests still expected to fail in DoltHub's prototype (2026-04-16).

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > Full repository once per machine, a Git worktree per worker (Gas Town: from a shared bare repository; crew members, the human workspaces, are separate clones; Gas City: worktree per unit of work in the parallel mode). No virtualisation or copy-on-write. Task state in a Dolt server.
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > Gas Town: the README claims 20-30 agents comfortably and speaks of 20-50 or more in its monitor view (self-reported); largest figure from an outside user is 13 workers at peak (DoltHub, 2026-04-16; self-reported by that user, production). Gas City: roughly 100 agents on one server with about 50 pull requests merged a day (maintainer, relayed by Every, 2026-05-19; production, not stated whether on one repository); hundreds of concurrent agents is Yegge's statement about a company he advises. The gascity repository itself merged 125 pull requests in 7 days through GitHub's merge queue (dashboard, 2026-11-22). Not these tools: Wheelhouse with 40 or more agents and 175-250 real commits a day (Yegge, August 2026).
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > Self-reported production use by the maintainers and a few diaries of outside users (two authors at DoltHub, one blogger, one workshop tester); no benchmark, no controlled comparison. Yegge says he only ever used Gas Town to build itself and never got its workers to run all night (essay, August 2026); DoltHub did build a prototype with it.
- **Coordination limit:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] Not measured for either tool. Expected from the design (inference): serial landing caps throughput at one branch per check run, per repository. The only figures come from Wheelhouse, the author's later system: with a 30-minute gate and 40 or more agents Wheelhouse's queue passed 100 and reached 166, batching with bisection stopped making progress, and he now lands 120-150 commits at once on main and lets agents repair it (self-reported production; about one week of use when written; Danjou points out that he is the only human on the codebase). Supervision is the other limit: Witness and patrol agents cost tokens even when idle.

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > Pre-merge, on the merged or rebased result, one branch at a time, commands set per repository. Two weaknesses: in Gas Town all commands are empty by default and empty means skipped; in both formulas a failure judged pre-existing lets the merge proceed once a bug is filed, which lets a broken or misconfigured test command pass everything (gastown issue 4469, 2026-07-12, observed on three branches; open). Pull-request mode relies on the forge's checks.
- **Broken main recovery:**
  > None automatic in the formulas: pre-existing failures become bug beads for other agents. Batch bisecting code in Gas Town would isolate a culprit before landing, see landing_model. The Gas City pr-review pack has a revert formula that opens a revert pull request, started by the Mayor. No known-good pointer.
- **Review model:**
  > Agent review. Gas Town: optional quality review by the Refinery, off by default, plus optional review gates. Gas City: formulas with several parallel review lanes and a synthesiser, then a pull request. Human review is left to the operator; the Gas City project reports 0% human-reviewed agent pull requests over 30 days.

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > Built for agents: gt and gc command lines, JSON output, bd for tasks, prompts, hooks, formulas and packs in TOML and Markdown; HTTP API and dashboard in Gas City; sixteen harnesses listed. The Refinery's steps are long shell scripts in prompts which the model may skip; issue 6148 (open) documents exactly that. The proposed atomic gc submit command, which would mark work done only after reading the branch back from the remote, was closed unmerged on 2026-09-08 (pull request 6149); its author keeps it as a fork patch.
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > Work survives crashes: open beads are picked up by a fresh agent, branches with conflicts are kept, Dolt keeps history of task state. Undo of landed code is ordinary Git. Known data-loss style faults are reported as issues, for example a clean-up step that deletes live worker state (gastown issue 4830, 2026-09-08, open).
- **Agent cost:**
  > High and partly independent of work done. Standing roles poll: one Refinery session ran about 3.5 days on an empty queue for about 1.5 billion cache-read tokens, estimated at about \$470 (gascity issue 2980, 2026-06-02, filed by a project bot, closed); Witness agents were about 69% of API spend in one deployment (gascity issue 458, 2026-04-08, user analysis, open). Role start-up context of about 131,000 tokens was reported (issue 6293, 2026-09-11, open). Gas Town users report about \$100 an hour. Some installs spent the user's credits on Gas Town's own issues until challenged (gastown issue 3649, 2026-04-14, closed as completed). Gas City adds gc costs.
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > Every agent has a name and a ledger of its tasks, branches and merges, so attribution to agent and session is good inside Beads. No sandbox: Gas City documents that configured commands are trusted code, strips secret-looking variables from controller helpers, and fixed a case where text sent to a Claude session could approve a permission prompt (v1.5.1). Isolation between projects failed in DoltHub's test: an agent found and used another project's write token. No per-folder permissions.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > An LLM agent (the Refinery) under an orchestrator; workers only push a branch and hand over. In Gas City's default formulas the last step is a pull request and the forge or a human lands it.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Documentation and code are open and were read; claims of scale are self-reported. User reports from outside the project exist for Gas Town from January to April 2026 and are mixed; for Gas City one tester (May 2026) and issue reports. No measurement of conflicts or landing throughput. Main weakness: documentation, prompts and code disagree on how landing works.
- **Independent reports:**
  > DoltHub makes Dolt, the database both tools run on, so it is an informed user rather than a neutral one. Tim Sehn, 2026-01-15: four pull requests, the first merged with failing integration tests, all closed as not good. Sehn, 2026-03-24: impressed by output, parallelism helped mostly early, workers pushed straight to master for hours until he switched to pull requests. DoltHub, Neil Macneale, 2026-04-16: works, cannot truly trust the code, token leak between projects. Mike Lady, 2026-01-04: 41 pull requests in about ten hours of use (5 in the first three hours, 36 in the last four), enthusiastic, no quality data. Every (Katie Parrott, Mike Taylor), 2026-05-19: learn from the ideas, skip the toolkit for now; relevant above 10 parallel sessions. Hacker News on v1.0 (April 2026): complaints about tokens, Beads faults, no known product. Julien Danjou (sells a merge queue), 2026-08-18: agrees with Yegge's queue arithmetic, argues that attribution of a failure to the change must be kept; about Wheelhouse. Vanja Petreski, undated (about November 2026): the queueing argument is sound, the rest is one self-reported system. Simon Willison, 2026-08-04, quotes the collapse of Gas Town.

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > Studying role design, task ledgers and hand-off protocols. Gas City: experienced teams that already run more than ten sessions, accept its operations load, and land through pull requests and an existing merge queue.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > Gate that fails open; merge steps as prose that a model skips; phantom hand-offs; stalled queues when the notification is not sent (gastown issue 4734, 2026-08-19) or when pull requests fall behind; silent reverts; merge-queue settings in the rig file not read by the Refinery (fix proposed three times, pull request 4724 open); idle token burn; secrets visible across projects; behaviour tied to one model generation (Gas Town and Opus 4.7); a retired upstream; large backlog of open issues in Gas City.
- **Minimum useful scale:** ⚠
  > [uncertain] Every's tester suggests more than 10 parallel Claude Code sessions before looking closely at Gas City. Below that, worktrees plus the forge's merge queue do the landing part with less. One tester's opinion (2026-05-19); no cost-benefit data.
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Gas Town: skip; retired by its author, in maintenance only, and its advertised bisecting queue was not found in the running path. Gas City: pilot at most, on a side repository, in pull-request mode with the forge's own merge queue as the gate and idle patrol roles switched off or capped. For conflict reduction alone, skip: the core adds no file-level coordination, and the one overlap check found sits in an optional pack and compares planned file lists only.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Gas Town: skip. Gas City: wait; the claim of hundreds is not documented, and a single LLM merge processor per repository is serial, so landing must move to a server-side queue. The author's report from Wheelhouse, his later private system, points the same way: at about 40 agents and a 30-minute gate the landing queue reached 166 and bisecting stopped making progress (self-reported production).
- **Transferable practice:**
  > One landing path per repository; workers hand over a pushed branch and never touch main. Verify the branch on the remote before marking work done, in code and not in a prompt. Return conflicts as new tasks with both commits and keep the branch. Fail closed: no tests run, or a red main, means hold. Check for silent reverts (a file equal to an older landed version). Keep polling out of the model. Store tasks, dependencies and claims in a ledger outside the session. Budget for standing roles. Plan for the moment when commits per day exceed gate capacity.

#### Sources

- **Primary sources:**
  > gastownhall/gastown README, changelog, architecture document, refinery prompt, formula mol-refinery-patrol v16, batch.go, read 2026-11-24, https://github.com/gastownhall/gastown ; gastownhall/gascity README and docs, v1.5.1 notes (2026-10-24), https://github.com/gastownhall/gascity and https://docs.gascity.com ; Refinery pack, https://github.com/gastownhall/gascity-packs/tree/main/gastown ; dashboard, 2026-11-22, https://github.com/gastownhall/gascity-project-dashboard ; Yegge, Gas Town page (undated, read 2026-11-24), https://yegge.ai/gastown.html ; Yegge, author's note on Welcome to Gas City (May 2026), https://yegge.ai/listings/welcome-to-gas-city ; Yegge, The Shape of Things to Come, Part 1, August 2026, https://yegge.ai/essays/the-shape-of-things-to-come/ ; Gas City, Inc., https://gascity.com ; issues and pull requests: gastown 3538, 3649, 4469, 4724, 4734, 4830, 4840 and gascity 458, 2980, 6148, 6293, 8559, 8709, 9194, 9295 (for example https://github.com/gastownhall/gastown/issues/4469 and https://github.com/gastownhall/gascity/issues/2980). Independent: https://www.dolthub.com/blog/2026-01-15-a-day-in-gas-town/ ; https://www.dolthub.com/blog/2026-03-24-a-week-in-gas-town/ ; https://www.dolthub.com/blog/2026-04-16-two-weeks-in-gastown/ ; https://dev.to/mike_lady_d0e50f634af72dd/10-hours-with-gas-town-out-of-a-possible-48-2272 ; https://every.to/context-window/inside-the-100-agent-software-factory (2026-05-19) ; https://hn.svelte.dev/item/47770124 (mirror, April 2026) ; https://dehora.net/journal/2026/2/initial-thoughts-on-welcome-to-gas-town (2026-02-28) ; https://julien.danjou.info/blog/steve-yegge-deleted-the-wrong-thing (2026-08-18) ; https://vanja.io/yegge-agent-city-four-claims/ ; https://simonwillison.net/2026/Aug/4/steve-yegge/
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > Bisecting queue: README, changelog 0.9.0 and architecture text say yes; role prompt, formula v16 and the missing caller say one at a time; the method was GitHub code search for the two functions plus reading refinery.go, mq.go and engineer.go; that cannot prove absence and the code was not run. Whether Yegge still uses Gas City after August 2026 is not stated. The batches of ten that Danjou attributes to Yegge are an arithmetic example in the essay. Yegge's original Medium posts and The New Stack article returned errors and were not read, so the first-hand text on the Refinery from January 2026 is missing. The repository carries no retirement notice although the author's site does. Cost figures are single self-reports at different model prices. Every's figures for about 100 agents are second-hand. Issue reports are single users; several were written by agents.

**Unverified fields (2):**

- coordination_limit
- minimum_useful_scale

### CarterT27 scion

*Layer: B open-source Scion-style*

#### Basics

- **Name:** CarterT27 scion
- **Canonical identifier and namesakes:**
  > https://github.com/CarterT27/scion (owner/repo CarterT27/scion). Cargo.toml names https://github.com/scion-scm/scion as the repository, but that URL and the scion-scm account both returned 404 (2026-11-24), so CarterT27/scion stays canonical. Not published on crates.io (publish = false). Namesakes: Anthropic's internal Scion (the design it copies; no code released); GoogleCloudPlatform/scion (unrelated agent-orchestration testbed); other reimplementations with the same name, tekeburak/scion and stigenai/scion (the latter presents itself as CSG Scion); the SCION Internet architecture and its repositories (for example MystenLabs/scion-rs). culebraluxe/forgevcs is a separate project that vendors this code.
- **Maker:**
  > Carter Tran, one person (GitHub profile: student at the University of Chicago); all 207 commits are his. The README calls it an independent clean-room implementation that is not affiliated with Anthropic. Much of the code was written by Claude Opus agents: .workflow/wave.js runs one Opus agent per work package in its own git worktree plus a fresh reviewer agent, for 34 packages in 7 waves.
- **What it is:**
  > A server-centric version control system in Rust that rebuilds the design Anthropic described on 2026-10-13: 23 crates and 4 binaries (scion, scion-server, scion-daemon, scion-import) that talk gRPC. A commit is a changeset stored as a delta against its first parent, with BLAKE3 ids and zstd compression. Metadata lives in CockroachDB (23.2 or later, SERIALIZABLE), objects in an S3-compatible store; servers are stateless by design. Agents commit to the server and then call land, which rebases the change on the server and moves the ref with one compare-and-swap. Version 1 covers one repository; a regional cache tier, virtual file system, garbage collection, multi-repository support, two-way Git sync and land policies (reviews, CI gates) are listed as deferred.
- **Problem addressed:**
  > By design: landing contention (server-side rebase plus compare-and-swap, so the client does not retry because main moved), workspace cost (sparse checkout, per-node cache) and part of textual conflicts (line-level three-way merge at landing). Throughput is a goal, but no landing throughput is published. Not addressed: semantic conflicts (an explicit non-goal in the SPEC), task collision (no claims or locks) and test gating.
- **Sync philosophy:**
  > Isolate, then central compare-and-swap with replay: each agent works on its own unnamed changesets and the server rebases them onto the moving ref at landing. No pre-write claim, no write-time detection, no CRDT or patch theory.

#### Availability

- **Public availability:**
  > Public source since 2026-10-14. An outsider can build and self-host it, but there are no releases, tags, prebuilt binaries, Docker images or hosted service (GitHub API, 2026-11-24). In effect a source-only developer preview: the project's own rule (docs/testing.md) claims conformance only on a full test run, and the full tier has failed in every nightly CI run.
- **Licence and hosting:**
  > Apache-2.0, self-host only. It depends on CockroachDB, which is not open source: the CockroachDB Software License applies from v24.3.0 and to later patches of 23.1-24.2 (CockroachDB licensing FAQ, read 2026-11-25). culebraluxe/forgevcs shows that a port to PostgreSQL is possible, in a separate code base.
- **Price:**
  > Free software (2026-11-24). Running costs: CockroachDB (the free Enterprise tier is for organisations under USD 10 million revenue and requires telemetry and yearly renewal; a single-node start needs no key), an S3-compatible bucket, servers, TLS certificates and an Ed25519 signing key. No hosted offer and no price list.
- **Maturity and activity:**
  > Version 0.1.0, created 2026-10-14. The SPEC was committed at 04:26 UTC that day and the last implementation wave merged at 13:09 UTC, so most of the 4.47 MB of Rust dates from about nine hours of parallel agent work. Last code commit 7d445a07 on 2026-10-15; last commit e0ffadf9 on 2026-10-16 (README only). 207 commits, no releases, no pull requests, 27 forks, 320 stars (GitHub API, 2026-11-24; about 194 on 2026-10-16 and 245 on 2026-10-26 according to dated third-party notes). The only issue (#1, 2026-11-02, an offer of a SPIFFE login) has no reply after three weeks. CI: the 3 latest push runs are green (the first 4 failed), but all 41 nightly runs from 2026-10-15 to 2026-11-24 are red because scripts/check.sh --full fails each time. The logged causes are a runner out of disk on many nights before 2026-11-03, an intermittent symlink test, and a seed-guard test that forgevcs read as a test defect rather than a product bug. The separate CockroachDB plus MinIO job passed in 35 of the 41; its six failures were symlink and revision tests, and the 100-client landing test passed every night. No known production users.
- **Durability and vendor continuity:**
  > Dormant: no commit for over five weeks and no reply to the only issue, while the author was active in other repositories on 2026-11-17 to 2026-11-19; it is not archived and nothing was announced. One maintainer, no CONTRIBUTING or SECURITY file. Data durability rests on CockroachDB and S3; a backup-retention document exists (scheduled backups, 30-day cap). No data-loss report was found. Possible successors: culebraluxe/forgevcs, which vendors the code (last push 2026-10-23, also quiet), and ArekCzarnik/scion, the only GitHub fork ahead of upstream (44 commits, 2026-11-01).
- **OS support:**
  > Linux and macOS clients; Windows clients are explicitly unsupported in version 1. WSL is not mentioned; it should behave like Linux, but that is an inference. CI runs on Ubuntu, the author's benchmarks on macOS. Building needs Rust 1.98.0 and protoc.

#### Git relation

- **Git compatibility:**
  > One-way import only. scion-import copies one Git ref at a time into one Scion ref, incrementally and fast-forward only, and keeps Git commit ids so git:\<sha> lookups work. No Git protocol, no Git client, no mirror, no export. docs/git-gateway.md (742 lines, 2026-10-15) is marked as a draft for scoping and has no code; even that plan is a projection, not two-way sync. Git submodules and LFS are not supported.
- **GitHub and CI integration:**
  > Replaces GitHub rather than working with it: no pull requests, webhooks or status checks. GitHub appears only as the login provider for people (device flow). CI would have to check out through the scion CLI with a service principal and, by convention, move a green/\* ref (the SPEC reserves the name for CI). No GitHub Action or CI recipe ships for users.
- **Migration and exit:**
  > Adoption: run CockroachDB, S3 and the server, set up TLS, keys and (for people) a GitHub OAuth app, import one ref, then move every tool from git to the scion CLI. A self-reported synthetic import of a real repository (t3code, 5,573 commits, 28,068 files) took 23 min 18 s. Partial adoption means a side copy fed one way from Git, with no way to send landed changes back. Exit: no export tool; you can check out files or read history through the API, but Scion-native history cannot become Git commits unless you write the converter.

#### Conflict handling

- **Landing model:**
  > The agent runs 'scion commit', which uploads missing blobs and creates a changeset that the server sees at once (no separate push), then 'scion land [--ref main]'. On Land(ref, head) the server: (1) reads the ref tip and version strongly and re-reads the access policy on every attempt; (2) takes the linear stack from head down the first-parent chain to the first ancestor of the tip (merge commits in the stack are rejected); (3) looks up each member's stable ChangeId in a 'landed' index, so a repeated request for the same source is skipped and a different source for a landed ChangeId gets ALREADY_LANDED; (4) checks write permission for every path in the deltas; (5) rebases the members in order onto the tip; (6) writes ref, ref log and landed rows in one transaction that is conditional on the ref version. If another land won, it backs off with full jitter (5 ms base, 500 ms maximum) and repeats everything, up to 32 attempts, then returns a retryable UNAVAILABLE/land_contention and leaves the ref unchanged. No lock, queue, lease or batching; several servers may share one store (tested with 3 in one process). No test or review runs before the ref moves. A one-file land at stack depth 5 costs 15 metadata transactions, and 35 when it needs three attempts (self-reported, synthetic: counters in tests).
- **Conflict detection point and granularity:**
  > At landing, or earlier if the agent asks for a rebase or merge. Only paths in the change's own delta are compared, with per-path three-way rules; when both sides edited the same text file, the server runs a line-level merge called line-diff3-v1 (plain Myers diff; overlapping or adjacent hunks conflict unless identical). Conflict kinds: content, add/add, modify/delete, symlink, binary, redacted, file/directory. No rename detection (a rename is a delete plus an add). The author reports about 93% agreement with git merge-file on random small-alphabet files and full agreement on unique-line cases (self-reported, synthetic). This granularity is the author's own choice: Anthropic has not published its rule. Semantic conflicts are not detected.
- **Conflict resolution:**
  > The agent that landed resolves it. The server never writes markers: the gRPC error MERGE_CONFLICT carries a ConflictReport with clean_entries and, per conflict, the path, kind, base/ours/theirs entries and hunk line ranges, so conflict is data at the API level. The CLI is weaker: 'scion land' exits 3 and its JSON error gives a code, subcode, exit code and a message with the count of conflicting paths, but no path names (read from code and schemas, not run). Next steps: 'scion rebase --onto main' (needs a clean working copy; its JSON lists the conflicting paths; it writes diff3 markers for text and side files for the rest), edit, 'scion resolve \<path>', 'scion commit' (keeps ChangeId, author and message), 'scion land'. 'scion rebase --abort' undoes it. A conflict outside the sparse checkout aborts with out_of_scope. Nothing is resolved automatically beyond the clean line merge.
- **Stale base behaviour:**
  > A moved main is not a reason for rejection: the server rebases the stack itself, and by design the cost depends on the change, not on how far main moved (skip pointers). Only a real path or line conflict goes back to the agent. After landing, the working copy moves to the landed result; if local edits are in the way it stays put and reports moved=false. A lost race costs the agent nothing unless all 32 server attempts fail.
- **Pre write coordination:**
  > None. No claims, file locks or messaging; .scion/lock only stops two processes from using the same working copy. Feature work consists of unnamed changesets, so there is no branch list that shows what other agents are doing.
- **Conflict rate effect:**
  > None published: no measurement by the author or anyone else of how often changes conflict with this system compared with Git. The test suite checks correct behaviour on constructed cases only (non-overlapping lands all succeed, overlapping but clean lands merge, true conflicts are refused). Reasoning only: it uses the usual line-level three-way merge, so it should refuse roughly the same changes as Git, and more where a file was renamed. It removes retries caused by a moved main, not the conflicts themselves.

#### Scale

- **Checkout and storage model:**
  > Server: CockroachDB for metadata and small objects (trees and metadata up to 64 KiB stored inline), an S3-compatible store for blobs, content-addressed. Client: materialised checkout, full or sparse by path prefix, with a per-node daemon cache (verified, bounded, 50 GiB by default, concurrent requests coalesced). No virtual file system, copy-on-write worktrees or file watcher; blobs up to 100 MiB are stored whole. Self-reported, synthetic, one Apple M4 Pro with Docker CockroachDB and MinIO, t3code repository: full checkout 14.96 s vs Git 2.90 s; sparse apps/web 0.97 s vs 0.21 s; with a warm daemon 10.19 s and 0.45 s, so Git was faster there. An in-process, in-memory benchmark (self-reported, synthetic, Apple M4 Pro) shows the opposite (10,000 files, full checkout 1.59 s vs Git 2.22 s) but leaves out the database, object store and network.
- **Largest demonstrated concurrency:**
  > 100 concurrent landing clients on one ref across 3 in-process servers that share one store, all with non-overlapping changes, all landed: a synthetic correctness test by the author (self-reported), run on the in-memory backend on every push and on single-node CockroachDB plus MinIO in the nightly external job, where that test passed all 41 times. No landing throughput is published. My reading of those CI logs is 92-219 s from the start of the test binary to the end of this test (debug build, shared GitHub runner, includes setup, commits, checks and parallel tests), which is not a throughput figure. Tests for 1,000 clients and for 300 overlapping clients exist in the code, but I found no log of them running. The published concurrency benchmark (1/4/8 parallel jobs, self-reported, synthetic, one laptop) reports 0.220 s vs 1.059 s for Git at 8 jobs for a 'one-file commit, published'; that step uploads a changeset and does not move main, so it is not landing.
- **Scale evidence type:**
  > Synthetic only, by the author: correctness tests (up to 100 clients on real backends, seconds to minutes) and laptop benchmarks (up to 8 jobs; 897 plus 504 jobs in total). The author says it is not a fleet-scale reproduction. No production use, simulation or model. Outside runs: culebraluxe/forgevcs ran the unmodified test suite (2026-10-16) and stigenai/scion re-ran the in-memory benchmark (2026-10-20).
- **Coordination limit:**
  > None published; the SPEC lists scale targets as open (D-12). Inference from the code only: every lost race repeats the full rebase and its metadata transactions, with no batching on the single main ref, so throughput on one ref is bounded by the length of one land, and after 32 lost attempts the client must retry. The agent count at which this starts to hurt is unknown.

#### Quality gates

- **CI gating:**
  > None inside the product. Landing runs no tests and has no batching, speculation or affected-target analysis; the SPEC leaves testing to a service outside, after landing, and lists land policies (CI gates, reviews) as deferred.
- **Broken main recovery:**
  > Only a naming convention: a CI service may move a green/\* ref to the last known-good changeset. No bisect, no revert command, no automatic revert; an administrator can force the ref back.
- **Review model:**
  > None. Stable ChangeIds exist so that an outside review tool could follow a change across revisions, but there is no review feature, approval rule or pull-request equivalent.

#### Agent operability

- **Agent interface:**
  > CLI 'scion' with login, whoami, checkout, sparse, status, diff, commit, amend, goto, rebase, resolve, land, log, blame, show, cat and redact; --json output with versioned schemas (schemas/v1), an error envelope and fixed exit codes (0 ok, 1 error, 2 usage, 3 conflicts, 4 dirty). A gRPC API documented by its proto files (Object, Query, Write, Ref, Auth and Admin services). Non-interactive use through service principals (SCION_SERVICE_SECRET or SCION_TOKEN). Missing: an MCP server, a skill or instruction file for agents that use it, a revert command, and a CLI command to create refs (only the RPC, with admin rights).
- **Undo and recovery:**
  > Changesets are immutable and on the server once committed. amend, goto, rebase --abort and a crash journal (pending_operation) cover local mistakes; \<ref>@\<version> addresses any earlier state of main through the ref log. Undoing a landed change means landing the opposite change by hand or an admin force update: there is no undo or revert command.
- **Agent cost:**
  > No token or setup measurements published. Two commands per change (commit, land) and no retry loop when main moved. Extra costs: agents know git, not scion, and no agent instructions ship; after a land conflict the CLI user needs a second command to learn which paths conflict; checkout on real backends was slower than Git in the author's own test.
- **Access scope and provenance:**
  > Identity: people log in with GitHub; agents are named service principals with a shared secret (one per agent is possible); PASETO tokens last 12 hours. Permissions: acl.toml with longest-prefix read and write rules per path and rules for ref update and force; it hot-reloads, applies to past content too, and is re-checked on every landing attempt. Content redaction (quarantine, purge) works without changing ids. Ref log and audit log record the principal; the server sets the creator on each changeset, while author is free text. No field for session, model or prompt (only generic metadata extensions through the API). No sandbox. Documented limits: restricted directory names and commit messages are visible to every reader. Per-principal rate limit, 500 requests/s by default.
- **Who runs synchronisation:**
  > The agent triggers it (commit, land); the server does the rebase and handles races. The agent is left with real conflicts and with running tests before landing. Nothing decides who works on what, so that needs an outside orchestrator.

#### Evidence

- **Evidence quality:**
  > Mostly author self-report, but more checkable than usual: a full SPEC, open benchmark scripts with raw data, reference-model, differential and linearizability tests, and public CI logs. Two outside re-runs, one by a project that vendors it and one by a competitor. Main weaknesses: no landing throughput, no production use, and a full test tier that has never been green in nightly CI (logged causes: runner disk space, an intermittent symlink test and a seed-guard test). The in-memory benchmark numbers look better than those on real backends.
- **Independent reports:**
  > (1) culebraluxe/forgevcs (0 stars, vendors the code): docs/BASELINE.md (2026-10-16) ran the unmodified commit e0ffadf9 on macOS, 1,337 tests passed and 0 failed in 19 min 56 s, and in the full tier 1,445 passed and 1 failed (a test-seed guard, not a product bug); this is an independent, synthetic test run. It judged its own PostgreSQL port ready for a controlled pilot, not for prime time (2026-10-22). Its pilot (3 agents, 46 of 46 checks, 2026-10-18) and soak (10 agents on 10 separate repositories, 712 HTTP attempts, 0 lost refs, 2026-10-22) are the fork's own synthetic runs of a modified engine and say little about contention on one ref. It also found that a conflict report filtered by read permission can hide a conflict on a path the caller may write but not read (TECH_DEBT TD-12). (2) stigenai/scion, a competitor: re-ran the in-memory benchmark on an Apple M5 (report of 2026-10-20; independent of the author, synthetic, by a rival); the code builds and beats Git there, while their own system was faster in all 8 cells. (3) tekeburak/scion, a competitor: issue #20 (2026-10-15) recommends borrowing its complexity gates and layer check, and its competitive notes record it as quiet since 2026-10-16. (4) rana/continuum assessment (2026-10-19): production qualification still open, benchmarks leave out disk costs. Hacker News (Algolia) and Lobsters returned no discussion; Reddit could not be searched. A moses-y blog post (2026-11-12) was left out because it gets the licence and the commands wrong.

#### Fit

- **Best for:**
  > Reading and testing: teams that design their own landing service or want a precise, testable description of a Scion-like system. Also a base for a fork, if you are ready to own 23 Rust crates and CockroachDB.
- **Failure modes and risks:**
  > Abandonment: one author, dormant since 2026-10-16. No way back to Git. Heavy stack (CockroachDB with a non-open licence, S3, TLS, keys). Optimistic retries without batching may thrash on one hot ref (inference, no measurement). Nothing stops a semantically broken merge: no tests, review, bisect or revert. The CLI land conflict has no path names. TD-12 (reported by forgevcs): a hidden conflict on paths that are writable but not readable. A rename plus an edit becomes a conflict. Nightly CI that is always red can hide regressions. Backend-sensitive: a CockroachDB statistics problem made 8 concurrent commits take 1.27 s until a fix brought it to 0.20 s (1a49df05, self-reported, synthetic). Written by agents in about nine hours, with no known human audit. Windows clients unsupported.
- **Minimum useful scale:**
  > No threshold published. Researcher judgement: not below several hundred agents, or a repository where clone and checkout cost dominates; below that, worktrees plus a merge queue give more (tests, review, Git) for less work.
- **Fit today 5 to 30 agents:**
  > Skip (lab use only). It builds and its landing path is well tested, but there is no release, active maintainer, Git exit, test gate or revert, and it needs CockroachDB plus S3. At 5-30 agents its main gain (no client retry because main moved) is what a merge queue already gives. Compared with feder-cr/git-ai (Go, v0.4.2 of 2026-11-05, Git objects underneath, batched landing lane, post-merge bisect and revert, cheap exit): git-ai is the more practical pilot. Compared with bnsd55/openscion (Rust, v0.1.0 signed binaries of 2026-11-01, active, PostgreSQL plus files): openscion is easier to run and publishes landing figures, but treats two changes to the same path as a conflict, while this project merges lines.
- **Fit target hundreds of agents:**
  > Skip as a product; keep as a design reference and look again only if development resumes or a fork takes over. For: lock-free landing across several servers, line-level merge on the server, idempotent landing, fine path permissions and redaction, and the most thorough specification and tests of the three. Against: no landing throughput at any scale (feder-cr/git-ai self-reports 794-1,944 lands/min for up to 100 agents, bnsd55/openscion 28-32 lands/s for up to 200 writers; both synthetic and self-reported, and not comparable with each other), no batching on the hot ref, correctness shown only up to 100 clients, no CI gating or recovery, no Git exit, no active maintainer.
- **Transferable practice:**
  > Give every change a stable id and keep a 'landed' index, so a repeated land request is harmless. Let the server rebase and retry with full-jitter backoff instead of asking the agent to pull and push again. Re-check permissions and policy on each attempt. Return conflicts as data (paths, kinds, line ranges, plus what merged cleanly), and make sure the CLI passes it on. Make the rebase deterministic (no wall clock) so that retries give the same ids. Count storage operations per land in tests as a guard against growth. Test landing with a reference model and linearizability checks under kills and delays. Build method: freeze a SPEC and interfaces first, then give parallel agents work packages with disjoint file ownership and a fresh reviewer each (34 packages in about nine hours by commit times). Lesson to copy too: check that the full test tier really runs and is green.

Commits 7d445a07 (2026-10-15), da0a3932, 1a49df05. https://github.com/CarterT27/scion/issues/1 (2026-11-02). CI: https://github.com/CarterT27/scion/actions/runs/45405051549 (2026-11-24). Independent: https://github.com/culebraluxe/forgevcs (docs/BASELINE.md 2026-10-16, docs/M1-agent-pilot.md 2026-10-18, docs/SOAK-2026-10-22.md, TECH_DEBT.md TD-12); https://github.com/stigenai/scion/blob/main/docs/reports/2026-10-20-openscion-workloads.md; https://github.com/tekeburak/scion/issues/20 (2026-10-15) and docs/competitive-notes.md (last changed 2026-10-26); https://github.com/rana/continuum (docs/history/01-scion-assessment.md, 2026-10-19). Context: https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/redesigning-source-control (2026-10-13); https://docs.cockroachlabs.com/docs/stable/licensing-faqs (read 2026-11-25).
- **Uncertainty notes:**
  > Nothing was built or run for this assessment: behaviour comes from the code, SPEC, schemas, CI logs and other people's reports. Landing throughput, behaviour above 100 clients and the coordination limit are unknown; the limit is an inference, and no other project's figures are borrowed for it. Why development stopped is not stated. The CLI conflict output was read, not tested. WSL support is an inference. Sources disagree once: a tekeburak/scion note says the repository was pushed on 2026-10-25, while the GitHub API gives 2026-10-16; the API is used. Whether stigenai/scion copies an OpenCSGs repository is unconfirmed (that repository returns 404). Star history comes from dated third-party notes; only the 2026-11-24 count is from the API. Reddit could not be searched.
